# Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt (Chapter 29) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**A House Built of Silk: The Name That Announces the Theme**

Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt, "The Spider," takes its name from a single image in verse 41 — *The parable of those who take protectors apart from God is that of the spider that makes a house. Truly the frailest of houses is the spider's house, if they but knew* (29:41). Nothing else in the sūrah is about spiders. Yet the image is a key that opens every one of its sixty-nine verses, because the spider's web is the Qur'an's chosen emblem for the architecture of the unprotected life. A web is engineering of astonishing precision: the spider spins from its own body, works with geometric regularity, and produces a trap that genuinely catches flies. It also genuinely shelters nothing. Rain passes through it, a child's hand destroys it, an ox's breath disperses it. This is exactly the sūrah's diagnosis of every refuge a human being constructs apart from God — political alliances, inherited creeds, wealth, reputation, tribe, ideology, the self's own ingenuity: impressive while functioning, weightless under the first real pressure. The sūrah was revealed to people who were learning this diagnosis in their bodies — persecuted believers in Makkah whose families, clans, and whole social order were being stripped away from them — and its answer is not pessimism but re-engineering: move the house, so to speak, from silk to the Rock.

**The Sūrah of Trial**

If the web gives the sūrah its image, the word *fitnah* gives it its spine. The sūrah bursts into the revelation with a question that has never stopped echoing: *Does mankind suppose that they will be left to say, "We believe," and that they will not be tried?* (29:2). The verb *fatana* originally meant to melt gold and silver in the fire so that dross separates from metal, and the sūrah uses this family of words across its whole span: trial of persecution (29:2–3, 10–11), trial of prosperity and delay (29:4, 52–53), the trial of parents' pressure (29:8), the trial of the fire that became a garden for Abraham (29:24), the trial of the body's desires in Lot's people (29:28–29), the trial of economic cunning in Midian (29:36), the trial of the drowning, shaking, and crumbling ends of the proud (29:40), and finally the trial of the storm at sea that strips a person down to honesty (29:65). The sūrah's theology of suffering is correspondingly precise: affliction is not evidence of divine abandonment but the standard procedure by which God authenticates claimants. Al-Ṭabarī, summarizing the earliest commentators, glosses verse 2: God informs them that He tests the people of faith with hardship precisely so that the true believer is distinguished from the false, "for God knows the truthful of them and the liars before any trial occurs, but He establishes the proof against His servants through what they themselves experience." Ibn Kathīr adds the practical corollary: whoever reads this sūrah and does not expect tribulation has not read it.

**Chronology, Occasion, and the Disputed First Ten Verses**

The overwhelming majority of exegetes classify al-ʿAnkabūt as Makkan, and the internal evidence strongly supports this: its rhetoric of persecution, its short verses, its concentration on ʿaqīdah, resurrection, and prophetic history rather than law all belong to the Meccan profile. A substantial minority report, however, that the opening verses — most say through verse 10, some through verse 11 — were revealed in Madinah, and a small group (transmitted from Ibn al-Jawzī's catalogue) considers the sūrah Madinan with Makkan insertions. A related report from ʿĀʾishah states that the Prophet ﷺ would recite Sūrahs 29 and 30 together in his prayers, sometimes followed by Sūrah 36 — al-Rūm being the sūrah that opens with the very same claim this sūrah opens with: *Alif. Lām. Mīm. The Romans have been defeated* (30:1–2), so that the pair moves from the metaphysics of trial to its most concrete contemporary illustration: the defeat and imminent reversal of Byzantium. The reconciliation most commentators prefer is elegant: the sūrah is Makkan in bulk, but its first verses about emigration and persecution speak to circumstances that matured in Madinah, and revelation frequently returns to a verse for a new occasion. In the standard lists of chronological order the sūrah falls in the late Makkan group, near al-Rūm — though the numbering itself differs between the transmissions of Mujāhid, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Suyūṭī, a caution against over-confidence in any exact placement.

**The Structure: Six Movements in One Argument**

The sūrah is carefully composed, and its sequence of themes is argumentative rather than accidental. (1) Verses 1–13 establish the law of trial, the emptiness of bare profession, the non-transferability of sins, and the family crisis — a believer's parents urging apostasy — that was the sharpest real-world test in Makkah. (2) Verses 14–43 unfold sacred history as a laboratory: Noah's nine centuries and fifty, Abraham's argument and his fire, Lot's besieged household, Shuʿayb's market-fraud, then ʿĀd, Thamūd, Korah, Pharaoh, and Hāmān — each destroyed by a different element, so that verse 40 amounts to a catalogue of every means of ruin available to creation, with the conclusion: *God wronged them not, but themselves did they wrong* (29:40). (3) Verse 41 places at the centre of this history the spider's parable — the thesis of the whole sūrah — and verse 43 explains why parables are the medium God chose for such knowledge. (4) Verses 44–51 turn from creation's signs to the Book's sign: prayer as moral armour, dispute with the People of the Book "in the most virtuous manner," and the unlettered Prophet as evidence that no human authorship stands behind the Qur'an. (5) Verses 52–63 answer the disbelievers' demands: why punishment is delayed, why miracles are withheld, why provision is stretched and narrowed, and why the very idolaters already know the answer to "who created the heavens and the earth?" (6) Verses 64–69 close, as the spider's web opened, on the frailty of the world — *The life of this world is naught but diversion and play* (29:64) — and the sūrah's final promise, one of the most quoted sentences in the Qur'an: *But as for those who strive for Us, We shall surely guide them in Our ways* (29:69). The ring-composition is exact: the sūrah begins by warning that a claimed faith untested is no faith, and ends by promising that a striven-for path will be widened with guidance.

**The Universe of Worship and the Question of the Weak**

What distinguishes al-ʿAnkabūt among the Makkan sūrahs is its sociological realism. It is preoccupied with people whose faith is real but whose nerve is not. Verses 2–3, 8–11 address precisely such people — those who wished to believe but feared the punishment of men as though it were God's, those whose mothers starved themselves to bring them back to polytheism, those who would not emigrate. The sūrah never calls them hypocrites in bulk; verse 10 describes a man who "says *we believe in God*" and then buckles, and verse 11 hands the classification to God: *Surely God knows those who believe, and surely He knows the hypocrites.* This is a striking pastoral achievement: the community is taught to distinguish the failure of courage from the failure of faith, while being taught that courage is nonetheless obligatory and that "was not God's earth vast enough that you might have migrated therein?" (4:97) remains a standing question over every comfortable silence. Classical commentators, especially al-Qurṭubī in his legal extrapolations, drew from these verses the rulings on residence under persecution, the limits of obedience to parents, and the obligation of *hijrah*; and the sūrah's own remedy for the frightened is not exhortation alone but a change of scale — the world in verse 64, the grave in verse 57, the rooms of the Garden in verse 58 — as if to say: fear shrinks when perspective widens.

**The Qur'an's Longest Dialogue with Its Rivals**

Al-ʿAnkabūt also contains one of the most complete statements of Islamic self-understanding among scripture-bearing people. Verse 46 commands dispute "save in the most virtuous manner" and dictates the shared confession: *our God and your God are one, and unto Him are we submitters* (29:46), while verses 47–49 insist that the Qur'an's evidence is not a spectacle but its clarity, preserved in the hearts of the learned, and verse 48 offers as external evidence the Prophet's well-known illiteracy, which forecloses the charge of human composition. The sūrah thus pairs theological firmness with procedural gentleness: no ambiguity about truth, no licence for abuse in its pursuit. Al-Ṭabarī's refusal to accept the abrogation of verse 46 by the sword-verse — "a verse is not abrogated except on definitive proof" — remains a landmark in the jurisprudence of interfaith relations, cited again and again by modern scholars precisely because the verse's manners are not optional.

**How to Read the Commentary That Follows**

The verses below are presented one at a time, each as an independent study: the Arabic vocabulary and morphology in plain terms, the sūrah's own internal cross-references, the actual texts of relevant Qur'anic passages and authentic ḥadīth with their collections named, the positions of al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī, Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Taymiyyah and other classical authorities, the occasions of revelation where they are reported, and the psychological, social and contemporary reach of each verse's argument. Verses that the printed source discusses in groups have here been separated and given individual treatment, since each verse carries its own burden in the sūrah's argument. Where a report is weak or disputed, that status is stated rather than concealed; where the exegetes differ, the disagreement is set out with its reasons. The single aim is the aim of the sūrah itself: that the reader should not merely "be left to say" a belief, but should understand what he or she is saying, why it is true, and what it costs.

---

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:1

> **Alif. Lām. Mīm.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Letters and a Whole Epistemology**

The sūrah opens, as al-Baqarah, Āl ʿImrān, Luqmān and al-Sajdah do, with three isolated consonants pronounced as their names rather than their sounds: *alif-lām-mīm*. In Arabic they are called *al-ḥurūf al-muqaṭṭaʿāt* — "the cut-off letters," letters severed from the words they normally build. Twenty-nine sūrahs begin with such letters, and this combination occurs five times in the Qur'an: at 2:1, 3:1, and here at 29:1, 30:1 and 32:1 — a clustering that the tradition itself noticed, since the pairing of al-ʿAnkabūt and al-Rūm in the Prophet's ﷺ prayer (transmitted from ʿĀʾishah) is exactly a pairing of two *Alif. Lām. Mīm* sūrahs that both deal with history under God's judgment. The very fact that fifteen Makkan sūrahs and fourteen Madinan ones are prefaced in this way is itself a datum: the letters belong to no single occasion, theme, or legal moment, and any interpretation that ties them exclusively to one context has already missed their function.

**What the Earliest Scholars Said and Why They Stopped Short**

The honest reporting of the exegetical tradition is that the Companions and the generation after them did not produce a single agreed explanation. Al-Ṭabarī, whose method is to enumerate and then weigh, gives two opinions and declines to force a third: some of them said these are letters that only God knows their meaning — that is, not that they are meaningless, but that their intended reference is not disclosed — and others said they are names of the sūrahs, or names of the Qur'an itself, as al-Qurṭubī records in this very passage, noting the view that *Alif Lām Mīm* functions here as a title for the sūrah or for the Book. Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah stated the governing etiquette with characteristic brevity: these letters are "a secret of God (*sirr Allāh*); He places them where He wills, and we do not expound them." Ibn Kathīr reports that sentence and adds the standard Salafī position — affirm, recite, and do not press beyond the transmitted material. This restraint is not intellectual laziness. It is an epistemic discipline deliberately taught by the text: the believer learns, at the head of a sūrah, that some things in God's speech are to be received before they are mastered.

**The Strongest Interpretive Reason: A Challenge Issued in Ordinary Bricks**

Beside the position of "only God knows," the majority of analytically-minded exegetes have favoured a second line, and it is the one the surrounding verses most strongly support. Alif, lām and mīm are the ordinary bricks of Arabic. Every hearer owned them; no one could claim a special dialect, a foreign register, or secret vocabulary. By beginning with the alphabet, the sūrah announces: *the miracle you are being offered is assembled from letters you already possess* — and yet no one has been able to produce its like. Al-Rāzī argues this at length, holding that the letters are a challenge (*taḥaddī*) and an allusion to the inimitability of the Qur'an, as if to say: this Book made of your own letters is beyond you. Ibn Taymiyyah, in his treatment of the muqaṭṭaʿāt, similarly regards them as among the most eloquent openings precisely because they function as attention-holding summons (*tanbīh*) that then allow the miracle-claim to land. The sequence in this sūrah bears that reading out: the letters are followed immediately by the demand for proof under trial (29:2–3), by the exposure of false judgment (29:4), and by the statement that the Qur'an consists of *clear signs in the breasts of those who have been given knowledge* (29:49). A sūrah that opens with letters and closes its argument with "signs" has made its point about what kind of evidence it is offering: not a circus of prodigies, but a text.

**The Phonetics of the Opening: A Small Lesson with a Long Shadow**

Linguists and adab writers observed something tactile about this particular trio. *Alif* is the first letter of the Arabic abjad and is articulated from the deepest part of the throat — an opening made with the least possible movement of the tongue; *lām* sits in the middle of the alphabet and is formed at the front of the mouth, the tip of the tongue rising to the gum behind the teeth; *mīm* closes the sequence with the lips pressed shut, one of the last articulations in the classical ordering. The three letters therefore traverse the mouth from its farthest recess to its very rim. The Arabic rhetorical tradition drew the moral: God opens the sūrah with the beginning, the middle and the end of human speech-formation, indicating that the whole instrument of the hearer's own expression has been laid on the table as the standard of comparison. Whatever weight one gives that observation — and it is a matter of *balāgha*, not doctrine — it does capture a real feature of Qur'anic pedagogy: the Qur'an is a miracle *inside* language, and language is treated as something created, measured and owned by God. It is not nothing that the *Hurūf* at the head of a sūrah are the same letters a child learns.

**The Arithmetic Reading and the Limits of Number-Mysticism**

There is an early report, transmitted in ʿAbd al-Razzāq's *Muṣannaf* and elsewhere, in which Ibn ʿAbbās is said to have unpacked the letters by their numerical values (*al-ḥisāb al-jummel*, abjad): alif = one, lām = thirty, mīm = ninety, yielding a period. Some later writers applied such reckonings to these five *Alif Lām Mīm* sūrahs. Whether or not the attribution is sound, it is instructive for another reason: the tradition's serious exegetes never made such arithmetic a proof-text, and al-Suyūṭī in *al-Itqān* records the number-reports while making clear that the letters' meaning remains with God. Contemporary readers meet a more seductive version of the same temptation — modern campaigns that claim a mathematical code built on the muqaṭṭaʿāt, sometimes counting occurrences of letters to generate an arithmetic miracle, occasionally with claims so tendentious that the scholars of the Muslim world publicly repudiated them and, in several countries, formally declared their consequences (including the claim that the Qur'an was "sealed" with nineteen) to be outside Islam. The principled answer is contained in the classical restraint itself: a numeric pattern, if one exists, is an ornament, never a foundation. Doctrine is established by clear text, not by arithmetical feats, and any method that requires the reader to count letters while ignoring what the verse plainly says has inverted the relation between signifier and meaning. Al-ʿAnkabūt itself teaches the same lesson twice over: the disbelievers demanded spectacle-signs (29:50), and were answered with a Book (29:51).

**Why the Believer Still Pauses at These Letters**

There is an experiential reason the muqaṭṭaʿāt have survived fourteen centuries of recitation without being explained away. The worshipper who recites *Alif. Lām. Mīm.* is momentarily disarmed: the familiar has been made unfamiliar, and attention — that scarce resource — is gathered. Preachers and teachers exploit the same device: an abrupt silence before a statement; a speaker who begins with a single word rather than a sentence. The letters also discipline the ego of the reader. The first thing a Muslim does upon opening this sūrah is to pronounce something he cannot fully claim to understand — and to do so in a posture of reverence. Then, and only then, does the sūrah tell him what understanding will be tested by: *Does mankind suppose that they will be left to say, "We believe," and that they will not be tried?* (29:2). The link between verse 1 and verse 2 is thus not decorative. Uncertainty about meaning is the first lesson, and the trial of that meaning is the second.

**The Modern Relevance of Admitting a Limit**

Every intellectual community has its muqaṭṭaʿāt — the points at which its own foundational text declines to be fully transparent to its readers' demand for total explanation. In a culture of instant answers, where any ambiguity in a source is treated as evidence against it, the opening of this sūrah is quietly counter-cultural: a scripture that says "here is something whose meaning I have not spelled out" is not thereby weakened; it is teaching its readers how to read — with submission preceding comprehension, with questions honestly held, and with the awareness that mastery is not the appropriate first relation to revelation. This is why the classical scholars could simultaneously say "God knows best what is meant" and write volumes exploring what the letters suggest: restraint and inquiry were not contradictions. The competent reader knows which questions are answerable and how to hold the others. That is a skill transferable to any discipline worth learning.

**A Sūrah Announced Before It Is Explained**

Finally, the letters function as a title-bearing preamble, which is why many exegetes counted them as verses in their own right. In almost every canonical *mushaf* tradition, *Alif Lām Mīm* is numbered as the first verse of this sūrah; the reciter pauses there and counts it in the sixty-nine. The sūrah has therefore, in its very first breath, declared itself a named, numbered, recited object — a unit of worship and not merely of information. Before the spider spins its frail house, before Abraham walks into the fire, before the hypocrite's nerve is exposed and before God promises to guide those who strive, three letters are laid down like a foundation stone: the Word of God is unlike what it resembles, and it will be understood by those who let it, in its own time, mean what it means.

## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:2

> **Does mankind suppose that they will be left to say, “We believe,” and that they will not be tried,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Refutes an Assumption Before It Is Spoken**

The verse is framed as an interrogative of reproach: *a-ḥasiba al-nāsu an yutrakū an yaqūlū āmannā wa-hum lā yuftanūn* — "Did people reckon that they would be left to say 'we believe,' while they are not put through the fire?" The Arabic *ḥasiba* means to reckon, to suppose, to calculate something as being the case; the same root gives us *ḥisāb*, account. The implied verb of thinking is the target, not the lips. Nobody in the verse is accused of having said "we believe" falsely; the accusation is of having *assumed* that saying it settles the matter. The double *an* is decisive for the grammar: *yutrakū an yaqūlū* — "be left [merely] to say." Being "left" (*tark*) here means being left unexamined, unengaged, not called upon. The whole verse, in other words, attacks a theory of religion: the theory that the claim is the completion. This is why the sūrah's opening does its work in the makkan environment of persecution rather than in a lecture hall; the sentence that sounds abstract in comfort — "you will be tested" — was in Makkah a description of Tuesday.

**Fitnah: The Metallurgy of Belief**

The verb *ftana* is one of the Qur'an's great physical metaphors. In pre-Islamic usage it meant to apply fire to gold or silver so that its alloy burns away and its real value is exposed; poets and lexicographers use it for testing coin, and the Qur'an extends it to testing people (cf. 22:11, *wa-min al-nāsi man yaʿbudu Allāha ʿalā ḥarf* — the man who "worships God on one edge," turning at the first blow, quoted below). Trial in this worldview is not punishment inflicted on a defendant but assay performed on metal: the fire does not create the gold, it shows whether there was any. Note also the passive, *lā yuftanūn* — "they are not tried" — because the point is that the testing is done *to* them, not achieved *by* them. Human beings do not choose the terms, timing, or intensity of their examination. This single grammatical choice quietly demolishes the notion that faith is a self-administered state whose progress one schedules. The Examiner is named in the next verse, and He does not require the results: *Surely God knows those who are truthful, and surely He knows the liars* (29:3). The trial exists for the tested, not for the tester.

**The Occasion: A Martyr's Grieving Family and the Believers Stuck in Makkah**

Two principal occasions of revelation are reported, and they are complementary rather than contradictory. The first, transmitted by Ibn al-Jawzī, al-Qurṭubī and al-Wāḥidī, ties verses 2–3 to Mihjaʿ, the client of ʿUmar and the first of the Muslims to be killed at Badr: his mother and wife were overcome with grief, and revelation came to establish that belief does not exempt a person from suffering for God's sake. The second, reported by al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī and Ibn al-Jawzī, concerns a group of Meccan believers — among them, in several accounts, weak and enslaved Muslims — who attempted the emigration to Madinah, were intercepted and beaten back by the polytheists, and then felt their faith pressed to breaking point. When the opening of this sūrah reached them they attempted the road again; some were killed, some got through, and a later verse confirmed their standing: *Then indeed thy Lord, for those who emigrated after being oppressed, then strove and were patient, surely thy Lord thereafter is Forgiving, Merciful* (16:110). That the same two verses could be sent down for a bereaved family and for frightened travellers is exactly as it should be: the law of *fitnah* is general, and grief and cowardice are the two most common shapes it takes.

**The Qur'an's Consistent Verdict: Nobody Is Exempt**

The claim refuted in this verse — *āmannā*, therefore *we are left* — is answered in the Qur'an so often that the answer amounts to a doctrine. To this sūrah's own nearest cousin:

> *"Or did you suppose that you would enter the Garden without there having come to you the like of that which came to those who passed away before you? Misfortune and hardship befell them, and they were so shaken that the Messenger and those who believed with him said, 'When will God's help come?' Yea, surely God's help is near."* (Qur'an 2:214)

And:

> *"Or do you suppose that you will enter the Garden while God has not yet known (*yaʿlam*) those among you who strive, and has not yet known the steadfast?"* (Qur'an 3:142)

> *"We will certainly try you until We know those among you who strive and are steadfast, and until We test (*nablu*) your reports."* (Qur'an 47:31)

The verb *yablū* (from *balāʾ*, trial) and *yaftin* belong to the same family of ideas, and the pairing with *mujāhidīn* and *ṣābirīn* in 47:31 shows what is being measured: effort and endurance, the two capacities that only pressure reveals. The reason this is built into the design of creation is stated outright:

> *"He who created death and life that He might try you as to which of you is best in deed."* (Qur'an 67:2)

Note the standard of the assay there: not "most," but *aḥsan* — "most beautiful" in action. The trial is not a survival contest but a beauty contest conducted under difficulty.

**What the Ascetics and the Theologians Drew From It**

Al-Tustarī (d. 283/896), cited by al-Sulamī in this very passage, said: *"Truly affliction is a doorway between the people of spiritual knowledge (ahl al-maʿrifah) and God."* His logic is that ease leaves the self unexamined and therefore unfit for intimacy with God, while hardship breaks the two veils — attachment to comfort and confidence in one's own state — through which no knowledge of God passes. Ibn Kathīr reads the verses practically: God informs this community that being tried is a *sunnah* concerning His servants, so the believer should prepare, not hope to be spared. Al-Rāzī extracts a further argument: if trial were absent, obedience would be indistinguishable from habit, and praise for habit is incoherent; moral credit requires a situation in which refusal was possible. Ibn Taymiyyah, arguing against the quietist drift of his age, repeatedly insisted that *fitna* is not a sign of God's anger but of His pedagogy, and that a community that expects to be spared the trials of the prophets has, in that expectation itself, already deviated from prophecy.

**The ḥadīth Attached to This Verse**

The Prophet ﷺ spoke to precisely the psychological state this verse addresses, and the tradition preserved his words alongside it. Al-Bukhārī (5641) and Muslim 2573 transmit from Abū Hurayrah and others:

> *"No fatigue, illness, sorrow, harm, or distress befalls a Muslim — even a thorn that pricks him — but that God thereby expiates some of his sins."*

Al-Tirmidhī (2399) transmits from ʿĀʾishah:

> *"Affliction does not cease to befall the believing man and woman in their selves, their children, and their property, until they meet God with no sin upon them."* — graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Ḥākim and others.

And in al-Bukhārī (5652) and Muslim, Ṣaʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ reported: "I said, 'Messenger of God, which people are most severely tried?' He said, *'The prophets, then the next best and the next best. A man is tried according to his religion; if his firmness in religion is strong, his trial increases…'"* These are not consoling platitudes appended to a hard verse; they specify its mechanism. The trial is simultaneously an assay (v. 3's manifestation of the truthful and the liars) and an erasure of dross — which is why the believer's posture toward suffering in Islam is never pure endurance but, more often, bewildered gratitude.

**The Psychological Machinery of the Sentence**

Modern psychology, which has studied post-traumatic growth extensively, describes what this verse assumes: people do not become more stable by being protected from difficulty but by integrating it into a coherent account of who they are. A faith that has never cost anything cannot inform character, because it has never been consulted; it remains a belief *about* the person rather than a commitment *of* the person. Conversely, unprepared suffering does damage that faith alone cannot automatically repair — which is why the Qur'an pre-notifies rather than surprising. The verse is a piece of inoculation theology: expose the assumption *before* the fever arrives. Note its tact as well: it addresses *al-nās*, "mankind," not "you, the weak." The correction is offered to the whole human race, including the reader, so that no one can receive it as an insult aimed at somebody else.

**Where This Verse Lands Today**

Every movement, including religious ones, contains members whose commitment is proportional to the pleasantness of the experience — volunteers who leave when the work becomes unglamorous, new believers who vanish the first time their practice costs them a relationship, an income, or a reputation. The sūrah does not condemn the desire for comfort; it denies the logic of exemption. Applied to a single person today, it asks a concrete question: at what price would you stop saying this? If there is such a price and you know it, you have not been "left"; you are simply in a trial that has not yet begun. If there is no such price, the verse invites you to verify that in the arena rather than in the imagination, because the only proof that a claim of faith carries is the willingness of the claimant to be tried — and to be tried *well*, with the beauty that 67:2 sets as the standard.

**A Final Observation on the Comma**

The verse does not end. It stops mid-syntax, and the next verse continues *wa-la-qad fatannā* — "though We have indeed tried those before them." The broken sentence is itself the rhetoric: a question without its answer is an unsettled mind, and the sūrah leaves us there for one breath, precisely as the person under trial feels — the question asked, the answer still arriving.

## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:3

> **though We have indeed tried those who were before them? Surely God knows those who are truthful, and surely He knows the liars.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Layers of Emphasis in Half a Line**

The Arabic is *wa-la-qad fatannā alladhīna min qablihim*. Count the confirmation devices: the opening conjunction *wa* joining the clause to what precedes, the oath particle *lām* attached to the verb, and *qad* marking the perfect tense as certainly realized. Arabic rhetoric counts this as a triple emphasis — the most heavily loaded construction available for a simple past statement. Why pile up certainty on a fact nobody disputes? Because the dispute was never about whether people suffer; it was about whether their suffering *means* something within God's governance. The verse asserts, with every grammatical resource available, that trial is not an anomaly in the divine conduct but its established precedent — *the way We have already dealt with those who came before.*

**The Precedent as Proof-Text**

"Those before them" (*alladhīna min qablihim*) is glossed by al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī as the communities of the earlier prophets — the followers of Moses and Jesus and others who were persecuted for holding to belief and endured. The technique is quintessentially Qur'anic: an uncomfortable present demand is met not with a new argument but with a reference to a pattern already documented. Compare the closely parallel formulation:

> *"Do you suppose that you will enter the Garden while there has not come to you the like of what came to those who passed away before you? Poverty and hardship afflicted them and they were shaken."* (Qur'an 2:214)

and the sūrah's own later restatement of the same method: *So each We seized for his sin* (29:40), and *But if you deny, then communities have certainly denied before you* (29:18). This sūrah will spend twenty-seven verses (14–40) re-laying the precedent in full detail; verse 3 is the announcement of that argument before the evidence arrives.

**Why an All-Knowing God Tests: The Central Question of the Verse**

The second half of the verse answers the objection that the whole passage provokes. If God already knows who is truthful and who is lying, what does a test accomplish? Al-Ṭabarī's answer, quoted approvingly by nearly everyone after him, is that God's testing is not for information but for manifestation (*li-yatabayyana*): He makes the true nature of each person evident *to that person and to others*, so that the unfolding of events corresponds to what He has known from before, and no one can claim at the end that they would have been different under other conditions. Al-Rāzī sharpens the logic: a judgment that is only ever exercised in a world without pressure is an untested claim; the test converts a claim into an accomplished fact. Ibn Kathīr stresses that the recompense attaches to what is enacted, not to what is merely potential.

This is the point at which the verse speaks against a background of real theological controversy. The classical schools — Ashʿarī and Māturīdī alike — affirmed God's prior, comprehensive, eternal knowledge of all that will occur; the objection was never "does God know?" but "then what is the point?" Some Muʿtazilī-leaning and later rationalist readers preferred to read *li-yaʿlam* ("so that He knows") in verses like 2:155 and 47:31 as "so that it becomes known," explicitly to protect God from acquiring information. Here, at 29:3, that problem does not arise: the verb is *yaʿlamu* in the indicative as a statement of fact — God *does* know — and the trial is disclosed as the mechanism by which knowledge becomes biography. Ibn Taymiyyah treats the issue squarely: God's knowledge is not the cause of the servant's act, and the act's manifestation in time is not an addition to God's knowledge; the sequence is real for the creature and unnecessary for the Creator, which is exactly the structure this verse expresses.

**The Pastoral Payoff: Trials as X-Ray, Not Verdict**

Readers in distress habitually misread their condition. The believer who is crushed by persecution wonders whether God has withdrawn; the person whose sincere effort keeps failing suspects hypocrisy; the community that loses everything assumes it has lost God. This verse reframes the entire diagnostic: suffering is the assay that *distinguishes* the sincere from the false, and the fact that you are being tried is therefore not evidence against your faith but evidence that your faith is being taken seriously enough to be verified. Al-Tustarī's "affliction is a doorway" (cited on the previous verse) is the same insight from the interior side. And dross removal continues to be operative: as the hadith literature cited under verse 2 makes plain, the trial doubles as expiation, so that even a trial the believer fails imperfectly can yield purification — the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"God says: When I test My servant by taking the two he loves [his eyes] and he patiently endures, I shall replace him with Paradise."* (al-Bukhārī 5653, from Anas ibn Mālik)

**The Two Categories, and the Humility They Enforce**

Notice that the verse sorts humanity into exactly two groups — the truthful (*ṣādiqūn*) and the liars (*kādhibūn*) — but assigns the sorting entirely to God's knowledge. There is no command here to label. The community is told that God knows; it is not told to pronounce. That is the difference between the Qur'an's treatment of the wavering Meccan believers and its treatment of the deliberate hypocrites of Madinah: in this sūrah, verses 10–11 describe the man whose nerve fails and then immediately hand the classification over — *Surely God knows those who believe, and surely God knows the hypocrites* (29:11). A social ethic follows from the grammar: assess yourself rigorously, assess others charitably, and leave the final ledger to the only party who has full information. Communities of belief fracture more often from premature internal verdicts than from external attack, and the verse's structure prevents the verdict from being taken out of God's hands by an anxious committee.

**Ṣidq: The Quality This Sūrah Will Spend Sixty-Six Verses Defining**

*Ṣidq* (truthfulness) in the Qur'an is not primarily accuracy of statement; it is congruence between the inward and the outward under pressure. The same root gives *ṣiddīq*, the title of Abū Bakr, and the sūrah's own *ṣādiqīn* cluster in verse 8 with *ṣabr* (patience) and *taqwā*, exactly as in the well-known ḥadīth of ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ:

> *"Truthfulness leads to righteousness, and righteousness leads to the Garden, and a man keeps telling the truth until he is written with God as a ṣiddīq. Beware of lying, for lying leads to wickedness, and wickedness leads to the Fire, and a man keeps lying until he is written with God as a liar."* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2607, in the parallel from Ibn Masʿūd; Aḥmad's Musnad from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr)

The reason the trial reveals *ṣidq* rather than mere knowledge is that the lie is cheap only while it costs nothing. Every human being, in every age, can identify the point at which their own declared convictions begin to require payment; that point is the assay.

**The Cross-Reference That Frames the Whole Sūrah**

Two other passages should be read beside this one to see the full arc of the idea. First, the warning that the test is not limited to persecution but extends to prosperity:

> *"We will try you with something of fear, hunger, and loss of wealth, lives, and fruits; and give glad tidings to the patient."* (Qur'an 2:155)

Second, the passage that gives trial its purpose in the moral order:

> *"Had God willed, He could have given them victory; but that He might try some of you by means of others."* (Qur'an 47:4)

The first expands the *forms* of trial; the second explains the *mechanics* — that people are, in a real sense, the trial of each other. Persecutors test the persecuted; the patient test the impatient; the generous test the miserly; parents and children test one another (29:8). This is the metaphysics behind this sūrah's very concrete situation, and it is why verse 3's abstract-sounding epistemology ("God knows the truthful and the liars") is the warrant for verse 40's verdict ("God wronged them not, but themselves did they wrong"): if the test is real, the result belongs to the tested.

**What the Verse Asks of Its First Audience**

For the intercepted emigrants of Makkah — the people for whom, in al-Ṭabarī's and al-Qurṭubī's reports, these verses came down — verse 3 was a permission slip and a summons at once. A permission slip, because their failure to get out did not disqualify them: the trial's outcome is not their exposure to God as news but their own gradual formation. A summons, because precedent does not console those who intend to stop: "those before you were tried *and persevered*." When the verses reached them, the reports say, they attempted the emigration a second time, knowing some would die. That is the exact behaviour a verse like this produces in a serious reader: not relief from the risk, but the resolve to undergo the risk with the company of everyone who has done so before.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:4

> **Or do those who commit evil deeds suppose that they will outstrip Us? Evil indeed is the judgment they make!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mirror Held Up on the Other Side**

Verse 2 addressed those who think belief exempts them from trial. Verse 3 gave them precedent and the reassurance of being known. Verse 4 now turns the same searchlight around and points it at the persecutors: *am-i-ntafaqa alladhīna sayyiʾū al-aʿmāl an yasbīqūnā bi'sa mā yaḥkumūn* — or rather, in the sūrah's compressed Arabic, *a-ḥasiba alladhīna ʿamilū al-sayyiʾāti an yasbiqu minā? bi'sa mā yaḥkumūn.* The two groups are structurally identical, and the sūrah is making a claim about symmetry: both the frightened believer and the triumphant oppressor are operating on the same false premise, namely that the present arrangement of things is the final one. The wavering Muslim assumes the trial will not come; the comfortable wrongdoer assumes it has already been cancelled. Both are wrong about the same thing — the horizon.

**"Outstrip Us": The Image of a Race No One Wins**

The verb *sabaqa* is a racing term: to run ahead, to outstrip, to reach the finish first and leave the pursuer behind. In pre-Islamic usage, the one who "outstrips" escapes with the prize and cannot be caught; to be "outrun" is to be left in the dust. The Qur'an repeatedly denials this possibility to God's enemies (cf. 8:59, *wa-lā yaḥsabanna alladhīna kafarū sabaqū innahum lā yuʿjazūn* — "let not the disbelievers suppose that they have outstripped; they cannot frustrate"; and 59:17, where the hypocrite's boast of protection ends *inna Allāha lā yuʿjizuhu shayʾun*). The image is precisely chosen: escape implies a race, and the race implies that God *is chasing*. The disbeliever's mistake is not that he thinks he has gotten away but that he thinks there is no pursuit at all. Note the first-person plural majesty, *minā* — "outstrip *Us*" — a rhetorical intensifier that makes the pursuer not a rival athlete but the Creator of the track. The verse therefore does something unusual: it grants the wrongdoer's own premise (that life is a contest with outcomes) and then corrects only the timetable.

**bi'sa: The Grammar of Censure**

The closing clause, *bi'sa mā yaḥkumūn* — "evil is what they judge" — uses Arabic's dedicated idiom of condemnation. *Bi'sa* ("it is wretched") is the mirror of *niʿma* ("it is excellent"), and these two verbs function in Arabic as evaluative predicates rather than ordinary descriptions; they are used for blame and praise respectively. When the Qur'an wants to register moral verdict on a conclusion, a purchase, a bargain, or an opinion, it uses this construction with *mā* introducing the thing evaluated. It appears in the sūrah's neighbourhood with force: *bi'sa mā sharaū bihi anfusahum* — "wretched is that for which they sold their own selves" (2:102, 9:9); and in this very chapter, *bi'sa mathalu al-qawmi* — "wretched is the example of the people who denied Our signs" (7:177). Here the object of censure is *ḥukm* — judgment, verdict, calculation, decree. What is called "evil" is thus not primarily their desire for comfort, nor even their violence, but their *reasoning*: the inference from "nothing is happening to me now" to "nothing will ever happen to me." Al-Rāzī, in his usual analytical mode, points out that their judgment fails on both available standards of evidence: it is neither supported by rational demonstration nor by transmitted report, and so has no foundation whatsoever — hence, "evil indeed is the judgment they make."

**The Theology of Delay: istiḍrāj**

The single most important concept for this verse in the whole of Islamic theology is *istiḍrāj* — a gradual drawing-on, being lured forward by degrees while the ground gives way beneath. The Qur'an's own formulation is indispensable:

> *"So when they forgot that whereof they had been reminded, We opened for them the gates of everything, until in the very moment when they rejoiced in that which they were given, We seized them suddenly, and behold, they were in despair."* (Qur'an 6:44)

And:

> *"And let not those who disbelieve suppose that Our reprieve for them is good for their souls. We only respite them that they may increase in sins; and for them is a humiliating punishment."* (Qur'an 3:178)

The Prophet ﷺ gave the technical vocabulary to the same reality:

> *"When you see God giving a servant of the world's goods what he loves while he persists in disobedience, that is nothing but istidrāj — a drawing-on by degrees."* Then he recited the verse of the gates opened for everything (6:44). (Aḥmad 14287; al-Ḥākim graded it ṣaḥīḥ, and Dhahabī concurred in his *Talkhīṣ*)

Ibn al-Qayyim devoted sustained discussion to this in *Iʿlām al-muwaqqiʿīn*, arguing that the sūrah's pairing of delay and doom is the strongest possible refutation of the reasoning of impunity. In al-Bukhārī's *Ṣaḥīḥ* one finds the chapter heading that records this reading of the Makkan period: the salaf used to say, "prosperity after sin is istidrāj." This is the intellectual core of the verse: the wrongdoer reads God's forbearance as God's endorsement, and the verse's charge is that this is a *reasoning error*, a bad act of judgment, before it is a moral failure.

**Why the Argument Lands: The Statistics of Unpunished Crime**

The claim that unpunished wrongdoing implies its permissibility is not ancient. It is a species of a general fallacy that recurs in every age and is now empirically quantified: the "survivorship" error of inferring the safety of a course of action from the fact that visible failures are absent. A corporation that has not been fined does not thereby become lawful; a diet that has not yet produced symptoms is not thereby sound; a lie that has not yet collapsed a relationship is not thereby true. The verse's answer, "evil is the judgment they make," is a demand for the correct variable: the question is not "what happened?" but "what is being permitted to happen, and by whom?" Al-Ṭabarī paraphrases: they think that they can escape Us and prevent Our reaching them, and no one can do that — and *their judgment* is this presumption that God's respite is incapacity or neglect.

**The Psychological Portrait: Arrogance as a Cognitive Style**

Notice how the sūrah describes its object: not "the disbelievers" but *those who do the deeds of evil* and who *suppose*. The verse therefore identifies a type of mind rather than a census of people. The mind that assumes impunity is not a mind that has reasoned to that conclusion from evidence; it has arrived at a comfortable conclusion and manufactured a reading of the world to support it. Modern psychology names the mechanism motivated reasoning, and its religious cousin is the "prosperity as divine approval" inference that every age reinvents. The sūrah's rebuttal is characteristically economical: it does not enumerate the plagues that came upon Pharaoh, Korah, ʿĀd and Thamūd until verse 40; it first destroys the inference. Once the assumption that a delay is a dismissal is broken, the historical evidence in the rest of the sūrah becomes overwhelming in the plainest sense of the word.

**Social Reach: What Happens to a Community That Adopts This Judgment**

The verse's consequences are not confined to individual sinners. A society that treats its most fortunate members as the most virtuous — that reads wealth as proof of merit, power as proof of right, and the absence of immediate consequences as proof of justice — has institutionalised *bi'sa mā yaḥkumūn*. One of the most structurally important verses in the Qur'an on this point is the reply of Mūsā's people's antagonists and the answer given to them:

> *"Indeed, Pharaoh exalted himself in the land and divided its people into factions…"* (Qur'an 28:4)

and the general law:

> *"And We made the most weak in the people heirs of the east of the land and its west, which We had blessed."* (Qur'an 7:137)

The sūrah's own verse 39 will complete the thought about Pharaoh, Hāmān and Qārūn: *they waxed arrogant upon the earth, and outstripped [Us] not*. Impunity-in-appearance plus arrogance equals the exact combination that Qur'anic history repeatedly shows ending in seizure. That is the sūrah's sociology as well as its theology, and its warning applies to institutions no less than to individuals — a point the Makkan audience, whose whole civic pride rested on their sanctuary's prestige, would have felt immediately.

**Read Verse 5, and the Verse Ceases to Be a Threat**

Finally, the sequence is important. Verse 4 ends with the destruction of the oppressor's false hope; verse 5 begins with *man kāna yarjū liqāʾ Allāh* — "whoever hopes to meet God." The pivot from threat to aspiration is deliberate. The sūrah is not primarily interested in frightening the powerful but in re-orienting the possible: if there is a meeting, then delay is not dismissal, respite is not exemption, and the last word belongs to the Reckoning. The person who has internalised that sentence cannot be ultimately intimidated by the successful wicked — which was precisely the survival information the persecuted Meccan believers needed most.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:5

> **Whosoever hopes for the meeting with God, God’s term is coming. And He is the Hearing, the Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Cure for a Calculus Is a Deadline**

Verse 4 demolished the reasoning of impunity; verse 5 replaces the missing variable — a fixed date. *Man kāna yarjū liqāʾ Allāfa-inna ajala Llāhi la-ātin* — "whoever hopes for the meeting with God, God's appointed term is certainly coming." The syntax is conditional in form but declarative in force: it does not say "if you hope for it, it will happen," as though the meeting depended on the hoping; it says that *for anyone who is living on the assumption of a meeting*, here is the fact that makes the assumption true — an *ajal*, an appointed term, is *āt*, and it is coming. The sūrah has placed, at the exact hinge between the two suppositions of verses 2 and 4, a sentence about certainty. Trials are certain for the believer (v. 2); reckoning is certain for the wrongdoer (v. 4); and the mechanism that makes both real is the *ajal*, the deadline that belongs to God alone.

**yarjū: The Psychology of Hope in Qur'anic Arabic**

The verb *rajāʾ* in classical Arabic is not wishful thinking. It denotes a desire joined to an expectation of its realisation, and — crucially for this context — it is regularly used of fear-tinged expectation as well, so that the commentators (al-Qurṭubī citing earlier authorities, and al-Zamakhsharī in *al-Kashshāf*) explain the verse as covering both senses: whoever *hopes* for the meeting, and whoever *fears* it, is addressed alike, because the one who truly expects it will be both. Al-Jurjānī and later definitional writers gloss *rajāʾ* as "the heart's expectation of what it loves," which entails at minimum that what one hopes for determines what one attends to. The theological tradition built on this: al-Ghazālī in the *Iḥyāʾ* devotes a whole book to *ḥusn al-ẓann bi-Allāh* (good opinion of God) and insists that genuine hope is incompatible with continued, untroubled disobedience — a hope that produces no change of behaviour has, by his definition, ceased to be hope and become wishful delusion (*tamannī*). The Qur'an keeps the two apart. Mere wishing, *tamannī*, belongs to those who say *"none shall enter the Garden save one who is Jew or Christian"* (2:111), a claim answered in the very next verse: *"Nay — whoever submits to God his whole self, being virtuous, has their reward with their Lord, and no fear shall be upon them, neither shall they grieve"* (2:112). And a deed undertaken without this hope's discipline is like a smooth rock bearing a little soil, which a single downpour leaves bare (2:264).

**liqāʾ Allāh: Meeting a God Who Is Not Locally Absent**

The "meeting with God" (*liqāʾ Allāh*) is a technical term of the Qur'an, used of the moment when the creature's destination ceases to be reported and becomes experienced: *"those who are certain they shall meet their Lord, and that to Him they return"* (2:46); *"Say: whoever hopes to meet his Lord, let him work a righteous deed, and let none associate anything in the worship of his Lord"* (18:110); and in this sūrah, at 29:5 and again at 29:23, *those who disbelieve in God's signs and in the meeting with Him*. The unbelievers' own verdict is spoken in advance: *"they denied the meeting with God, and so they were lost"* — cf. 10:45. Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Zamakhsharī list its referents: the moment of death, the meeting with the Angel of Death, the standing of the Resurrection, the Reckoning, and the Recompense — and, for some, the beatific vision itself, the "looking upon their Lord" attested in 75:22–23. The phrase is deliberately paradoxical: God is not met as one meets a person at a place, yet the Qur'an speaks of being *returned* to Him as one speaks of an ordinary destination: *"to God the affair of all is returned"* (11:123), *"and you shall be returned to the Knower of the unseen and the witnessed"* (62:8). The point of the phrase, as al-Rāzī observes, is to convert an abstract belief in an afterlife into an inter-personal event: the sūrah does not say "whoever hopes for Paradise" but "whoever hopes to *meet God*," placing the reward inside the relation rather than beside it. The classical discussion of whether the believers see God in the Garden is introduced in the commentaries here through the hadith of Suhaib:

> *"When the people of the Garden enter the Garden, a call is made: 'O people of the Garden, God has for you a promise which He will fulfil today.' They ask, 'What is it? Has not our balance been heavy, our faces made bright?' Then the veil is lifted and they look upon Him; and nothing they have been given is more beloved to them than the looking upon their Lord, mighty and glorious."* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 181)

**ajal: The Single Most Behavioural Word in the Verse**

An *ajal* is a term, a limit, an end-date; its plural *ājāl* is used of the span allotted to a life, and the derived adjective *muʾajjal* means deferred to a fixed time. The Qur'an's usage is remarkably consistent: everything that is not God is bounded, and only the bounded things are on the calendar. *"And it is He who created the heavens and the earth in truth, and the day He says 'Be' — it is, His word is true, and to Him belongs the dominion on the Day the trumpet is blown… knowing that Your Lord has appointed for every people an ajal"* — cf. 18:86, 42:47, *wa-law shā'a la-jāʿala lahum ajalan fīhi riḍāhum* (actually 42:15's "an appointed term"). And in this sūrah, verses 53–54 will deploy the same term as a defence of the delay of punishment: *lawlā ajalan musamman la-jāʾahum al-ʿadhāb* — "were it not for an appointed term, the punishment would have come upon them." Verse 5 and verse 53 are the two halves of a single argument: the same *ajal* that guarantees the believer's hope guarantees the wrongdoer's alarm. The universe of the sūrah is not a place of indefinite postponement; it is a place of appointments, and the difference between hope and dread is only which side of the appointment one has arranged.

**"He is the Hearing, the Knowing": The Two Names That Complete the Sentence**

The closing pair, *wa-huwa al-Samīʿ al-ʿAlīm*, appears at the end of the similar verse 29:20 and dozens of times elsewhere; here its fit is exact and the commentators note it. *Samīʿ*: He hears the one who says "I hope to meet You," including the prayer in the breast that no one else hears, and — as al-Rāzī points out — the duʿāʾ of the oppressed, which is the very situation of this sūrah's first audience. *ʿAlīm*: He knows whether the hope is sincere or merely verbal — the exact discrimination announced in verse 3, *God knows the truthful and knows the liars*. The pairing therefore answers both halves of verse 2's question: hope is heard, and the hope that is only claimed is known. The two names also carry a promise and a warning together: He hears the supplication of the afflicted (a comfort), and He knows the scheming of the persecutor (a threat) — the verse is doing both jobs at once because it sits at the hinge of the passage.

**A ḥadīth That Unpacks the Verse: The Hadith of ʿĀʾishah on "Loving the Meeting"**

The Prophet ﷺ's words recorded in al-Bukhārī (6504) and Muslim (2683) make this verse's real subject — the psychology of expecting the meeting:

> *"Whoever loves the meeting with God, God loves the meeting with him, and whoever hates the meeting with God, God hates the meeting with him."* ʿĀʾishah (in one narration) said: "O Messenger of God, do we not all hate death?" He said, *"That is not the case. Rather, when the believer is given glad tidings of God's mercy, pleasure and Garden, at that moment he loves the meeting with God and God loves the meeting with him; but when the disbeliever is given glad tidings of God's punishment and wrath, at that moment he hates the meeting with God and God hates the meeting with him."*

Two lessons the commentators draw. First, the "hope" of the verse is not a mood but a direction of the heart measurable by what one expects to find at the end — a distinction ʿĀʾishah's question was designed to draw out. Second, God's "love" and "hatred" here are reciprocal, relational, and occasioned by the servant's own state, which fits the sūrah's insistence (v. 6) that whoever strives strives for himself: nothing is added to God by human hope, and nothing withheld. Ibn Taymiyyah cites such texts repeatedly in defence of real, non-metaphorical divine attributes that nonetheless do not imply change in God's essence — a rare instance where this verse's closing pair becomes a locus for the discussion of divine attributes.

**The Reformatting Power of an Appointment: Analogy and Everyday Logic**

Human behaviour reorganises itself around deadlines with a reliability that no exhortation achieves. A student who has an examination in three months studies; a traveller with a flight at six sets an alarm. Al-Baqarah's verse on debt — *"if you are travelling and cannot find a scribe, then pledges held… and if one of you entrusts another, let the one entrusted return the trust"* (2:283) — legislates precisely because contracts without terms collapse. The Qur'an's use of *ajal* applies that known behavioural law to the biggest possible case: the believer's time becomes coherent because the horizon is fixed, and the wrongdoer's escape becomes illusory for the same reason. It is why the sūrah can move from v. 5 to v. 6 without a seam: once the appointment is real, "hope" ceases to be emotion and becomes a schedule; and once it becomes a schedule, striving is no longer a fee imposed by a needy master — it is the obvious thing for a person with an appointment to do.

**The Verse's Answer to Escapism and to Despair**

Two opposite failures of hope are corrected here at once. The first is spiritual materialism: the hope that "insha'Allah" functions as a substitute for action, the assumption that God's mercy means no appointment is fixed. The second is the despair of the believer who has been tried so long that they can no longer imagine the meeting at all — the very condition of the Meccans stranded by the Quraysh, for whom these verses were revealed according to Ibn al-Jawzī and al-Qurṭubī. To them the verse whispers that the *ajal* is not conditional on their capacity to feel hopeful; it is coming whether or not their hearts are steady, and the One who hears their whispered "we believe" is the One who knows it. This is the sūrah's method throughout: it neither lowers the standard nor abandons the failing, and this verse is the standard case of that balance.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:6

> **And whosoever strives, strives only for himself. Truly God is beyond need of the worlds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Empties the Idea of a Needy Heaven**

*wa-man jahada fa-innamā yujāhidu li-nafsihi, inna Allāha la-Ghaniyy ʿan al-ʿālamīn.* The pivot from verse 5 to verse 6 is the sūrah's most important piece of logical architecture. Having said that the appointed term is coming — the sentence that makes hope rational — it immediately removes the only false motive a person might have for striving: the notion that God requires the service. The grammar is emphatic, with *innamā* restricting the scope of the verb to the self: "only, and for nothing other than himself, does one who strives strive." The name that grounds it, *Ghaniyy*, is not "wealthy" in the sense of having a large account but "self-sufficient in the absolute," needing nothing at all; the addition *ʿan al-ʿālamīn*, "from all the worlds," makes the exclusion universal — no created order, in any respect, is a remedy for a divine lack, because there is no lack. The Qur'an states it in other registers: *"whoever is grateful, is grateful only for his own soul; and whoever is ungrateful — surely my Lord is self-sufficient, generous"* (27:73); *"if you disbelieve, you and all on earth together, God is surely self-sufficient, praiseworthy"* (14:8); *"God does not need anyone from the worlds"* (3:97).

**jihād: The Root, the Effort, and What It Cost to Say It**

The verb *jāhada* is from *juhd* (also *jihd*), meaning exhaustion of capacity, toil undertaken at the limit of one's strength; *mujāhada* is the mutual form, denoting effort against something that offers resistance — the self, the devil, the enemy, the difficult situation. In Arabic the derived noun *mujāhid* is "one who exerts," which is why the classical jurists insisted that *jihād* in its primary legal sense is the exertion of effort in God's cause, whether with hand, tongue, wealth, or sword, and that the *jihād al-nafs* — the struggle against one's own lower self — is not a later mystical invention but an application of the same root, supported by the well-known report of the "greater jihād" whose chain is disputed but whose meaning the scholars of conduct widely endorsed. Here the sūrah uses the bare intransitive — "whoever strives" — and thereby includes every form of religious exertion under one law: whatever the mode, the benefit accrues to the striver. The verse also quietly redefines what "striving for God" means, since the sūrah's last verse will say *those who strive for Us* (29:69): "for Us" and "for himself" are not contradictions, because God has arranged things so that the person who works for Him is the one who is paid — as al-Rāzī observes, the master here is the sole party who has nothing to gain and yet hands the whole profit to the worker.

**Moral Reflexivity: Every Deed Curves Back to Its Doer**

The principle that deeds return to the doer is one of the Qur'an's most heavily documented. Al-Zamakhsharī glosses the verse: "your striving is for your own soul, since He does not need your obedience; as He says, *whoever works righteousness, it is for his own soul; and whoever does evil, it is against it* (41:46; 45:15)." And the load-bearing verse of individual responsibility that follows in this very sūrah (29:12–13) rests on the same axiom:

> *"…and no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another; and if a heavy-laden soul calls for its burden to be carried, nothing of it shall be carried, even by a near relative."* (Qur'an 35:18; cf. 6:164; 17:15; 39:7; 53:38)

This is a moral theory with three consequences. (1) No one can be your saviour, since the account is personal; the sūrah's own verse 12 will dismantle the recruiters' offer, "follow our way and we shall bear your sins." (2) No one can be your debtor, since the good you do profits the doer first; the Qur'an's stunning formulation is that God has *purchased* the selves and wealth of the believers, and the "price" is the Garden: *"God has purchased from the believers their selves and their wealth, in return for the Garden for them"* (9:111) — a transaction in which the seller gains everything and the buyer gives everything. (3) Despair is irrational, because your failures, equally, "reach" no one but yourself in the sense of wounding God: the person who sins "does not frustrate" the Lord of the worlds (cf. 2:264's *God is free of need, forbearing*).

**The Divine Needlessness and the Old Controversy over Ingratitude**

This verse has a precise history in kalām. The Muʿtazila held that gratitude to a benefactor (*shukr al-munʿim*) is rationally obligatory upon God's servants in a way that binds even God to reward, since it would be ugliness (*qubḥ*) for the Wise to let thanks go unpaid; some of them read the whole of divine reward as a kind of obligation. Al-Ashʿarī and the majority answered with verses such as this one: He cannot be obliged by anything, for He is *Ghaniyy ʿan al-ʿālamīn* — reward is not a debt extracted from God by our service but a gift He undertook by His own promise, and His promise is truth. Al-Rāzī in *al-Maṭālib al-ʿāliyah* presses the point further: if God were in need of worship, worship would be a commodity with a price, and piety would collapse into a barter; the self-sufficiency of God is what makes religious action a relation of love rather than a transaction of necessity. Ibn Taymiyyah, in *al-ʿAqīda al-Wāsiṭiyya* and his longer creedal works, treats divine *ghinā* as one of the attributes that establish God's lordship: He is praised not because He needs praise but because He is in Himself worthy of it; *"God is free of need of what they associate"* (16:88) and *"praise be to God, who has not taken a son"* (17:111) belong to the same grammar of a gratitude that adds nothing. This is the reason the sūrah's believers can be told to strive and yet not to think that they are doing God a favour — a balance that protects piety from both indifference and self-importance.

**A ḥadīth qudsī That Reads Like This Verse Expounded**

The most exact commentary on *inna Allāha la-Ghaniyy ʿan al-ʿālamīn* is the ḥadīth qudsī transmitted in Muslim (2577) from Abū Dharr, in which God says:

> *"O My servants, you cannot reach My harm, so you cannot harm Me, and you cannot reach My benefit, so you cannot benefit Me. O My servants, from the first of you to the last, from human and jinn, were they to be as pious as the most pious heart among you, it would not add anything to My dominion; and were they, from the first of you to the last, as sinful as the most sinful heart among you, it would not diminish anything from My dominion…"*

The sūrah's moral economy and the ḥadīth are the same statement in two registers: effort is neither required by God nor wasted on Him; it is, therefore, entirely converted into the striver. Ibn ʿAbbās's terse gloss in the early tafsīr literature — "God does not need the striving of any striver, and no one's deed is weightier for having been commanded" — is preserved because it makes explicit what the verse's *innamā* implies: obligation and profit are on opposite sides of the ledger, and that inversion is the reason worship in Islam is dignifying rather than exploitative.

**The Logical Shape of a Religion Whose God Needs Nothing**

Read as a proposition, verse 6 denies a class of theories that recur everywhere: systems in which the divine or the cosmic order requires human maintenance. Ancient cults fed their gods; some medieval theology spoke of human cooperation as completing redemption; and modern secular analogues persist — the cause that requires your burnout to be valid, the movement that measures your worth by your exhaustion, the leader who calls dissent betrayal because the project depends on his infallibility. Against all of these, the verse asserts a God whose needlessness makes every appeal to "God's interest" void: what the sūrah calls for is for *your* sake, and the Qur'an says so explicitly: *"O mankind, you are the needy toward God, and God — He is the Rich, the Praiseworthy"* (35:15). Two practical results follow, and the commentators stress both. First, no religious demand can be justified by claiming that God is short of it, so any authority that exploits piety by manufacturing divine need is to be resisted. Second, since nothing is extracted from you, your failures do not constitute a cosmic injury, and the believer is freed from a despair that treats one's sins as wounds inflicted upon the Almighty — which is precisely why the next verse promises to cover the bad deeds rather than complain about them.

**Modern Life under the Rule "Strives Only for Himself"**

Three ordinary applications make the verse's abstraction felt. (1) In work for others: the caregiver, the teacher, the volunteer who exhausts themselves and feels betrayed when the recipient does not thank them. This verse relocates the account: the deed was yours, and its profit is already secured by the One who needs nothing from it. The psychological result is a person who can serve without resentment, because they are not running a deficit. (2) In devotion: the believer who thinks that if God does not feel gratified, worship is pointless, or who measures prayer by emotional return. The verse's *Ghaniyy* means God is not the audience of your performance; He is its witness, and the beneficiary is you. (3) In public morality: the slogan "you are only doing this for yourself" is normally an accusation of selfishness; the Qur'an makes it the description of obedience, so that a society that internalises it stops needing to be shamed into virtue. The pious person is not a martyr to an needy heaven, and this is why the sūrah, immediately after this verse, can make its most generous promise about the same people — since nothing has been taken from God, everything can be given back to them.

**The Sequence: 5 → 6 → 7**

The order is: hope in the meeting (v. 5), self-regarding striving (v. 6), and then absolution plus reward at the highest rate (v. 7). Each is needed. Hope without the truth that the work profits you produces a servile piety; effort without hope produces fatigue; and both without the promise of forgiveness produce the despair of the perfectionist who has discovered that their record is bad. The sūrah, which is addressing people who are being tortured for their faith, lays out all three in eleven words each — an economy that is the opposite of compression.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:7

> **And those who believe and perform righteous deeds, We shall surely absolve them of their evil deeds, and We shall surely recompense them according to the best of that which they used to do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Halves of Divine Accounting**

After the assertion that striving profits nobody but the striver (v. 6), verse 7 states what the striver actually gets, and it is double: *la-nukaffiranna ʿanhum sayyiʾātihim wa-la-najziyyannahum aḥsana alladhī kānū yaʿmalūn* — the negatives cancelled and the positives paid at their highest rate. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Bayḍāwī both note the double *lām* of the oath attached to both verbs and the heavy *nūn* of emphasis on each — *la-nukaffiranna… wa-la-najziyyannahum* — so that God binds Himself, as it were, twice, by two solemn pledges, in a sūrah whose opening verse accused human beings of taking their own verbal claim far too lightly. The proportion is deliberate: human profession was casual; divine response is sworn.

**kaffara: The Soil That Covers and the Ledger That Clears**

The root *k-f-r* in its earliest Arabic sense is agricultural: *kafara* is to cover, and the *kāfir* of the plant is the farmer whose ploughing buries the seed. From this the language derives "to cover over" and, in the religious sense, "to bury the truth beneath a layer of denial" — the *kāfir* is not someone who lacks evidence but someone who has it and conceals it. The same root, applied to God's action toward sins, means that God covers the fault, and — as Ibn al-Qayyim explains in *al-Fawāʾid* — divine *maghfira* (forgiveness) literally means a covering that protects from the fault's consequence, while *ʿafw* means erasure, so that God sometimes does one and sometimes the other. Al-Rāzī notes the sūrah's preference here for *takfīr* rather than *maḥw*: not merely deletion but the shielding of a record that remains real yet can no longer harm. There is a third Qur'anic act, the most astonishing of the three — replacement: *"except those who repent, believe and do righteousness; for those God replaces their evil deeds with good"* (25:70). The progression across the three terms describes the therapeutic arc any morally serious person knows: the deed's power to destroy is cut, then its visibility, and finally even its sign.

**ajr: The Root of Reward and Why aḥsan Is Attached to It**

The verb *jazā* (from which *ajr*, "reward," is drawn) carries in its earliest Arabic sense the notion of passing through and of sufficiency — a *jazaʾ* is what meets a debt and lets both parties move on. So the reward here is not a tip appended to a service but a passage completed: the deed *travels* into its equivalent. Then comes the adjective that changes everything: God promises to recompense *aḥsana alladhī kānū yaʿmalūn* — "the best of what they used to do." The grammarians of the tafsīr tradition, al-Zamakhsharī prominent among them, treat this as a case of an ellipsis that must be supplied, and they supply it in two directions, both preserved by al-Qurṭubī. Either: "We shall reward you for the best of your deeds, and We shall overlook the worst of them" — so the reckoning is conducted at a person's peak rather than their average, and a year containing one sincere night is paid on that night's account; this is close to al-Ṭabarī's gloss. Or: "We shall give you, in return, the best" — so that *aḥsan* describes not the deed received but the gift returned, and the exchange is asymmetric by its very terms. The Qur'anic material supporting the second is abundant: *"Whoever brings a good deed shall have ten like it, and whoever brings an evil deed is recompensed only by its like"* (6:160); *"God does not waste the reward of those who make things beautiful"* (9:120) — and *al-muḥsinīn*, "those who make beautiful," is the very word with which this sūrah will close (29:69). Whatever direction the adjective takes, the arithmetic favours the servant; and the sūrah's own later verse 27 will state the same asymmetry about a single man, Ibrāhīm: *nā'ulhu ajrahu fi al-dunyā wa-innahu fī al-ākhiraati mina'l-ṣāliḥīn*.

The promise in the second clause is also, in Arabic, a promise of *beginning again*: the participle *kānū yaʿmalūn* is the habitual imperfect, so God's reward attaches to a *pattern* of conduct over time, not to an isolated peak experience. Trial was said in verse 2 to be continuous; recompense here is equally continuous, and the correspondence is not accidental.

**The ḥadīth of Revalued Intentions**

The most precise illustration of verse 7's second half is Ibn ʿAbbās's report of what the Prophet ﷺ said his Lord had written, transmitted in Muslim (131):

> *"God has written the good deeds and the evil deeds, then made that clear. Whoever intends a good deed and does not do it — God writes it for him as a complete good deed at His presence; and if he intends it and does it, God writes it for him as ten good deeds up to seven hundred, up to many multiples. And whoever intends an evil deed and does not do it — God writes it for him as a complete good deed; and if he intends it and does it, God writes it as a single evil deed."* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 131; al-Bukhārī 6491, on the authority of Ibn ʿAbbās)

A bad intention *abandoned* is recorded as a full good deed: the ledger does not simply cancel; it converts. This is exactly the logic of *najzī* at its most generous, and it explains why the sūrah's promise is paired, at verse 9, with admission *into the company of the righteous* — a community whose members have been through this arithmetic. Related to the first half of the verse, the expiation of the ordinary round of worship is documented: the Prophet ﷺ said, *"The five prayers, Friday to Friday, and Ramadan to Ramadan are expiation for what is between them, provided major sins are avoided"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 233). Al-Nawawī's discussion in *al-Majmūʿ* records the majority view that minor sins are covered by these means while major ones require repentance — a distinction that keeps the promise of 29:7 immense without making it cheap, and answers the quietist reading in which "God is merciful" licenses carelessness, the same error the sūrah had addressed in 29:2.

**The Three Components of the Righteous Deed**

The source commentary, following al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī's analysis, records that the *ʿamal ṣāliḥ* has three actualisations: in the heart by belief, reflection and affirmation; on the tongue by witnessing to what the heart holds, which implies right intention; and in the limbs by obedience and worship. This tripartite account is not decoration; it is the exegete's answer to the verse's conjunction — *āmanū wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt* — faith and action welded by *wa* so that neither is counted without the other, against the Murjiʾa who held that deeds are not part of faith's definition, and against the Khārijites who made grave sin a matter of unbelief. Ibn Taymiyyah's *al-Īmān* treatises cite precisely verses like 29:2–7 to argue for the position he calls that of the *ahl al-sunna*: faith is speech, belief and action; it increases and decreases; and it can be annulled only by that which contradicts it, not by every sin. The verse's structure is the argument: if the promise attached to belief alone, "and did righteous deeds" would be idle; if deeds alone carried it, the sentence would have begun differently. That a promise so carefully drafted should also be so generously executed is the sūrah's way of teaching that God's exactness in law is matched by His extravagance in giving.

**Why Being Absolved Is a Different Thing from Being Pardoned**

Here the sūrah speaks to its original audience with unusual directness. The people it addresses have done terrible things: they worshipped stones, some of them persecuted, some of them sat with the enemy at Badr under duress (as Ibn ʿAbbās held in the occasion of v. 10), and — in the case of Saʿd's mother and dozens like her — they threatened, starved, and broke oaths over the faith. The promise of v. 7 that the idolater who comes to belief has their previous record cleared was understood as a specific mercy for the Makkan convert: al-Qurṭubī records the consensus that *"Islam annihilates what was before it"* and that *"one who repents is as though he had no sin"* — the latter being the Prophet's ﷺ own words to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, who asked about the sins he had "worn out" in hostility, and heard: *"Do you not know that Islam destroys what was before it, and that migration destroys what was before it, and that ḥajj destroys what was before it?"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 121). For the person in the grip of shame, verse 7 is not an abstraction about accounting but a permission to stop being their own prosecutor.

**The Social Logic of a Community that Absolves**

Read as a social principle, the verse's first clause does something no amnesty in human history has managed to do: it guarantees not merely the suppression of punishment but the covering of the deed itself, and it does this in the same breath as it restores the person's record. The social result is a community that can absorb a former persecutor without pretending he never existed (the deed is real) and without holding it over him (the consequence is removed). Makkah's conquest was conducted on exactly this logic: *"Go, for you are free"* — the words of the Prophet ﷺ to the Quraysh who had exiled and tortured his community (Aḥmad; al-Bayhaqī in *al-Daʿawāt*; cf. the Qur'anic model at 12:92, where Yūsuf says to his brothers, *"No reproach upon you today; may God forgive you"*). A society built on that verse can rehabilitate; a society that has only the categories of exposure and punishment cannot.

**The Psychological Reach: The Best of What You Used to Do**

Two modern experiences complete the picture. The first is the tyranny of the average: performance culture evaluates a person by their mean output, and the believer who has failed habitually concludes that God's file contains nothing but failures. Verse 7 says that God reads a life at its best point, not its average, and that He *substitutes* the best for the rest. The second is rumination: the person with a past cannot stop mentally re-watching it.
