The promise that God covers, removes, and re-values the record is not a licence for moral laziness — the same sūrah has just insisted that no one's striving is wasted and no one's burden is transferable — but it is a command to accept a verdict of closure on a case that God has already decided not to try again. The ethical force of a religion is not primarily in the weight it gives to guilt but in the weight it gives to forgiveness; and this verse, sitting in the middle of a Makkan sūrah about persecution, hands that forgiveness to people who needed both.

**A Note on the Verse's Grammar of Certainty**

Two more accents deserve mention. First, the verse uses *kānū yaʿmalūn* — the imperfect after *kāna*, "they used to do," which in Arabic marks a habitual past. God's promise attaches not to isolated acts but to a way of life sustained over time; the sūrah's whole opening about trial is a preface to this: what is rewarded is a pattern. Second, *la-nukaffiranna* and *la-najziyyannahum* are both first-person plural verbs of divine majesty: the covering and the revaluing are not delegated to an intermediary, an intercessor, or a priest; the sūrah is addressed to people for whom mediation was the entire architecture of religion, and here it simply does not appear. What is between them and God is only what they did — and the best of it is what He takes.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:8

> **And We have enjoined man to be good unto his parents. But if they urge thee to ascribe as partner unto Me that whereof thou hast no knowledge, then obey them not. Unto Me is your return, whereupon I shall inform you of that which you used to do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Bequest Written into Human Nature**

*wa-waṣṣayna al-insāna bi-wālidayi ḥusnā* — "and We have bequeathed to the human being, concerning his two parents, beauty of conduct." The verb chosen is not *amarā* (command) but *waṣṣā*, the causative of *waṣā*, which in Arabic is the word for a dying person's final instruction — a *waṣiyya* is the testimony a person lodges while the account of this life is closing. The same verb is used in the inheritance legislation of 4:11–12 and in the creedal list of 6:151: *"Say: come, I will recite what your Lord has forbidden to you: that you associate nothing with Him, and with parents beauty of conduct (bi'l-wālidayin iḥsānan)…"* Al-Rāzī remarks on the eloquence of the choice: a bequest binds the heir precisely because it comes from one who can no longer enforce it, and the duty toward parents is placed on that footing — you owe it even when they can no longer compel, threaten, reward, or watch. The object of the bequest is *ḥusnā*, "beauty," the same noun-family as *iḥsān*, with which the whole sūrah will conclude (29:69, *God is with the beautiful-doers*). The arc from verse 8 to verse 69 is thus a single word: the child's first school of *iḥsān* is the parental home, and the reward of all *iḥsān* is God's company.

**jāhadāka: When the Family Becomes the Battlefield**

The clause *wa-in jāhadāka ʿalā an tushrika bī mā laysa laka bihi ʿilm* is the sūrah's most arresting piece of vocabulary. The verb is the same *jihād* of verse 6 — "and whoever strives, strives for himself" — and it is here applied to a mother and father pressuring their child. The effect is to say, in one word, that a family quarrel over God can be as hard as a war, and to prepare the reader for the resolution already given in verse 6: even here, the one who struggles is struggling for his or her own soul. The continuation, "concerning that which you have no knowledge," sets the standard for refusal: the ground on which you resist your parents is not preference but epistemology. You do not worship these things because you lack knowledge of their divinity, and lack of knowledge is not a matter of sentiment — as the sūrah's own neighbours insist: *"Is there for you any knowledge? Then produce it, if you are truthful"* (27:75), *"They follow nothing but conjecture and what the self desires"* (53:23), *"You named them, you and your forefathers — God sent down for it no authority"* (53:23). The clause thus quietly generalises: obedience is suspended wherever a demand has no warrant in what one knows, and the very first thing one knows is that God has no partner.

**The Occasion: Ḥamnah's Three-Day Hunger Strike**

Al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī and al-Wāḥidī transmit from Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ himself that this verse — with 31:14 and 46:15 — came down about him. When his mother Ḥamnah bint Abī Ḥudhayfa learned of his conversion she swore that she would neither shelter from sun and wind nor eat nor drink until he renounced Muḥammad ﷺ; three days passed, she was near death, and her son, torn, came to the Prophet ﷺ. God's answer was the verse: keep beauty of conduct toward her, but do not obey in this. Al-Bukhārī records the account in his book of tafsīr, and the Saʿd-narration is the classic instance in the jurists' chapters on *ṭāʿat al-wālidayn*. Saʿd himself reported that the sentence *"I have bequeathed to the human being concerning his parents"* was revealed concerning him, and that he was a case in which the Qur'an was recited at the very moment of his family's crisis. The detail that matters for every later reader is what the verse does *not* do: it does not authorise estrangement, does not permit harshness, does not withdraw the duty of maintenance, and does not revoke the mother's threefold priority (see below). It draws a single, narrow, absolute boundary — and the boundary is drawn at the point of *shirk* alone.

**"Consort with Them Kindly": The Sūrah's Sister-Verse**

The complete ruling is not in this verse but in its twin, which must be read beside it:

> *"But if they strive with you to associate with Me that of which you have no knowledge, then do not obey them, and consort with them in this world in a recognised manner of goodness (wa-ṣāḥibhumā fī al-dunyā maʿrūfan), and follow the way of whoever turns to Me. Then to Me is your return, and I shall inform you of what you used to do."* (Qur'an 31:15)

Three clauses, three doctrines. The prohibition of obedience is limited to the specific demand. The command of *maʿrūf* — literally "what is recognised as right by sound custom and reason" — makes ongoing kindness a duty of the same order as the refusal, and the ḥadīth literature shows how literally this was taken: Asmāʾ bint Abī Bakr came to the Prophet ﷺ asking about her mother Qutayla, who had visited during the truce of Ḥudaybiyyah while still polytheist, longing for a gift and Asmā's attention; and he told her, *"Maintain ties with your mother"* (al-Bukhārī 2620; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1003). It is on the basis of this that the revelation of 60:8 was understood: *"God does not forbid you, concerning those who did not fight you on account of religion nor drive you from your homes, from showing goodwill and doing justice to them"* (60:8). Third, the verse defers the verdict: *"Then to Me is your return, and I shall inform you."* The believer is not the last judge of their own family — and this is the same clause as 29:8's ending. The reconciliation of a religiously divided household is, in this sūrah's scheme, a matter for the Day when all parties are told the truth at once, by the only arbiter both sides accept.

**The Other Side of the Ledger: What the Parents Are Owed Anyway**

The verse's opening half is not swallowed by its exception, and the tradition is emphatic about the standing debt. The Prophet ﷺ said, when asked who had the greatest claim on his companionship: *"Your mother, your mother, your mother, then your father"* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 5971; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2548). He ﷺ said: *"A child cannot repay a parent, unless he finds him in ownership and buys him and frees him"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1510) — the image being that even the total gift of a lifetime is a fraction of what was received. And the pairing this sūrah implies — that disobedience to parents stands beside polytheism among the greatest of sins — is stated in a Prophetic ḥadīth explicitly: Abū Bakrah reported that the Prophet ﷺ asked three times, *"Shall I not tell you of the greatest of the major sins? Associating partners with God, and being undutiful to parents"* — and he reclined, then sat up and repeated it (al-Bukhārī 2654, 6675; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 87, where Anas adds that the verse *"and We have enjoined man concerning his parents"* (31:14) was understood by the Companions to be joined to it). Al-Tirmidhī transmits: *"The Lord's pleasure follows the parent's pleasure, and the Lord's anger follows the parent's anger"* (al-Tirmidhī 1900), whose chain some assessed as weak while its meaning the moralists of every school deployed. The result is the settled position: nothing in a parent's claim is cancelled by their unbelief, and everything in it is cancelled at the single point where it asks for a partner against God.

**Why the Boundary Is Drawn Exactly There**

The logic is worth stating plainly, because both extremes recur in every generation. The verse's first principle is that *convictions are not owed to anyone*: belief cannot be transferred by affection, since agreement is not a currency but a response to evidence, and a parent can no more be obeyed into shirk than into a false arithmetic. Its second principle is that *conduct is owed to everyone*: behaviour is not a declaration of belief but a debt of justice, and a child's refusal to lie about God does not extinguish the mother's right to a kind word, to care in age, to a funeral, to inheritance. The two principles hold simultaneously only if the moral evaluation is located in the act rather than in the person — which is precisely why this sūrah ends, as it began, on the theme of being tried rather than being certified. Ibn Taymiyyah, in his *fanāwa* on parental rights, formulates the classical rule with characteristic precision: obedience is obligatory in what is *maʿrūf*, forbidden where it entails disobedience to God, and — in the third case, the case of this verse — accompanied by continued kindness, because the parent's claim over the child's conduct does not derive from the parent's correctness.

**The Hunger Strike Repeated: Emotional Coercion and Its Proper Answer**

Ḥamnah's tactic is the oldest instrument of family pressure and the least changed by time: the parent who fasts, weeps, falls ill, withdraws affection, threatens suicide, disinherits, or performs visible misery until the child yields. Its effectiveness lies in making the child's conscience the lever against the child's conscience. The verse's response is a masterpiece of de-escalation: it neither capitulates to the coercion nor demonises the coercer. It concedes the parent's pain — Ḥamnah was genuinely suffering, and Saʿd was genuinely torn — while refusing the demand; and it refuses it by an appeal to knowledge rather than to defiance ("that whereof you have no knowledge"), which is a form of speech that leaves honour intact on both sides. Al-Jurjānī's *Taʿrīfāt* notes that *maʿrūf* in the jurists' usage is precisely the recognition that a person's rights survive one's disagreement with them. The applied version is what Muslim converts from every background ask for when they call for help at the kitchen table: not tactics for winning, but a way of holding firm and loving at the same time. This verse is that way, and 31:15 provides the manner.

**A Note on the Verse's Ending, and Its Function in the Sūrah**

The final clause, *ilayya rujūʿukum fa-unabbiʾukum bimā kuntaum taʿmalūn* — "to Me is your return, then I shall tell you what you used to do" — is the sūrah's third such eschatological close (cf. 29:5, 29:22, 29:57), and it performs a structural job. Having legislated the hardest possible human conflict, the verse hands the outcome to the Reckoning, which simultaneously deters the child from contempt ("your parent is your trial, and God is watching the account") and consoles them ("your refusal is not a crime requiring your family's verdict, because a verdict is coming that supersedes all family verdicts"). Al-Qurṭubī's comment is short and to the point: in this clause is the proof that the person who is wronged by those closest to him should lift his eyes to the Day when he will be told, in the presence of both parties, what each had done. And the same verse will soon be illustrated by the sūrah's own case-studies: a man thrown into fire by his own society and its patriarch (29:24), and a prophet named as the father of the very people who rejected him (29:16).

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:9

> **And those who believe and perform righteous deeds, We shall surely cause them to enter among the righteous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reward That Is a Company**

Verse 7 promised that evil deeds would be covered and the best of deeds rewarded; verse 9 adds a third gift, and it is not a place but a population: *wa-la-nudkhilannahum fī al-ṣāliḥīn*. The preposition is *fī* — "into, among" — not *ilā* ("toward") and not the locative *fī* of a garden. What God promises here is admission into a category of persons. Al-Ṭabarī records the two readings of the Companions' successors and declines to choose: either they enter the Garden *in the company of* the righteous, or they are *counted among* the righteous — enrolled on their roll, classified with them, assigned their rank. The second reading is the more striking, because being counted among a group is a statement of identity, not of real estate. On the Day when every human being's file is read, this sūrah's promise is that the file of the weak-yet-sincere believer will be bound in the same volume as the prophets.

**Why Companionship Is the Point of the Sūrah's Whole Argument**

The context gives the promise its force. The people addressed have been expelled: cut off from clans, stripped of the protection networks of Makkah, in some cases starved and beaten by their own kin (29:8), and unable to leave (29:2–3's occasion). Their wound is social as much as physical, and the Qur'an answers it on its own level. The human being who loses their society does not primarily need reassurance about square footage in the afterlife; they need to know that they have not lost membership as such, and that the membership they have been denied will be granted in a form that cannot be revoked. This is why the sūrah, when it reaches its narrative centre, does not describe the Garden first but the *company*: Abraham is given *ʿajilan* his reward in this world and in the next he is "among the righteous" (29:27); Lot believes him and flees into the same company (29:26); and the sūrah closes on the promise that God is *with* the beautiful-doers (29:69).

**The Qur'anic Theology of Being "With" and "Among"**

Two verses make verse 9's logic explicit, and both belong to this sūrah's own family of ideas. The first is the charter of Islamic company:

> *"And whoever obeys God and the Messenger — those will be with those upon whom God has bestowed favour: the prophets, the truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous; and excellent (ḥasuna) are those as companions!"* (Qur'an 4:69)

The final clause is an exclamation of admiration at the *rafīq* — the mate, the desk- neighbour, the comrade — not at the destination. The second:

> *"And be with the truthful (wa-kūnū maʿa al-ṣādiqīn)."* (Qur'an 9:119)

That verse commands in the present tense what 29:9 promises in the future: association with truthful people is to be entered now, and completed later. Al-Zamakhsharī notices this continuity of reward and duty: the believer who has attached themselves to the righteous in this world finds the attachment ratified in the next, and the one who avoided them in this life will find no mechanism of re-entry there. The ḥadīth literature makes the same point in its sharpest register. Al-Bukhārī (6168) and Muslim (2640) transmit from Anas the famous exchange:

> *"A man asked the Prophet, 'When is the Hour?' He said, 'What have you prepared for it?' He said, 'Love of God and His Messenger.' He said, 'You are with the one you love.' Anas said: 'We were not, after Islam, more joyful than by that.'"*

Joy, not relief: Anas names it as the greatest news anyone received. And the converse is equally taught: *"A person is upon the way of his intimate friend, so let one of you look at whom he takes as an intimate friend"* (al-Tirmidhī 2378; Abū Dāwūd 4833 — graded ḥasan by al-Tirmidhī and others).

**The Sorting of Society and Its Reversal**

Read against the Makkan setting, verse 9 is an announcement of a reversal of rankings. Pagan Makkah sorted people by lineage, wealth, and allegiance to the sanctuary's cult; the strongest men of Quraysh held that their ancestry and their custodianship of the Kaʿba placed them at the head of the world. The verse says that the final sorting will be by moral category, and that a slave, a client, and a merchant who believe and act rightly are admitted — by the same verb used elsewhere of entry into the divine presence — into the society of the righteous, in which the only rank that counts is *ṣalāḥ*. When the Prophet ﷺ appointed Bilāl ibn Rabāḥ — a man who had been an enslaved Abyssinian, tortured under a rock in the Makkan sun for saying "one" — to be the voice that called the whole city to prayer, and when men of Quraysh standing in the rows had to answer that call behind him, the re-sorting announced in this verse had begun in public. This is also why the promise is expressed as *nudkhil* — "We shall cause to enter" — a divine action: entry into the company is not something a person can achieve by self-assertion, and no committee, tribe, or party can block or grant it.

**al-Ṣāliḥūn: What "Righteous" Means When It Is a Class**

*Ṣāliḥ* in Arabic carries two registers that the verse fuses. In its intransitive use it means "to be in good order, sound, uncorrupt" — *ṣalaḥa'l-amr*, the matter was set right, and *ṣalāḥ* is the opposite of *fasād*, corruption; the same root gives *ṣalāḥ*, the prayer, because the prayer is the act by which the self is put back into order. In its human use *al-ṣāliḥūn* names those whose inward and outward are both sound — belief that orders the heart, and deeds that order the world. That the promised companionship is precisely *this* class, and not "the sinless," matters: the sūrah has just said in verse 7 that these people have evil deeds which God covers, and in verse 10 that among the believers are some whose nerve fails. The company of the righteous, in this sūrah's vocabulary, is a company of people whose record has been repaired, not a company of people who never broke. This is confirmed in the sūrah's own narrative — Lot is admitted into the company (29:26), and the entire patriarchal line of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob is enrolled in it (29:27), a line whose members the Qur'an elsewhere discusses with the honesty of prophetic infallibility only in transmission, not in biography.

**The Mechanics of "Cause to Enter": Emigration Foretold**

The word *nudkhil* also picks up the sūrah's central practical issue: *hijrah*. The emigrant leaves a place of unbelief in order to be with the believers, and the sūrah, in verse 56, will command exactly that — *"O My servants who believe! Truly My earth is vast; so worship Me"* — and in 16:41 promises a good place in this world as well as the next. Here in verse 9, emigration is projected onto its eschatological scale: entry into the Garden *is* the completion of entry into the community, and the sūrah's language treats them as one act. Ibn Kathīr sees this continuity and writes: the reward of those who emigrate and are tried is that God admits them where the righteous are, so the small company on earth and the great company in the Garden are a single assembly, the *jumʿa* of God. The ḥadīth of the seven whom God shades on the day there is no shade but His (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 660; Muslim 1031) belongs to the same idea — the shading is by God, in the company where no rank, tribe, or wealth mediates.

**Why the Community Matters Theologically, Not Only Socially**

The verse rebuts a permanent temptation in religious individualism: the notion that a person and God suffice, that faith is a private arrangement between the solitary soul and its maker. The sūrah's answer is structural — the reward itself is relational — and the tradition has drawn from it a theory of religious community that runs from the congregational prayer to the *ṣuḥba* of the Sufis to the *jamāʿa* of the jurists. Al-Ghazālī, in the *Iḥyāʾ*'s book on companionship, writes that the believer's need for a righteous company in this world is the world's foretaste of what verse 9 promises, and that the one who cannot bear the company of the righteous in the near should not console themselves with hopes of it in the far. This is also the sociological insight, restated in every study of moral formation: character is transferred by proximity, habit, and imitation far more effectively than by instruction, which is why the divine pedagogy in this sūrah so rarely addresses isolated individuals and so often addresses a "you" that is plural.

**The Practical Test the Verse Applies to the Reader**

Finally, verse 9 asks a question that is easier to answer than most: whom do you want to be among? Not "what do you want to have" or "where do you want to be" — the sūrah has made the company the object of the promise. Every person's real biography is partly composed of the sets they have been admitted to and the sets they have refused; the profession of faith expressed in verse 2 will be adjudicated (29:3) partly by the company a person sought, and the "trial" this sūrah announced will in practice often look exactly like a choice between the safety of one crowd and the cost of another. Verse 9 says that the latter is the only crowd whose membership survives, and — since the verb is *nudkhil*, "We shall cause to enter" — that whoever genuinely seeks it will not have to force the door.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:10

> **Among mankind are those who say, “We believe in God”; but if he is harmed for the sake of God, he takes persecution from men to be as God’s Punishment. Yet if help comes from thy Lord, he will surely say, “We were indeed with you.” Does God not know best what lies within the breasts of all creatures?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Portrait of a Man Whose Fear Has the Wrong Address**

*wa-mina'n-nāsi man yaqūlu āmantu bi-Allāhi fa-idhā ūdhiya fī Allāhi jaʿala fitnata'n-nāsi ka-ʿadhābi Llāh* — the sentence describes no doctrine and denies no creed. Everything in it is about a proportion. The man says he believes in God; when harm touches him for God's sake, he treats the harm done by people as though it were equal to the punishment of God. His error is not a proposition but a scale: two realities have been weighed and found equivalent in his heart, and the sentence that follows will show him behaving accordingly at both ends — fleeing when the created threaten, and rushing to claim partnership with the victors when God's help arrives. This is the sūrah's third and most penetrating study of the failure of nerve, after verse 2 (the claim to be left untested) and verse 3 (the assay that sorts the truthful from the liars).

**fitna of People, Punishment of God: The Grammar of the Comparison**

*fitnata'n-nāsi* — "the trial/affliction of people" — continues the sūrah's vocabulary from verse 2 (*lā yuftanūn*), so the word "persecution" in the translation is literally the same noun used for God's testing: persecution is a *fitna*, and this man's sin is that he turns it into an equivalence. The classical exegesis offers two closely related readings of *ka-ʿadhābi Llāh*, both reported by al-Ṭabarī. (1) He regards the persecution of men as being *as terrible as* God's punishment — therefore he flees from it as one would flee from divine chastisement, and in doing so he makes the fear of creatures the operative fear in his life. (2) He thinks that the affliction sent by men *is* a sign of God's punishment falling on him — so he concludes that God has abandoned him, and takes the persecutors' violence as God's verdict, and "reverts" to escape what he now believes is heaven's own sentence. Al-Qurṭubī prefers the first and says it is the more eloquent (*afrad*), because it indicts the heart's arithmetic: the man has installed the created in the position of the Creator as the object of ultimate dread. Both readings are theologically identical in effect, and the second explains why the *fitna* verse 2 promised and the *fitna* described here are the same word — the trial that comes from people is misread as the punishment that comes from God.

**The Sūrah's Case Against a Mis-scaled Fear**

Elsewhere the Qur'an legislates exactly this correction of proportion. It is the doctrine that fear of God is not one fear among others but the only one that determines the rest:

> *"It is only the devil who threatens you with his allies, so do not fear them; fear Me, if you are believers."* (Qur'an 3:175)

> *"Those who convey God's messages and fear Him, and fear none besides Him; and sufficient is God as Reckoner."* (Qur'an 33:39)

And the Prophet ﷺ's own diagnosis of the mechanism is the most quoted sentence on this point:

> *"Whoever seeks the pleasure of people by incurring God's displeasure, God will leave him to the people."* (al-Tirmidhī 2414; graded ḥasan by some, and the wording is transmitted in various chains; its meaning is illustrated by the Qur'anic verse "and among people is he who says…")

The sūrah's second half supplies the converse — *"if help comes from your Lord, he will surely say, 'we were indeed with you'"* — and this is the reason the passage indicts not only cowardice but opportunism. The same man who over-estimated the persecutors' power now over-estimates their defeat. His behaviour at both ends is identical: he is a barometer of whoever is currently ascendant, and the only variable he has left out of his calculation is the One who is always ascendant. Al-Rāzī, drawing out the logic of the verse: a person whose actions track present power rather than persistent truth has, in effect, no creed; he has a weather-vane.

**The Report from Mujāhid and the Report from Ibn ʿAbbās**

The occasions add specificity without narrowing the scope. Mujāhid is quoted by al-Qurṭubī and al-Wāḥidī to the effect that the verse describes people who professed faith with their tongues but fell into unbelief under trial — that is, that the description is of a *type* rather than a fixed list of names. Ibn ʿAbbās, in a report also preserved by al-Ṭabarī, connects it to a concrete group in Makkah: believers whom the Quraysh dragged out with them to Badr as human shields or as a levy, so that they marched in the polytheist army, and who were then reproached by revelation in the Madinan verse about those "whose souls the angels took while they wronged themselves," who answered *"we were oppressed in the land"* and heard the question *"was not God's earth vast, that you should emigrate therein?"* (4:97). The same group is linked back to 16:110, which opens a way of repentance for those who emigrated after oppression, strove, and were patient. Whatever the exact identification, the sūrah's psychology is unmistakable: fear of people produces, in the religiously weak, a chain of compromises — silence, then presence at the wrong parade, then participation — and each step is rationalised by the scale-illusion of verse 10, "this is as God's punishment; I had no choice." The Qur'an's answer is that there was a choice, and its name is emigration (29:56; 4:97).

**"Does God not know best what is in the breasts?"**

The closing rhetorical question is the sūrah's standard closing for exactly this kind of case; it appears in this wording repeatedly in the Qur'an — *"Does He not know, who created? And He is the Subtle, the Aware"* (67:14), *"Does not God know the secret and the private whisper? And He is the Knower of the unseen and the witnessed, who knows what lies in the breasts of all creatures?"* (67:13; cf. 3:119; 8:43; 57:6; 64:4). The phrase *dhāt al-ṣudūr*, "the contents of the breasts," in Arabic idiom is the seat of intention and motive; and the sūrah's insistence here is double-edged. Toward the man described, it is a rebuke: your fear was not a fact of nature but a hidden preference, and the One who sees preferences sees this one. Toward the community, it is a restraint: you do not see the breast, so you do not judge it — which is why the sūrah, in the next verse, leaves the classification to God: *Surely God knows those who believe and surely He knows the hypocrites* (29:11).

**The ḥadīth That Explains the Mechanism: Hearts and the Weaving of Trials**

Ḥudhayfa ibn al-Yamān reported the Prophet ﷺ saying:

> *"Trials are presented to the hearts, one after another, like a mat being woven strand by strand. Whichever heart absorbs them is marked with a black spot, and whichever refuses them is marked with a white spot; until the hearts become two kinds: one white like a smooth stone, which no trial harms as long as the heavens and the earth endure; and the other black and mottled like a reversed cup, knowing no good and recognising no evil except what it has obeyed from its own desires."* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 144; cf. Muslim 145; al-Bukhārī 6885 on the black-and-white spots)

The image is the exact counterpart of verse 10. The trial (*fitna*) does not arrive as a verdict but as a repeated presentation; each one is either refused or absorbed, and the residue is a colouring of the heart — the very organ the verse's closing question names. The verse's portrait of a man who took the persecution of people as God's punishment is a portrait of a heart mid-weaving, not of a finished case; which is why, in the sūrah's own treatment of him, he is not yet called a hypocrite.

**Weak Faith Is Still Faith: The Sūrah's Pastoral Distinction**

Read together, verses 10 and 11 make one of the most humane distinctions in the Book. Verse 10 describes the failure of nerve; verse 11 does not sentence it. The Prophet ﷺ established the ranking that the two verses imply:

> *"The strong believer is better and more beloved to God than the weak believer, while there is good in both. Be keen for what benefits you, seek God's help, and do not give up; if something befalls you, do not say 'if only God had willed and if only I had done such-and-such,' but say 'God has decreed and what He willed He did' — for 'if only' opens the work of the devil."* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2664, from Abū Hurayrah)

"The strong believer is better" is precisely the sūrah's hierarchy in the Makkan situation — strength meaning here not muscularity but the capacity to bear harm "for God's sake" without converting it into God's punishment. Yet "there is good in both" is the sentence that keeps the community from purging the frightened. The whole first section of this sūrah operates inside that double emphasis, and it is why verse 2's challenge was addressed to *mankind* and not to a blacklist.

**The Second Half: "We Were Indeed with You"**

*wa-la-in jāʾa naṣrun min rabbika la-yaqūlunna inna maʿakum* — and the parallel in Sūrat al-Nisā' is even sharper: *"Those who lie in wait for you: if a victory comes to you from God, they say, 'Were we not with you?' But if the disbelievers have a share, they say, 'Did we not gain mastery over you and defend you from the believers?'"* (4:141). Here is the whole psychology in one sentence: the same mouth claims the victors' company in their success and the victims' protection in their defeat, and the only party it never mentions is the God who gives the victory. Al-Zamakhsharī's gloss is the psychologically exact stroke: they "claim the share of the spoil while disowning the rights of the friendship they declined when it was costly," which is why the verse ends by referring the case to the knowledge of what is in the breasts. Al-Zamakhsharī's note is worth transmitting: such a man "counts himself among the victors in order to share the spoils, and is the first to disown the community in a time of cost; his company exists only when the company is profitable." Social theory would describe the same behaviour as free-riding on a collective: the person who takes the group's benefits without bearing its costs, and who is detected only when the group's survival depends on everyone bearing cost. The sūrah's answer to the free-rider is not punishment in this world but exposure in the next (29:11, 29:13) and the refusal of transferable responsibility (29:12).

**What This Verse Is Asking Its Reader, in Any Century**

Applied today, verse 10 is a question about relative weight, and the settings are mundane. A person who conceals their convictions at work, not out of prudence but because the social cost is magnified in imagination until it out-weighs a divine command, has performed the operation the verse describes: *fitnat al-nās ka-ʿadhābi Llāh*. So has the believer who keeps faith only in the company that makes keeping it easy, and so has any community that measures loyalty by attendance at the moment of victory rather than at the moment of risk. And the verse's remedy is likewise concrete: shrink the created to its size by fixing attention on the scale of the Reckoning (29:5, 29:13, 29:53–55), and remove the situation that produces the disproportion when removal is possible (29:56). The verse, in short, does not scold the frightened into pretending they are not frightened; it shows them that their fear has been misfiled, and offers to help them re-file it.

**A Last Linguistic Observation**

Note that the sūrah, in the space of four verses, has used three different words for the human being: *al-nās* (v. 2, "mankind" as a public), *al-insān* (v. 8, the individual as a nature), and here *man* ("whoever," the generic relative pronoun) — the pattern of a sūrah that is writing about types rather than names. Verse 10's subject is *mina'l-nāsi man*, "among people, someone": a category with a face, not a file. The reader is expected to recognise himself, and the sūrah has arranged its vocabulary so that no reader can safely assume he has merely read about somebody else.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:11

> **Surely God knows those who believe, and surely He knows the hypocrites.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sentence That Ends the Community's Litigation**

*wa-la-yaʿlamanna Allāhu alladhīna āmanū wa-la-yaʿlamanna al-munāfiqīn.* As in verse 3 and verse 7, the clause is loaded with the oath *lām* and the emphasis *nūn*, because its subject is the one thing a community under persecution most needs to know and least has the capacity to determine: who among us is genuinely in. The verb is not "God will judge" but "God knows" — a statement of an attribute rather than of an event. The effect on the reader is twofold and both effects are intended. To the person whose nerve wobbled in verse 10, the sentence is protection: the case is closed in the only court whose verdict matters, and no committee of the pious may reopen it. To the person who exploits the community's hospitality, it is a threat: the surveillance you have evaded is not evaded, and the Knower of the breasts has your file.

**munāfiq: The Animal That Lives in Two Holes**

The word the sūrah uses at this early moment is worth dwelling on, because its Arabic etymology is a piece of social analysis. In the lexicographers' accounts — al-Azharī in *Tahdhīb al-lugha*, Ibn Farīs in *Maqāyīs al-lugha*, and the *Ṣiḥāḥ* of al-Jawharī — *nifāq* is derived from the *nāfiqāʾ*, the desert rat's burrow, or from the jerboa's hole (*yardiʾ*), which has two openings: the animal appears at one and vanishes through the other, so that the observer who watches either mouth never catches it entering or leaving. Al-Qurṭubī and others record that the Arabs called the hypocrite *munāfiq* on this analogy, and the lexicographers add that a *qāsiʿāʾ* hole with two mouths is precisely what a dissembler's religion resembles: a burrow with two exits, in which a man goes out toward the believers when the believers are strong and out toward the disbelievers when they are ascendant, never caught, never committed. This etymology is not a philological curiosity; it states the Qur'anic definition more accurately than most theological summaries: the *munāfiq* is defined not by a wrong belief but by the availability of both exits. Which is why the source commentary on this verse notes that in the Qur'anic context hypocrisy indicates "moral wavering in addition to conscious dissembling," so that one who is unable to commit and always keeping options open can be called a *munāfiq* in a real, if secondary, sense.

**A Word in the Wrong Century? The Chronological Puzzle**

The source notes that this is "most likely the first use of *munāfiqūn* in the chronological order of revelation," and adds that hypocrisy "did not become an issue within the Muslim community until the Madinan period." The tension is only apparent, and its resolution is one of the standard illustrations of how the sciences of revelation handle such cases. Three positions are on record. (1) Those who hold that the sūrah's first ten or eleven verses were revealed at Madinah (a view transmitted in Ibn al-Jawzī's and al-Qurṭubī's catalogues) take the term as belonging to that Madinan block; on this view verses 2–11, spoken about the emigration crisis, were sent down when the phenomenon of the double exit had become a civic problem. (2) Those who insist the whole sūrah is Makkan explain that the *word* was current in Arabic — the language had the rat-hole metaphor long before Madinah had the political type — and that the verse describes the dissembling of the weak-faithed in Makkah rather than the organised *khābijah* ʿAbdullāh ibn Ubayy would later lead. (3) A third group, reflected in ʿĀʾishah's reports and in al-Wāḥidī's *Asbāb*, holds that the revelation speaks of both at once, since the same behaviour is continuous: the man who denies God under pressure in Makkah and the man who denies God under pressure in Madinah are the same species, and the sūrah's moral vocabulary is deliberately not confined to one generation. None of the three readings disturbs the verse's function, which is to assign the sorting to God.

**The Qur'an's Anatomy of Hypocrisy, Elsewhere**

The sūrah here gives only the name; elsewhere the Book gives the physiology, and the description in al-Baqarah is its charter: *"And among the people are some who say, 'we believe in God and the Last Day,' though they are not believers. They seek to deceive God and those who believe, and they deceive only themselves, though they do not perceive. In their hearts is sickness, so God has increased their sickness; and for them is a painful punishment for what they used to fabricate"* (2:14–16). Three features recur in every passage: the dissembler's *theological* emptiness is not ignorance but the refusal to let belief cost anything; the *social* function of their speech is to buy safety; and the *psychological* result is disease — *maraḍ fī qulūbihim*. In Sūrat al-Munāfiqūn, a Madinan sūrah, the portrait is completed with a single devastating sentence about their oratory: *"And when you see them, their bodies please you; and if they speak, you listen to their speech — as though they were logs propped up"* (63:4). Ibn Kathīr, on 29:11, stresses that the sūrah's two clauses do not simply list two groups: they answer the demand implicit in verse 10, for the disbelievers' recruiters promised to carry the converts' sins, and the hypocrite's hope of passing unnoticed is the same promise in a different currency. God's knowing both classes is the verse's refutation of both deals.

**The ḥadīth of the Practical Hypocrite**

The Prophet ﷺ used *nifāq* in a second, ethical sense, and this is the sense that governs the everyday application of the verse. From ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr, in al-Bukhārī (33) and Muslim (59):

> *"Whoever has four qualities in him is a pure hypocrite; and whoever has one of them in him has a quality of hypocrisy in him, until he abandons it: when he is entrusted he betrays, when he speaks he lies, when he promises he breaks, and when he disputes he transgresses."*

The significance for this verse is that the Qur'anic term is not a category one may apply at will to doubtful believers. Al-Nawawī's comment on the ḥadīth is that the "hypocrisy" in question is practical (*ʿamalī*), which does not expel a person from the religion, and that the ḥadīth therefore disciplines the speaker's conduct rather than licensing him to judge another's faith. That distinction is exactly what verse 11's syntax enforces: God *knows* the *munāfiqūn* — the verb of knowledge is reserved to Him — while the community is given verbs of action, not verbs of diagnosis.

**The Legal Fruit: Probing Hearts Is Forbidden Because Only God Can**

The most consequential development of this verse in Islamic law is the ruling that a person's inward state is not to be investigated. The community's courts judge by profession, by evidence, and by outward conduct; they do not judge belief, intention, or secret consistency. The proof-text most often marshalled is the case of Usāma ibn Zayd, who killed an enemy who, at the point of the sword, said *lā ilāha illā Allāh*. When the Prophet ﷺ learned of it he kept repeating:

> *"You killed him after he said 'there is no god but God'? How will you deal with 'there is no god but God' when it comes on the Day of Resurrection?"* Usāma said, "He only said it to save himself from the spear." He said, *"Did you tear open his heart so that you could know whether he said it or not?"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 83; al-Bukhārī 6874)

The ḥadīth's rhetorical question — *afala shaqaqta ʿan qalbihi* — is the sharpest formulation in all the literature of the principle stated in 29:11. Where the sūrah says "God knows the hypocrites," the law infers: and therefore you are not the tribunal; you are, at most, the witness of deeds. This is the reason the classical jurists insisted on *ḥukm al-ẓāhir* — ruling by the apparent — even for people they privately suspected; and it is one of the most under-appreciated achievements of Islamic public morality, since a community that is barred from testing the sincerity of its members is a community in which hypocrisy is punished only at the level of its visible consequences, while the private struggle of the man described in verse 10 remains his own and God's.

**Prayer as the Revealer: The Sociology of Costly Attendance**

Why, then, does the sūrah place such weight on trial rather than on profession? The Prophetic material answers with an illustration that any sociologist of religion would recognise. Al-Bukhārī (657) and Muslim (651) transmit:

> *"The heaviest prayers for the hypocrites are the night prayer and the dawn prayer; and were they to know what is in them, they would come to them even crawling."*

A claim that costs nothing can be made by anyone; a practice that costs sleep, weather, and freedom is claimed only by those who mean it. The tradition's entire ethic of congregational worship rests on this: the ḥadīth of ʿUthmān — *"Whoever prays the night prayer in congregation is as though he had spent the night in worship"* (Muslim 656) — and the Prophet's ﷺ remark that the "smallest prayer" is the one in which the worshipper's attention is present. Verse 11's two-fold divine knowledge is the theological ground for this: God sorts, and the ordering of a community's practices around genuine cost is how that sorting becomes visible to the community itself. Which is precisely the function attributed to trial in verse 3, "so that the truthful and the liars are made manifest," and the same function the sūrah will assign to striving and patience in 29:69 and 29:59.

**The Verse's Quiet Mercy**

The verse is read too quickly when taken only as a warning. Its second half terrifies the dissembler; its first half comforts the anxious. The weak Meccan believer, who had been frightened into silence and then beaten into a "recantation" he did not mean, is here told that his faith — not his behaviour under torture — is the object of God's knowledge, and God's knowledge is the only measure that counts. The entire arc of this opening passage of the sūrah is captured in that: verse 2 said you will be tried; verse 3 said God knows the sincere and the false; verse 10 said the fear of people can be mistaken for the fear of God; and verse 11 concludes that the verdict on which of these a person has done is handed to the One whose knowledge never errs. No human institution, including a religious one, is granted the competence to say who is in. What is granted is the duty to keep going, to worship in the heavy hours, and to leave the sorting to the Knower of the breasts.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:12

> **Those who disbelieve say to those who believe, “Follow our path and we shall bear your sins.” But they bear not aught of their sins. Truly they are liars.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Recruiter's Clause: "We Shall Bear Your Sins"**

*wa-qāla alladhīna kafarū lilladhīna āmanū-ittabiʿū sabīlanā wa-ḥmilū khaṭāyānā wa-mā ḥamilū min khaṭāyāhim min shayʾ* — the imperative of invitation, "follow our road," followed immediately by an indemnity: "and [let] our sins be carried." The Arabic has the recruiters saying *wa-ḥmilū khaṭāyānā* in a form that the commentators explain as either a command in the sense of a promise ("assume our faults — we will pay for them") or a coordinated assurance: the sin of your conversion is on our account. Whatever the syntactic route, the offer is one that recurs in the history of every pressure group: leave the truth and come to us, and we will take responsibility for what it costs you. Al-Ṭabarī paraphrases: they say to them, "be on our way, enter into our religion, and whatever of sin you bear on account of us shall be borne by us and not by you — we will carry it and you will be free." Then the Qur'an's four-word demolition: *wa-mā ḥāmilū min khaṭāyāhim min shayʾ* — "and they carried nothing of their sins, of anything."

**Ẓimm: The Commercial Vocabulary of a False Guarantee**

The word the sūrah uses in verse 13 for the load the leaders will actually carry is *wihr* (burden), but the notion of the guarantee in this verse belongs to the semantic field of *dhimma* and *ẓiʾmāʾ*: a *dāmin* is a surety, one who binds his own liability to another's debt. Islamic commercial law is precise about suretyship because the Qur'an legislates it at 2:283, and its rules give the verse its bite. A surety can guarantee a debt *only with the creditor's assent*, and no one can guarantee what is not transferable by nature — a bodily penalty, a personal act, a crime. What the recruiters are offering is a surety for a liability that admits no surety, to a creditor who has not agreed. The sūrah's *bi'sa* verdict, "they are liars," is thus not primarily a moral insult; it is the technical conclusion that the contract is void, not merely sinful. The idiom of the Arabic emphasises this: *kāḏibūn* after *mā ḥamilū… min shayʾ* closes the argument, since a liar is precisely one whose speech fails to correspond to what is, and here the correspondence is total — nothing was borne.

**The Law of Non-Transferability: Six Verses Say It Once**

The verse's principle is stated with unusual frequency in the Qur'an, and its recurrence in identical terms is itself an argument that it was a live issue: *"Say: shall I seek a lord other than God, while to every soul is owed what it has earned? None shall bear the burden of another"* (6:164); *"Whoever is guided is guided only for himself, and whoever goes astray goes astray only against himself; no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another"* (17:15); *"…and no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another. If a heavy-laden soul calls for its burden to be carried, nothing of it shall be carried, even by a near relative"* (35:18; the same clause with *yā ʿabādī* in 39:7; and *"that no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another — and that there is nothing for the human being except what he strove for"* in 53:18–25). The last of these is the sūrah's own complement: verse 6 above said "whoever strives, strives for himself," and 53:39 adds "there is nothing for the human being except what he strove for." Ibn Kathīr notes that verses such as these were revealed in refutation of the polytheists' promise, and that some early readers — including a report from Mujāhid — held that the Makkan verse 53:38 was later qualified, in the case of a *leader* who misguides others, by verses such as 16:25 and this sūrah's verse 13: the leader bears the load of his own crime of misleading, without in any way lightening his followers'. That is not transfer; it is multiplication on the recruiter's own account, and the two positions are reconciled by all the mainstream exegetes.

**Why the Offer Works: The Logic of an Insurable Conscience**

Every system that recruits by demanding a betrayal of conscience has to answer for the conscience, and the answer is almost always an indemnity clause. "Everyone does it." "They'll take responsibility." "You're only following orders." "Think of your family; they'll understand." Psychologists have a name for the mechanism — diffusion of responsibility, the reduction of felt accountability that occurs when an act is presented as delegated to or shared by a group — and the experiments that established it (the obedience studies of the 1960s being the most famous) showed precisely how much conduct is moved by the presence of an authority who says *the fault is mine*. The Qur'an's answer, given here to a small persecuted community in Makkah, is a piece of metaphysics used as moral hygiene: responsibility is non-transferable because the self is non-transferable. You are not a division of a corporation; your deeds are the only thing that remains attached to you when the group dissolves. The practical value of such a teaching is that it removes the one excuse that makes conformity feel free — and the sūrah will proceed in verses 13 and 68 to say that the fabricated absolution will not merely fail, but will be *questioned*.

**"Follow Our Path" — The Sabīl Claimed by the Wrong People**

The word *sabīl* (road) is one the Qur'an uses of every system of life, and it is the standard term for religion in this sūrah and its neighbours: God says of the hypocrites who claim the believers' victory, *"were we not with you?"* (4:141), and the Qur'an speaks of *"the paths of God"* when it means a way of life undertaken deliberately (cf. 2:212; 16:69, *"the paths of your Lord, made easy"*). The point is that the recruiters' phrase is a *claim* about a road, not the road itself, and the Qur'an elsewhere strips it of authority by describing it: *"Do you see the one who takes his own desire as his god, and God leaves him upon knowledge and seals his hearing and his heart?"* (45:23). Ibn ʿAbbās's one-line gloss on this family of verses, preserved in the tafsīr literature, is that *sabīl* here means "your way of life and your religion," and that the phrase exposes the polytheists' method: they did not argue for their creed, they recruited for it, and the currency they offered was absolution. A reader of this sūrah, in any generation, should notice how little that method has changed: the offer is rarely "our doctrine is true," and almost always "come with us and don't worry about the cost."

**A Structural Reading: Why This Verse Is Where It Is**

The passage from verses 2 to 13 has been built up from the inside of the believing community to its outer boundary: the wavering believer (v. 10), God's exclusive knowledge (v. 11), and now the recruiters outside (v. 12). This order matters because it identifies the same fault from both directions. The weak believer's failure was to overestimate the weight of the created; the recruiter's promise is to offer to remove the weight entirely. Both are attempts to escape the premise established in verse 3 — that the assay of a life is done by what each person bears. Verse 13 completes the reversal by telling the recruiters what they will in fact carry, and only then (v. 14) does the sūrah begin the long history of peoples who followed a *sabīl* because their fathers had. The placement is pedagogy: before the reader is shown Noah's people, Abraham's, Lot's, Shuʿayb's, ʿĀd, Thamūd, and Pharaoh, he is told what it is worth to have been recruited, so that he can read the whole catalogue of nations as a single case of verse 12 multiplied.

**Practical Conclusions the Jurists and Moralists Drew**

Al-Qurṭubī extracts from the verse the rule that no sin may be committed on the instruction of any person — sovereign, scholar, parent, or employer — because the sūrah has closed the mechanism of transfer; and this is the same ground as 29:8's "then obey them not." The Sufi writers drew the interior lesson: the one who tells you "I will carry it" is the *nafs* and the *shayṭān*, whose method is always to underwrite what they cannot pay, and al-Muḥāsibī's *al-Riʿāya* repeatedly returns to this verse to warn against delegating one's conscience to a spiritual director, a group, or a mood. And the moralists, from Māwardī's *Adab al-dunyā wa'l-dīn* onward, read the verse as a general law of social life: authority can direct conduct but cannot absorb guilt, which is why the classical theory of governance insists that the ruler's command does not transform a wrong into a right. Every serious account of the Eichmann defence — "I was only following orders" — is an argument that this verse has already settled: a liability that cannot be transferred cannot be discharged by obedience.

**"Truly They Are Liars" — and What Follows**

The verse closes with a verdict and verse 13 will open with an oath: *wa-la-yaḥmilunna authārahum wa-authāran min authārihim*. The liars are not said to be powerful, wealthy, or numerous; they are said to be *untrue*. The sūrah has reduced the conflict to a question of correspondence between claim and reality, and the trial announced in verse 2 is, in the end, simply the moment when correspondence is tested. A reader who wants to be safe from recruiters in any age needs only the two facts this verse teaches: nothing is being offered that can be delivered, and nobody at the far end of the road will be standing there to carry what he promised to carry.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:13

> **Surely they will bear their own burdens, and others’ burdens along with their own, and on the Day of Resurrection they will surely be questioned concerning that which they used to fabricate.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ledger That Closes Verse 12**

Verse 12 recorded the recruiters' promise; verse 13 records the audit: *wa-la-yaḥmilunna authārahum wa-authāran min authārihim wa-la-yus'alunna yawma'l-qiyāmati ʿammā kānū yaftarūn.* Three elements, in order: the sin of their own disbelief; a further load from the sins of those they misled; and a question — a specific interrogation about the *iftirāʾ*, the fabrication they manufactured. The verse answers verse 12 twice over. The recruiters will not carry the converts' sins — those remain the converts' own — but they will carry two loads of their own: their unbelief and the weight of the corruption they introduced. The offer is void in both directions, and the sūrah's word for the additional load, *min authārihim*, is deliberately vague enough to include everything they originated: the doctrine, the ritual, the custom, and the social pressure.

**awzār: A Word Borrowed from the Caravan**

The plural *authār* of *wihr* (also *wizr*) is taken from the beast's load, the cargo-straps of a camel; the same root gives *wāzira*, a vizier — the one who carries the ruler's burden, an administrative office named from a pack-saddle. In the Qur'an the word retains that physicality: sin is not an abstraction but a *weight*, a cargo that a soul must carry on a road that has no rest-houses, which is why the verse describing the Day says *"if a heavy-laden soul calls for its burden to be carried, nothing of it shall be carried, even by a near relative"* (35:18). Ibn ʿAbbās's gloss on the present verse, transmitted by al-Ṭabarī, is exactly this: their own burdens are their unbelief, and "burdens along with their own" are the weights they laid on others — "they carry their own sins and the sins of those who obeyed them, without anything being lessened from the sins of the followers." Al-Rāzī, who treats the passage as a locus of the doctrine of acquired responsibility, presses the point: the additional load is not an injustice because it is *earned by the leader's own act* of misguiding; no one is punished for another's deed, but everyone is punished for the deeds he caused in others — which is why the same principle grounds the reward for guidance in the ḥadīth:

> *"Whoever calls to guidance, his reward is like the rewards of those who follow him, and it lessens nothing from their rewards; and whoever calls to misguidance, upon him are its sins like the sins of those who follow him, and it lessens nothing from their sins."* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2674, from Abū Hurayrah)

That ḥadīth is the mirror-image of verse 13, and it explains why the sūrah can promise the striver that all his striving is for himself (29:6) and yet make the misleader carry what he set in motion: causation is a form of acquisition, and what is acquired is owed.

**"They Used to Fabricate" — iftirāʾ and the Sūrah's Central Charge**

*Yaftarūn*, from *iftirāʾ*, is the sūrah's own recurring accusation. Its root means to stretch out a hide so as to work it into a shape — hence "to fashion, to construct, to produce something that had no prior basis," and in its moral use, "to forge." The Qur'an uses it for the invention of divine partners (6:21, 6:93, 6:144; 7:37; 10:17; 11:18; 16:116), for the claim of revelations not received (6:93, 10:38, 11:13, 46:8), and here for the absolution-clause of verse 12. In this sūrah, the verb occurs in its closing verses as the definitive description of the disbelievers' project: *"Who does greater wrong than one who fabricates a lie against God or denies the truth when it comes unto him?"* (29:68). The thread is consistent: idolatry, false promises of forgiveness, and the attribution of partners are all treated as *fabrication* — human production mistaken for divine givenness — and so the interrogation of verse 13 is a forensic examination of a workshop: the liars will be asked about what they manufactured, and the gods they forged will not answer for them: *"And the Day He gathers them all, then says to those who associated partners: 'Your place — you and your partners!' Then We separate them, and their partners say: 'You were not worshipping us'"* (10:28; cf. 16:86–87, where the associates disown their worshippers and the worshippers swear, "by God our Lord, we were not polytheists").

**The Question and Its Function: A Reckoning That Is Also an Exposure**

The verse's third element, *wa-la-yus'alunna… ʿammā*, deserves emphasis because the Qur'anic "questioning" is not always an inquiry. God's question on the Day — *"Where are My partners whom you used to claim?"* (28:72) — is rhetorical in one sense and real in another: real as the disclosure of evidence, rhetorical as the establishment of the proof against them, since He already knows. The sūrah uses the two ideas together: verse 3 established that God's knowledge precedes the test; verse 13 adds the interview that follows it. In al-Qurṭubī's summary, "the questioning is the questioning of humiliation (*suʾāl taqrīʿ*), not of information." This is why the interrogation of verse 13 attaches specifically to *what they fabricated*: the fabricated is precisely the thing that cannot be defended by appeal to any authority — a forged document can be defended only by a forger. Compare the parallel of al-Naḥl, which supplies the same sequence with more detail:

> *"On the Day of Resurrection God will degrade them, and say: 'Where are My partners, for whose sake you used to press your claims, regarding those you opposed?' Those who were given knowledge will say: 'Indeed, degradation today and evil are upon the disbelievers, whose souls the angels take while they are wronging themselves.'"* (Qur'an 16:27–28)

and the exchange in 16:86–88, where the associates "will be of no help to them."

**Leadership as Burden-Multiplication: The Political Reading**

The verse is, read socially, a treatise on the responsibility of influence, and the ḥadīth literature supplies its legal complement:

> *"Each of you is a shepherd, and each of you is responsible for his flock: the ruler is a shepherd and responsible for his subjects; a man is a shepherd over his household and responsible for them; a woman is a shepherd in her husband's house and responsible for it; a servant is a shepherd over his master's property and responsible for it."* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 893; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1829, from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿUmar)

Four forms of authority and one rule: the wider your reach, the more *authār* you carry. The sūrah's verse is stronger, because it adds that a leader's sin is not only his own but is multiplied by every imitator — which is why the classical mirror-for-princes literature treats the ruler's private vices as public crimes, and why Māwardī's *al-Aḥkām al-sulṭāniyya* makes the ruler's example part of the definition of his office. Read in this key, verse 13 is the Qur'an's most concentrated warning to teachers, imams, editors, founders, and parents: what you normalise is charged to you. And it is equally a warning against the romanticisation of charismatic leadership — the "follow our path" of verse 12 is spoken by a leader, and a leader's assurance is the most expensive commodity in the world.

**The Consolation Hidden in the Verse**

The sūrah's first audience was being told to stay where they were weak; verse 13's twin, 16:25, tells them why the recruiters are not to be envied: *"so that they may carry their burdens in full on the Day of Resurrection, and something of the burdens of those they mislead without knowledge."* A persecuted minority, watching its persecutors prosper, is here given the balance sheet of the future — a promise of justice, and one that requires no action on their part. The sūrah's theology of the *ajal* (29:5, 29:53) supports this: the delay is not exemption, and this verse supplies the item the delay conceals — accumulated, compounding, and then formally questioned. Ibn Kathīr reads the verse with the Makkan situation in view: the chiefs who recruited, coerced and boasted of their sanctuary's privileges will find that those they recruited disown them, and that the disowning is mutual. The Qur'an's picture of that exchange is one of its most terrible: *"the Day when the friends become enemies to one another, except the reverent"* (43:67); *"those who were followed disowned those who followed them, and they saw the punishment, and the ties between them were cut. And the followers will say: if only we had one return, we would disown them as they have disowned us"* (2:167; cf. 33:67–68). And the verdict handed down on both parties at once — *"for each is a double portion"* (7:38) — is the exegetes' standard proof-text beside 29:13's two loads.

**Modern Reach: Diffused Guilt and the Question That Comes Anyway**

Contemporary institutions are engineered for the distribution of responsibility — committees, chains of command, algorithms, anonymity, the "we all did it" defence, and the modern variant of "we shall bear your sins": the assurance that the system absorbs moral accountability. Verse 13 describes the mechanics of exactly this and denies its efficacy twice: the load does not transfer (v. 12), and it multiplies in the hands of whoever introduced the practice (v. 13). Two consequences for any reader. (1) No organisational cover converts an act into a non-act; the person who performs an obvious wrong inside a sanctioned structure has, on this sūrah's account, only added the recruiter to the list of the interrogated — and the recruiter's presence there does not remove him from it. (2) The person who has shaped others' behaviour should expect their own file to include their influence: this is, in Islamic ethics, not a threat so much as a description of what a life is made of. The sūrah's promise of questioning is thus at the same time its most serious dignity: you are asked about what you fabricated, which means you are treated as an author, not an instrument.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:14

> **And indeed We sent Noah unto his people and he remained among them a thousand years, less fifty; and the flood seized them, for they were wrongdoers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Case File of Nine and a Half Centuries**

With verse 14 the sūrah stops arguing and starts producing evidence. *wa-la-qad arsalnā Nūḥan ilā qawmihi fa-labitha fīhim alfa sanatin illā khamsīna ʿāman* — and the choice of opening is itself the argument: the very same *lām* of oath and *qad* of realisation that confirmed the law of trial in verses 2–3 now confirms the career of the man who exemplifies it. Trial is real, precedent is real, and the first precedent offered is the longest in the Book: nine hundred and fifty years of preaching. No other figure in scripture, Islamic or otherwise, is credited with a prophetic tenure of that length, and the sūrah mentions it here not as an edifying statistic but as a rebuttal. Its audience were believers who had held out for perhaps twenty years and were being asked to keep holding out while the persecutors prospered (29:4). Against that, verse 14 says: the first man to whom this was done spent almost a millennium in it, and his people's answer to all of it was the refusal quoted in verse 18.

**labitha, sanah, ʿām: Small Words Doing Large Work**

Two linguistic notices repay attention. First, the verb: *labitha* (to remain, to stay) rather than *makatha* or *bātha* — the root *l-b-th* in Arabic carries the sense of a prolonged, embedded dwelling, the same root the Book uses for the length of a stay as it will look at the end: "the Day He gathers them, it will be as though they had not remained but an hour of the day, and they will recognise one another" (10:45), and "on the Day they see what they are promised, it will be as though they had not remained but an evening or its morning" (79:46); Noah's own prayer in the sūrah of the Poets asks, at the end of his long stay, "give me judgement and join me to the righteous, and place for me a tongue of truth among the later ones" (26:83–84). The verb therefore frames Noah's nine centuries as *duration under difficulty*, and it names exactly the quality verse 2 demanded of the Makkan believers: not a short heroism but a long stay. Second, the numbers: "a thousand years, *except* fifty" — and the sūrah shifts the word for "year" mid-phrase, from *sanah* to *ʿām*. Arab rhetoricians treat such variation (*tanawwuʿ al-alfāẓ*) as a stylistic device that relieves monotony while the meaning stays identical, and most lexicographers regard the two as simple synonyms. A second school, however, records a real distinction: *al-ʿām* from plenty and the year of abundance, *al-sanah* from *sunū* — leanness — and the year of drought and scarcity. On that reading, the shift in a single clause quietly encodes the texture of nine centuries of obedience: famine-years and fruitfulness-years, counted in both vocabularies, which is how a long patience actually feels from the inside. The device of naming the total and then subtracting — *illā khamsīna* — also has a rhetorical effect the commentators notice: it fixes the number twice over, first as the roundness of a millennium, then as its shortfall, so that the hearer cannot round off in his own favour.

**The Arithmetic of the Sentence, and the Purpose It Serves**

Al-Ṭabarī's report of the earlier authorities is careful: the ninety-five0 years named here is the duration of Noah's *call* among his people — his stay in their midst — not necessarily the whole of his life; some transmitters, drawing on the reports circulating among the People of the Book, held that his entire lifespan was a millennium less fifty, and note that Genesis 9:29 gives exactly 950 years for Noah's whole life. Whether the agreement of figures is taken as a shared prophetic tradition or as a literary coincidence, the Qur'an's deployment of the number is distinct: it makes the figure do pastoral work. A millennium of daʿwah with, according to the majority of the exegetes' reports, only a handful of believers (the sūrah's own next verse will count them as "the companions of the ship") is the most extreme possible answer to the question of verse 2 — "do you suppose you will be left untested?" The sūrah will generalise the same point twice in the next verses: *if you deny, then communities have certainly denied before you* (29:18), and *We will certainly try you until We know those of you who strive and are steadfast* (47:31). The length is not decoration; the length is the argument.

**"The Flood Seized Them, for They Were Wrongdoers"**

*fa-akhadhahumu'l-ṭūfānu wa-hum ẓālimūn.* The word *ṭūfān* is from *ṭāfa*, "to go around, to circle" — the same root as the *ṭawāf* around the Kaʿba — so that the term names a deluge that encircles and surrounds, cutting off every exit; it is the word chosen to describe water that came, as the tradition puts it, "from the valley and from the sky," so that nothing remained outside it. The grammar of the sentence gives the causation with two devices at once: the perfect verb of sudden seizing — *fa-akhadhahumu'l-ṭūfānu*, the verb used throughout this sūrah for punishment arriving unbidden (*fa-akhadhathumu'l-rajfatu*, 29:37; *fa-kullan akhadhnāhu bi-dhanbihi*, 29:40) — and the accusative circumstantial clause *wa-hum ẓālimūn*, "while they were wrongdoers," which the grammarians read as explicative of the cause. The punishment is not a reaction to Noah's preaching; it is the closing of a case built out of their own wrongdoing — and the same construction will be repeated in the sūrah's conclusion about all the nations: *God wronged them not, but themselves did they wrong* (29:40). Between those two verses the sūrah is able to say, without further argument, that the moral order is intelligible: catastrophe is not arbitrary.

**The Justice Question, Answered in Advance**

Because the sūrah's first audience had suffered, its fourth verse had to say a word about why the flood came only at the end. The classical exegetes draw from the 950 years a rule about divine patience: al-Qurṭubī notes that God's punishment is not hastened by His anger but released only when the proof is complete — Noah warned, Noah reasoned, Noah was mocked, Noah endured, and only then was the ark built in public, in the middle of a desert, for decades, according to several reports, as a standing final notice. Ibn Kathīr's paraphrase of 29:14 makes the pedagogy explicit: God informs the Makkan believers that the fate of the deniers was destruction after long forbearance, so that their own present respite is not a licence. And al-Rāzī adds the metaphysical corollary that such a punishment cannot be called unjust, since every individual among that people had received the full measure of warning the species could receive — the principle that 4:165 states as the purpose of sending messengers at all: *"so that mankind should have no plea against God after the messengers."* The flood is thus the sūrah's answer to verse 4's "evil is the judgment they make": the delay is not denial, and the delay is finite.

**A Prophet's Loss, and the Sūrah's Family Theme**

One feature of the Qur'an's Noah-material must be read into this verse, because the sūrah leaves it in silence and mentions it elsewhere: this nine-hundred-and-fifty-year vocation did not save his household entire. *"He called out to his son — and he was apart — 'O my son, ride with us, and do not be with the disbelievers.' He said: 'I will take refuge on a mountain; it will protect me from the water.' He said: 'There is no protector today from God's command, except whom He mercies.'"* (11:42–43). When verse 14 is followed immediately in verse 15 by "the companions of the ship," the sūrah's own arithmetic is stated — those aboard were a small number, and one of Noah's own blood was not among them. That is the exact lesson 29:8 legislated for the convert whose parents press him toward unbelief: kinship does not adjudicate truth, and the duty of kindness does not guarantee shared destiny. The Makkan fathers and sons who were now divided by faith were told, in the person of the longest-preaching prophet, that a family split on God is not a failure of love nor a sign of loss: it is the shape prophecy takes in a household.

**What the Verse Asks of Its Reader Now**

Two applications close the verse. The first is a rebuke of short horizons: a person who expects reform to complete itself within a season, a movement whose second decade feels like defeat, a parent who has supplicated for an adult child for twenty years, a teacher whose best student has not yet appeared — all are addressed by a sūrah that gives a millennium and fifty as a *unit* and counts its outcome as success because the messenger's duty was proclamation (29:18). The second is the reverse: the long patience is not the same as a long impunity. The flood came at the end of the ninth and a half centuries, and verse 40's catalogue will show that in every case the interval was finite. The verse therefore instructs both the persevering and the presuming at once — the persecuted are told that their trial has precedent and an end; the persecutors are told that their respite has an end too. The sūrah's own later verse puts both in one sentence: *"God's term is coming"* (29:5).

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:15

> **Then We saved him and the companions of the ship, and We made it a sign for the worlds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**thumma: The Conjunction That Does the Theology**

*thumma anjaynāhu wa-aṣḥāba's-safīnati wa-jaʿalnāhā āyatan li'l-ʿālamīn.* The opening *thumma* in Arabic marks not merely sequence but interval and weight — "and then, after all that, and at the right distance of time from it…" The sūrah could have used *fa-*; it did not. The chosen conjunction slows the sentence exactly where the narrative needed slowing: after the flood-verse's sudden seizing, there is a pause before salvation is mentioned, as if to let the reader understand that deliverance is not the mirror-image of destruction. The one who saved and the one who destroyed are the same agent, and the sūrah's point is that between the two acts there stands a period of waiting during which Noah built, preached, and was mocked. Salvation, in other words, is not the absence of catastrophe but its right ordering. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the verse praises Noah's company as "the people of the ship," and that the *thumma* separates the rescue of the believers from the drowning of the deniers to indicate that each was accomplished at its own appointed time.

**"The Companions of the Ship": a Community Named by Its Vessel**

The phrase *aṣḥāb al-safīna* is grammatically and theologically rich. *Ṣāḥib*, in Arabic, is the one who is attached to something — companion, owner, associate; and the plural here is used rather than "his family" or "his followers," so that the saved are identified not by blood but by the fact of being aboard. Al-Ṭabarī records the disagreement about their number: some said they were Noah's children and wives; others transmitted a larger company — ten, eight, seventy-two, or seventy-eight persons, divided evenly between men and women — and al-Zamakhsharī mentions that the reports differ so widely precisely because the revelation did not fix the number. That is itself instructive: the sūrah's interest is in the *category*, not the census. Anyone, of any lineage, who is on board is a "companion of the ship," and the phrase has become in the Qur'an's own afterlife a standing metaphor for the saved community — cf. the Prophet ﷺ's image of the believers' company as a ship:

> *"The likeness of the one who holds to God's limits and the one who transgresses them is like a people who drew lots in a ship: some of them gained the upper deck, some the lower. When those below wanted water, they passed through those above, and said: if we make a hole in our portion we will not harm those above us. If they leave them to themselves, all will perish; and if they take hold of their hands, all will be saved."* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 2493)

The Makkan community — small, mixed in lineage, some free and some enslaved, and physically aboard a project of God rather than a tribe — heard in this phrase both an identity and a rule: salvation is by the vessel, not the family.

**"We Made It a Sign for the Worlds" — What Is "It"?**

The feminine pronoun of *jaʿalnāhā* has been referred to three antecedents by the grammarians and exegetes, and each reference opens a layer of meaning. (1) It is the ship: Ibn Kathīr, following earlier reports, transmits that the vessel remained on the mountain (Jūdī, named at 11:44: *"O earth, swallow your water; O sky, hold back"*) and that generations afterwards people visited it and drew benefit from the sight. Al-Ṭabarī records reports that its planks survived in that region, and the point of such a relic is precisely what 29:35 will say about Lot's town: a *sign* requires an address that outlives the event. (2) It is the *āya*, the token or the event of destruction and deliverance taken together; al-Ṭabarsī in *Majmaʿ al-bayān* makes this reading carry the eschatological sense: the separation of Noah's company from his people on the water is a sign of the separation of believers from disbelievers at the Resurrection — the flood as a rehearsal of the division of the *ṣirāṭ*. (3) It is the *qaṣīṣah*, the recited narrative — the story as a durable object, made a sign for the *ʿālamīn*, "all the worlds," not only for Noah's contemporaries. This last reading is the one the sūrah's own vocabulary supports, since it will say of the Qur'an, *"Nay, it is clear signs in the breasts of those who have been given knowledge"* (29:49): a story retold and memorised becomes a sign, and a story that becomes a sign is a story that has not finished its work. The phrase *"yassarnā al-Qurʾāna li'l-dhikri fa-hal min muddakir"* — "We have made the Qur'an easy for remembrance; is there anyone to remember?" (54:17), said in the context of Noah's people (54:9–16) — belongs to the same idea.

**Signs for Whom? The Universal Address**

The dative *li'l-ʿālamīn* is emphatic, and the sūrah's use of *āya* language in this passage should be read as a set: verse 15 "a sign for the worlds," verse 24 "in that are signs for a people who believe," verse 35 "a manifest sign for a people who understand," verse 44 "in that is a sign for the believers," verse 49 "clear signs in the breasts of the learned." The epistemology is consistent: the sign exists, but the sign does not compel; it is legible according to the capacity and orientation of the reader. This is what makes verse 15 the sūrah's answer to the demand for spectacle in verse 50: the prophets' deliverances *are* signs, publicly left behind — a mountain-top ship, a burnt-and-cooled fire, a ruined town, a recited narrative — and the objection that no sign has come is a statement about the objector, not about the evidence. Al-Rāzī makes this the pivot for a wider point: if every claimant were confirmed by an irresistible prodigy, the trial declared in verses 2–3 would be dissolved, and with it the moral significance of belief.

**The Ship, the Water, and the Mercy That Prefers Neither**
