One feature of the Noah-material is highlighted by the commentators precisely because the sūrah compresses it: Noah was saved "and the companions of the ship," and the sūrah does not say "and the best of them." The Qur'anic account elsewhere makes the human company mixed enough — a son lost, a wife excluded (in the parallel case of Lot, 66:10) — and the tradition's moral is drawn accordingly: the ship is a mercy for those who board, not an award for those who excel. Al-Qurṭubī, citing the earlier exegetes, notes that the ark was a place of inconvenience: cramped, crowded, provisioned with what could be carried, and surrounded by a world that had ended; deliverance and comfort are not the same thing, and the sūrah, writing to people who would find their own deliverance in a hard emigration to Madinah, does not pretend otherwise. The phrase *aṣḥāb al-safīna* thus functions as a description of the believer's condition in this world: saved, and not yet at home.

**How the Verse Applies the Sūrah's Opening Argument**

Verse 2 asked whether people suppose they will be left to say "we believe" and not be tried. Verse 15 now answers with a single case: the trial lasted nine centuries and fifty years; the boat held; and the whole thing was left as an address to everybody. The sūrah has thus completed in two verses the syllogism with which it began. Trial is universal (v. 2); it is the ancient method with earlier peoples (v. 3); and its outcome — separation — is the only monument that survives (v. 15). What is "made a sign for the worlds" is not primarily the drowning of the persecutors, which the powerful tend to dismiss as legend, but the *persistence* of the saved minority's story: a wooden vessel, a small company, and a recited narrative that outlives every empire that mocked it. That is the sūrah's real argument with verse 4's presumption of impunity.

**A Final Note on the Verse's Own Echo**

The sūrah's next move will be to name a man who, in the Qur'an's own words, is "the father" of the very nations about to be discussed (29:16). The transition from Noah to Abraham is the transition from the deliverance of a few by water to the deliverance of one by fire, and the two verses are shaped alike: trial, refusal, salvation, sign. Ibn Kathīr notices the symmetry and reads it as the sūrah's consolation technique: God does not console His messenger with abstract promises but with the careers of those who came before, arranged so that the hearer can find his own story inside the older one. The reader of this sūrah is meant to end in exactly that position — inside the story, aboard, counted among "the companions of the ship," and reading a sign addressed to the worlds.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:16

> **And [We sent] Abraham, when he said to his people, “Worship God and reverence Him. That is better for you, if you but knew.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Name in the Accusative: The Grammar of Continuation**

*wa-Ibrāhīma idh qāla li-qawmihi* — the syntax is compressed, and the exegetes discuss what governs the accusative *Ibrāhīma*. The preferred reading is that it is conjoined to *Nūḥan* in verse 14, with the verb of sending carried over: "We sent Noah… and Abraham, when he said." The effect is a single continuum of mission: one verb of divine sending governs several careers. This is the sūrah's method of writing prophetic history — not a series of isolated legends but one chain with one sender. Notice, too, that the sūrah introduces Abraham with the words *idh qāla*, "when he said," not with his genealogy, his miracles, or his exile: the man is defined by his address. Al-Zamakhsharī remarks on this economy that the sūrah needs only the moment when speech was made, because the argument is the miracle.

**The Two Commands and Their Order**

*Uʿbudū Allāh wa-ttaqūhu* — two imperatives, and the order is not interchangeable. Worship is the positive act: the direction of the whole self toward God. Reverence (*taqwā*) is the negative act, in the grammatical sense of prevention: the root *w-q-y* means to shield, to place between oneself and harm, and its object is the thing feared — here God's displeasure, and by extension every act that would incur it. Ibn Taymiyyah's definition of *ʿibāda* is the widest in the tradition: "an all-embracing term for everything God loves and is pleased with, of words and deeds, inward and outward" — so that worship names the whole of life rightly ordered, and taqwā names the guardrail that keeps the ordering from collapsing under pressure. Al-Ghazālī, defining taqwā in the *Iḥyāʾ*, uses the image the tradition loves: it is like walking through a thorny path, where the traveller rolls up his sleeves and moves carefully, tucking his garments in — not fleeing the road, but refusing to walk it carelessly. That is exactly the Makkan situation: the believers were not told to abandon the road of public life but to walk it in a way that keeps them from being snagged by every thorn — and the "garments" to be tucked away, the sūrah will say in verse 8, are the very claims of kinship when they catch on idolatry.

The pairing carries a psychological insight the commentators spell out. Worship without reverence produces form without continuity: rites performed with no sense of accountability decay into habit, and the Qur'an's own verdict on that condition is unsparing — *"Greatly hateful it is with God that you say what you do not do"* (61:3), and *"the likeness of those charged with the Torah and who did not carry it out is the likeness of the donkey carrying a load of books"* (62:5). Reverence without worship produces inhibition without direction: a person who only avoids does not become anyone. The sūrah gives Abraham's people both in four words — a direction and a brake.

**"That Is Better for You": The Argument from Benefit**

*Dhālikum khayrun lakum in kuntaum taʿlamūn.* The comparative *khayr* is chosen over any language of divine claim; Abraham does not say "God has a right over you" but "this is better for you," and the sūrah will repeat the identical move in its own voice when it reports the argument about provision in the next verse. The principle is not incidental: the Qur'an's own summary of all messengership is *"We sent down to you a Book in which is your mention (dhikrukum) — will you not then understand?"* (21:10), and the same *khayr*-language places the entire law under the heading of human benefit: *"Do you see one who takes as his god his own desire, and God leaves him upon knowledge, and seals his hearing and his heart?"* (45:23) frames the alternative not as disobedience to a tyrant but as a bad bargain. The classical jurists built on this their doctrine of *maṣlaḥa*: al-Shāṭibī's *al-Muwāfaqāt* argues that the whole of the *sharīʿa* came to secure necessities — religion, life, intellect, lineage, property — and quotes precisely the prophets' style of address as evidence, "for no messenger ever called his people to what was God's loss if they refused; he called them to what was their gain if they accepted."

The second half of the sentence — *in kuntaum taʿlamūn*, "if you were knowing ones" — is the address that respects the hearer. Abraham is not commanding assent; he is inviting judgement, with the conditional leaving the verdict in their hands. This is the sūrah's consistent epistemology: parables are set forth "so that none understands them save those who know" (29:43); the Qur'an is "clear signs in the breasts of those who have been given knowledge" (29:49). Revelation in this sūrah never asks for suspension of the faculty it is addressing.

**Why the Sūrah Sets Abraham Here**

The placement after Noah is deliberate. Noah supplies the argument from duration — nine hundred and fifty years of preaching (29:14); Abraham supplies the argument from content, because he is the prophet whose people's error was the same as Quraysh's: worship of what their fathers worshipped, images that cannot provide. The Qur'an elsewhere gives Abraham's own diagnosis of inherited religion — *"he said to his father and his people: what are these statues to which you are devoted? You and your fathers are in manifest error"* (21:52–54) — and his people's only reply is recorded as: *"We found our fathers worshipping them"* (21:53). By naming the second argument after the first, the sūrah has covered both excuses its audience faced: "this has gone on too long, so perhaps we are mistaken" (answered by Noah) and "everyone around us does this, so it cannot be wrong" (answered by Abraham).

There is also a structural reason: Abraham is the patriarch of the Makkan Quraysh themselves. Their sanctuary was his building (2:127), their prestige his inheritance, and the sūrah's charge — that they worship what cannot provide — is made by the only ancestor they all honoured. The Qur'an's claim of continuity rests on this: *"Say: God speaks the truth; follow the creed of Abraham, a man of pure faith; and he was not among the polytheists"* (3:95); *"Say: my Lord has guided me to a straight path, an upright religion, the creed of Abraham, a man of pure faith"* (6:161). The Prophet ﷺ's teaching placed all prophecy in that one family:

> *"I am the most worthy of Jesus son of Mary in this world and the next; the prophets are brothers on their father's side, their mothers different, and there is no prophet between us and him."* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3442; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2365)

To bring Abraham into the argument is therefore to bring the Quraysh's own best claim into the prosecution.

**Abraham's Method as a Template**

The three verses that follow unfold a curriculum, and it is worth naming the stages so that verse 16's place is seen: (1) affirm God and call to reverence (16); (2) expose the object of worship by its inability (17); (3) appeal to the record of history (18); (4) invite them to consider cosmogenesis and resurrection (19–22). The order is instructive for every form of religious argument: begin with what is highest and true rather than with the opponent's error; test the opponent's objects by the standard they themselves accept (provision, benefit, harm); then produce the historical case; then, only then, the metaphysical one. The sūrah's own later verse on argumentation with the People of the Book will prescribe the manners (29:46), and here it prescribes the sequence.

**"If You But Knew" — the Sūrah's Constant Refrain**

The formula *law kānū yaʿlamūn* / *in kuntaum taʿlamūn* recurs in this sūrah at 29:16, 29:41, 29:43 and 29:64, and its function is always the same: to attribute the failure of belief not to lack of evidence but to lack of application. Al-Rāzī comments that such phrases are "rebukes in the shape of conditional clauses," because the knowledge required is not esoteric — it is the ordinary use of sight, memory, and arithmetic. A reader who finishes verse 16 should notice how little it demands: worship the One, and treat Him as a real account against which you will be measured. The sūrah has just said that nine and a half centuries of patience is not unusual (v. 14) and that a vessel holding a few people is the only monument that survives (v. 15); verse 16 puts the same lesson in a single human voice — Abraham, at the beginning of a quarrel with the most conservative institution of his world, leading with two words and a promise of benefit.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:17

> **You only worship idols apart from God, and you only create perversion. Truly those whom you worship apart from God have no power over what provision may come to you. So seek your provision with God, worship Him, and be thankful to Him; unto Him shall you be returned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**innamā: The Particle That Subtracts Everything Else**

*Innamā taʿbudūna min dūni Allāhi awthānan wa-takhluqūna ifkā.* The limitation particle *innamā* — "nothing but," "merely" — occurs twice in a single sentence, and the double limitation is the argument. First: your worship is *nothing but* the worship of statues. Second: your whole enterprise is *nothing but* fabricating a lie. The rhetoric of *innamā* is subtractive: it leaves no residual dignity in the practice. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that Abraham is here, in effect, granting the idolaters their own vocabulary — he does not say "you worship gods that are false," he says "you worship statues, and that is the entire fact." The point is epistemological honesty as a method: define your opponent's act by what it observably is, not by what he calls it. The same discipline is required by the sūrah's own law of argument in verse 46, and it is the reason the Qur'an's polemic so rarely descends to abuse: it is content to describe.

**awthān and ifk: Two Words That Expose the Practice**

*Wathan* (plural *awthān*) is an idol that has been set up. The root *w-ṭ-n* carries the sense of fixing, settling, being established — lexicographers cite *wathan* used of water collected and motionless in a basin, and of a stone that has been planted in place. An idol is thus, etymologically, a thing that has been put somewhere and stays there. Contrast that with the God of the next verses, who "originates creation and brings it back" (29:19), who "sends down water from the sky and revives the earth after its death" (29:63), who "stretches and straitens provision" (29:62). The idols do not send rain; they sit. The verse's second charge — *takhluqūna ifkā*, "you create a lie" — is even more compressed. *Ifk* is from *a-f-k*, "to turn over, to invert": the *ifk* is a statement turned upside down, reality inverted, and the term became so attached to calumny that the episode of the Slander against the Prophet's wife ʿĀʾisha is called *al-ifk* in the Qur'an itself (24:11–20). And the verb applied to the idolaters is *khalaqa*, "to create." That is the sūrah's sharpest irony: they are called creators of unreality. Ibn Kathīr records the early gloss: "you fabricate a lie and call these statues gods," so that they *make* what does not exist — an anti-creation, the production of an object that has no warrant either in the world or in revelation. This sūrah has already used the same analysis of the polytheist's verdict in verse 4: *bi'sa mā yaḥkumūn* — evil is the judgment they make. The making and the judging belong together.

**The Acid Test: Provision**

*Fa-inna alladhīna taʿbudūna min dūni Allāhi lā yamlikūna lakum rizqan.* Now comes the criterion. The idols cannot provide sustenance; therefore they cannot be partners with the One who does. The argument from *rizq* is the Qur'an's most-used proof for tawḥīd because it is the argument every hearer can run for himself: who feeds you when the caravan is delayed, when the rain fails, when the harvest burns? Compare the sūrah's own later version, addressed to the same people: *"O mankind, remember God's blessing upon you. Is there any creator other than God who provides for you from the sky and the earth? There is no god but He; how then are you turned away?"* (35:3). And compare the sharpest single line in the Qur'an's critique of images, which this verse is the makkan precursor of:

> *"O mankind, a parable is struck, so listen to it: those you call upon besides God could not create a fly, even if they gathered together for it; and if the fly snatches a thing from them, they cannot recover it from it. Weak is the seeker and the sought."* (Qur'an 22:73)

Note the standard the fly-parable sets: the measure of a god is whether it can *do* something, not whether it can be defended. Al-Rāzī generalises the principle: any being whose existence, survival, and effect are all borrowed cannot be the source of your provision, and what cannot give life cannot give livelihood. The Qur'an's verdict on such worship is recorded elsewhere as *ʿibāda* of a precise kind: *"You worship besides God that which has no power over your provision"* — and in 16:73: *"They worship besides God that which owns no provision whatsoever, from the heavens or the earth, and they cannot."*

**Three Commands, in the Order a Person Actually Needs Them**

*Fa-btaghū ʿinda Allāhi'l-rizqa wa-ʿbudūhu wa-shkurū lahu.* The verse's conclusion is a small curriculum, and its order is exact. (1) *Seek your provision at God's door* — the practical reorientation, expressed by the verb *btaghā* (to seek with effort), which the sūrah's audience could apply that very season, since their livelihood was trade and their trade was under threat from the same men they were being told to stop trusting. Note the adverb *ʿinda* — "in the presence of, with": seeking is directed to God but not exempted from effort; the birds in the Prophet's ﷺ saying do not stay in the nest: *"Were you to trust in God with true trust in Him, He would provide for you as He provides for the birds: they set out in the morning with empty bellies and return in the evening with full ones"* (Aḥmad's Musnad; al-Tirmidhī, *Kitāb al-Zuhd* — graded ḥasan; and Ibn Mājah, *Kitāb al-Fitan*). (2) *Worship Him* — the vertical order, which the seeking does not replace. (3) *Be thankful to Him* — the disposition that converts possession into relationship; without it, provision becomes self-congratulation, which is precisely the syndrome the sūrah diagnosed in verse 4 ("do they suppose they have outstripped?"). The sūrah's closing verses will return to all three: provision stretched and straitened (29:62), the heavens and earth created (29:61), and the world's life as "diversion and play" (29:64) for those who got the order wrong.

The fourth clause — *ilayhi turjaʿūn* — supplies the sanction. The return is what makes the sequence consequential: if there is no return, gratitude is sentiment and the search for provision is mere economics. The sūrah's eschatology is thus attached to the most material of human activities, which is one of the Qur'an's most distinctive claims: the way a person earns a living is a religious act or a form of worship of something else.

**Thankfulness as an Intellectual Position**

*Shukr* in the Qur'an is not politeness; it is an account of causation. The ingrained human habit — attributing one's success to one's skill, one's network, one's market timing — is what the verse dismantles, and the sūrah's own vocabulary will make the alternative explicit in 29:20, *"Say: journey through the earth and see how He began creation"*: causation, properly traced, ends in God, and the person who traces it that far is the one who gives thanks. The classical literature distinguishes the levels of *shukr*: of the tongue, of the heart, and of the limbs — the last being what Ibn al-Qayyim calls "that you use the very blessings God gave you in what He loves," and what the ḥadīth of the two grateful habits exemplifies: *"How excellent a habit: two words light on the tongue, heavy on the scale, beloved to the Merciful — subḥān Allāhi wa-bi-ḥamdih, subḥāna'llāhi'l-ʿAẓīm."* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6406; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2694). That 29:17 ends in gratitude and 29:18 in the concession "if you deny, so did the nations before you" is not a softening: a message whose final offer is thanks cannot force belief, and the sūrah knows it.

**The Verse's Address to Every Subsequent Idolatry**

Read as a criterion rather than as anti-pagan polemic, verse 17 is a test that can be applied to any object of trust: does it have power over your provision, or none? Wealth has none — it is itself provided (29:62). A patron has none — he is fed from the same source. A state has none — *"God outspreads and straitens provision for whom He will among His servants"* (29:62). A movement, a market, a celebrity, an algorithm: all of them fail the provision test in the same way the stone did. This is what the Sufi writers mean when they say that trust in other than God is a *shirk* of practice even when the tongue's creed is intact — and the sūrah itself makes the connection in the very next verse's theme: to worship that which cannot provide is, in the language of 29:41, to house oneself in the frailest of houses. The idolater of Makkah and the anxious wage-seeker of the present age are being offered the same sentence: seek your provision where provision actually originates, and then, and only then, will thankfulness be rational rather than a formula.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:18

> **But if you deny, then communities have certainly denied before you; and naught is incumbent upon the Messenger but the clear proclamation.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Concession That Ends an Argument**

*fa-in kadhdhabū fa-qad kadhdhabat umamun min qablikum, wa-ʿalā'r-rasūli illa'l-balāghu'l-mubīn.* Verse 16 invited, verse 17 proved; verse 18 stops. The conditional is not a threat but a concession — *if you deny, then so be it* — and the reason is that the sūrah's argument has already been made on the merits. What follows is a statement about the limits of the enterprise itself, in two clauses: the historical one (denial is not evidence against the message, because every message has been denied) and the functional one (the messenger owes delivery, not reception). The pivot from "you" to "he" — from the people addressed to the messenger described — is a deliberate shift of attention: the verse is written to console the one who bears the message as much as to warn the ones who refuse it, which is precisely why the sūrah, in its Makkan context, needed it.

**"Communities Have Denied Before You": The Consolation Genre**

The Qur'an's most repeated pastoral device is this exact sentence in other words. Two passages put it beside ours:

> *"If they deny you, so did the nations before them deny their messengers; and to God belong all affairs."* (Qur'an 35:4; cf. 22:42, 41:13, 6:34)

and the verse that gives the principle its full formulation:

> *"So perhaps you will destroy yourself with grief over their deeds, if they do not believe in this discourse. Surely We have made all that is on earth an adornment for it, that We may try them as to which of them is best in deed."* (Qur'an 18:6–7)

Verse 18 is the Makkan sūrah's own version of that remedy, and note how the sūrah's argument has been preparing it: Noah preached for nine hundred and fifty years (29:14) and was refused; Lot was menaced in his own house (29:29); Shuʿayb was told he would be expelled (7:88). The "denial before you" is therefore not a platitude but a completed induction — the sūrah has produced the evidence, and verse 18 simply states the conclusion so that the messenger's heart may settle. Al-Rāzī's observation is theologically careful: the clause also denies the disbelievers their inference that their strength proves their right. If success were the criterion of truth, no prophet would have died unsucceeded in his own lifetime; the criterion is the *clear proclamation*, and that criterion is one God can verify.

**"Naught Is Incumbent but the Clear Proclamation"**

The phrase *al-balāgh al-mubīn* is a technical term of the Qur'an for the limit and the completeness of the messenger's task. It recurs with the same formula in the sūrah's own neighbourhood and throughout the Book: *"If you turn away, know that upon Our messenger is only a clear proclamation"* (5:92); *"your duty is only to convey; upon Us is the reckoning"* (13:40); *"he who obeys the Messenger obeys God; and whoever turns away — We have not sent you to be a guardian over them"* (4:80); *"So remind — you are only a reminder; you are not a controller over them"* (88:21–22). The verb *ballagha* means to reach a destination; a *balāgh* is what has arrived intact, and the sūrah's adjective *mubīn* — "clear, making things clear" — completes the definition: the message must be *delivered* and it must be *understood as offered*. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the verse is therefore also a verdict on the manner of proclamation: unclear preaching is a failure of the messenger's one duty, since the duty is *clear* conveyance. The classical ethic of daʿwa follows: total responsibility for clarity, honesty, and effort; zero responsibility for outcomes; and no use of coercion, since coercion is not proclamation.

**Two Guidance: The Distinction That the Verse Preserves**

The theology here is precise, and the sūrah itself supplies the key. The Qur'an distinguishes guidance as *showing the way* (hidāyat al-dalāla) from guidance as *enabling the walk* (hidāyat al-tawfīq), and it says so in the plainest terms about Abraham's own uncle, at the height of the Prophet's ﷺ personal grief:

> *"You do not guide whom you love, but God guides whom He wills, and He knows best those who are guided."* (Qur'an 28:56)

And the ḥadīth of Khaybar makes the same division a rule of effort rather than of resignation. Al-Bukhārī (3009) and Muslim (2406) transmit from Sahl ibn Saʿd:

> *"By God, for God to guide one man through you is better for you than red camels [the most prized of possessions]."*

The verse's structure — do your duty, leave the result — is thus not indifference: the messenger is told to care about the *one* variable he controls. The Makkan community, watching its neighbours harden, is here given the definition of success that would otherwise be unavailable to it. The sūrah restates the same standard twice more, in the middle and at the end: the believer is told to attend to the two things actually within his power — *"Recite what has been revealed to you of the Book, and establish the prayer"* (29:45) — and the whole chapter closes on the promise that effort, not outcome, is what God rewards: *"But as for those who strive for Us, We shall surely guide them in Our ways"* (29:69).

**Two Guidance: The Distinction That the Verse Preserves**

The theology here is precise, and the sūrah itself supplies the key. The Qur'an distinguishes guidance as *showing the way* (hidāyat al-dalāla) from guidance as *enabling the walk* (hidāyat al-tawfīq), and it says so in the plainest terms about Abraham's own uncle, at the height of the Prophet's ﷺ personal grief:

> *"You do not guide whom you love, but God guides whom He wills, and He knows best those who are guided."* (Qur'an 28:56)

And the ḥadīth of Khaybar makes the same division a rule of effort rather than of resignation. Al-Bukhārī (3009) and Muslim (2406) transmit from Sahl ibn Saʿd:

> *"By God, for God to guide one man through you is better for you than red camels [the most prized of possessions]."*

The verse's structure — do your duty, leave the result — is thus not indifference: the messenger is told to care about the *one* variable he controls. The Makkan community, watching its neighbours harden, is here given the definition of success that would otherwise be unavailable to it: the sūrah restates the same standard twice more, in its middle and at its end — the believer is told to attend to what is actually within his power, *"Recite what has been revealed to you of the Book, and establish the prayer"* (29:45) — and the chapter closes on the promise that effort, not outcome, is what God rewards: *"But as for those who strive for Us, We shall surely guide them in Our ways"* (29:69).

**Why "If You Deny" Is Not the Sword-Verse's Opposite**

The verse is often read as one of the "forgive and turn away" verses, and its history in the exegetical literature includes the question whether such Makkan patience was abrogated by the later legislation of fighting. Al-Ṭabarī's principle (which he applies explicitly to this sūrah's verse 46 and to the turn-away verses generally) is that abrogation requires definitive proof and that the two are not in contradiction, since they address different situations: 29:18 legislates the *attitude of the preacher* — no compulsion, no despair, no self-destruction over refusal — while the Madinan verses legislate the *conduct of a polity* under attack. Ibn Kathīr states the same distinction with his usual simplicity: upon the messenger is proclamation, and upon the ruler is judgement; the one does not release the other, and neither permits revenge in the preacher's own person. This reading matters today because the two errors it excludes are both live: the error of forcing belief, and the error of abandoning clarity on the grounds that results are not ours to command.

**The Verse as a Cure for Outcome-Anxiety**

Any vocation in which a person's effort is not reliably converted into visible success — teaching, preaching, medicine in its long cases, parenting, reform, translation of a difficult book — is vulnerable to the despair verse 18 forbids, and to its twin, the vanity that mistakes an audience for a verdict. The verse's two clauses supply the correct account: the *before-you* clause relativises every rejection by giving it a history; and the *only-proclamation* clause relieves the worker of a burden he was never assigned. What remains is a standard of workmanship, not of harvest: was it clear, was it honest, was it complete? The sūrah that opened with "you will certainly be tried" (29:2) and ended this section by naming the striver's profit as himself (29:6) has now closed the loop: the trial of the preacher is not whether people believe, but whether he proclaimed — and the "clear" of *al-balāgh al-mubīn* is the whole of the examination paper.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:19

> **Have they not considered how God originates creation, then brings it back? Truly that is easy for God.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Argument Inserted into the Middle of a Story**

Most of the commentators — al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī, Ibn Kathīr among them — treat verses 19–23 as an interruption in the Abraham-narrative, resumed at verse 24; others read them as a continuation of Abraham's own speech, so that the prophet himself is the one who says "have they not considered." Whichever is preferred, the sūrah's logic is the same: it has just quoted Abraham's argument from provision (29:17) and his concession (29:18), and now it turns to the audience directly, because the argument from origins does not require Abraham's presence. This is a characteristic Qur'anic technique — the narrative paused so that the reader may be addressed — and its effect here is to make the readers of the sūrah the second party in the dialogue the Makkan audience refused to join.

**a-lam yaraw: The Verb of Seeing That Means Understanding**

*alam yaraw kayfa yubdi'u Allāhu'l-khalqa thumma yuʿīduhu.* The verb *raʾā* in the Qur'an regularly governs not the eye but the insight: *"have they not seen the birds above them, spreading and folding their wings? None holds them but the Merciful"* (67:19); *"Do they not look at the sky above them, how We built it and adorned it, and that it has no flaw? And the earth — We spread it and set therein firm mountains, and grew therein every beautiful kind — a sight and a reminder for every penitent servant"* (50:6–8). The negative question *a-lam yaraw* — "have they not seen?" — presupposes that the seeing has been available the whole time. Al-Rāzī comments that the particle combination "have they not" is used when the evidence is close and only attention is lacking; when the evidence is far, the Qur'an uses "do they not reflect" or "do they not reason." This is one of the sūrah's deepest claims about the human condition: unbelief in this Book is most often not a shortage of data but a shortage of looking.

**yubdīʾ … yuʿīd: The Two Verbs of the Whole Argument**

The root *b-d-ʾ* gives *ibdāʾ*, "to begin, to bring into being without precedent," and its participle *badīʿ*, from which the Qur'an describes God as *badīʿu's-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* — "the Originator of the heavens and the earth" (2:117; 6:101). The second verb, *īʿāda*, "to return, to do again," completes the pair: not a second, different creation but the re-doing of an act already demonstrated. The argument is an a fortiori of the strongest type: if the first production happened without model, without material at hand, and without any prior claim on the doer, then its repetition is easier by every measure, and a thing whose first instance is established cannot be dismissed because its second seems improbable. The Qur'an makes this exact inference in other words, and always in reply to the same objection:

> *"He presents to Us a parable and has forgotten his own creation. He says: 'Who will give life to bones while they are decayed?' Say: He will give them life who produced them the first time; and He is, of all creation, Knowing."* (Qur'an 36:78–79)

> *"It is He who produces creation, then returns it, and it is most easy for Him."* (Qur'an 30:27)

> *"Are We fatigued by the first creation? Nay, they are in confusion about a new creation."* (Qur'an 50:15)

> *"Say: shall I take besides Him any protector, Originator of the heavens and the earth, who feeds and is not fed?"* (Qur'an 6:14)

The Qur'an treats origination and return as a single divine act — *huwa yubdi'u wa-yu'īd*, "He it is who originates creation and repeats it" (85:13; cf. 30:11; 10:34) — one verb of beginning and one of return, so that the second is not a new problem but the same power exercised again. The sūrah's *thumma* ("then") signals order, not effort.

**"Easy for God": A Phrase That Set Off a Debate**

*inna dhalika ʿalā Allāhi yasīr.* The word *yasīr* — "easy, slight" — raised a question in kalām, because "easy" and "difficult" are ordinarily predicates of an agent who exerts effort, and no effort can be conceived in the Omnipotent. Al-Zamakhsharī, reading with the Muʿtazilī instinct for divine transcendence, glosses: "nothing strains Him, so what He wills is not laborious; the negation of difficulty is the negation of incapacity, not the confession of degrees." His opponents — al-Rāzī in his own commentary, and after him Ibn Taymiyyah, who attacks *al-Kashshāf*'s phrasings in several places — replied that the phrase is not a statement about degrees of exertion in God but about the matter itself: what you find impossible, He finds nothing at all; the word *yasīr* is directed at the listener's estimation, not at the agent's labour. Ibn Taymiyyah's general rule resolves the case: the Qur'an describes matters relative to the one for whom they are hard — *"and indeed it is great, except for those whom God has humbled"* (2:45: *wa-innahā lakabīratun illā ʿalā'l-khāshiʿīn*) — while affirming that "nothing is difficult for You" in the proper address to God. The verse thus does double duty: it removes the objection of implausibility ("how can the dead rise?") and simultaneously refuses to imagine a God who labours. Compare 14:20: *"Do they not know that God, who created the heavens and the earth, is able to replace them with their like? And He has appointed an end, about which there is no doubt"* — and 19:9, of the impossible birth of Yaḥyā: *"thus; your Lord says it is easy for Me, and I created you before when you were nothing."* The formula's function in every case is the same: the precedent of *creation from nothing* outflanks the difficulty of *re-creation*.

**Why the Sūrah Places This Here**

The Makkan audience's demand was for a sign (29:50); their derision was about resurrection (as 36:78 records in the voice of the man who crushed an old bone and blew on it). Verse 19 answers both at once by relocating the burden of proof: the sign already being exhibited — the ongoing, empirically checkable fact that things begin — is precisely the sign that would be needed for their return. This is also why verses 19–23 are inserted before the story resumes: the sūrah is about to narrate the fire that refused to burn Abraham (29:24), and a hearer who has accepted that *originating and returning* is easy will not find a cool fire remarkable. The sequence makes the miracle a corollary rather than an exception: one who believes that He begins creation and repeats it will accept without argument that He can command the fire: *"O fire, be coolness and peace upon Abraham"* (21:69).

**The Discipline of Considering, and Its Modern Form**

Two applications follow from the verse's method rather than its conclusions. First, the verse institutes *observation of the ordinary* as a religious exercise. Nothing in it points to a spectacular event; it points at birth, growth, decay, seed, season — the data any illiterate Makkan possessed. In a modern register, the same discipline underwrites the entire scientific enterprise as a form of worship: to look closely at what God made is obedience to a command to "see," which is why the tradition's natural philosophers did not have to choose between investigating the world and revering its maker. Ibn al-Haytham's insistence on experiment and al-Bīrūnī's comparative study of religions both belong to a culture that read verses like this as instructions. Second, the verse supplies an antidote to the specific modern form of despair about human possibility: the sense that what has collapsed cannot be rebuilt. Character damaged, communities broken, lives spent in the wrong direction — the sūrah's argument is addressed to those who find return implausible: the God who originates does not find re-origination difficult. Its promise is a metaphysical claim before it is a psychological comfort, but the comfort follows, and it is why the sūrah that began with trial can end with guidance (29:69).

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:20

> **Say, “Journey upon the earth and observe how He originated creation. Then God shall bring the next genesis into being. Truly God is Powerful over all things.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command to Travel, Issued in the Desert**

*qul sīrū fī al-arḍi fa-nẓurū kayfa badʾa'l-khalq.* The verb *sīrā* is an imperative of physical movement, and the sūrah is addressing people whose entire economic life was movement: Quraysh lived by the two annual journeys, the winter caravan to Yemen and the summer one to Syria — an event the sūrah's neighbours in the canon make into the proof of God's favour: *"for the accustomed security of Quraysh, their accustomed security in the winter journey and the summer journey"* (106:1–2). What the verse does is to put the trade road at the service of theology. On that road a traveller passed the settlements of the Thamūd with their carved façades, the ruins of the ʿĀd in the sand of al-Aḥqāf, the Midianite stations, and the Petraean and Nabatean works of the southern Jordan — the very places the Qur'an names elsewhere: *"And the people of al-Ḥijr denied the messengers… and they hewed of the mountains houses to live in"* (15:80, 82). The command to "journey and see" is therefore not a romantic invitation but a specific instruction to read the landscape of the Arabian peninsula as an open archive.

**How "Observe" Functions: The Evidence of Two Kinds**

The sūrah's travel-commands divide, as its own vocabulary shows, into two investigations. One is cosmological: how creation was *begun*. The other is historical: how denial *ended*. The first is here; the second is in the closely parallel verses:

> *"Say: journey through the earth and then see how the end of the deniers was."* (Qur'an 27:69; cf. 6:11, 30:9, 35:44, 40:21)

> *"Many were the ways of life that passed before you; so journey through the earth and see how the end of the deniers was."* (Qur'an 3:137; cf. 16:36)

The pairing is exact and intentional: a traveller who examines origins and ends is examining the same God's work in two registers — the making and the judging. This is why verse 20 ends with omnipotence ("Powerful over all things") while verse 19 ended with ease ("that is easy for God"): the first closes the argument about resurrection, the second about punishment. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the sūrah has placed the two commands back to back so that no reader mistakes God's power for mere power or His ease for mere facility: He is both able to raise the dead and able to end civilisations.

**badʾa'l-khalq: The Empirical Ground**

The phrase *kayfa badʾa'l-khalq* — "how He began creation" — asks for the *how*, not merely the *that*. The Arabic *kayfa* invites description: in what order, by what stages, with what regularity. And the Qur'an, everywhere it makes this demand, supplies the data to look at: the human embryo in its stages (23:12–16), the seed split open by God (6:95), the camels and how they were created (88:17), the heavens in layers (67:3), the alternation of night and day (3:190), and the resurrection of dead land by rain (29:63). The classical exegetes accordingly read verse 20 as a permission — even an obligation — to study. The jurists' books on knowledge devote chapters to the permissibility — sometimes the communal obligation — of learning languages, reckoning and medicine, and several of them quote the maxim that "wisdom is the lost property of the believer" (transmitted in al-Tirmidhī's *Sunan*, *ʿIlm*; its chain is weak, its practice broad). The adab and philosophical literature likewise took travel for observation as a recognised educational duty: al-Bīrūnī composed his comparative study of India on the explicit principle that knowledge requires going, and Ibn Khaldūn's *Muqaddima* treats the observation of peoples and their rise and fall as the raw material of the science he was founding. The verse's grammar supports that tradition: the two imperatives joined by *fa-* ("journey… and observe") bind movement and reflection in one act, so that travel without observation is incomplete and observation without travel has no object.

**al-nasheʾa al-ukhrā: Resurrection Named as a Second Growing**

*thumma sayunshī'u'n-nasheʾata'l-ukhrā.* The word *nashʾa* is from the root *n-sh-ʾ*, used of the rising of a star, the springing up of plants from the ground, and the growth of a child into maturity; *nasheʾa* is thus "a producing, a coming-into-being," and the sūrah's phrase "the next genesis" (or "the later becoming," as some render) describes resurrection as *another growth*, not as a repair of the old. The advantage of the term over *baʿth* ("sending forth") is that it carries the imagery of germination: as seed becomes plant, so dust becomes body. The Qur'an uses the pairing repeatedly: *"God sends the winds, so they raise a cloud… and sends down water… then with it He gives life to the earth after its death"* (30:48; cf. 29:63), and *"as He produced you from your mothers' bellies knowing nothing, God made for you hearing, sight and hearts"* (16:78). The definite article on "the next genesis" — *the* other one, the one already determined — makes the future event more certain than the past one, because the past one is evidence for it. Al-Rāzī's summary of the verse: the first creation is a demonstrated fact, the second is its sequel; whoever denies the sequel while accepting the fact has contradicted himself, not the prophet.

**"Powerful over All Things" — and the Question of Divine Ability**

*inna Allāha ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr.* The formula, which closes dozens of passages, is the Qur'an's standard answer to every objection of the form "this is impossible." Its scope, *kulli shayʾ*, is the site of a classical debate with two opposite errors to avoid. On one side, the philosophers who limited divine power to what is intrinsically possible and denied that God can do the logically contradictory — a position the kalām authors largely accepted, and which al-Juwaynī and al-Ghazālī defended by defining omnipotence as power over the *possible* (mumkin) rather than over "anything whatever," since "God" and "no God" is not a thing at all but a verbal confusion. On the other side, the literalists who demanded that God be said to do whatever is imaginable, including the impossible; the mainstream refused this too, on the ground that impossibility is not a hidden power but an unintelligible phrase. Ibn Taymiyyah's treatment is the subtlest: God's power is complete and nothing weakens Him, yet "the impossible" is not an object of power, and He does as He wills while never willing what He has promised not to do — His wisdom and His veracity being themselves aspects of His act, not constraints from outside. Read with that precision, the sūrah's clause means what it says: the resurrection of bodies is within the range of a power that is limited by nothing except the unintelligible.

**Two Practical Lessons for the Reader**

First, the verse consecrates curiosity. A believer who reads geology, population history, embryology, or the fate of collapsed societies is not conducting a neutral activity later made pious by interpretation; the activity is itself the obedience commanded. This has a real consequence for how religious communities treat intellectual labour — it cannot be confined to the devotional sciences without disobeying a verse that orders *sīrā*, movement into the world. Second, the verse disciplines presumption. Travel and observation were also the makkan elite's own experience; they had seen the ruins and heard the accounts and drawn no conclusion. What separates their seeing from the seeing the verse commands is not more information but the intention of the gaze: one looks to enjoy the scenery of the past, the other looks to be instructed by it. The sūrah, which began with the trial of the believer and the impunity-in-appearance of the denier, now makes the road the place where both are corrected: the striver sees that the One who begins does not lose interest, and the persecutor sees that the strong peoples who came before were not "outstripping" anything (29:4), they were merely being respite.

**The Verse in Its Sūrah**

Read structurally, verse 20 is the sūrah's hinge between the arguments and the narratives. It is the last of the passages that reason about God's power (19–23); after it, the sūrah resumes the Abraham-story with the fire (24) and then runs the catalogue of peoples and their destructions (28–40), which is verse 20's second command — "see how the end of the deniers was" — executed at length. The reader has been told to travel; the rest of the sūrah is the itinerary.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:21

> **He punishes whomsoever He will and has mercy upon whomsoever He will, and unto Him will you be returned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sovereignty Stated Without Apology**

*yuʿadhdhibu man yashāʾu wa-yarḥamu man yashāʾ, wa-ilayhi tuqlabūn.* Three clauses, in a sequence the sūrah has chosen carefully: punishment first, mercy second, return last. The verse comes immediately after the command to journey and observe God's beginning of creation (29:20) and just before the sentence that removes all escape (29:22); its place is between the demonstration of power and the demonstration of inescapability, and its function is to say that the power displayed in cosmogenesis is *governed*, not merely vast. A universe that is omnipotently sustained but morally unruled would be a cosmos of force; the verse denies that, in the same breath as it denies that anything rules over God. He "punishes whom He wills and shows mercy to whom He wills": the will is unrestricted by any claimant, and its two operations are both real.

**No One Can Present a Claim on God**

The first theological consequence the exegetes extract is the negation of *ījāb* — the idea that something becomes binding upon God. Al-Ashʿarī and his school made this class of verse — *"He does as He wills"* (11:107), *"you will not will except that God wills"* (76:30; 81:29) — the charter of their position. Against them the Muʿtazila held that God must do the best (*al-ṣalāḥ wa'l-aṣlaḥ*) for His creatures, and must reward the obedient and not torment them unjustly, since otherwise He would be acting in vain. The Ashʿariyya answered with precisely this verse: nothing is obligatory upon God; what He does is just *because* He does it, since there is no prior standard to which even He must conform. Al-Rāzī, who transmits the argument in *al-Maṭālib al-ʿāliya* as well as in his *Tafsīr*, is candid about the difficulty: a pure voluntarism makes "justice" an equivocal word, since God's acts would be called just merely by being His. Ibn Taymiyyah's answer, which became the most influential in later Sunni theology, is that the verse denies *obligation imposed from outside* while the rest of revelation affirms qualities *within* God's own nature that make injustice impossible: He has written mercy upon Himself (6:54, 39:10), He is not pleased for His servants with disbelief (39:7), He would never wrong a grain-seed's weight (4:40), and He has *promised* — a promise is a self-binding that is not a compulsion. Thus "He punishes whom He wills and has mercy upon whom He wills" is read by the whole tradition not as divine arbitrariness but as divine freedom: no human being is owed Paradise by creation, and no human being is shut out of it by necessity.

**The Mercy Clause and the Sūrah's Own Arithmetic**

The order of the two clauses, punishment then mercy, is the more troubling; and the tradition's response is instructive. Al-Bukhārī (7404) and Muslim (2751) transmit:

> *"When God completed creation, He wrote in a book that rests with Him above the Throne: 'My mercy precedes — or: overpowers — My wrath.'"*

So the sūrah's first-listed item is the *rarer* one, while the second is the one that "precedes." The same pair reappears in the sūrah's own narrative: the flood seizes Noah's people (29:14) and the companions of the ship are saved (29:15); Abraham's people say "burn him!" and God saves him (29:24); Lot's town is destroyed and his family is extracted (29:32–34). In every case the destruction and the deliverance are both acts of a single will, and in every case the saved group is small and the destroyed group large. Yet the sūrah's final word is not "He punishes whom He wills" but "God is with the beautiful-doers" (29:69). This is the sūrah's own exegesis of verse 21: the will is free, and its disclosed direction is mercy.

**"Unto Him Will You Be Returned": The Sentence That Prevents Fatalism**

The third clause, *wa-ilayhi tuqlabūn* — "and to Him you are turned back," from *q-l-b*, the root of turning over, of things flipped from one state into another — does more than repeat the return-theme of 29:8 and 29:17. Because the verse has just said that reward and punishment depend on the divine will alone, the hearer might draw the inference the Jabriyya drew: if all is will and nothing is desert, effort is pointless. The sūrah closes that door in the same sentence by making the return the frame of an examination: you will be *turned back* to the One who will judge, and 29:6 has already fixed the accounting: whoever strives, strives for himself; and 29:40 will spell out the verdict of the whole catalogue: "God wronged them not, but themselves did they wrong." Al-Qurṭubī records the interpretive principle that keeps the tension: the verses of God's unrestricted will and the verses of human responsibility are both to be held, and a verse of one class may not be used to abolish a verse of the other. Ibn Kathīr glosses our verse simply — He forgives whom He wills, and punishes whom He wills; to Him is the return, so do not despair of His mercy and do not be secure from His punishment.

**The Two Pastoral Extremes the Verse Treats at Once**

The verse addresses two opposite spiritual illnesses with one medicine. The first is presumption: the sinner who reasons that because God is sovereign and merciful, punishment will not touch him — the logic the sūrah has already called "evil is the judgment they make" (29:4). "He punishes whom He wills" removes the sinner's ability to manage God. The second is despair: the penitent who believes their record has disqualified them permanently — "He shows mercy to whom He wills" removes the accuser's ability to close the case. The Qur'an's most quoted sentence on that second illness is the sūrah's own near-homonym in al-Zumar:

> *"Say: O My servants who have transgressed against their own souls, do not despair of God's mercy; surely God forgives sins all together. It is He who is the Forgiving, the Merciful."* (Qur'an 39:53)

and the ḥadīth of the man who killed ninety-nine souls, in which a scholar told him there was no repentance for him, and the Prophet ﷺ said that he had killed a second soul by closing a door God had opened (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2766; cf. al-Bukhārī 3470). The verse's divine freedom is therefore not a threat to the sinner's hope; it is the *ground* of it. If mercy were only a debt, it would be administered by accountants; because it is a will, it can surprise.

**Why the Verse Sits in a Passage About Persecution**

Finally, the sūrah's Makkan audience heard verse 21 with a specific terror in mind: that the powerful men who were torturing them were beyond reach. The verse's declaration that God's punishing will is not negotiable is the answer to verse 4's presumption and to verse 12's offer of borrowed protection, and it is also the answer to the persecuted believers' own faint-hearted fear (29:10) that the punishment of men is equal to the punishment of God. The two clauses re-scale the whole social order: the torturer is within the reach of "He punishes whom He wills," and the victim is within the reach of "He shows mercy to whom He wills." And because the return is certain, the re-scaling is not a wish. This is what makes verse 21, read in context, not an abstract decree of predestination but the sūrah's boldest act of justice — a community of slaves, clients and merchants being told that the only will that finally decides who is punished and who is embraced belongs to the One who is neither.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:22

> **You thwart not on earth, nor in Heaven; and apart from God you have no protector or helper.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**There Is No Elsewhere**

*wa-mā antum bi-muʿjizīna fī al-arḍi wa-lā fī's-samāʾ, wa-mā lakum min dūni Allāhi min waliyyin wa-lā naṣīr.* The verse begins by closing space. The verb is *iʿjāz*, from *ʿajz* — incapacity, being rendered powerless; to "be muʿjiz" is to put someone out of the running, to frustrate a pursuer by outstripping him, and the sūrah has used the same idea in verse 4 (*an yasbiqu*) and will use it again of Pharaoh and his company (29:39: "and outstripped [Us] not"). Here the denial is comprehensive and includes every direction: no place exists at which the divine pursuit terminates. Al-Ṭabarī records the two readings of the second limb: either "nor can the inhabitants of the heavens frustrate Him," or "even if you were in the heavens you could not escape" — and the second, he prefers, because it makes the point against the person who imagines altitude, remoteness, or concealment as safety. The Qur'an's own gloss on the same thought is unforgettable:

> *"O assembly of jinn and men, if you are able to pass through the regions of the heavens and the earth, then pass through — you shall not pass through except by a sultan [an authority]."* (Qur'an 55:33)

And: *"Wherever you may be, death will overtake you, though you be in towers raised and fortified"* (4:78); and *"He is with you wherever you are"* (57:4). The same clause runs through the Book in almost identical words: *"and there is for them no protector or helper beside God"* (2:107, of the disbelievers; cf. 4:148, 9:116); *"then they will find for themselves no protector or helper beside God"* (46:32); *"they will not escape"* (72:12); and *"know that you cannot frustrate [God], and that He is with you wherever you are"* — cf. 2:255, 57:4.

**walī and naṣīr: The Two Kinds of Support a Human Being Can Buy**

The verse's second half denies both categories of ally, and Arabic keeps them distinct. A *walī* is a near one — the root *w-l-y* gives *wilāya*, proximity, guardianship, patronage, and the client-patron bond that structured pre-Islamic Arabia (*ḥilf*, *mawālā*); the *walī* is the person who is close enough to defend you in advance, the one whose name is on your contract and whose clan stands behind you. A *naṣīr* is a helper from outside — *n-ṣ-r* gives *nusra*, aid, vindication, the ally who arrives when the fight is already joined. To have neither is to have no structural protection and no rescue: pre-emptive defence and emergency relief, both void. That is the exact condition of the Makkan believers the sūrah is addressing, because they had, in worldly terms, been stripped of precisely these two things: their clans' protection by apostasy, and external rescue because they were poor and few. The verse therefore is not addressed only to the persecutors; it is a description of the whole human situation, and the Qur'an's point is that the persecutor's situation is the same as the victim's in kind, differing only in the temporary illusion of leverage.

**The Spider's House in Advance**

Verse 22 is the thesis of the sūrah stated as negation, and its positive image arrives in 29:41: whoever takes a *walī* other than God takes a house of spider-silk. Between those two verses the sūrah will run the entire parade of failed protection: Korah relying on his wealth and being swallowed (29:40, cf. 28:81 "he relied on his clan"); Pharaoh needing Haman to bake him a tower so he could ascend to the heavens and see Moses' God (28:38) — a mythic literalisation of the very phrase "nor in Heaven" — and Lot's people unable to help the guests in his house (29:33 "he was constrained from helping them"). Even prophets cannot be *awliyāʾ* who override the divine will: Abraham's plea for Lot's town is answered with "We know better who is in it" (29:32). This is the sūrah's systematic demolition of the notion that closeness, status, or intercession can function as a *ḥiṣn*, a fastness: the verse's grammar is the argument, and the narratives are the exhibits.

**"He Does Not Need Anyone": The Theology of the Negative Sentence**

Read with what precedes it, verse 22 completes a triad: God needs nothing from the worlds (29:6), He does whatever He wills (29:21), and nothing escapes Him (29:22). Together these define a conception of divinity that the sūrah regards as the minimum content of belief, and it is worth noting that each clause denies a specific human projection. "God is beyond need" denies that He can be flattered; "He punishes and shows mercy whom He wills" denies that He can be managed by rites or claims; "you cannot thwart Him" denies that He can be outrun. Ibn Taymiyyah repeatedly uses this triad against two errors of his age: the saint-cults that treat proximity to a holy person as a legal immunity, and the philosophical theology that makes God's acts necessary consequences of His essence rather than acts of will and mercy. For him the verse means: no creature can be made a jurisdiction, and no divine attribute can be turned into a compulsion.

**The Psychological Layer: Flight as a Response to Guilt**

The instinct the verse denies — that distance will solve what conscience has raised — is among the most reliably documented in human behaviour. The fugitive is a figure in every literature, and the pattern in ordinary life is subtler: the person who changes cities to leave a promise unkept, who accepts the job far away, who builds a wall of new circumstances behind which the old account is assumed to be void. The verse's answer is not moral panic but a rational correction: if the account is with God, relocation has no effect; if it is with God, then "wherever you may be, death will overtake you" (4:78). The practical liberation in the same sentence is equally real — and it is what the persecuted believers needed most: the One from whom you cannot flee is the One to whom you can flee, and the sūrah will command exactly that (29:26 "I am fleeing to my Lord"; 29:56 "My earth is vast"). Exile from God is impossible; exile *to* God is a vocation. The whole rhetoric of the verse turns on that single inversion, which is why it is placed before the Abraham-fire narrative: a man who cannot escape God can be thrown into a fire and be safe.

**A Social Lesson in the Denial of "Protector or Helper"**

Taken as a maxim for communities, the verse forbids the two things that corrupt religious and political life: patronage and saviour-worship. A group whose members seek security through *walāya* — the closeness of a chief, a family name, an office — has replaced the covenant with a clientele; and a group whose members wait for a *naṣīr* from outside, a deliverer who will arrive to vindicate them, has replaced effort with dependency. Against the first, the sūrah insists that no clan-tie survives the Day when "friends become enemies, except the reverent" (43:67), and it will make Abraham disown even his father (9:114); against the second, it insists that help comes from God through the striving of the striver (29:6, 29:69), and that God's term has an appointed date that no rescue accelerates (29:53). The Makkan believers, being asked to leave their protectors and to trust a distant, unseen helper, were told in one sentence which of the two they were really losing: neither. And this is why the sūrah's consolation is not "you will find better patrons," but "you never had any, and you never needed any."

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:23

> **Those who disbelieve in God’s signs and in the meeting with Him, they despair of My Mercy, and for them there shall be a painful punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Denials That Close the Door**

*alladhīna yaẓunūna annahum mulāqū Allāhi* is the phrase of verse 5's hope; here the sūrah states its negative: *ulāʾika alladhīna kafarū bi-āyāti rabbihim wa-bi-likāʾihī* — they deny/reject His *āyāt* and His *liqāʾ*. The pairing is exact and not redundant. God's signs are the evidence laid out in the world, in the self, and in the Book — this sūrah has been naming them since verse 14 and will name them again in 29:35 and 29:44; the meeting is the accountability that makes the evidence matter. To deny the signs is to say that there is nothing to learn; to deny the meeting is to say that there is nothing to answer for. The sūrah's diagnosis of unbelief is thus a double loss of contact: with revelation and with consequence. And it is why the verse's conclusion is despair rather than merely punishment: a person who has removed both God's speech and God's judgement has removed the two channels through which mercy reaches a creature.

**ūʾisa min raḥmatī: Is Despair Inflicted or Elected?**

The verb is the passive of *yaʾisa*, "to despair" — *ūʾisa*, "they have been made to despair" — and the grammar of the clause was discussed by the exegetes precisely because the passive invites the reading "God has cut off their hope." Al-Ṭabarī's report favours the sense in which the despair belongs to them: they are the ones who have no hope of attaining mercy, and God's act is not a refusal but a confirmation of the situation they have produced. Al-Zamakhsharī reads it the same way in effect: "they are desperate, because they have no means of hope," the passive conveying that their desperation is *established* against them, not that a capricious sentence was passed. Compare the Qur'an's idiom, in which the sinner "buys" guidance for error (2:16) and "God seals the heart of every arrogant tyrant" (40:35) — the sealing is real, and it is the shape the heart has chosen. Al-Rāzī's summary is worth transmitting: the verse is a statement of fact about the state of the one who rejects signs and the meeting, not the arbitrary withdrawal of an offer from someone who accepted it.

**The iltifāt: A Change of Person Inside the Sentence**

The sūrah has been speaking *about* God in the third person — "God knows," "He punishes whom He wills," "unto Him you are turned" (29:11, 21, 22) — and here, in the middle of the verse, the voice shifts to the first person: *My Mercy*. Arab rhetoricians call this *iltifāt*, the turn from one person to another within a passage, and they regard it as a device that heightens immediacy and emotional charge. Al-Zamakhsharī, whose *Kashshāf* is the locus classicus for detecting such turns, treats it here as a signal: what is being denied is not "mercy in general" but *My* mercy — mercy in its personal, addressable form, the mercy a creature could have appealed to by name. The shift also makes the sentence read as a divine declaration of the verdict rather than a report about it, and the Makkan hearer, accustomed to the third-person formulas that open this part of the sūrah, would have felt the abruptness of the first person at precisely the point where hope is being closed.

**How the Qur'an Itself Links Mercy to Belief in the Signs**

The most illuminating parallel is in al-Aʿrāf, where God's mercy is expressly *written* for exactly the class of people this verse excludes:

> *"…and My mercy encompasses everything; I shall write it for those who guard against evil, who pay the zakāt, and who believe in Our signs — those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered prophet they find described in the Torah and the Gospel."* (Qur'an 7:156–157)

The conditional is not arbitrary favour but a disclosed order: the mercy that "encompasses everything" in its extent is *written* in its application for those who believe in the signs — which is the precise complement of 29:23. Read together, the two passages answer the two opposite errors of the moral life: presumption, which says mercy is owed to everyone regardless, and despair, which says it is owed to no sinner. The Qur'an says: mercy is universal in capacity, directed by response, and closed to no one who turns. That is also why the sūrah immediately before this verse was able to say "He shows mercy to whom He wills" (29:21) without cancelling the promise; the will that is free is not capricious, because it has declared its own character.

**The Sign of the Reversed Expectation: Two ḥadīth**

The Prophet ﷺ made the believer's expectation of mercy itself a matter of divine response. In al-Bukhārī (7405) and Muslim (2675) he reports his Lord as saying:

> *"I am as My servant thinks of Me, and I am with him when he mentions Me; if he mentions Me in himself, I mention him in Myself, and if he mentions Me in a gathering, I mention him in a better gathering."*

Despair, in that light, is not merely a feeling but a theological error with consequences — a false claim about God which God, as it were, honours. And the same Prophet ﷺ used the image most calculated to break despair permanently. Al-Bukhārī (5999) and Muslim (2253) transmit from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb that captives were brought, a woman was searching frantically, found a child, clasped it to her breast and nursed it; and he asked the people:

> *"Do you think this woman would throw her child into the fire?"* They said, "No, by God, not while she is able to prevent it." He said, *"This woman is more tender than that; and God is more tender to His servants than this mother to her child."*

That is the answer to every reader who has heard 29:23 and applied it to himself rather than to his persecutors. The verse's verdict is not "God will not forgive" but "those who refuse both evidence and accountability have left themselves no route to forgiveness" — and the route, which is the acknowledgment of the signs and the meeting, is open at any hour before death: *"on the day some faces will be white and some faces will be black"* (3:106) is a description of the last state, not a prohibition of the penultimate one.

**The Verse as the Mirror of Verse 5**

Place 29:23 beside 29:5 and the sūrah's architecture becomes visible. There: *"Whosoever hopes for the meeting with God — God's term is coming"*; here: *"Those who disbelieve in the meeting — they despair of My mercy."* The same object — *liqāʾ Allāh* — produces, when hoped for, an appointed term that consoles, and when denied, an appointed term that destroys. Nothing in God has changed; the variable is the direction of the creature's expectation. The sūrah has thus, in twenty-three verses, completed a single circle around one idea: the meeting with God is the fact that determines the meaning of everything else — of trial (vv. 2–3), of impunity (vv. 4, 52–55), of persecution (v. 10), of the past (vv. 14–40), and of the world's brevity (v. 64). The final clause, *wa-lahum ʿadhābun alīm* — "a painful punishment" — uses *alīm*, a word whose root *ʿ-l-m* is shared with *ʿalīm*, "knowing": the commentators sometimes note the near-homonym, and while the two words are etymologically distinct, the phonetic echo in the sūrah that has just said "God is Knower of all things" (29:62) and will say "He is the Hearing, the Knowing" (29:5, 60) makes the punishment sound like knowledge made felt: the One who knows everything about you is the One you meet, and there is nothing about you that He has not accounted for.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:24

> **Yet the answer of his people was but to say, “Slay him, or burn him!” Then God saved him from the fire. Truly in that are signs for a people who believe.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence That Has No Content: Argument Terminating in a Verdict**

*fa-mā kāna jawāba qawmihi illā an qālū-qtulūhu aw-ḥarriqūh.* The restrictive construction — "their answer was *nothing but*" — is doing the whole work. Abraham has made a case: worship and reverence (16), the inability of idols to provide (17), the precedent of nations (18), the evidence of creation and return (19–22). Verse 24 reports not a rebuttal but a sentence: *kill him, or burn him.* The word *jawāb* (answer) is used with heavy irony — this is not an answer to the argument but the substitution of a decision for one. Al-Rāzī draws the general law from it: when a party has no reply, what follows is not silence but violence, because the argument has already been won and both sides know it. Note also the plural of command — *uqtulūhu, ḥarriqūhu* — addressed by the group to itself: the sūrah is depicting a *council* that deliberates, chooses between two penalties, and selects the more exemplary one. The parallel accounts supply the machinery: *"They said: burn him, and help your gods"* (21:68), and *"They said: build for him a structure, then cast him into the blazing fire"* (37:97). The pyre was to be a public work, engineered and monumental — a building erected in order to make an execution into a spectacle. The choice of burning over killing had a purpose: a killing removes a man, a burning teaches a city.

**The Fire, and the Two Words God Spoke to It**

*fa-anjāhu'llāhu mina'n-nār.* The sūrah narrates the whole miracle in three words — God saved him from the fire — and elsewhere gives it in God's own speech to the element: *"We said: O fire, be coolness and peace (bardan wa-salāman) upon Abraham"* (21:69). The two nouns are the point of the miracle, and the commentators dwell on them. Coolness (*bard*) removes the harm of heat; peace/safety (*salām*) removes fear and injury, so that the fire did not merely fail to burn, it became a place free of dread. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the fire's own nature was not abolished by visible magic but *commanded*, which is why this verse belongs immediately after the metaphysical argument of 29:19–22: the God who originates creation and can repeat it can also address a part of it. This is the sūrah's answer to any conception of miracle as a break in nature: nature is not a law that binds God but a company of obedient things that can be spoken to, and the fire obeyed because it was already a servant. Ibn Taymiyyah, arguing against both the philosophers' necessary emanation and the theologians' occasionalist arbitrariness, insisted that the miracle is a *kharq al-ʿāda* — a departure from the customary — performed by the same One who set the custom, which is why it authenticates rather than confounds.

**The Abrahamic Word the Prophet ﷺ Also Said**

The tradition supplies an extraordinary link between this verse and the sūrah's own occasion. Al-Bukhārī (4563) transmits from Ibn ʿAbbās:

> *"'God is sufficient for us, and an excellent Guardian' — Abraham said it when he was thrown into the fire, and Muhammad said it when they said: 'The people have gathered against you, so fear them' (3:173)."*

The verse 3:173 context is the near-victory of the Quraysh's confederacy, and the identification of the two situations is the Prophet's ﷺ own. This is why our passage, verses 22–24, reads as a single unit: "you cannot thwart God, and you have no protector or helper beside Him" (22); "He punishes whom He wills and shows mercy to whom He wills" (21); and then the case in point — a man with no protector, in a fire, saved (24). The Makkan believer being threatened by the same tribe, in the same city, is told that the sentence he fears and the sentence that was spoken at Abraham's burning are in the jurisdiction of the same court.

**"In That Are Signs for a People Who Believe"**

*inna fī dhālika la-āyātin li-qawmin yuʾminūn.* The plural *āyāt* for one event is deliberate: in the burning were several signs at once — the failure of the plotters' arithmetic (a pyre built for one man and a man preserved before a city), the obedience of fire, the survival of a single conscience against a whole society, and the fact that the story is still being told fourteen centuries later while the builders of the pyre are not named in any calendar. Al-Ṭabarī, on the plural, notes that one deliverance can carry many signs because many kinds of reader can extract from it. The dative matters as much as the noun: "for a people who believe" — the sign does not manufacture faith but is read by one. This is the sūrah's epistemology in all its repetitions: "a sign for the believers" (29:44), "a manifest sign for a people who understand" (29:35), "none understand them but the knowing" (29:43).

**What the Sūrah Does Not Say — and Why That Matters**

The Qur'an restricts the narrative with remarkable severity: no name of the king who ordered the burning, no measurement of the pyre, no duration, no description of Abraham's feelings, no account of those who died with him or for him. The extra-Biblical legends — that the king was a tyrant called Nimrūd ibn Kanʿān, that the fire was lit for days, that birds carried water to it, that the fire became a garden in which Abraham sat — are transmitted in the histories of Ibn Isḥāq and Ibn al-Mundhir, in the commentary literature from Kaʿb al-Aḥbār and Wahb ibn Munabbih, and in the wider stream of *isrāʾīliyyāt*. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr handle these reports with a rule the student of tafsīr must know: they may be recited but not relied upon, neither affirmed nor denied, since the Prophet ﷺ said, regarding the People of the Book, *"Do not confirm the People of the Book and do not deny them"* (al-Bukhārī 7363). The restraint is pedagogically sound. The sūrah's argument does not need the scenery; it needs the fact that a man who was burned for telling the truth was not burned.

**A Theological Caution Attached to the Verse**

Ibn Kathīr's comment on 29:24 deserves repetition because it prevents a misreading this sūrah's whole first section forbids. The verse says God *saved him* — it does not say that God saves every believer from every harm. The sūrah's own opening has already told us that Mihjaʿ was killed at Badr, that some of the blocked emigrants were killed (vv. 2–3 and 16:110), and that *every soul shall taste death* (29:57). So verse 24 is not a formula for physical immunity; it is a demonstration that the plot cannot finally succeed. The distinction was important for a community whose members were losing relatives and whose enemies quoted their own prosperity as proof. The promise of this sūrah is not exemption from trial but the assurance that trial is not the last word, and Abraham's fire is that assurance in its most dramatic form: the fire happened, and he walked out of it, and nothing further was required.

**The Verse's Application to Every Age**

Every serious challenge to an established order eventually receives the same reply, and it is worth naming the pattern the verse isolates. When the argument is answered by a proposal for elimination, three things have become clear: the argument was not answered; the answerers have decided in advance that they will not change their minds, since killing is a method for ending a dispute rather than resolving it; and, as a consequence, the disputant has nothing further to do except continue to be right in public, because the burden of demonstration has shifted to the act itself. Ibn al-Qayyim's aphorism in *al-Fawāʾid* belongs here: God saved Abraham from the fire and did not save him from being thrown into it — "so the servant should not grieve when the trial comes, and should not presume when the deliverance comes." For the persecuted believer of any century, that sentence is the sūrah's whole message in one image: the deliverance and the ordeal are both administered by the same hand, and the fire is not, in the end, an argument against the man in it.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:25

> **And Abraham said, “You have taken idols apart from God on account of affection between you in the life of this world. Then on the Day of Resurrection you will disown one another, and you will curse one another; your refuge will be the Fire, and you will have no helpers.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Diagnosis No One Expected: Idolatry as Social Affection**

*qāla innamā-ttakhadhtum min dūni Allāhi awthānan mawaddata baynikum fī'l-ḥayāti'd-dunyā.* The sentence is one of the most penetrating lines in the Makkan Qur'an, because Abraham does not say "you worship stones because you are ignorant," nor "because your fathers misled you." He says: you took these as *the bond of affection between you* — and the syntax, which some grammarians read as a *mafʿūl la-ajlih* (a clause of cause or purpose, "for the sake of affection"), makes the idols the means to a social end. The community holds together because it kneels together: the same stone, the same festival, the same sacrifice, the same prohibition, the same table. The idols are the constitution of the group. This is why their rejection of Abraham was never an intellectual disagreement — you cannot argue a person out of a bond — and why the Qur'an's own vocabulary of *mawadda* is so double-edged: affection is praised as the bond of the faithful (the Medinan Ansar are commended because *"they love whoever emigrated to them"* — cf. 59:9) and censured as the bond of the complicit. The religion of the Makkan assembly was not, first of all, a metaphysic; it was a membership. Al-Rāzī notes the force of *fī'l-ḥayāti'd-dunyā* placed at the end: the affection is real, but it is located inside a term — this life — and the term has an expiry.

**mawadda: The Qur'an's Word for Warm Bond, Used Here as an Accusation**

*Mawadda* (from *w-d-d*, from which *wadūd*, "the Loving," is one of the names of God, and *wudd*, "love") is not a word for lust or for affection in general; it names the durable, chosen bond of goodwill. The Qur'an uses it for the marriage bond (*"and He placed between you affection and mercy"* — 30:21), for the ties that make a person cling to children and wealth (*"know that your possessions and your children are nothing but a trial, and that God has with Him a great reward"* — 64:15), and — tellingly — for the very ties that the Qur'an warns a believer not to let override the truth: *"O you who believe, do not take My enemy and yours as allies, extending to them affection, while they have denied what has come to you of the truth"* (60:1). Set 60:1 beside 29:25 and the sūrah's psychology is complete: the Qur'an never denies that social affection is a power; it insists on knowing what it is *for*, and warns that a bond built on an act of worship will be severed at the one moment when being severed matters.

**"Then on the Day of Resurrection You Will Disown One Another"**
