*thumma yawma'l-qiyāmati yakfaru baʿḍukum bi-baʿḍin wa-yalʿanu baʿḍukum baʿḍā.* The reversal is not merely that the bond ends; it is that the bond inverts through the same intensity. *Kufr* here has its root meaning — to deny, to repudiate, to disown (the *kāfir* is the one who buries the truth and denies it) — and the mutual form is exact: each party denies the other. Abraham's people worshipped the idols for the sake of one another; on that Day they will disown one another for the sake of themselves. The Qur'an describes the scene elsewhere with the same symmetry: *"The friends on that Day are enemies to one another, except the reverent"* (43:67); *"when those who were followed disown those who followed, and they saw the punishment, and the ties between them were cut off. And the followers will say: if only we had one return, we would disown them as they have disowned us"* (2:166–167); and in the exchange inside the Fire, where the followers appeal to the men they obeyed and receive nothing but abdication: *"when they argue in the Fire and the weak say to those who were proud: we were your followers — can you then avert from us a share of the Fire? They will say: if God were to guide us, we would guide you; it is the same for us whether we cry out or hold patiently; there is for you no escape"* (40:47–48; cf. 7:38–39). The point of the sūrah's inclusion of this detail in Abraham's mouth is argumentative: he is telling them what their own arrangement is really made of. If it survives only while everyone benefits, it is not an affection but an agreement; and agreements, unlike affections, can be repudiated.

**Curse, Refuge, and the Word for Home**

*wa-yalʿanu baʿḍukum baʿḍan* — the *laʿna* is from *l-ʿ-n*, "to be cast far away": the invocation is that each be driven from mercy, and the mutual form turns the assembly (*nadwā*) into a hall of reciprocal excommunication. Then *wa-maʾwākumu'n-nār* — "your refuge, your home, your place of return is the Fire." The word *maʾwā* (from *ʾ-w-ʾ*, to settle, to take residence) is used in the Qur'an for both destinations: the Qur'an says of the reverent *"then surely the Garden — it is the refuge (al-maʾwā)"* (79:41), and of the deniers *"evil is the refuge and the station"* (25:66; cf. 3:151; 40:60). Its use here is the last stroke of the irony: the idolaters chose the Fire as their *maʾwā* by choosing the bond that led to it, and the sūrah's syntax gives it to them with a possessive — "your refuge" — as though God were confirming their own reservation. The final clause — *wa-mā lakum min nāṣirīn* — repeats the doctrine of 29:8, 29:22, and 29:67: the community's mutual protection dissolves at the moment when protection is needed, and the helpers are unavailable. Al-Qurṭubī: "the verse proves that what is founded on other than God, however closely it appears bound, is bound for separation."

**Why Abraham Says This — and What the Verse Teaches About Attachment**

In the narrative, this is the last speech-act before Lot's flight (29:26); Abraham has argued about provision (29:17), about precedent (29:18), and about resurrection (29:19–22). Now he names the motive. The order is deliberate and it is a method: you cannot dismantle a practice until you have identified the human need the practice is meeting. Once the need — belonging — has been named, it can be offered elsewhere, which is exactly what the sūrah does two verses later when it describes Lot fleeing to his Lord and being admitted into the company of the righteous (29:26–27). This is the reason Islam's moral legislation so rarely asks a person to abandon a good without substituting a better one: the *mawadda* of the idol-assembly is not annulled, it is transferred to the company of *al-ṣāliḥūn* (29:9), whose bonds, the sūrah insists, do not become enmity on that Day (43:67 says "except the reverent" — the only friendship that survives). The psychology here is unusually precise: the fear that keeps a person inside a bad community is not doubt but the prospect of loneliness, and the sūrah answers it by promising a company, by commanding the emigrant to seek one (29:56), and by warning that the company for which the truth is abandoned will end in cursing.

**A Modern Case of the Same Analysis**

Anyone who has watched a person lose their standing for leaving a group — a sect, a party, a business network, an online community, a family firm — recognises Abraham's diagnosis. The group's rituals are the visible part; the binding force is the affection of members for one another and the fear of losing it. The sūrah's insight applies in both directions, and its critique is symmetrical. To the individual, it asks: is your adherence grounded in the truth of the thing, or in the warmth of the circle? To the circle, it asks a harder question: what kind of community requires worship as its condition of membership, and what kind of love is threatened by a disagreement? The verses that answer this in the Qur'an are not the polemic but the ethics: the command to keep kindness with a parent who disagrees (29:8), the command to dispute with the People of the Book "in the most beautiful manner" (29:46), and the statement that the only friendship that does not become enmity is the one founded on reverence (43:67). A community built on *mawadda* for God's sake is, on this sūrah's account, the only one that survives its own members' honesty.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:26

> **And Lot believed him and said, “Truly I am fleeing unto my Lord. Truly He is the Mighty, the Wise.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Words of Faith, and Then a Journey**

*fa-āmana lahu Lūṭ* — "and Lot believed him." The clause is the shortest in the Abraham-cycle and among the most consequential in the sūrah, because it is the only verse in which the Qur'an tells us explicitly that a named prophet believed another named prophet. Abraham's preaching had produced, in the account of this sūrah, exactly one convert — a man who believed the message in order to lose everything. The verb is intransitive-plus-particle (*āmana lahu*, "believed him," rather than "believed in him"), which the grammarians note in this construction as the idiom of accepting what a person says: Lot believed Abraham's words about God, and then, because he believed, he left. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss is precise: "Lot affirmed Abraham's call, and he was the only one of that people to believe, and he emigrated with him." The sūrah's point is stated in a word: faith is not a private conviction but a reason to move.

**hājir: A Participle, Not a Past-Tense Event**

*innī hājirun ilā rabbī.* The form is the active participle — "I am one who migrates" — which in Arabic presents the action as a state and a continuing disposition, not as a completed transaction. This is the sūrah's own theology of emigration in a single morphology: the hijrah is not something a person *did*; it is something he *is*, because the heart that has left one place for God's sake continues to leave other places for the same reason. The root *h-j-r* in Arabic carries the sense of separating and of forsaking, and from it also come *hajr* (intellect, because it *holds a person back* from what is base) and *ḥijr* (a sacred enclosure, a place set apart): the same family of words names the act of leaving, the faculty that restrains, and the sanctity of the place one goes to. The sūrah's next verses will add the political dimension: for the Meccan believers this participle was a practical instruction that cost them their homes, and it is quoted again in the sūrah's own command at 29:56 — *"My earth is vast, so worship Me"* — while the Madinan revelation made the failure to act on it a matter of judgment: *"was not God's earth vast enough that you should emigrate therein?"* (4:97).

**"Unto My Lord": The Destination Named, Not the Place**

Notice what Lot does *not* say. He does not say "I am going to Canaan," or "to Harran," or "to a place of safety." He says *ilā rabbī*. Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr both observe that the emigrant who names his destination has a plan; the emigrant who names his Lord has a trust. The Qur'an preserves both registers — Abraham says elsewhere *"I am going to my Lord; He will guide me"* (37:99), and the Prophet ﷺ's own hijrah was planned with a guide, a route through the south instead of the north, a hiding in the cave of Thawr, and a camel bought at price (al-Bukhārī 3653, 3911) — so the "fleeing to the Lord" is not recklessness about means but a relocation of the ultimate reliance. The tradition's phrase for this is *al-firrār ilā'llāh*, "fleeing to God," taken from the imperative of the same root in the sūrah that names the purpose of all creation: *"Therefore flee to God; I am for you, from Him, a plain warner"* (51:50). The sūrah of Lot and the sūrah of *al-Dhāriyāt* use the same verb and the same preposition, and the meaning in both is: leave what you were leaving for what you are going toward.

**"The Mighty, the Wise": The Two Names That a Refugee Needs**

*innahu ʿAzīzun Ḥakīm.* The pairing here is not ornamental, and the classical exegetes noticed why these two names complete the sentence. The *ʿAzīz* is the One whose power cannot be resisted and whose protection cannot be broken through — precisely the quality a person needs who has just given up the only protection he had (his clan, his city, his legal standing). The *Ḥakīm* is the One whose acts are situated with precision, who places things where they belong — precisely the quality a person needs who is giving up his own plan for a life. To flee to power without wisdom would be to place oneself at the mercy of force; to flee to wisdom without power would be to admire an intention that cannot act. Al-Qurṭubī records the saying of the early ascetics on this point: God is Mighty, so nothing you left can reach you through Him; God is Wise, so nothing you lost was wasted. This is also the sūrah's answer to 29:22's sentence "you have no protector or helper apart from God": the one who has no *walī* and no *naṣīr* has acquired an *ʿAzīz* and a *Ḥakīm*.

**The Sūrah's Argument Completed by a Single Verse**

Verse 26 performs the sūrah's own opening argument. Verses 2–3 asked whether people would be left to say "we believe" without being tried, and answered that God knows the truthful and the liars. Here, in one line, is the truthfulness becoming visible: Lot believes, and the belief costs him everything, and he moves. Nothing else in the passage is required to demonstrate his *ṣidq*, and this is why the sūrah's placement of the narrative after the theory is not illustrative but evidential — the story is the assay. It also explains the verse's function in the controversy about which the sūrah's first verses were revealed: according to Ibn ʿAbbās, as transmitted by al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī, those unable to emigrate from Makkah were reproached (4:97) and then forgiven when they did emigrate (16:110). Against that background, Lot's sentence is not an ancient saint's biography; it is the precedent of a prophet who did exactly the single thing the Makkan believers were being asked to do, at the same cost and on the same ground.

**A Note on the "Fleeing" in Islamic Ethics**

The verse's participle became a technical term in the Muslim moral vocabulary, and its extension is instructive. Al-Ghazālī's book of *kitāb al-firrār ilā'llāh* in the *Iḥyāʾ* — the longest section of that work, comprising the third quarter of the whole — takes its title from the grammar of this verse: the "fleeing" is not flight *from* the world in the sense of escape from responsibility, but flight *to* God out of whatever diverts, so that the ghazālīan "fleeing" includes leaving a corrupt assembly, leaving a haram income, leaving an unjust land, and leaving one's own excuses. This is also the tradition's reading of the Prophet ﷺ's saying on emigration after the conquest of Makkah — *"there is no emigration after the conquest, but jihad and intention; and when you are called to battle, go"* (al-Bukhārī, *Kitāb al-Jihād*; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1353, from Ibn ʿAbbās) — where the legal obligation of leaving a specific land ends while the moral structure of the act, "flee to God," does not. The jurists accordingly distinguished the ruling and the meaning: the *ḥukm* of *hijrah* from Makkah was abrogated when the city became Muslim, and the *maʿnā* of *hijrah* — leaving what God disapproves for what He loves — remains incumbent, since the Prophet ﷺ defined the Emigrant as *"the one who abandons what God has forbidden"* (Bukhārī 10; Muslim 40). Verse 26 is the verse that supplies the Qur'anic model for that definition.

**The Quiet Heroism of Being the Only One**

Finally, the verse should be read for what it says about numbers. Lot was not the leader of a movement, nor one among a crowd of converts; the Qur'an's account of the townspeople leaves one believer, and even his wife is not with him (29:32). A community's moral condition cannot, on this sūrah's reckoning, be measured by the size of its faithful remnant, and the verse's consolation for the isolated believer is not "you will gather a crowd" but "the Mighty, the Wise is the destination." The sūrah has already said that a thousand years and fifty of preaching produced a ship-load (29:14–15), and it will say that a man thrown into a fire was saved alone (29:24). Its arithmetic is deliberately the opposite of the world's: the truth is not counted by heads, and the one who stands with it is not, in any real sense, alone.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:27

> **And We bestowed upon him Isaac and Jacob, and We established prophethood and scripture among his progeny. We gave him his reward in this world, and in the Hereafter he shall truly be among the righteous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**wahba: The Grammar of an Unbought Gift**

*wa-wahabnā lahu Isḥāqa wa-yaʿqūba.* The verb is *wahaba*, "to give as a gift" — not *kasaba* (earned), not *awrathā* (inherited), not *bāyaʿnā* (exchanged). The distinction is the sūrah's own theological vocabulary: in the Qur'an, children, prophethood, kingdom, and mercy are habitually described as *hiba*, and the reason is stated in the surrounding narrative, since Isaac's birth was announced to a barren old woman (11:71–73; 51:28–30) and Jacob's to a childless one (19:7 of Yaḥyā, the same grammar). What God gives is given where no claim exists and no capacity is visible. And note the order of the gift's arrival in the parallel text: *"When he withdrew from them and from what they worshipped besides God, We gave him Isaac and Jacob, and made each of them a prophet"* (19:49). The withdrawal in 19:49 is the abandonment of his people — the same act this sūrah has been celebrating, in Abraham's case at 29:16–26 and in Lot's at 29:26. The gift, in that sequence, is not a consolation prize; it is the replacement announced by the sūrah's own logic: whoever loses a people for God's sake is given a people by God's hand.

**A Son and a Grandson, Named Together**

The sūrah's "Isaac and Jacob" is the genealogy of blessing compressed: Jacob is Isaac's son, not Abraham's, and Arabic usage freely calls a grandson a *walad* — the language of inheritance and of prayer, not a biological claim. The Qur'an says so itself when it puts the patriarchs' dying words in the mouth of Abraham's household: *"or were you witnesses when death came upon Yaʿqūb, when he said to his sons: what will you worship after me? They said: we will worship your God and the God of your fathers, Ibrāhīm and Ismāʿīl and Isḥāq — one God, and to Him we submit"* (2:133). The point of that verse, in this context, is that the covenant is transmitted as a confession and not as a blood-fact: the sons promise to worship *one* God. And the sūrah is careful about which descendants are counted: Isaac's line and Ishmael's line are distinguished (19:49 names Isaac and Jacob; 2:125–129 names Ishmael with Abraham in the raising of the foundations of the House), and the Prophet ﷺ's belonging to the house of Ishmael is the sūrah's own unspoken claim — the prophethood "established among his progeny" includes the Arab line as well as the Israelite one.

**"We Established Prophethood and Scripture in His Progeny"**

*wa-jaʿalnā fī dhurriyyatihi an-nubuwwata wa'l-kitāb.* The verb *jaʿala* here means "to place, to set, to constitute," and the two gifts are distinguished with deliberate care: prophethood is the personal office — the sending, the speech received — and *kitāb* is the durable text, the scripture that survives its recipient. Their pairing explains the sūrah's own claim a few verses later, when the Book itself is defended: *"Nay, it is clear signs in the breasts of those who have been given knowledge"* (29:49). A prophet without a book is a moment; a book is a mechanism for transmitting the moment. The same two-word gift appears in 2:136 and 3:84 ("what was sent down to you and what was sent down to Abraham and Ismail…"), and it is the theological ground of the Islamic claim to be not a new religion but the continuation of that family's office: *"Say: God speaks the truth; so follow the creed of Abraham, a man of pure faith, and he was not among the polytheists"* (3:95).

**The Limit of the Promise: A Covenant That Does Not Cross Conscience**

The verse's comfort could be misread as a doctrine of privileged lineage, and the Qur'an forecloses that reading in the very passage in which Abraham is appointed to leadership:

> *"And when his Lord tried Abraham with words, and he fulfilled them, He said: I am making you a leader for the people. He said: and of my descendants? He said: My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers."* (Qur'an 2:124)

That single clause — *lā yanālu ʿahdī ẓ-ẓālimīn* — is the sūrah's own principle stated at its source: election in Islam is functional, not ontological; it attaches to obedience and lapses with injustice. Al-Ṭabarī's treatment of 29:27 keeps the balance: the progeny were honoured with the office because it was bestowed on them, but the office was not a guarantee of the persons, as the history of Israel's prophets, whose rejection this sūrah has just described (29:23–24, and the pattern running through 29:26–40), amply shows. This is why the sūrah ends its Abraham-segment with the individual — "he shall be among the righteous" — and not with a tribal claim.

**"We Gave Him His Reward in This World"**

*wa-ātaynāhu ajrahu fī ad-dunyā.* Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr enumerate the worldly reward: the son granted in old age, the progeny of prophets, the friendship (*khalīl*), the safety from the fire, the settlement of his children at the House that all the peninsula would come to circumambulate, and above all the good name that never closes. The last is given its own verse in al-Saffāt:

> *"And We left for him [fame] among the later generations. Peace be upon Abraham. Thus does God reward the beautiful-doers. He was of Our believing servants."* (Qur'an 37:108–111)

Note the causal link in 37:110 — *kadhālika najzī al-muḥsinīn*, "thus do We recompense the beautiful-doers" — which ties this sūrah's Abraham to its own conclusion in 29:69 ("God is with the beautiful-doers"). The worldly reward, in other words, is not a bonus but the ordinary payment of the Qur'an's economy, which pays in this world before it pays in the next: *"what is with you runs out, and what is with God endures"* (16:96). The ḥadīth of the three that survive a man's death belongs to the same teaching: *"When a human being dies, his deeds are cut off except three: ongoing charity, knowledge by which people benefit, and a righteous child who prays for him"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1631). Abraham's "reward in this world" is precisely that: a charity, a knowledge, and a progeny that have not stopped praying for him.

**"And in the Hereafter He Shall Be Among the Righteous"**

*wa-innahu fī'l-ākhiraati mina'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn.* The phrase is identical in structure to the sūrah's promise to ordinary believers in 29:9: *lanudkhilannahum fī'ṣ-ṣāliḥīn.* That echo is the sūrah's pastoral climax. The friend of God is promised the same company as the frightened Meccan believer who could not emigrate — not the same rank, but the same assembly. Al-Zamakhsharī notices the economy of the phrase: no description of gardens appears here at all; the reward is stated as belonging, and the patriarch's final title is the moral category, not the geography. This is the sūrah's answer to the anxiety of comparison — the believer who thinks the saints will be somewhere better: the difference of degrees is real and unstated, and the difference of company is dissolved.

**What the Verse Teaches About Legacy**

Applied to a reader's own life, verse 27 reframes the question "what will remain of me?" in three parts. First, what is given cannot be manufactured: the verse names its own verb *wahabnā*, and the two things Abraham received — a son and a scripture — are of the class that human planning does not produce. Second, the durable legacy is the *institution*, not the reputation: prophethood and book, not monuments; which is why the tradition's most serious men invested in students and texts rather than in buildings. Third, the promise of the worldly reward is real but not immediate or complete: the sūrah says the reward was *given*, not that Abraham saw all of it — the hereafter clause remains, and the Prophet ﷺ's own saying that the true completion is the face of God is beside it. Ibn al-Qayyim's line in *al-Fawāʾid* fits the verse's double accounting: God has promised the reward twice, in this world and in the next, so that no good deed is a loss and no good deed is fully paid where you can see it.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:28

> **And [We sent] Lot, when he said to his people, “Truly you commit indecency such as none in the worlds committed before you.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet Who Came After the Father**

*wa-lūṭan idh qāla li-qawmihi* — the accusative again continues the government of *arsalnā* from verse 14: We sent Noah, and Abraham, and Lot. The sūrah's sequencing is historically and theologically exact, since the Qur'an elsewhere makes Lot a believer *in* Abraham (29:26 "and Lot believed him") and the tradition makes him his nephew or cousin. So the third mission in the chain is not a fresh revelation to a fresh people but the same creed carried into a second city by a man who joined it from outside — Lot is, in the sūrah's own vocabulary, an emigrant (29:26) who now preaches in the land he fled to. That detail matters: his authority is moral, not tribal, which is exactly why his people can mock him with the charge of "claiming purity" (27:56, *"if it were not for your partisans, we would have stoned you — you are not a people with any honour"*).

**fāḥisha: A Technical Word for the Gravest Public Wrong**

*innakum la-taʾtūna'l-fāḥishata mā sabaqakum bihā min aḥadin mina'l-ʿālamīn.* The noun *fāḥisha* comes from *fuḥsh*, whose semantic core is excess and manifestness: a thing that is *fāḥish* is one that goes beyond the limit and, by doing so, becomes conspicuous. Usūl writers, among them al-Āmidī and after him al-Shāṭibī, used this for a definition: the *fāḥisha* is the transgression whose ugliness is established in every revealed law — the act no prophet has ever permitted — which is what makes the word the right one for Lot's address: he is not announcing a new prohibition but identifying a category his hearers already recognise as out of bounds. The Qur'an uses *fāḥisha* for this act in this passage (29:28) and elsewhere for fornication generally (17:32 *"do not come near fornication; it is an abomination (fāḥisha) and an evil road"*; 4:15, 4:19, 24:19), and *munkar* for what is disapproved by right reason, with *fāḥisha* and *munkar* paired in the sūrah's own verse on prayer (29:45). The two words together describe the sin as both excessive in itself and offensive to others — a private enormity and a public injury at once.

**"None in the Worlds Committed Before You" — What the Verse Claims**

The commentators have been unusually careful with this clause, because a surface reading would be empirically false: other individuals, in other ages, had done other things. Al-Ṭabarī's and Ibn Kathīr's resolution, echoed by al-Qurṭubī, is that the negation is of a *people* (qawm) and of a *practice carried out openly and collectively*: no nation had before them done this so publicly, without shame, in assembly, and with coercion. The same idea appears in 7:80 with the identical words, and the parallel at 11:77–79 shows the specific horror the sūrah's next verse will enumerate — the men of the town rushing to Lot's house to assault his guests, so that he offered his daughters (that is, the women of the community in lawful marriage, as the majority interpret) rather than let a guest be violated, and they replied: *"you know we have no claim over your daughters, and you know well what we want"* (11:79). What is "unprecedented," on this reading, is not the act but the social organisation of it: an inversion in which the crime has become the custom and the custom is enforced. That is a category of moral collapse the Qur'an names directly, in the reproach it puts on the lips of the rejected Israelites: *"You do not forbid one another from an evil you commit — how evil is what you used to do!"* (5:79). The sin that destroys, on this analysis, is the sin a community stops prohibiting.

**The Qur'an's Own Account of What Happened There**

Nothing in 29:28–29 is complete without the sūrah's own elsewhere. Lot's preaching is given at length in Hūd, al-Shuʿarāʾ, al-Naml, al-ʿAnkabūt and al-Qamar, and in every version the accusation has the same three elements this sūrah names in verse 29: approaching men with desire, cutting the road, and committing the munkar in assembly. In al-Naml the people's answer adds one thing absent elsewhere — the political logic of expulsion: *"What is its reward but to expel them from your town? They are people who claim purity!"* (27:56), and in al-Aʿrāf the answer is the same challenge to the threat of punishment (7:82; cf. 29:29). Taken together, the accounts show that the offence charged against Lot was not a private ethics of desire but a civic one: the community's refusal to accept any standard outside itself, and its use of the majority's power to expel the minority that dissents.

**The Legal and Ethical Discussions the Verse Generated**

Classical Islamic jurisprudence treats "the act of the people of Lot" (*fiʿl qawmi Lūṭ*) as a *ḥadd* matter, and the schools differ markedly, a fact the exegetes record: the Ḥanafīs held no fixed *ḥadd* applies and prescribed *taʿzīr* (discretionary correction), citing the report that the Prophet ﷺ said *"if you find one doing what Lot's people did, kill the doer and the one done to"* (transmitted by al-Tirmidhī 1456, Abū Dāwūd 4474, from Ibn ʿAbbās — its chain was assessed as weak by multiple imams, including al-Dhahabī and others; al-Shāfiʿī's celebrated judgement was that the report cannot be used as proof, and the sound *ḥadd* is that of fornication, applied according to the person's status). ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb is reported in al-Muwaṭṭaʾ to have consulted the Companions, and ʿAlī's position — stoning irrespective of marital status, on the grounds that the act involves a violation of the "veil" God made sacred — is transmitted in Abū Dāwūd (4475) and discussed in the *fuqahāʾ*' books. Two further points must be added from the same tradition, because they are part of the same law and are usually omitted. First, the evidentiary bar is enormous: four eyewitnesses to the act itself, with the penalty falling on the accuser for *qadhf* if four are not produced (24:4, 24:13) — a rule the Qur'an itself states. Second, concealment of others' sins is commanded, not surveillance: *"whoever conceals a believer's fault, God conceals his fault in this world and the next"* (*wa-man sātara musliman sātarahu'llāhu fī ad-dunyā wa'l-ākhira*, Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2590), and the Prophet ﷺ never pursued a hidden act: *"a servant does not conceal another servant in this world but that God conceals him on the Day of Resurrection"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, *Kitāb al-Birr*, from Ibn ʿUmar). Whatever a contemporary reader concludes about criminal law, the sūrah's own interest is not in punishment of individuals at all; its interest is in what a *people* does when it legislates its own desires and expels those who object.

**The Verse's Real Subject: The Public Character of Sin**

Lot's accusation is, in the sūrah's structure, an instance of the argument running through verses 16–40: each people is destroyed not for a single vice but for the fusion of vice with arrogance and with the persecution of the one who warns. That is why the indecency named here is paired, in verse 29, with *qatʿ al-sabīl* (cutting the road) and with *al-munkar fī nadwāhum* (outrage in their assembly) — a triad of sexual violence, banditry, and public indecency. The sūrah is describing a society in which the strong take what they want from strangers and from each other, and defend the practice by expelling the moral objector. Read from that centre, 29:28 is not a verse about desire but a verse about what happens when a community has made its desire into its constitution. The Makkan audience heard the description with their own *haram* in mind: their security, their assemblies at the Kaʿba, their exposure of travellers' blood, and their treatment of the small group that objected.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:29

> **What! Do you come unto men, cut off the way, and commit reprehensible deeds in your gatherings?” Yet the answer of his people was but to say, “Bring us God’s Punishment, if you are among the truthful.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Charges, Ranked by Their Reach**

*a-taʾtūna'dh-dhukrāna wa-taqṭaʿūna as-sabīla wa-taʾtūna fī nadwākumu'l-munkar.* The three verbs describe a widening circle of harm, and the order is not rhetorical decoration. (1) *taʾtūna'dh-dhukrāna* — "you approach the males": the euphemism is typical of the Qur'an's vocabulary for sexual acts, and the phrase is deliberately impersonal — the men are named without possessive, as though the act objectified them too. (2) *taqṭaʿūna as-sabīl* — "you cut the road": the idiom for highway robbery, the crime against the stranger, the traveller, the person with no local protection. (3) *taʾtūna fī nadwākumu'l-munkar* — "you commit the outrage in your assembly": the crime transposed into institution, done where the town's men convene and agree. So the sequence runs from the violation of a person, to the violation of the outsider, to the violation made respectable by publicity. The sūrah's indictment is a sociology: a private wrong becomes a civic disease when it is performed in the place where the community conducts its business.

**nadwa: The Assembly Hall and What It Does to Conduct**

The word *nadwā* (pl. *nudwā*) is from *nādā*, to call out, to convene; the *nadwā* is the summons, the meeting, and the place of meeting — the *nādī*, which in turn gives us the modern Arabic for a club or society. In Makkah the *Dār al-Nadwa* was the council-house of Quraysh beside the Kaʿba, where the tribe's decisions were made, and it is where, in the sūrah's own later verses and in the Sīra, the plans against the Prophet ﷺ were discussed. The Qur'an's word for the crime scene is therefore chosen for a reason: it names the sin as a corporate act of the deliberating body, not the private failing of lonely men. Lot's accusation is that his people had made a meeting-place into a place where the outrage was *normalised*, and that the normalisation is worse than the act, because an act can be repented while a custom must first be overthrown. The same diagnosis underlies the verse quoted at 29:28 — *"they did not forbid one another from the evil they committed"* (5:79) — and the Qur'an's record of the same speech in Makkah itself, in the verse said to belong to the council's deliberations: *"And when they said: 'O God, if this is the truth from You, rain stones upon us from the sky, or bring us a painful punishment'"* (8:32) — a prayer for destruction uttered as a jeer, at the very House they claimed to guard.

**"Cut the Road": The Crime Against the Stranger**

*qatʿ al-ṭarīq / qatʿ al-sabīl* is one of the two great public crimes the Qur'an names (the other is *fasād fil-arḍ*), and Islamic law's treatment of *ḥirāba* (5:33–34: *"the recompense of those who wage war against God and His Messenger and strive to make corruption in the land…"*) is built on the principle that the bandit's offence is against the possibility of travel, trade, and trust. In Lot's town the "cutting of the road" is described in the parallel accounts in its most predatory form: the travellers who arrived were the objects of assault (11:77–79; 15:67–71), so that hospitality — the supreme law of the desert — was inverted into hunting. This is why Lot's distress in verse 33 is unbearable: the men came to him as guests under God's and Lot's protection, and his town came to him as a mob. To read only the sexual element of the story is to miss the sūrah's own emphasis: the guilt it documents is the corruption of the most basic institutions of safety — the body, the road, and the assembly.

**The Answer: "Bring Us What You Promise"**

*fa-mā kāna jawāba qawmihi illā an qālū-ati binā bi-adhābi'llāhi in kunta mina'ṣ-ṣādiqīn.* As at verse 24, the "answer" is a refusal of answer; the exclusive construction again reduces their reply to a single act of mockery. The demand for the punishment is a fixed motif in this sūrah and in the Qur'an generally: ʿĀd say to Hūd *"bring upon us what you threaten us, if you are truthful"* (46:22; cf. 7:70), Thamūd say to Ṣāliḥ *"bring what you promise us"* (7:77), and Makkah's disbelievers say *"if you are truthful, when is this promise?"* (67:25; cf. 27:71, 34:29, 36:48), while the sūrah's own verses 53–54 will meet the same taunt head-on. The psychology the Qur'an describes is consistent and recognisable: the mock-request for consequences is a way of converting evidence into theatre. Since the speaker's warning cannot be refuted, it is treated as a prophecy to be tested by taunting, and the taunter's own safety in the meantime is quietly assumed — the same assumption the sūrah called "evil is the judgment they make" (29:4). And there is a further function: the demand is made publicly, in the *nadwā*, so that the demand itself becomes a loyalty oath to the custom; to refuse to taunt is to be marked out.

**Recognition Refused: The Qur'an's Explanation of the Mechanism**

The sūrah's neighbours make clear that this is not ignorance. Of the town: *"do you not see that your Lord did with the people of ʿĀd of Iram… and with Thamūd who hewed the rock in the valley… and with Pharaoh of the stakes, who transgressed in the lands and spread much corruption there? So your Lord poured upon them the scourge of chastisement"* — cf. 89:6–13 — the guilt is stated as *transgression with knowledge*, and in the case of Makkah the Qur'an is explicit: *"those to whom We gave the Book recognise him as they recognise their own sons; and a group of them knowingly conceal the truth"* (2:146); *"when there came to them what they had already recognised, they disbelieved in it"* (2:89). The people of Lot asked for the punishment not because they thought it impossible but because the demand was cheaper than repentance, and because a community that has built its institutions on a practice cannot accept a correction that dismantles them. This is the reason the sūrah's God does not respond to such challenges by producing the punishment on demand: *"and they ask you to hasten the punishment… were it not for an appointed term, the punishment would certainly have come upon them"* (29:53). The refusal is mercy to the bystanders, and a stay of execution that they misread as vindication.

**Why the Verse Belongs in a Sūrah About Persecution**

The Makkan believers, whose own town's assembly was plotting (as the *Dār al-Nadwa* planned the night of the Hijrah), heard this passage as a mirror with two faces. One: the persecuting council is a *nadwā* of the same kind, and its confidence that nothing will come from above is exactly the confidence that verse 29:34 answers — "We are sending down on the people of this town a torment from the sky." Two: the believer who is unable to stop the custom is not required to; he is required to refuse it, to name it, and if necessary to leave — which is precisely what Lot does next, and what 29:56 commands. The sūrah never promises that the preaching will reverse a society's habits; it promises that the preacher will be extracted, and that the ruin of the habit will be left as a sign for whoever has a mind (29:35). That is the whole theodicy of the passage in four verses: a corruption is allowed to exhaust itself in public while its victims are removed from it.

**A Closing Observation on the Word for "Outrage"**

The third charge uses *munkar* — "that which is disapproved," from *n-k-r*, the root of *nakr* (strangeness, denial) and, tellingly, of *nakīr*, the name of one of the two interrogation angels in the grave, and of *munkar* in the sense of "harsh." The same root gives the Qur'an's word for ingratitude (*kufūr*, "the denial of a favour," paired in 29:66 with God's blessings) and for the familiar (its opposite, *maʿrūf*, the "recognised," which is the standard of good conduct in 2:263 and 29:8). Read with that etymology, Lot's people are accused of making the *munkar* — the unrecognisable, the strange — into the ordinary currency of their meetings, and of losing the *maʿrūf*, the recognised good. Every community's moral decline, in the Qur'an's vocabulary, begins with a re-labelling of the assembly: what was once strange becomes customary, and what was once recognised becomes strange.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:30

> **He said, “My Lord, help me against the people who work corruption.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Comes Right After the Taunt**

*qāla rabbi-nṣurnī ʿalā'l-qawmi'l-mufsidīn.* A single line, fourteen words in the English and four in the Arabic, and it is the pivot of the whole Lot-passage. The people of the town have demanded the punishment as a joke; the prophet does not answer them at all. He turns and speaks to God. The sūrah has just shown what a community can do to a dissenter — expel him (27:56), threaten him, mock his demand for consequences — and now it shows what the dissenter does in response, and the answer is the most economical description of prophetic method in the chapter: no counter-mockery, no threat, no mobilisation, and no despair; a prayer, an address, and a description of the opponents as *those who do corruption*. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss is characteristically practical: "help me against them by Your punishment, since they corrupt among Your creatures and call to their own practice" — that is, Lot asks for God's act, not for his own.

**"My Lord": The Name Chosen in the Hour of Anger**

He says *rabbi*, not *ilāhī* or *allāhumma*. The name *Rabb* — from *r-b-b*, denoting nurture, ownership, and the gradual bringing-up of a thing to its completion — is the name of one who is patient with what He has made and does not abandon it mid-growth. In a moment when a man's whole city has declared war on decency, the choice of the nurturing name is the sūrah's quiet instruction about how to speak to God when one's anger is at its highest. This is the same discipline the Qur'an teaches the final community: *"and by God's mercy you were gentle with them; had you been harsh, with a heart of stone, they would have scattered from around you"* (3:159), and the Prophet's ﷺ own refusal, when the angel of the mountains offered to close the two ranges on the people of Ṭāʾif who had stoned him until he bled:

> *"Nay — I hope that God will bring from their loins people who worship God alone, associating nothing with Him."* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3231; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2599)

The contrast is instructive rather than critical: Lot spoke after decades of refusal and in a town whose destruction had already been announced by the envoys in 29:31; his prayer is the prayer of a man whose warning has been exhausted, not a general licence for the believer to ask for his opponents' ruin. When the Prophet ﷺ was told "invoke destruction upon the polytheists," he answered *"I was not sent to curse; I was sent as a mercy"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2599, in the companion report of the same occasion).

**n-ṣ-r: What "Help" Means in the Qur'an**

The verb *naṣara* — "to give victory, to come to the aid of" — carries the sense of arriving from outside the struggle: *nusrah* is reinforcement, and the *nāṣir* is the one whose strength enters where the victim's strength ends. The sūrah denied in 29:22 that anyone has such a helper apart from God (*wa-mā lakum min dūni Allāhi min waliyyin wa-lā naṣīr*), and here Lot asks for precisely what 29:22 says only God can give. The correspondence is exact: verse 22 is the doctrine, verse 30 is its practice. And the sūrah's own definition of what help from God looks like is not the removal of the ordeal but the guarantee of the outcome: *"We will surely help Our messengers and those who believe in the life of this world and on the Day when the witnesses stand"* (40:51); *"so God sent down His tranquillity upon His messenger and upon the believers"* (48:4 and 9:26, of the cave and of Ḥunayn); and on the night of Badr, when the same cry was raised: *"When you cried out to your Lord for help, He answered you: I will reinforce you with a thousand of the angels, following one another"* (8:9) — and the sentence that qualifies it: *"there is no victory except from God"* (3:126). The verse 29:30's tiny word *unṣur* therefore asks for what God has already promised to give: not revenge, but the vindication that ends the tyranny.

**mufsidīn: Naming the Crime by Its Function**

Lot does not call them "the lustful" or "the proud"; he calls them *those who produce fasād*. The root *f-s-d* means to become corrupt, to spoil, to break what was sound, and its opposite *iṣlāḥ* — mending, setting right — is the word used by another prophet of this same sūrah-cluster, Shuʿayb, whom the sūrah will quote next (29:36): *"I only intend reform (iṣlāḥ) as far as I am able, and my success is only through God"* (11:88). The Qur'an's moral vocabulary is thus organised around one axis: repair or ruin. In the first pages of the Book, the angels' question already sets the frame: *"will You place therein one who corrupts in it and sheds blood?"* (2:30), and Makkah's persecutors are described in the sūrah's own terms as people who, when they turn away, *"strive through the land to corrupt in it and to break its ties"* — cf. 2:205. Naming an opponent by the *function* of their conduct, rather than by their identity, has a consequence the exegetes draw out: the demand is for the corruption to be stopped, not for the people to be annihilated — and in the event Lot's neighbours were offered a way out until the final hour (11:79: Lot's attempt to redirect them; 27:56: his offer of argument). Had he said "destroy them," the sūrah would have been teaching a different religion.

**Duʿāʾ as an Act, and Its Place in the Sūrah's Psychology of Trial**

The makkan believer being crushed by a *nadwā* of this kind is given here a practice, not a theory. Prayer in this sūrah is not a supplement to action but the action proper to a person who has no leverage: verse 45 will make the same claim in the sūrah's most quoted sentence about worship — *"recite what has been revealed to you of the Book, and establish the prayer; truly prayer prevents indecency and outrage"* (29:45). The two verses rhyme in function: Lot's *nṣurnī* is the prayer of one facing an outrage, and the commanded *ṣalāh* is the prayer of a community facing a corruption. The tradition's teaching about duʿāʾ fits precisely here — *"nothing repels the decree except supplication, and nothing increases life except righteousness"* (al-Tirmidhī 2139, from Thawbān; graded ḥasan by al-Tirmidhī, and some of its chains were strengthened by al-Ḥākim and al-Dhahabī) — and the same Prophet ﷺ warned against the impatient prayer: *"the servant's prayer is answered so long as he does not ask for something sinful or for the severing of ties, and does not say 'I prayed and I prayed and I was not answered,' then he gives up in despair"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2735; cf. al-Bukhārī 6340 on "ʿajjila"). The instruction the verses give the Makkan community is therefore complete in three parts: pray, and keep praying, and leave the timing to God — the third part being the sūrah's answer to the mockers' "bring it on" (29:29, 29:53).

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Answer to Political Despair**

Finally, note what the sūrah has done in the space of four verses: it has shown a corrupt public (29:29), a lone prophet with no constituency (29:26–30), a divine plan already in motion (29:31–32), and a deliverance promised for a household that includes one who is not saved (29:32–34). The political reading is severe and honest: a society can be past argument, a reformer can be powerless in the moment, and yet nothing has gone wrong. The verse's placement teaches the believer to hold both truths at once — that corruption is real and powerful, and that it is not the last instance of authority. That is why the sūrah does not leave Lot in verse 30. He asked for help against the *mufsidūn*; verses 31–35 show the help arriving, the deliverance being executed, and the ruin being left standing as "a manifest sign for a people who understand." The practical lesson for the reader is stated in the verse's own four words: when the argument is exhausted and the assembly is hostile, address God, describe the act for what it is, and go on living in the expectation that the corruption's end has already been written.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:31

> **And when Our envoys came unto Abraham with glad tidings, they said, “We shall surely destroy the people of this town; truly its people are wrongdoers.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Visit, Two Messages**

*wa-lammā jāʾat rusulunā Ibrāhīma bi'l-bushrā qālū innā muhlikū aḥli hādhihi'l-qaryati inna ahlahā kānū ẓālimīn.* The envoys are the same angels who announced the birth of Isaac in Hūd (11:69–73) and in al-Dhāriyāt (51:24–30); the sūrah here compresses both news into one breath — a son for the believer, and destruction for the town. The juxtaposition is not sentimental; it is the Qur'an's standing description of revelation itself, which is always simultaneously *bushrā* and *nudhur*: *"a bearer of glad tidings and a warner"* (2:119, 41:4), and of the Book: *"We sent down from the Qur'an that which is a healing and a mercy for the believers, and it increases the wrongdoers in nothing but loss"* (17:82). Al-Rāzī observes that placing the promise and the destruction in the same sentence, in the same house, in the same hour, is the sūrah's way of teaching that the two are not separate economies; the same grace that builds a prophetic household empties a corrupt city.

**The Etiquette of the Envoys: Why the Announcement Is Made to Abraham**

God's messengers do not announce the destruction to Lot. They announce it to Abraham, and the sūrah elsewhere shows why: Abraham is the one whose heart will argue about it, and whose argument is praised. *"And when Our messengers came with the glad tidings to Abraham, they said: we shall destroy the people of this town; its people were wrongdoers. He said: but Lot is in it! They said: we know best who is in it"* — this compressed version at 29:31–32, and at 11:74–76: *"when the argument of Abraham about the people of Lot came to Us… 'O Abraham, turn from this; the command of your Lord has come, and there will come upon them a punishment that cannot be averted.'"* Then the verdict on the man himself: *"Abraham was forbearing, compassionate, and penitent"* (11:75). Two lessons are recorded in the commentaries. (1) Intercession and pleading for the doomed is praiseworthy even when the outcome is fixed — the tradition of the Prophet's ﷺ saying *"the mercy of God is near to those who weep over their people"* belongs to this spirit. (2) The news is given to the one who must survive it: the believer is told what God is doing in the world not so that he can intervene, but so that his heart is prepared and his hands are free to leave.

**"This Town" — the Qur'an's Method of Unnaming**

The sūrah says *hādhihi'l-qaryah*, "this town," and never names Sodom; the same device recurs throughout the Qur'an's moral narratives (the town in Yāsīn, the owners of the garden in al-Qalam, the people of the city in 36:13–29), and al-Ṭabarī notes that the effect of the demonstrative is to point to a town the hearers can see in their own geography and imagination. The extra-Qur'anic identification — Sodom, al-Sadūm, a region around the Dead Sea — comes from the historical and geographical tradition, and the exegetes treat it as useful information rather than doctrine, since the verse's moral content does not depend on the coordinates. What matters in the sūrah's grammar is that the article and the demonstrative make the town a *case*: a place where wrongdoing became the condition of citizenship.

**Ẓālimīn: The Reason Given, and Its Limits**

*inna ahlahā kānū ẓālimīn.* The stated ground of destruction is wronging — and the sūrah uses the plural, *ahluhā*, "its people," which the commentators discuss with care, because it appears to include everyone. Their resolution is provided elsewhere in the Qur'an itself: the destruction is not the punishment of individuals for sin but the ending of a regime; those who were carried along in a *ẓulm* whose laws they enforced share in its guilt, and the Qur'an's categories for that are precise — the followers *and* the leaders are both punished, and the followers complain of it (2:167; 40:47–48). And the divine law of restraint is equally explicit on the other side: *"God would never destroy the towns unjustly while their people were people of reform"* (11:117); *"that is because your Lord would not destroy the towns for their wrongdoing while their people were heedless"* (6:131); and *"your Lord would not destroy the towns until He sends, in their mother-town, a messenger reciting to them Our verses"* (28:59) — and the sūrah of Lot itself makes the exception concrete in the next verse: one man and his believing household are extracted before the blow.

**A Structural Observation: The Turn from the Fire to the Flood of Stones**

Abraham's own story in this sūrah has just reached its climax — the fire that was commanded to be cool, and the escape from a society's verdict. Verse 31 continues the same subject in a new register: a man saved from a fire is now told about a town that will be destroyed from the sky. The sūrah is building its argument about divine punishment by degrees: the respite of the Makkan persecutors (vv. 4, 53), the deliverance of one individual (v. 24), and now the collective end of a city (vv. 31–35). Ibn Kathīr notes that the sequence leaves the hearer in no doubt about the sūrah's real audience: the people of Makkah have, like the people of the town, made outrage public and mocked the punishment; but the difference is also stated — Makkah has a prophet within it, which is why the punishment is deferred: *"God is not such as to punish them while you are among them"* (8:33). Abraham, informed of the destruction, is the type of the interceding servant; the presence of the Prophet ﷺ in Makkah is the reason the Quraysh are still standing. That is a consolation to the persecuted and a warning to the persecuting, delivered by one verse.

**The Same Scene in Two Other Sūrahs, Word for Word**

The Qur'an tells the distress of Lot three times, and the repetitions are the best commentary on our verse, because each telling keeps the same verbs and changes what is being taught. In Hūd: "and when Our envoys came to Lot, he was grieved for them and straitened in his breast, and he said: this is a terrible day. And his people came rushing to him" (11:77–78), where the emphasis is his weakness before the crowd; in the Moon, where the emphasis is the assault on the guest-right and what becomes of the assailants — their eyes effaced at the hour the punishment is served (54:33–42); and here, in the Spider, where the emphasis is the double message the envoys deliver in one breath: "truly its people are wrongdoers" as the reason for destruction (29:31), and "We shall surely save him and his family, save for his wife" as the measure of mercy (29:32). The verbal identity across the tellings is exact and instructive: in the sūrah of the Winnowing Winds the envoys' answer is quoted as "they said: do not fear and do not grieve, and we shall deliver you and your people, except your wife — she is among the delayed" (51:30), the very pair of imperatives our verse carries, so that "he was distressed, and his heart was straitened" is answered in three tellings by the same three-word cure. That fixity is the Qur'an's way of insisting that the report is not a legend told for effect: the horror, the straitness of the breast, and the reassurance recur, while the surrounding argument moves, and this is the pattern the sūrah will itself apply at 29:15 to Noah's rescue — a detail preserved for its sign-value rather than for its drama.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:32

> **He said, “Verily, Lot is in it.” They said, “We know better who is in it. Assuredly We shall save him and his family, save for his wife; she is among those who lagged behind.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument of Love, and the Answer of Knowledge**

*qāla inna fīhā Lūṭā. qālū naḥnu aʿlamu biman fīhā lanunjiyannahu wa-ahlahu illa mraʾatahu kānat mina'l-ghābirīn.* Abraham's single objection is not a plea of innocence for the town — he does not claim they are good people — but a statement of fact: *Lot is in it*. The logic is the logic of 11:117 and of the intercession that God permits: a town that contains a prophet cannot be treated as a town that has rejected all warning; the righteous man's presence is a claim upon the mercy. And the envoys' reply is the sūrah's answer to every such plea: *we know better who is in it.* The knowledge of God is not general but itemised: it knows who is in the town, who belongs to whom, whose name is on the register of the doomed and whose is not. That is precisely the doctrine on which the sūrah has been built from verse 3 — *God knows the truthful and knows the liars* — and its deployment here, at the moment of a city's destruction, gives it its most practical form: God's knowledge is not the cause of anyone's death; it is the reason no innocent person dies in a catastrophe. Al-Ṭabarī: "We know who is in it, and Lot is not among the wrongdoers, and we shall save him, so do not argue further."

**"We Know Better Who Is In It" — The Theodicy of Precision**

The Makkan situation to which this is addressed was, historically, a mixed population: open enemies of the message, secret believers who had not emigrated, children, and slaves whose conversion the tribe counted as its own property. The Qur'an's answer, voiced by the angels, is that the extraction is already computed. Compare the verse revealed about the very night the Prophet ﷺ left Makkah, when the plans of the *nadwā* were finalised: *"if it were not for believing men and believing women whom you did not know, lest you trample them and there befall you a blame on account of them without knowledge"* — the reason God withheld the destroyers from Makkah was the presence of unknown believers (48:25). The same clause, *"whom you did not know"*, is the exact counterpart of *"We know better who is in it"*: what the human eye cannot see in a crowd, divine knowledge itemises. This is one of the Qur'an's strongest answers to the objection that collective punishment is inherent in catastrophe narratives — God's punishments are not collective because His knowledge is not statistical.

**The Exception: "Except His Wife"**

*illā mraʾatahu.* Of Lot's household, one is excluded, and the sūrah does not soften it. Elsewhere the reason is stated: *"God struck as a parable for those who disbelieve the wife of Noah and the wife of Lot: they were under two of Our servants, righteous men, and they betrayed them"* (66:10). The commentators are emphatic about what the "betrayal" (*khiyāna*) was *not*: al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr both record the consensus of the early exegetes that these two women were not accused of sexual misconduct — a calumny that some of the *isrāʾīliyyāt* circulated and which the exegetes rejected on the ground that the prophets' wives could not be charged with fornication without their husbands' honour being violated — but of betrayal of the faith: Noah's wife gave her son away to the mocking crowd (cf. 11:42–43) and Lot's wife signalled to her people the arrival of the guests who were to be assaulted (a detail in the reports of the early exegetes, e.g. from Mujāhid and Qatāda). She is thus named here *"mina'l-ghābirīn"* — "of those who are past/lagging behind," from *gh-b-r*, whose meanings include the dust that settles on abandoned things and the past that has gone by; al-Zamakhsharī explains that the phrase makes her one of what is finished: from the same root come *al-ghubār*, the dust that settles on abandoned things, and *al-ʿāqiba*, the aftermath that follows — and the exegetes like to note the pairing, since to be "of the *ghābirīn*" is to be of the part that has passed, with no aftermath.

**The Verse's Sharpest Lesson: Household Is Not Salvation**

The exclusion of Lot's wife is the sūrah's most emphatic statement of a rule it has been asserting since verse 8: no relationship transfers merit. A woman can sleep in a prophet's house, rise in his household, be named among his *ahl* — and still be destroyed. The Qur'an draws the corollary in the same breath in 66:10: *"they availed them nothing whatsoever against God"* — Lot's prophethood and Noah's prophethood did not shield the women, and the same law was announced publicly by the Prophet ﷺ about his own kin: *"O Fāṭima bint Muḥammad, ask me of whatever wealth you wish — I cannot avail you anything against God"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 206; al-Bukhārī, *Kitāb al-Riqāq* and *Tafsīr Sūrat "Tabbat yadā Abī Lahab"*). That ḥadīth, addressed to the Prophet's own daughter, is the practical end of the whole sūrah-passage: the bond that saves is *mawadda* for God's sake (29:25), not genealogy, not marriage, not residence, and the only protection that survives is what 29:22 named — none, and all, besides Him.

**"Assuredly We Shall Save Him and His Family": The Grammar of Certainty**

The envoys' promise is expressed with the oath-lām and the nūn of emphasis — *la-nunjiyannahu* — exactly the manner in which the sūrah's own verses 7, 9, and 69 state their promises, and the correspondence is the sūrah's way of binding the two: the salvation of Lot is a case of the general promise *"those who believe and do righteous deeds, We shall surely cause them to enter among the righteous"* (29:9). The point is not that prophets get special treatment; it is that the same sworn promise covers everyone in the category. Al-Rāzī adds the philosophical observation that the angels' plural of majesty here (*naḥnu aʿlamu*, *lanunjiyanna*) is itself a sign of the gravity of the moment: the affairs of cities and of households are executed by the deliberate decree of God, and the angels speak in His voice as His commissioned servants — a phrase the exegetes use against any reading of the story as the angels acting on their own initiative.

**The Consolation of the Unknown Believer**

Read by the Makkan believer still trapped in the town, verse 32 has a use that the narrative does not advertise: it is about a person who cannot prove, to anyone and sometimes to himself, that he belongs — a man whose parents persecute him (29:8), whose wife does not share his faith (as Lot's wife did not), whose company is corrupt, and who has no way to make his position visible. The verse tells him that the record is being kept by a knowledge that is itemised, and that his extraction has already been decided. The same idea underlies the sūrah's closing promise of guidance to those who strive (29:69) and the verse on emigration: *"whoever emigrates in God's cause will find in the land many refuge and abundance"* (4:100). The one who is "in" the corrupt town is not, on this sūrah's account, of the town; and the "being in it" that matters — Lot in Sodom, the believer in Makkah — is a condition from which deliverance is already scheduled, provided that the person's heart has, like Lot's, declared the flight (29:26).

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:33

> **And when Our envoys came unto Lot, he was distressed on their account; yet he was constrained from helping them. And they said, “Be not afraid, nor grieve. We shall surely save thee and thy family, save for his wife; she is among those who lagged behind.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet Who Could Not Protect His Guests**

*wa-lammā jāʾat rusulunā Lūṭan sīʾa bihim wa-ḍāqa bihim dhraʿan.* The first verb is passive and unusual: *sīʾa*, "he was made to suffer by them" — grief inflicted on him *by the very guests who had come to help*. The second is an idiom of helplessness: *ḍāqa bihim dhraʿan*, literally "his arm was narrowed for them," and the *dhiraʿ* — the forearm, the span of reach, the measure of a man's capacity to act — becomes the organ of his impotence. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the aptness of the image: the arm is what a man stretches out to seize or to defend, and Lot's stretch came up short. The parallel account in Hūd adds the sentence that gives the moment its shape: *"and he said: this is a terrible day (hādhā yawmun ʿaṣīb)"* (11:77), where *ʿaṣīb* is the word for a day that is harsh, unyielding, contracted — the same semantic field as *ʿaṣab*, the sinew that tightens. The sūrah is showing what a prophet looks like when the means are gone: not serene, but straitened.

**Why the Distress, and Why It Matters Theologically**

The reason for Lot's agony is given in the other tellings: the envoys had come in human form, the men of the town had smelled a prey, and the law of the desert — that a host is answerable for the safety of the guest he has received — placed the whole weight of the violation on Lot's shoulders. He then made the offer that the sūrah's neighbours record: *"these are my daughters, they are purer for you"* (11:78), which the majority of exegetes interpret as the women of the community, lawful in marriage, since a prophet could not offer his daughters to a crowd for fornication — an explanation given by al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī and others, who also transmit the alternative that the term "daughters of a prophet" belonged to the community's women in the way that the believers are called "sons" of their leaders in the Qur'an's idiom. Theologically, the verse's purpose is not the embarrassment of the scene but its instruction: the messenger of God can be, at the very moment God is acting through him, utterly unable to act. The angels who will destroy the city cannot, in the form of guests, be defended by a single man; and the defence of the guests is not required of him, because it is not his to provide. Al-Rāzī draws the general rule from 29:33 and 11:77–78: prophethood does not entail the absence of fear, but the presence of the message; and the promise of deliverance is what turns the straitened arm into patience.

**"Do Not Fear, Do Not Grieve": The Formula of Divine Reassurance**

*qālū lā takhaf wa-lā taḥzan.* The two negatives joined by *wa* form a fixed phrase in the Qur'an, and its recurrences are the commentary on this one. To the mother of Mūsā, whose fear was that her cast-off child would be found: *"do not grieve; we shall bring him back to you and make him of the messengers"* (28:39; cf. the same consolation to her at 20:39, *"that her eye should be cool and she should not grieve"*). To Mūsā and Hārūn, before Pharaoh: *"do not fear; I am with you both; I hear and I see"* (20:46). To the two in the cave, when Makkah's trackers reached the mouth: *"do not grieve, for God is with us"* (9:40). And here, to Lot before an armed crowd. The pattern is exact and the sūrah's Makkan audience were reciting it from memory, because they too had been told that a small number with God is not alone (cf. 8:65: *"if there are among you a hundred patient ones, they will overcome two hundred; and if there are a thousand, they will overcome two thousand by God's permission; and God is with the patient"*). Two fears are excluded by the formula: fear of what will happen (*lā takhaf*) and grief for what has happened (*lā taḥzan*) — the future and the past — and both are answered by the announcement of the one act that matters: *"we shall surely save you and your household."* Ibn Kathīr's gloss is characteristically direct: do not fear them, for we are your protectors, and do not grieve for what you are losing, for we are your recompensers.

**The Promise's Small Print, and Its Justice**

The sentence of deliverance carries its exception at once — *illā mraʾataka kānat mina'l-ghābirīn*, "except your wife; she is of those who remain behind" — and the sūrah's placement is severe and necessary. Lot's terror was for his guests; God's answer includes a roster of the saved that the prophet himself does not compile. On the sūrah's theology, the household is not a unit of salvation but a collection of individual accounts (29:12–13), and the believer cannot carry even a spouse. The repetition of the exception in the space of two verses (29:32, 29:33) is the Qur'an's way of making the point stick, and it belongs to the sūrah's larger argument about ties: kinship is a claim of kindness (29:8), never a claim on God (29:22), and the Day that sorts will not be moved by the composition of a household.

**What the Envoys' Presence Does to the Story**

Finally, notice the narrative mechanism the sūrah exploits: deliverance arrives in the form of guests the town tries to violate, and the city's destruction is thus caused by the very act that the believers most feared. The men of Sodom come to the house where their end has already been written, and their assault on the strangers is the execution of the sentence. This is the sūrah's answer to 29:4's presumption that the prosperous will not be overtaken: what looks like the persecutors' opportunity is the closing of their case, and what looks like the believer's extremity is the arrival of the remedy. Al-Ṭustarī's line on verse 2 — "affliction is a doorway between the people of knowledge and God" — is enacted rather than argued in this verse: Lot's straitened arm is the moment at which the angels are in his house.

**The Pedagogy of Bad News: How the Envoys Speak to a Man in Distress**

Read as a passage on crisis, the verse models an order of speech that the sūrah's own ethic recommends. The envoys do not open with the rescue: they first answer the man's two feelings — "do not fear, do not grieve" — then name the scope of what will be saved ("we shall deliver you and your people"), then state the exception that is not negotiable ("except his wife"), then fix the schedule ("your appointed time is in the dawn," 11:81), and only then leave him to act. Four moves, and each one answers something the distressed person cannot answer for himself: whether he is alone, whether the deliverance is total, whose case is outside it, and when it comes. The Qur'an elsewhere praises precisely this order as prophetic method: "and speak to him gentle words (qawlan layyinan)" (20:44) is said of Pharaoh, so that gentleness is not a courtesy owed to the compliant but a technique for the hardest case; and the promise that accompanies such speech is our verse's own — the sūrah of the Dawn pairs the two sentences the envoys embody: "surely with the hardship comes ease; surely with the hardship comes ease" (94:5–6), repeated once so that the hardship can never be counted as more numerous than the ease. And in the sūrah of the Cow the reward for exactly this kind of patient listening is the same triple as 29:33's ending: "those upon whom are blessings from their Lord and mercy, and those are the guided ones" (2:157) — blessing, mercy, guidance, the three things the straitened heart in our verse was promised in one breath.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:34

> **Truly we shall bring down upon the people of this town a torment from Heaven for having been iniquitous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**rijz: The Punishment Named After the Filth It Cleans**

*innā munzilūna ʿalā aḥli hādhihi'l-qaryati rijzan mina's-samāʾi bimā kānū yafsuqūn.* The word *rijz* is one of the Qur'an's most carefully used terms for punishment, and its field of meaning explains why it is chosen here. In classical Arabic *rijz* denotes foulness, defilement, and loathsome filth; in the Qur'an it names idolatry itself — *"avoid the defilement (rijz) of idols"* (22:30) — and intoxicants and gambling as *"the work of Satan's handiwork"* (5:90), and in the account of Badr it names the moral pollution that God cleanses from the believers' hearts (*"and He removes from you the defilement (rijz) of Satan"* — 8:11). When the punishment of a town is called *rijz*, the name is therefore not arbitrary: the scourge is called by the name of the foulness it was sent to remove, and al-Ṭabarī's report preserves the double sense — a punishment that is itself abhorrent, inflicted because of an abhorrence committed. The correspondence between the word for sin and the word for penalty is the sūrah's way of stating that the two belong to one moral order, not that God is retaliating in kind; as verse 40 will say: *God wronged them not, but themselves did they wrong.*

**"From the Sky": The Direction of the Descent**

*Munzilūna … mina's-samāʾ.* The verb is *anzala* — the same verb used of sending down the Book (29:51: *"have they not suffice them that We have sent down to you the Book that is recited to them?"*) and of the rain that revives the dead land (29:63: *"who sends down water from the sky and thereby gives life to the earth after its death"*). This is a structural detail of the first importance: the sky is the direction of both mercy and judgment, water and stones, revelation and ruin. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the sūrah's hearers could not have missed the pairing, since the same sky that Makkah's farmers and herders depended on for rain is the sky from which the *rijz* descends upon a town that had made its outrage customary.

**"Because They Used to Sin": The Verb of Departure**

*bi-mā kānū yafsuqūn.* The habitual imperfect with *kānū* describes not one act but a way of life; and the verb *fasaqa* is precise. Its root *f-s-q* means "to go out, to emerge," and the *fāsiq* is literally the one who has gone out — the lexicographers illustrate it with the date that breaks out of its skin (*fa-saqat min qishrihā*), and the same root gives *fuwaisiqah*, the little one that goes out of its hole, applied in the Prophet's ﷺ speech to the rat and the gecko (in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī's chapters on creation the gecko is named *fuwaysiqa* and its killing is commanded, and the "five vermin that transgress" — the kite, the crow, the scorpion, the rat and the fierce dog — are listed in the books of pilgrimage of the two Ṣaḥīḥs as creatures that may be killed even in the sanctuary). Theologically, the description fits the people of Lot exactly as this sūrah has presented them: they did not hold a wrong belief about God's existence — 29:61 and 29:63 will show that even Makkah's idolaters affirm the Creator — but had *exited* the bounds of what they knew. Fisq in the Qur'an is thus less about passion than about placement: the person who has left his position. This is why the sūrah's remedy, in the case of the believers, is emigration — a physical re-entry into a territory where the bounds can be kept (29:56), and why the Makkan polytheists' own word for their condition is "we found our fathers in it" (21:53) — they had gone out and settled in the going-out.

**The Sūrah's Restraint as a Moral Fact**

It is worth noticing what verse 34 does not do. There is no description of the bodies, no catalogue of the town's last hours, no narrative of the wife's turning back, none of the excesses that survive in the extra-Qur'anic reports. The sūrah states the sentence, its direction, and its cause, and moves on in the next verse to the "manifest sign." Al-Ṭabarī, in the course of his commentary on the parallel accounts, restricts the additional details to what the transmitted reports can bear, and Ibn Kathīr warns against the legends of the *qiṣaṣ* literature — that the stones had faces, that the town was turned upside down with its dogs still howling, that Lot's wife was struck at dawn as she climbed a hill. The restraint is doctrinally meaningful: a scripture that narrates disasters as moral argument refuses to narrate them as spectacle, and a community that consumes the punishment of others as entertainment has not understood the verse at all. The Qur'an's own statement of the purpose of such accounts is the sūrah's next line — a *sign*, "for a people who reason" — and the difference between a sign and a spectacle is simply whether the observer intends to change.

**The Question the Verse Poses to Its First Audience**

Lot's town perished because its corruption had become its public order, and the sūrah's hearers in Makkah were being told, in the middle of a passage about their own persecution, that their city's security was not evidence of its innocence. That is the verse's function in the sūrah's argument: it converts the Makkan *ḥaram* from an assurance into a question. The people of the town had, like Quraysh, a sanctuary, a trade, a council, and a tradition; and the sūrah says that none of those four things protected them from *rijz min al-samāʾ*. Al-Qurṭubī notes the same pairing in the sūrah's later verse 67, where Quraysh's own security is held up against their ungratefulness: *"have they not seen that We have made a sacred, secure sanctuary, while people are carried off around them?"* — and the Makkan believers, who had no protection and no wealth, were the intended comfort: the sanctuary that matters is the one God grants, and the *rijz* that comes from the sky is not deflected by walls. And the same verse carries the warning to every prosperous community afterwards, in a form the tradition's preachers used constantly: punishment is not withheld by a city's monuments, but only by the presence of the good and their intercession, which is why the next verse speaks of those "who reason" rather than of those who marvel.

**Sijjīl, the Stones, and the Sūrah's Nearest Neighbour**

*rijzin min sijjīl*: the second word is not native Arabic, and the lexicographers of the classical tradition knew it; the report transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās and quoted by the commentators derives it from a foreign word for a hard, baked clay, while others take it as a name for the extremity of the punishment rather than for its material. The dispute is worth recording briefly only because the exegetes did not settle it by philology but by the parallel accounts, where the Qur'an explains its own vocabulary: stones rained upon a town whose height was made its low (11:82; 15:74), and stones "marked at your Lord's side — and it is not far from the wrongdoing people" (11:83). So the rain is at once ordinary matter and an addressed letter, and the word's difficulty is no obstacle to the verse's point. The Makkan audience heard *sijjīl* in a second, nearer place: the sūrah of the Elephant, "did He not send against them birds in flocks, striking them with stones of baked clay (ḥijāratin min sijjīl)" (105:3–4), the one place in the Qur'an where the same stone-word is attached to Makkah's own deliverance. The connection to the section this sūrah has just finished is not decorative: the sanctuary that 29:67 cites as proof of God's care for this town was defended once before, from without, by stones from the sky — but Lot's town was destroyed from above by the same kind of rain, and the difference between the two weathers is not God's power but the towns' characters. Makkah had a prophet within it and was spared; the town of Lot had a prophet within it and was not, because what he could not persuade them to abandon was the public, chosen practice of their outrage — "truly its people are wrongdoers" (29:31), "for having been iniquitous" (29:34). What the two accounts share is a theology of direction: the stones come "from the sky," so that the punishment is neither tribal nor political, and, coming from above, it leaves a trace visible to the passer-by who was never in the town at all — which is precisely what the next verse affirms: "and We have left therein a manifest sign for a people who understand" (29:35).

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:35

> **And We indeed have left of it a manifest sign for a people who understand.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**taraknā: God as the Author of Vestiges**

*wa-la-qad taraknā minhā āyatay bayyinatin li-qawmin yaʿqilūn.* The verb *taraka* — "to leave behind, to abandon, to let remain" — is doing more work than the English "we have left" suggests. In Arabic the verb is used both for leaving a thing and for leaving a thing *to someone*: *taraktu'sh-shayʾa li-flān* is to bequeath it. God "leaves" a sign from a destroyed town in the sense both of letting it survive and of handing it over. The sign is thus a bequest of the dead to the living, and the sūrah's other instance uses the same construction with the same meaning: of Noah's deliverance, *"and We made it a sign for the worlds"* (29:15), where the survivors and the vessel together are what remains to be read. Between the two verses the sūrah has produced its full theory of historical evidence: one sign left from mercy (an ark), one left from judgment (a ruin), and both "for the worlds" or "for a people who understand." Al-Qurṭubī's summary is straightforward: God left from that town a manifest token — whether the ruins themselves, the water that covers them, or the story that will not die — so that whoever reflects may know.

**āya bayyina: A Sign That Is Also Argued From**

The adjective *bayyina* (from *b-y-n*, the root of *bayān*, clarity, and of *tabayyana*, to verify) makes the sign not merely visible but evidential, and the same root gives the sūrah its recurring demand for demonstration: *"bring your evidence (burhān) if you are truthful"* (2:111; cf. 27:64). This is a small but important corrective to a superficial reading in which "signs" are miraculous sights. The verse does not say "We left a marvel"; it says "We left a clear sign for a people who *reason*," and the verb *ʿaqala* (in *yaʿqilūn*) is the sūrah's own last word on how ruins are used. The Arabs' understanding of *ʿaql* is materially relevant: the word's earliest concrete sense is the rope that tethers a camel's knee (*ʿiqāl*), and from it comes the definition the lexicographers and moralists gave the intellect — that by which a person is *restrained* from what he should not do. ʿAlī's much-quoted saying in the *Nahj al-balāgha* and the parallel report from Ibn Masʿūd run: *"the intellect is what the Merciful is worshipped by and what the Garden is gained by"* — transmitted in the adab and ḥadīth literature (al-Ḥākim's *al-Kunā wa'l-Asmāʾ*; and it is cited approvingly by the moralists as a definition, not a prophetic ḥadīth). Read with that definition, verse 35 says: the ruin is a sign for people who can stop themselves. The failure it diagnoses is not a lack of data but a lack of restraint.
