**The Sūrah's Own Instruction on Visiting Ruins**

That the verse has tourism in its eye is confirmed by the Prophet's ﷺ practice when the caravan of believers passed through al-Ḥijr, the rock-cut tombs of the Thamūd on the north-western route, on the way to Tabūk. Al-Bukhārī (*Ṣaḥīḥ*, *Kitāb al-Salāt*, *Ahādīth al-Anbiyāʾ*) and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2980 transmit:

> *"Do not enter upon these people who were punished, unless you are weeping; and if you do not weep, then do not enter upon them, lest what befell them befall you."*

Two instructions are compressed in that sentence, and they define the Qur'anic etiquette of the historical site. The first forbids the neutral visit: to look at the ruin of the wicked without grief is to look at it wrongly, because the viewer is not a spectator of their fate but a candidate for it. The second converts the site into an instrument of self-knowledge: weeping at the spectacle is the sign that the sign has been read. The Makkan caravans of the sūrah's first audience passed these places twice a year, and the sūrah's travel-command — *"journey through the earth and see how the end of the deniers was"* (3:137; 29:20) — was not a metaphor for them; it was their itinerary. Verse 35 now tells them what the itinerary is for.

**"For a People Who Understand": The Audience Is Selected, Not Excluded**

The dative *li-qawmin yaʿqilūn* has been read in two ways, and the second is the more important. Taken restrictively, the verse says only rational people will benefit — the sign does not profit those who will not think. Taken inclusively, it says that this sign is *designed* to be intelligible: it is not a coded omen requiring a diviner's training, but an open fact about what happens to a society that turns its outrage into a custom, and anyone who thinks can reach the conclusion. The Qur'an's own framing of the *ʿāyāt* of history supports the second reading: *"how many a town did We destroy while it was unjust, and it has fallen in on its roof, and a ruined well, and a palace raised high? Do they not travel through the earth, so that they should have hearts to reason with, or ears to hear with? For it is not the eyes that are blinded, but the hearts within the breasts are blinded"* (22:45–46). The blindness the Qur'an diagnoses is thus not intellectual capacity but the refusal of the moral inference; and the *bayyina* is clear precisely because no special faculty is needed to read it — only honesty.

**The Verse's Place in the Sūrah's Argument, and in the Reader's Life**

Verse 35 performs the function that verse 24 had announced — *"in that are signs for a people who believe"* — and it closes the Lot-section (28–35) by converting a destroyed town into a permanent address. From here the sūrah moves to Shuʿayb and Midian, to ʿĀd and Thamūd, and to Korah, Pharaoh and Haman (36–40), each of which the sūrah likewise grounds in something its audience could see: *"their fate is certainly evident to you from their dwellings"* (29:38). The accumulation is deliberate and its rhetoric is inductive: one case is a story, several cases are a law. And for the reader today, the verse's demand is not antiquarian curiosity nor sentimental awe but method. Anyone who has watched a prosperous institution dismiss an obvious warning has seen the psychology of verse 4 and verse 34 side by side, and anyone who has read, carefully and without self-exemption, the account of an organisation or a society that normalised what it had once known to be wrong has read a *āya bayyina* in the sense intended here. The verse promises that such reading is not wasted: the past was left on purpose, and it was left to be understood.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:36

> **And unto Midian, [We sent] their brother Shuʿayb; he said, “O my people! Worship God and hope for the Last Day, and behave not wickedly upon the earth, working corruption.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Their Brother": The Messenger's Citizenship**

*wa-ilā Madyana akhāhum Shuʿayban.* The phrase *akhāhum* — "their brother" — is a formula the Qur'an attaches to each of these missions (7:65 of Hūd, 7:73 of Ṣāliḥ, 7:85 of Shuʿayb, 11:50, 61, 78, 84, 26:105–168), and its repetition is argument. The messenger is not a foreign authority imposing an alien code; he is of the people, speaks their language, knows their trade, and shares their ancestry — and the claim to authority is therefore not one of pedigree but of commission. The Quraysh, whose entire self-respect was genealogical, could not dismiss this claim as the importation of a foreign god, and the sūrah's own audience was being told a second thing: if a man of your own blood tells you that your practices are corrupt, the objection "he is one of us, so he knows nothing" is void, and so is the contrary inference "he is one of us, so he must be wrong." Shuʿayb's brotherhood also explains the tone of the verses that follow, in which the same phrase recurs on his lips: *"O my people, do not let your quarrel with me strike you with the like of what struck the people of Noah or the people of Hūd or the people of Ṣāliḥ"* (11:89) — the appeal of an insider who has standing precisely because he has nothing to gain.

**Midian: The Commercial Town and Its Sin**

Midian (Madyan) was a region and a people of the north-western Arabian and southern Levantine corridor, on the caravan road that Quraysh itself used, and the exegetes and geographers accordingly locate its towns along the routes of trade. Its prophet's people are named in the Qur'an for a sin different from Sodom's: not sexual outrage but the corruption of exchange — *"do not diminish people's goods (wa-lā tabkhasū an-nāsa fī arḍihim mufsidīn)"* (7:85); *"give full measure and weight, and do not deprive people of their due"* (11:85); *"Woe to those who give short measure, who, when they take a measure from people, take it in full; but when they measure or weigh for them, give less. Do such people not consider that they will be raised for a Day tremendous — the Day on which mankind will stand before the Lord of the worlds?"* (83:1–6). The last of these verses is the key to the whole episode: the sūrah of *al-Muṭaffifīn* places a cheating scale and a Resurrection in the same breath, and so does ours. Fraud is not a lesser sin because it is polite; it is a denial of the Last Day in the form of an arithmetic.

**The Sermon in Three Clauses, and Why It Is a Whole Religion**

*yaqāli qawmi-ʿbudū Allāha wa-rjū'1-yawma'l-ākhira wa-lā taʿthaw fī'l-arḍi mufsidīn* — in the sūrah's Arabic the people are addressed with three imperatives, and the three are not a random sample. (1) *ʿUbudū Allāh* — the vertical axis: the oneness of God, the same opening Abraham used in 29:16, since the sūrah's prophets all preach a single creed in different towns. (2) *wa-rjū'ū'l-yawma'l-ākhir* — "and hope for the Last Day." The verb *rajāʾ*, hope, is chosen rather than fear, and that choice is the sūrah's own insight: belief in the Last Day in the Qur'an is not primarily dread but *expectation of being paid*, which is exactly the psychological remedy for a people who cheat, because the cheat's premise is that the ledger ends here. A man who short-weights a customer has assumed there is no reckoning; the verse's command is to *hope* for that reckoning as one hopes for a payday. (3) *wa-lā taʿthaw fī'l-arḍi mufsidīn* — "do not go about in the land, corrupting": the horizontal axis, expressed by a verb of motion (*ʿa-tā*, to go forth) that makes the corruption itinerant, professional, on the road. The three clauses describe the same man in three relations, and their order states the Qur'anic priority: God, then the horizon of consequences, then conduct.

**yāṭfifūn and the Ethics of the Measure**

The word for the Midianites' crime in the parallel passage, *ṭaṭfīf*, is from *ṭaff*, the lightness or scantiness of a thing, and *muṭaffif* is the one who lets the measure come out light — the etymology is a piece of social observation: the cheat's crime is not in what he takes but in the *thinness* of what he gives, and the Qur'an's word for it names the quality of the act rather than its violence. Note that this sūrah's verse 36 does not name the scales; it names the corruption in general (*mufsidīn*), the same word Lot used for his own people (29:30). The sūrah is drawing a line from Sodom to Midian: two cities with different vices and the same structure — a practice that harms others, a public that normalises it, a warning that is mocked, and a destruction recorded as a sign. This is why verse 37 can give Midian the same sentence it gave Thamūd, and why the Qur'an's ethic of the marketplace is treated as a matter of *ʿaqīda*: the classical ḥadīth literature on trade is severe in exactly this spirit — *"do not go out to meet the caravan [to buy goods before they reach the market]"* — the *tanājush* and *najāsh* prohibitions of *Kitāb al-Buyūʿ* (al-Bukhārī; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1515), which forbid the dealer from intercepting a seller's goods before the public price is known, *"the honest, trustworthy merchant is with the prophets, the truthful, and the martyrs"* (al-Tirmidhī 1209, from Abū Saʿīd; graded ḥasan), and the warning *"taking oaths makes the goods sell, and wipes out the blessing"* (al-Bukhārī, *Kitāb al-Buyūʿ*; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1600) — a sentence whose whole force is that a lawful transaction can be made unlawful by the manner of closing it.

**"Hope for the Last Day": The Doctrine Against Nihilism and Against Cynicism**

The middle clause of Shuʿayb's sermon repays the most attention, because the Makkan audience's pressure was to give up, not to cheat. A community being impoverished, boycotted, and expelled can conclude either that consequence is meaningless or that their own endurance is unrewarded, and the sūrah answers by putting "hope for the Last Day" in the mouth of a prophet addressing a town of swindlers: the Day is the day the small measures are made up, the debts are settled, and the patience of the cheated is counted. This is the same promise the sūrah will state in its own voice — *"whoever hopes for the meeting with God, God's term is coming"* (29:5), and *"those who believe and do righteous deeds, We shall surely absolve them of their evil deeds and recompense them by the best of what they used to do"* (29:7). Ibn Taymiyyah's treatment of *rajāʾ* in his *Kitāb al-ʿIlm*-adjacent writings and in the creedal works stresses the practical sense: hope for the Last Day is not the wish that it comes, but the decision to live as though it is already being recorded.

**The Verse's Social Diagnosis, and Why It Ends in Destruction**

Finally, note the sūrah's choice of what to accuse Midian of. Its sin was not a crime of passion but a corruption of trust — the kind of evil that has paperwork, that is conducted in public with the vocabulary of custom and contract, and that prospers for a long time without visibly hurting anyone except the person with no lawyer, no weighbridge, and no market power. The Qur'an names that kind of evil with a general term: *ifsād*. And the punishment of verse 37 — the shaking that leaves them crouching in their own houses — answers to the crime with a precision the sūrah's hearers would not miss: a people who made the road unsafe for strangers were struck in the most private place they had, and a people who made the scales untrustworthy found the ground itself unreliable. Al-Rāzī, on 29:36–37, notes this as a general feature of the Qur'an's punishment narratives: the penalty is the sin's own logic carried one step further, which is why they are "signs" rather than arbitrary acts of power — and why 29:40 can conclude, as the sūrah does, that God wronged them not.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:37

> **But they denied him, and so the earthquake seized them, and morning found them lying lifeless in their abode;**

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Sentence for a Civilisation**

*fa-kadhdhabūhu fa-akhadhathumu'r-rajfatu fa-aṣbaḥū fī dārihim jāthimīn.* Four clauses, twenty-two letters, and an entire people. The sūrah's style here is the rhetorical figure the Arabic critics call *ījāz maʿa'l-ḥashr* — brevity that collects — and the effect is not a shortage of content but a surplus of verdict: when a matter is finished, the telling is short. Compare the treatment of the same people in 7:91 and 11:94, which add only the detail that the punishment came in the morning. The Makkan audience, whose poets could spend a whole *qaṣīda* on a ruined camp-site, hear an entire nation dispatched in the space of a verse, and the disproportion is the point: the value of a people is not measured by the length of their monuments but by the fact that a single verb ended them.

**rajfa: The Ground That Refuses to Hold**

The word *rajfa* is from *r-j-f*, the violent shaking of a thing, and the Qur'an uses it for earthquakes — as in the verse of the seventy men whom Mūsā chose for the appointed time: *"and when the rajfa seized them, he said: my Lord, had You willed, You would have destroyed them before, and me as well?"* (7:155) — and the related *ṣāʿiqa*, the thunderbolt that struck the Israelites *"while you were watching"* (2:56). The same root is reserved, in the Qur'an's eschatology, for the convulsion of the Day itself: *"when the earth is shaken with its shaking, and the earth brings out its burden, and the human says: what is the matter with it? — that Day it will tell its news"* (99:1–4). Two things follow from the choice. First, the Midianites' crime was committed on the ground — they "went about in the land, corrupting" (29:36) and sat on the roads threatening travellers (7:86) — and the ground was the instrument of their end. Second, the earthquake is a punishment that cannot be resisted by any of the resources the powerful trust: walls, numbers, cavalry, elevation. It comes from beneath, and the sūrah had said in 29:22 that neither on earth nor in heaven can anyone frustrate God; verse 37 is the earth's half of that statement, and verse 34 the sky's. Al-Qurṭubī notes that Midian's fate is described in the same words as Thamūd's — *fa-akhadhathumu'r-rajfatu* / *aṣ-ṣayḥatu… jāthimīn* (7:73, 78; 11:67, 94) — and that the shared language of the two is itself the sūrah's claim that these are not isolated legends but one law applied twice.

**jāthimīn: The Camel That Kneels and Does Not Rise**

The participle *jāthim* is one of the most visual words in the Qur'an's punishment vocabulary. In Arabic *jathama* is of the camel folding its forelegs beneath it and settling at the place of water — the *juthūm* being the act of squatting — and lexicographers such as Abū ʿUbayda and others record that *jāthim* in poetry means a corpse, precisely because the dead man is compared to the beast that kneels and never rises, or to one thrown down on his face. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Qurṭubī both preserve the two images: they fell like kneeling camels, or lay prostrate on their faces, and the second reading carries the humiliation of posture. The scene the sūrah thus paints is not a battlefield but a household: people in their own homes, in the position of animals that have lain down to rest, and — the detail that stops the reader — not getting up. The phrase *fī dārihim*, "in their abode," completes the irony: the *dār* is the place of safety, and Midian's was the place of death; this sūrah's own verse 67 will speak of Makkah as *"a secure sanctuary, while people are carried off around them,"* and the audience could not fail to hear the parallel, which is the sūrah's implicit argument to Quraysh: a *dār* is safe only while the God who made it is obeyed in it.

**ṣabāḥa: The Morning Motif**

*fa-aṣbaḥū* — "and they came to the morning." The Qur'an's destruction narratives cluster at daybreak: Lot's town at sunrise (*"We rained upon them a rain — how was the rain of those who had been warned!"* — 26:173, and 15:73 *muṣriqīn*, "as the sun rose"), and the people of the ruined towns, whom the Qur'an says the living pass by daily: *"you pass by them in the morning and by night — will you not then reason?"* (37:137–138). Al-Ṭabarī, collecting these, notes that the morning is when people are most confident: tasks arranged, doors opened, the day's business begun. To be "found in the morning" is to have been left in the middle of one's plans. In Arabic the verb *aṣbaḥa* has also become a copula ("they became", "they were found"), and the sūrah exploits both senses at once: they *entered the morning*, and they *were* — permanently — what they had become. The psychology the device creates is the reader's: the catastrophe of the unrepentant is placed at the very hour of their security, which is the sūrah's answer to 29:4's "do they suppose they will outstrip?"

**"They Denied Him": The Crime the Verse Actually Records**

Notice that the verse's first clause is not about immorality. Midian's sin, in the sūrah's summary here, is *takdhīb* — the denial of the messenger — and this is the sūrah's consistent ordering. It said of Noah's people "they were wrongdoers" (29:14), of Lot's "because they used to sin" (29:34), and of Pharaoh, Haman and Korah that "they waxed arrogant" (29:39); but the operative verb of destruction is always the same, as in the sūrah's own later summary: *"who does greater wrong than one who fabricates a lie against God or denies the truth when it comes to him?"* (29:68). The moral of the catalogue is not that God punishes particular vices, but that He ends a society that has settled on rejecting the truth — the vices are the symptoms, and the *takdhīb* is the diagnosis. For the Makkan believers who were being asked to stay loyal to a man their city had declared a liar, this is the sūrah's strongest support: their neighbours' sin was precisely the sin that had destroyed Midian, and the sequence in verses 36–40 shows it running from denial to ruin in four steps.

**The Verse's Restraint and Its Purpose**

One more feature is worth naming, because it disciplines the reader. The sūrah says the earthquake seized them and that they lay in their homes; it does not describe how they died, whether they called out, or what the survivors — there being none — left behind. Its interest is in the *fact* and the *cause*, not in the scene; and its very next verse will make the same structure visible in the landscape for anyone who travels (29:38). This is the difference between the Qur'an's treatment of catastrophe and the ancient Near-Eastern annalistic and legend-making literature that surrounds it, in which divine punishment is elaborated into spectacle. Al-Bayhaqī and the historians transmit details from the older narratives about Midian — that Shuʿayb was blind in one eye, that his people were struck by a heat so intense they sought shade and found none, that a cloud consumed them — and the exegetical rule, stated by al-Ṭabarī for these *isrāʾīliyyāt*, is that they may be recited, not believed, and never used as proof. What the sūrah gives instead is a single, checkable pattern: a people, a warning, a denial, a seizure, and a ruin that later travellers pass. The pattern is the sign; everything else is scenery.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:38

> **and ʿĀd, and Thamūd; [their fate] is certainly evident to you from their dwellings. Satan made their deeds seem fair unto them and thus turned them from the way, though they had been discerning;**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Evidence in Real Estate: "Evident to You From Their Dwellings"**

*wa-ʿādan wa-thamūda wa-qad tabayyana lakum min masākinihim.* The verb *tabayyana* — to become clear, distinct, verified, as in 2:187 *"until the white thread becomes distinct (yatabayyana) to you from the black thread of dawn"* — turns the past into something a person can *check*. The sūrah's claim is archaeological in the plain sense: you know about them because you pass their houses. Makkah's caravans on the northern route went through and past the territory of Thamūd at al-Ḥijr (Hegra, Madaʾin Ṣāliḥ), whose monumental rock-cut façades are among the most striking surviving works of the Nabataean world, and the region of al-Aḥqāf in the south-east was associated in Arab memory with ʿĀd of "Iram of the pillars, the like of which had not been created in the lands" (89:7–8) — a people whose self-description was the scale of their building: *"do you build on every high place a monument for amusement, and take palaces as though you were to abide forever?"* (26:128–129). The sūrah's argument from their *masākin* is thus the sūrah's argument from their architecture: the buildings survived the builders, and their survival is the proof. Verse 20 had commanded the journey, and verse 38 says the journey's evidence is already under your feet.

**What the Dwellings Say**

Read closely, the two peoples' buildings are used for opposite lessons, and the sūrah knows it. ʿĀd were destroyed *in spite of* the magnitude of their works: *"as for ʿĀd, they were destroyed by a screaming, violent wind which He imposed upon them for seven nights and eight days in succession, so you would see the people therein destroyed as though they were hollow trunks of palms. Do you see any remnant of them?"* (69:6–8) — and Thamūd, who "hewed the mountains with skill" (26:149), were taken by a cry. Magnitude therefore did not save, and skill did not save; what remains is the stone and the silence, which is precisely the answer to those in Makkah who reasoned from the prestige of their sanctuary. The rhetorical question of 69:8 — *"do you see any remnant (bāqiya) of them?"* — is the sūrah's own gloss on "evident from their dwellings": the dwellings remain; the people do not. That is why the Prophet's ﷺ etiquette for such sites was grief, not admiration: *"do not enter upon these people who were punished unless you are weeping"* (al-Bukhārī; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2980). And on that march at al-Ḥijr, the tradition adds that water was drawn from their wells for the animals and dough kneaded there, and the Prophet ﷺ ordered that the provisions taken from it be given as fodder to the camels — a practical measure of ritual concern about the water of a punished place, reported in the Ṣaḥīḥ accounts of that journey.

**tazyīn: The Adornment of the Ugly**

*wa-zayyana lahumu'sh-shayṭānu aʿmālahum fa-ṣaddahum ʿani's-sabīli wa-kānū mubṭirīn.* The verb *zayyana*, from *zīna*, "adornment," names the mechanism the Qur'an regards as the fundamental one in moral failure: not the forcing of an act, but the *presentation* of an act as beautiful. The devil does not make men do what they hate; he makes what is ugly look like something worth doing. The Qur'an's account of the same phenomenon in Saba is nearly verbatim: *"and Satan made their deeds fair-seeming to them, so that he turned them from the way, and they are not guided"* (27:24) — and the principle is stated as a general law elsewhere: *"and thus did the partners of the polytheists make fair-seeming to many of those who associated the killing of their children, in order to ruin them and to confuse for them their religion"* (6:137); *"do you consider one whose evil deed has been made fair-seeming to him and who sees it as beautiful?"* (35:8); *"We have made fair-seeming to every community its own deed"* (6:108). Ibn Kathīr's gloss on 29:38 is the practical one: Satan adorned for them their disbelief and their rejection of the messengers, and made them regard it as a credit, and their own excellence, and their forefathers' tradition. The word of the source commentary — "they considered disbelief and the rejection of the messengers to be a mark of their excellence" — identifies the exact mechanism: adornment works by *attaching a virtue-label* to a vice. A people does not call itself corrupt; it calls itself modern, strong, ancestral, or sensible.

**ṣadd: The Blocked Road**

The next verb is *ṣadda* — to turn away, to bar, to obstruct — and the sūrah has chosen the vocabulary of roads to describe the work of Satan: he does not only persuade, he blocks. The same verb is used of those who "turn people away from God's path, seeking to make it crooked" (7:45, 7:86), of the devil who vows "I will sit for them on Your straight path, then come at them from before and behind" (7:16–17), and of God's making for every prophet "an enemy among the criminals" (25:31). The Midianites and the Thamūd were also a road-people, who cut the road and sat on the highways to intimidate (7:86), and the sūrah's image matches: as they obstructed travellers, they were obstructed from the path. The mechanism of obstruction in the Qur'an is almost always internal, by adornment, rather than external, by coercion: "he has no authority over those who believe and put their trust in their Lord; his authority is only over those who take him as master" (41:99–100) — and the verse in which Satan addresses the assembled on the Day is the definitive statement of how little the "adornment" compelled:

> *"And Satan will say, when the matter has been decided: God promised you a promise of truth, and I promised you and failed you. And I had no authority over you, except that I called you and you responded to me. So do not blame me; blame yourselves."* (Qur'an 14:22)

That is the sūrah's own answer to the objection that *tazyīn* excuses the sinner: the devil's plea is that he only invited, and the verse that precedes ours says the same of the punished people, since it records the adornment *and* their discerning.

**mubṭirīn: The Camel That Strays Into the Wadi**

The final word of the verse is one of the Arabic language's most vivid moral images, and the source's translation ("though they had been discerning") reflects a real difficulty in it. *Mubṭir* is from *baṭar*: recklessness, arrogance that over-looks danger, the conduct of an animal that has thrown off its restraint. The Arab grammarians and the exegetes of al-Ṭabarī's transmission explain the word with an image: *baṭarat al-bākira* — the young she-camel, frightened by a sound or a shadow, bolts away from her pasture and drives straight into a wadi to her death, in full sight of everything, with her eyes open. Ibn ʿAbbās is quoted in the tafsīr literature glossing "mubṭirīn" as *mutaghāffilīn* — those who affect heedlessness, who can see and choose not to see. On that reading, the sūrah's clause is a single word for a condition modern psychology calls *willful ignorance* or *motivated blindness*: the power to discern is not absent, it is declined. The exegetes who read the word as "ruined, cast into destruction" (the other early gloss: *hālikīn*) and those who read it as "arrogant" both preserve the point: the bolted camel is destroyed and to blame, and the sūrah does not consider these two facts a contradiction.

**The Verse's Application, and Its Cure**

Verse 38 is the sūrah's most general claim about how communities fall, and its structure gives the diagnosis in three moves: the visible evidence (their dwellings), the internal mechanism (adornment), and the culpability of the discerning (baṭar). Every later reader of a fallen institution can run the same analysis: what was once recognised as wrong was re-labelled as normal, then as admirable; the road was blocked not by a tyrant but by a culture; and the people concerned were not stupid. The sūrah's own cure is also contained in the sequence, and it is the same as verse 9's: since *tazyīn* works by association — through the assembly, the *nadwā*, the market, the school — the antidote is a different company, the righteous (29:9), together with a different standard of beauty. For the Qur'an the standard is not the community's approval but God's: *"do you consider one whose evil deed has been made fair-seeming to him…?"* (35:8) is answered only by a person whose taste has been re-trained by revelation, which is why the sūrah pairs its histories of adornment with the command to establish prayer (29:45), the one act that repeatedly re-attaches beauty to what is actually beautiful.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:39

> **and Korah, and Pharaoh, and Hāmān! Indeed, Moses brought them clear proofs, but they waxed arrogant upon the earth, and outstripped [Us] not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Trio: Money, Office, and the State**

*wa-Qārūna wa-Firʿawna wa-Hāmān.* The verse completes the catalogue of verse 38 by naming three individuals instead of two tribes, and the order is startling. In the Qur'an's other lists the king usually comes first: *"God struck a parable for those who disbelieve… and for those who believe, Pharaoh's wife"* (66:11); *"we sent to Pharaoh a messenger"* (73:15). Here the financier is named before the tyrant, and the minister of works third. The three are, in the sūrah's own vocabulary, the three powers that make a tyranny possible: wealth that buys, a state that commands, and an administration that builds the propaganda — for Haman is presented in the Qur'an as the official who is ordered to fire the clay and raise the tower (28:38), and as the one to whom Pharaoh delegates: *"Haman, light a fire for me over the clay and make me a tower"* (28:38), and in 40:36 the same charge is addressed to "Haman" again while the nobles are called *al-malaʾ*. Al-Ṭabarī records that Haman here is a man of Pharaoh's court, the overseer of building and stone-quarrying, and he is explicit that this is not the Haman of the Purim story in Esther; a coincidence of names in translation has led some modern readers astray, and the exegetical tradition's caution should be retained. The effect of the verse, then, is a structural analysis of oppression rather than a list of villains: a Qārūn provides the money, a Pharaoh the authority, a Hāmān the works.

**Qārūn: The Man Whose Keys Were Heavy**

The Qur'an's portrait of Korah is one of its most complete case-studies of wealth, and it belongs beside this verse. *"Qārūn was of Moses's people, but he treated them with tyranny, and We had given him of the treasures such that their keys were a burden to a group of strong men. When his people said to him: do not exult, for God does not love the exultant — and seek, in what God has given you, the abode of the Hereafter, and do not forget your portion of this world, and do good as God has been good to you, and do not seek corruption in the earth, for God does not love the corrupters"* (28:76–77). Three features of that account should be carried into 29:39. First, Qārūn is *min qawmihi* — from within the community, not outside it: the corruption of a people is usually funded by their own. Second, the wealth is described in a way that makes the body do the moral thinking — the *keys* themselves were too heavy for strong men (an image al-Rāzī treats as deliberate hyperbole that teaches that the value lay in the chests, whose keys alone exhausted the bearers). Third, the counsel given to him is the soundest economic advice in the Book: seek the next abode *in* what you have been given, do not forget your portion of this world, and do not seek corruption — a triad in which world-enjoyment, otherworldliness, and social responsibility are not competing claims but one instruction. And when he answered *"I was given it only on account of knowledge I have,"* the Qur'an replied with a question and a verb: *"does he not know that God had destroyed before him generations who were mightier than he in strength and richer in accumulation? … So We caused the earth to swallow him and his dwelling, and there was for him no group to help him against God, and he was not of those who avert themselves"* (28:78, 81). Qārūn is thus the verse's proof that wealth is neither God's approval nor a defence: the same earth he stood on took him.

**Pharaoh and Hāmān: The Tower, and the Refutation of the "Sign" Argument**

*fa-ghayyara* — the tower of 28:38 was to be a piece of engineering in the service of theology: *"make me a tower, so that I may reach the means — the means of the heavens — and look upon the God of Moses; and I do think he is a liar."* The sūrah's point about that project is not that it failed but that its premise was absurd: a god to be inspected from a height is a god already inside Pharaoh's own category of things. That is the exact epistemology of idolatry the sūrah has been arguing since 29:16–17 and 29:38 — the attempt to place the divine among the items available to inspection. And the response God gave to Pharaoh, in the sūrah's own summary here, was not a miracle-destroying tower but the *clear proofs* sent to him: *"Indeed, Moses brought them clear proofs (al-bayyināt)"* — the staff, the white hand, the years of drought, the flood, the locusts, the lice, the frogs, and the blood (7:130–133), each of which the Qur'an says the Egyptians "waxed arrogant" over (7:133; 10:75; 23:46; 28:39). Ibn Kathīr notes the lesson: the verse removes the excuse of the unlearned, since clear proofs were present, and the refusal of them is what the sūrah calls *istikbār*, arrogance. The evidence was not insufficient; the disposition was corrupt.

**istikbār: The Sin the Three Share**

*fa-stakbarū fī'l-arḍ.* One verb covers the financier, the king and the minister, and the choice is precise: not *kufr* but *istikbār* — the claim to be above. In the Qur'an this is the primal sin, older than human history: *"and when We said to the angels, 'bow to Adam,' they bowed, except Iblīs — he refused and was arrogant (istakbara), and he was of the disbelievers"* (2:34); and it is the sin that makes evidence inert: *"when there came to them what they had already recognised, they disbelieved in it"* (2:89). The definition of pride the Prophet ﷺ gave is the most exact commentary on 29:39:

> *"Pride is rejecting the truth and despising people."* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 91, from ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd; and in the same ḥadīth a man asked: "what about a man who loves that his garment and his sandal be handsome?" He said: *"God is beautiful and loves beauty; pride is rejecting the truth and scorning people."*)

That definition explains the verse's moral unity: Qārūn's "I was given it on account of my knowledge" is the same act as Pharaoh's "I know no god for you besides me" (28:38), and both are the rejection of truth plus the contempt of those who bring it — the financier's contempt for his people, the king's for a slave with a staff. The ḥadīth's second clause, about beautiful clothing, belongs here for a reason: pride in the Qur'an is not about the appearance of goods but about the position of the self.

**"And They Did Not Outstrip" — The Verse's Frame and the Sūrah's**

*wa-mā kānū sābiqīn.* These three words close the sūrah's longest argument and repeat its earlier thesis verbatim: *"do those who do evil deeds suppose that they will outstrip (yasbiqu) Us? Evil is the judgment they make!"* (29:4). The ring is exact and the technique is classical composition: the claim of verse 4 is quoted back at verse 39 after the evidence, so that the reader's memory is audited by the argument rather than flattered by it. Al-Rāzī draws out the sense of *sābiq* here — the racing metaphor in which the pursued is left behind — and states the theology: they were not unable to be caught; they were never able to run. The phrase also answers the Makkan audience's specific temptation. Their enemies were prosperous, mobile, fortified, and unharmed by the drought that was itself one of the proofs (and cf. 44:15, where the respite after the threat of the smoke is named: "We shall lift the punishment a little, but you are returning"), and the sūrah insists, in one clause, that the delay of seizure is not a failure of pursuit: *"and let not the disbelievers suppose that they have escaped — they cannot frustrate Us"* (3:178); and 68:44–45, *"deal with them, for their affair is with God… and let them enjoy themselves; your punishment is coming"* — leave them to it, for what they do is before their eyes (52:28–29; cf. 6:44, 29:53). The verse's past tense, "they did not outstrip," is the sūrah's verdict, already written, over the men who could not yet see it.

**The Verse as an Analysis of Every Prosperous Corruption**

Finally, the reason this triad still reads as current affairs is that its structure recurs: a concentration of wealth that produces a claim to superiority, a state that treats itself as the final authority, and an administrative apparatus whose function is to make the arrangement look inevitable. The Qur'an's diagnosis attaches to each of the three the same verb and the same end, and its warning is therefore not directed only at individuals of extreme wealth or power but at the *arrogance* available to anyone with leverage — a point that the ḥadīth's definition makes unavoidable, since "scorning people" is accessible to a man with a better garment. The Makkan believer who had nothing was being told two things at once: the three tyrants of the age were, in the Qur'an's account, already finished (29:39), and the same verdict had been prepared for anyone who, having been given clear proofs, chose to be above them.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:40

> **Each We seized for his sin. Among them are some upon whom We sent a torrent of stones, and among them are some whom the Cry seized, and among them are some whom We caused the earth to engulf, and among them are some whom We drowned. God wronged them not, but themselves did they wrong.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict of the Whole Passage in One Sentence**

*kullan akhadhnāhu bi-dhanbihi.* Everything the sūrah has been assembling since verse 14 — Noah's millennium and a half, Abraham's fire, Lot's town, Midian's morning, the dwellings of ʿĀd and Thamūd, and the trio of Korah, Pharaoh and Haman — is drawn together in one clause with an accusative of distribution: *each one, We took*. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the economy of the construction: the *kull* distributed over plural subjects, with the different punishments listed in the following clauses, allows a single divine verb to govern the entire history of the punished, so that the sūrah can say in eight words what a chronicle would need chapters to say. And the preposition is *bi-dhanbihi*, "because of his sin," not "because of their sins" — each was taken for what *he* did, which is the principle the sūrah laid down in verse 12 and 13: burdens are not transferred, and here, at the end of the list, they are not shared either.

**The Four Elements: A Deliberate Catalogue of Creation's Means**

*fa-minhum man arsalnā ʿalayhi ḥāṣibatan, wa-minhum man akhadhathu ṣ-ṣayḥatu, wa-minhum man khasafnā bihi'l-arḍ, wa-minhum man aghraqnā.* Four verbs, four media, and — as the classical commentators and the source's own note observe — the whole repertoire of physical destruction available to the created world: stones from the sky (the *ḥāṣibah* sent on Lot's people — *"We rained upon them stones of layered baked clay"* — 11:82, 15:74; and cf. 17:69, where God threatens to send over the ungrateful a *ḥāṣib*, a storm of pebbles), the *ṣayḥa*, the Cry, a sound (Thamūd and Midian — 11:67, 94), the swallowing of the earth (Qārūn — 28:81), and water (Pharaoh and Haman — 28:40; 10:90; and, in the wider narrative, Noah's people — 71:25). The rhetorical effect of the list is not mere variety: it demonstrates that no element is loyal to a sinner. Air, sound, ground and sea — the four media of life — become four instruments of ending, and the sūrah's hearers, whose survival depended on the same four (the trade winds, the caravan's calls, the wells, the Red Sea route), are told that the world is not a neutral stage but a set of servants with an owner. The choice of *ḥāṣib* for stones is telling in itself: *ḥaṣab* is gravel and small pebbles, and the word suggests that what destroyed a town was not boulders but ordinary, countable grit — the smallest things do the largest work when God sends them, which is why the same sūrah chose a spider and a fly for its parables (29:41; 22:73).

**Why the Passive and the Active Alternate**

The grammar of the verse deserves a moment. In two of the four clauses God is the explicit agent (*arsalnā, aghraqnā, khasafnā bihi*), and in two the punishment itself is the grammatical subject: *akhadhathu ṣ-ṣayḥatu* — "the Cry seized him," and, earlier, *fa-akhadhathumu'r-rajfatu* (29:37). Arabic rhetorical analysis treats such alternation as deliberate: the created instrument is sometimes made the subject to indicate the swiftness and autonomy of the event as experienced — the Cry "took" them, as a beast takes its prey — while the divine first-person verbs make the same fact a personal act of judgment. Al-Rāzī, who notices these shifts constantly, uses them in exactly this way: the Qur'an alternates between naming the means and naming the Agent so that the reader never attributes the event to nature alone nor to an arbitrary will alone. This is, in miniature, the Qur'an's whole doctrine of causation: the causes are real, and they are not independent.

**"God Wronged Them Not, but Themselves Did They Wrong"**

*wa-mā ẓalama-humu'llāhu wa-lākin kānū anfusa-hum yaẓlimūn.* The clause is the sūrah's theodicy in ten words, and its Arabic construction places the object of the wronging in the emphatic position: not "they wronged" but "*themselves* were they wronging." The verb *ẓalama* means to put a thing in a place that is not its own, to do wrong by *placing wrongly*; and the claim that God "did not wrong them" therefore denies a specific charge — that the punishments were mis-aimed, that the doses exceeded the offence, that the respite was a trick. The sūrah has already built each of the four cases to exclude this: Noah's people had nine centuries and fifty years (29:14); Lot's town was warned in the presence of a prophet and his household was extracted (29:32–33); Midian heard from "their brother" (29:36); Korah was counselled by his own people (28:77); Pharaoh was given nine signs (17:101) and the choice ("let him who wills believe and who wills disbelieve" — 18:29). The Qur'an elsewhere puts the same conclusion in the mouths of the punished: *"and they will cry out therein: our Lord, bring us out — we will act righteously, unlike what we used to do"* (35:37), and in the verse that pairs most exactly with ours: *"and your Lord is not at all unjust to the servants"* (41:46), and *"the word is not changed with Me, and I am not at all unjust to the servants"* (50:29). The tradition's anchor-text on the point is the ḥadīth qudsī in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2577), which also illuminates this sūrah's sixth verse:

> *"O My servants, I have made wrongdoing forbidden to Myself and have made it forbidden among you, so do not wrong one another."*

It belongs to the same argument as 29:40, and the classical exegetes use it to shut the door on any theology in which suffering is a divine caprice: injustice is not merely absent from God's conduct; it is excluded from the very conception of His.

**The Children, the Bystanders, and the Limits of the Verse**

Any honest reading of this verse must face the question it provokes, and the tradition faces it. If each was taken "for his sin," what of those in the towns who had no sins — children, and the uninvolved? The sūrah's own resources answer in three lines. (1) The Qur'an states the general principle that God destroys *no* town except one whose people are wrongdoers (28:59) and never while its people are reformers (11:117), so that the destruction is always moral, not indiscriminate. (2) The Qur'an also states that God does not punish a people until He has sent a messenger (17:15, 28:59), so that "their sin" includes the refusal of a warning that reached them. (3) As for those who die in such events without having reached the age or the means of accountability, the tradition's principle is that judgment of them is reserved: when the Prophet ﷺ was asked about the children of polytheists he said *"God knows best what they would have been doing"* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1384), and in the eschatological test of them, reported in Aḥmad and others with disputed chains, they are tested in the fire and enter it or come out of it — a matter the scholars did not build doctrine upon. What is not permitted, in this sūrah's terms, is to conclude from a catastrophe that God has been unjust; the verse's own sentence "but themselves did they wrong" is meant to be read as the sūrah's last word about *those it is describing*, not as a general theory of every death.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Pivot to the Parable**

Structurally, 29:40 is where the argument of verses 2–40 is complete, and 29:41, the spider, follows as the *moral* of the catalogue rather than as a new subject. The sūrah has shown the four elements turning on the people who trusted in other than God; verse 41 then generalises: whoever takes a protector apart from God has built in silk. The connection is reinforced by verse 40's own word-choice: the wrongdoers' mistake was not that they sinned but that they relied — on wealth, on fortification, on the crowd, on their own cleverness — and reliance is the very thing the spider's house measures. And in the sūrah's design, verse 40's closing "they wronged themselves" also answers verse 2's opening accusation: if the outcome is self-inflicted, then the trial of verse 2 was not an injustice, and the striver of verse 6 profits himself, and the guidance of verse 69 follows striving. Two verses at either end of the sūrah say "for himself," and verse 40, at its centre, says the same of ruin — a perfect chiasmus: whatever a person gains or loses, he has, in the last analysis, brought upon himself, and the One who seizes is not the author of the wrong.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:41

> **The parable of those who take protectors apart from God is that of the spider that makes a house. Truly the frailest of houses is the spider's house, if they but knew.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Names the Sūrah**

*Mathalu'lladhīna-ttakhadhū min dūni Allāhi awliyāʾa kamathali'l-ʿankabūti-ittakhadhat baytan, wa-inna ahna'l-buyūti la-baytu'l-ʿankabūti law kānū yaʿlamūn.* This is the centre of the sūrah — verse forty-one of sixty-nine — and the whole chapter has been arranged to arrive at it. The persecutors of 29:2–13 had their protectors; the nations of 29:14–40 had theirs (wealth, towers, cities, assemblies, numbers), and verse 40 said that none of it availed. Verse 41 now supplies the single image that explains why: they had each built a house, and it was a web. The word *mathal* (parable) in Arabic is from *m-th-l*, "to be like," and the sūrah has, by its own testimony, been teaching by parables since verse 43 will say so outright: *"these are the parables; We set them forth for mankind"* (29:43). The parable is not decoration for the Qur'an; it is the instrument by which the unseen is made visible, and this is its most famous Makkan instance.

**ʿAnkabūt: The Name of the Thing**

*ʿAnkabūt* is the spider, and the lexicographers treat the word with their usual precision: al-Azharī in *Tahdhīb al-lugha* and the authors of *al-ʿInāya* and *al-Muʿrib* report that *al-ʿankabūt* is properly the female spider (the male being *ʿankabūth* with a *thāʾ*, in one reading), and that the name is related to a root meaning thickness or density, since the creature's body is thick-set; the same word is applied by the Arabs to a night that is dark and dense ("a night of *ʿankabūt*" — *laylatu ʿankabāta*). The Qur'an's phrase for the web is *bayt*, "house," and this is one of the verse's most careful touches: the spider's product is called a *house* because that is what it is to the spider — the place where it lives, lays its eggs, and waits — and the whole argument depends on the word. The sūrah is not mocking the spider's engineering; it is measuring the thing against its function. A house exists to protect, and this one does not.

**What the Web Actually Is, and Why That Makes the Image Better**

The parable is exact because the animal is real. A spider's silk is, in physical terms, remarkable: drag-line silk has a tensile strength per unit weight that modern materials science compares with steel and Kevlar, the thread is laid with geometric regularity, tensioned by the animal's own calibrated pulling, and renewed when broken. No one reading this verse should imagine that the Qur'an is calling the spider incompetent; it is doing something subtler. For all its properties, the web shelters nothing that matters: rain destroys it, a passing animal wipes it out, wind tears it, and a hand can remove it without an effort. It catches flies; it does not keep out the world. That is precisely the sūrah's claim about political alliances, patronage, wealth, and the protective prestige of an ancestry (the very things Makkah trusted): they may be intricate, impressive, and productive of small prey, and they are not, in any emergency that counts, a house. Al-Rāzī makes the point with his usual analytical directness in *Mafātīḥ al-ghayb*: a house is a shelter, and a shelter is measured by what it keeps out; the spider's structure keeps out nothing, so by definition the spider has not built a house but has merely produced a trap. And the phrase "the frailest of houses" is thus a deliberate contradiction in terms — a *bayt* that is the weakest of *buyūt* is a house only in name, which is the exact condition of every refuge that replaces God.

**"The Frailest": ḥunū, the Weakness That Bends**

*ahna* is from *h-n-w/h-w-n*, whose core sense is weakness, bending, and decay — *hanā* is the feebleness of old age (*haram*), and *hunwa* the decline of a thing. The comparative *ahna* does not say "weak" but "the weakest," and the superlative is chosen because a house is the paradigm of protection: what is weaker than a house is not even a house. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss is: "the flimsiest and most fragile of houses, in that it neither shelters from heat nor from cold, and it is torn by the wind and destroyed by what touches it; and such is the refuge of whoever takes a protector apart from God — it is weaker before God's command than a web before a hand." Note, too, the sūrah's closing clause, *law kānū yaʿlamūn*, "if they but knew": the fact is empirically available to anyone who has looked at a spider's web; what is missing is the inference. The verse is not asking for a new datum about spiders but for the application of a datum everyone owns, and this is the Qur'an's standard epistemology of parables.

**"Take Protectors": The Range of awliyāʾ**

The word *awliyāʾ*, the plural of *walī*, is broader than "idols," and the source commentary on this verse lists the three applications the tradition recognises: those who take other human beings as protectors (cf. 4:139, 5:51, 9:23–24), those who take satans (7:27, 18:50, 41:25), and those who take idols (2:257, 13:16). The Qur'anic background makes all three continuous: *walāya* is the client-patron bond — protection in exchange for loyalty — and the sūrah's whole argument is that a bond of that kind, with anyone, becomes shirk when it is the place where one's security actually resides. God is named *Walī* repeatedly in this Book ("your protector, and He is the best of helpers" — 3:150; "God is the protector of those who believe" — 2:257), and the verse's charge is not "do not have friends" but "do not house yourself in them." This is why the sūrah of the spider also commands kindness to the unbelieving parent (29:8) and gentle argument with the People of the Book (29:46): the Qur'an's prohibition concerns trust, not affection. The same distinction is stated in the most severe terms in a hadith that belongs here:

> *"Whoever dies while calling upon, besides God, a rival [equal], enters the Fire."* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 4497, from Ibn ʿAbbās)

and in the ḥadīth qudsī of the false partnership: *"I am the most self-sufficient of partners; whoever does a deed for another along with Me, I leave him to that partner of his"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2985). The web is what a "partner" is: a structure that looks like support and, when tested, is found to be nothing at all.

**The Hidden House: Riyāʾ as Inner Polytheism**
The source commentary on this verse adds an interior reading, and it is the more demanding: "this verse also points to the hidden polytheism of those who worship God to be seen by men (2:264; 4:38, 142; 8:47), for they have, in effect, taken a god other than God." The logic is exact: the person who performs devotion for an audience has made that audience the source of the act's value, and has therefore housed himself in the opinion of others. The web is a fine image for that condition too, because it is both a trap for others and a snare for its builder: reputation catches applause and cannot catch death. The Prophet's ﷺ warning about the smallest polytheism belongs here — *"the thing I fear most for you is the minor polytheism: riyaʾ"* (a report to that effect from Maʿmar ibn ʿAbdullāh is transmitted in Musnad Aḥmad and judged ḥasan by some of the later muḥaqqiqūn) — and the Qur'an's own description of those who "show off to people and do not remember God except a little" (4:142) is the sūrah's portrait of the *munāfiq* of 29:10–11 seen from the inside. On this reading, verse 41 is addressed to the believing community as much as to Makkah, and that is how the sūrah's own ending — "God is with the *muḥsinīn*", the beautiful-doers (29:69) — answers it: the opposite of a web-house is an act done for the Face that does not perish.

**A Parable of Self-Spun Shelter**

One feature of the animal's biology gives the image its deepest reach, and the Sufi writers did not miss it: the spider spins the house out of its own body. The web is not gathered, quarried, or given; it is secreted — the structure is made of the self. So too every refuge a person constructs out of himself — his competence, his cleverness, his willpower, his social credit — is literally self-spun, and is therefore exactly as durable as the spinner. the Sufi teachers made the same point in their own idiom, and it became a staple of the manuals of conduct: to rely on the deed is a diminution of reliance on the Doer, since the deed, however sound, is created and contingent; and the preachers and poets of that tradition used the spider repeatedly for the self that spins, out of its own substance, the structure it then mistakes for safety until something stronger than the spinner arrives. The Makkan verse itself states it plainly: whoever takes a *walī* apart from God has made a house out of himself, and houses made out of selves are the weakest houses.

**Why This Verse Is the Sūrah's Centre**

Structurally, 29:41 does three things at once. It answers the opening question: the believer who thinks "we believe" suffices and the denier who thinks escape is possible are both relying on a web — one on his profession, the other on his power. It closes the historical catalogue: the four elements of 29:40 are simply what happens to web-houses when God sends what He sends; and it opens what follows, from verse 44 onward, where the sūrah turns from the world's signs to the Book, because the Qur'an is the one thing that is not self-spun — it is "sent down" (29:51), from outside the human capacity to produce (29:48), and therefore not a web. Al-Zamakhsharī sees the connection and notes that the verse is placed immediately after the annihilation of the four punished classes, as though the sūrah were to say: these were not weak people but weak houses. And the last clause — *law kānū yaʿlamūn* — leaves the judgment where the sūrah has been leaving every judgment: not with the failure of evidence, but with the refusal to draw the inference from evidence everyone has. The verse is thus the sūrah's single test: look at a web, and ask honestly what you are living inside.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:42

> **Truly God knows whatsoever they call upon apart from Him. And He is the Mighty, the Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Single Clause That Dissolves the Whole System**

*inna Allāha yaʿlamu mā yadʿūna min dūnihi wa-huwa'l-ʿAzīzu'l-Ḥakīm.* Verse 41 had said that the protectors are a web; verse 42 now states why that is knowledge enough for God to dispose of them, and why no protection is being lost. God *knows* what they call upon instead of Him, and "knows" here carries its full weight: He knows the worshipped as what it is — a thing with no power, no hearing, no knowledge, and no share in creation — and He knows the worshippers as what they are — deliberate, accountable, not ignorant by necessity. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss runs: "God knows which of what they call upon has no reality and which they have been wronged by, and He is the Mighty who cannot be resisted, the Wise in whom there is no error." Ibn Kathīr reads it as the ground of recompense: He knows the idols and He knows their worshippers, so He will judge between them, and the idols will disown those who invoked them. The sentence's brevity is the sūrah's style at the summit of an argument: after the spider, no further demonstration is needed, only the statement that God is informed.

**"Knows What They Call Upon" — and What the Called Upon Cannot Do**

The verse's claim about the invoked is elaborated elsewhere in the Book, and those passages are the natural commentary. *"Say: call upon those you claim, apart from Him; they do not possess the removal of a harm from you nor its transfer. Those whom they call upon themselves seek the means to their Lord — whichever of them is nearest — they hope for His mercy and fear His punishment"* (17:56–57) — a passage that dismantles the whole theory of intermediary protection by showing that the intermediaries are themselves petitioners. And: *"who is further astray than one who calls upon, besides God, those that will not answer him until the Day of Resurrection, and they are heedless of his invocation? And when the people are gathered, the idols become their enemies and say: you used to worship us"* (46:5–6). Al-Qurṭubī notes the irony the sūrah preserves in 29:42: the "protectors" are *known* by God, which is to say they are objects, not subjects; a known thing is a thing, and a thing cannot shelter. This is why the sūrah of 29:22 ("you have no protector or helper apart from God") is quoted in these commentaries as the verse's partner: the idols are known, and only the Knower is mighty.

**al-ʿAzīz al-Ḥakīm: The Two Names Chosen for the Two Parties**

The pairing at the verse's end is the most concentrated piece of argument in this section, and the exegetes' treatment of it repays attention. Every one of the ninety-nine names in the Qur'an, when it concludes a verse, is selected for the meaning of that verse; here two are named, and each answers a different failure in the scene just described. The *ʿAzīz* — the Invincible, the Majestic whose might admits no resistance — is the quality the idols do not have: the spider's house is frail because its owner is not ʿAzīz, and the one who wants a protector needs might in the protector. The *Ḥakīm* — the Wise who places things in their right places and does nothing in vain — is the quality the worshippers do not have: to build a house out of silk against the weather of eternity is the definition of unwise action, and the verse's rebuke of the *choice* is carried by this name. Al-Rāzī makes exactly this double assignment in his commentary on the formula, and the same two names are used, in the sūrah, to close the parallel passage on Abraham and the fire: "He is the Mighty, the Wise" (29:26 — Lot's own words, which are the emigrant's answer to the same situation). Read as a pair across the two verses, 29:26 and 29:42 say the same thing to the two opposite temperaments: to the frightened, "He is Mighty" — your Protector is not weak; to the self-satisfied, "He is Wise" — your reasoning is being watched by a wisdom you have not consulted.

**The Might That Explains the Delay, and the Wisdom That Explains the Parable**

There is a theological question the names answer, because the sūrah has raised it: if the web is nothing and its worshippers are known, why is anything allowed to continue? The answer in the two names: the *ʿAzīz* does not need to act immediately to be able to act, and the *Ḥakīm* defers for reasons that are themselves part of the instruction. The first clause removes the fear that God is unable to stop the powerful (cf. 29:4 "do they suppose they will outstrip Us?" and 29:39 "they did not outstrip"); the second removes the suspicion that the deferral and the parable-making are pointless. This is the sūrah's consistent handling of divine patience: punishment has an *ajal* (29:5, 53), parables are struck so that someone might understand (29:43), and the respite is therefore not indulgence but pedagogy — an education of those who will learn, and an accumulation of evidence against those who will not.

**A Practical Teaching: The Industry of Access**

Verse 42, taken with 17:56–57 and 46:5–6, contains one of the Qur'an's most durable social analyses: the religion of intermediaries. Wherever blessing, safety, or status is imagined as flowing through a chain of access — a patron, an office, a family name, a saint's tomb treated as a jurisdiction, a party, a broker, a "connection" — the worshipper's error is not that he values people, but that he has relocated causality to a creature, and to a creature whom God *knows*. The Makkan situation was precisely this: the Quraysh derived their standing from their custody of the sanctuary and their ties of alliance, and the sūrah's answer, in this verse and the next, is that what is known is not the knower, and what is known cannot know you. Ibn Taymiyyah's polemic against the deification of persons and shrines rests on this class of text, and his positive concern in the same passages is the one the sūrah names: God alone is the *Walī* who is ʿAzīz, and the only access He recognises is a person's own faith and righteousness (cf. 45:19 — *"whoever does righteous deeds does so for his own self, and whoever does evil does so against it"*). The classical ethic of *tawakkul* follows directly: al-Ghazālī's discussion of reliance in the *Iḥyāʾ* — where he distinguishes reliance on causes, which is permitted as a habit of mind, from reliance on the causal agent besides God, which is a breach of the covenant of lordship — is a technical restatement of 29:41–42: the causes are the web, and the web is known.

**What the Verse Leaves the Reader With**

Two conclusions, each practical. First, an inventory: the sūrah invites the reader to name the things he calls upon besides God in the sense of housing himself in them — the job, the savings, the network, the passport, the institution, the reputation — and to ask of each, without exaggeration, what it is: a real thing with a real but limited function (a web catches flies), or a protector in the sense the verse means (a house that keeps out what matters). Second, a reassurance: the clause *inna Allāha yaʿlamu* means that nothing in the arrangement has escaped notice — neither the fraud of the false refuges nor the honest weakness of the people relying on them — and that the knowledge of the ʿAzīz al-Ḥakīm is the same attribute the sūrah has been appealing to since verse 3 ("God knows the truthful and knows the liars"). The passage that began with the assay of belief closes, here, with the Assayer's perfect information about the objects people substitute for Him. Nothing about the web, or about the spider, or about the one who hides in it, is unknown — which is why the parable ends in a warning and not in a despair.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:43

> **These are the parables: We set them forth for mankind. But none understand them, save those who know.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Explains Its Own Method**

*wa-tilka'l-amthālu naḍribuhā li'n-nās, wa-mā yaʿqiluhā illā'l-ʿālimūn.* "Those" — the spider, the drowning sea, the hardened cities, the vanished nations — are *amthāl*, parables, and the verb chosen for how they are handled is *naḍaba*, "to strike," because a parable in Arabic is something struck like a coin or pitched like a tent: it is set up, and it can be taken down. Al-Ṭabarī glosses *naḍribuhā* as "We liken one thing to another so that the meaning may be recognised," and he notes that the demonstrative *tilka*, "those," refers back to what has just been recited rather than to parables in general, so that the verse is a caption on the sūrah's own twenty preceding verses. The same formula closes a passage in sūrah al-Ḥashr, and its partner wording is the commentary one needs: *"We present the parables to people so that they may reflect"* (59:21). Two purposes, then, are stated for a parable: it is addressed to *all* mankind (*li'l-nās*, the widest possible audience, including the illiterate and the child), and its return is *ʿaql*, understanding, which not everyone supplies. The verse's second clause is therefore not an elitist qualification but an honest one: the instrument is universal, the uptake is not.

**What a mathal Does**

The Arabic root *m-th-l* carries three senses that are all operative here, and the classical lexicographers list them together: *mithl* means "like, equal," so a parable is a thing whose likeness is drawn to another thing; *mathal* also means a saying that has become famous and travels ("his word went as a *mathal* among the Arabs"), so a parable is by nature memorable and portable; and *mathal* can mean a marvel or a strange thing, so a parable is also something that arrests. Al-Zamakhsharī, in *al-Kashshāf*, observes that the Qur'an's parables have the third property and use the second to achieve the first: they strike the mind so that what is abstract in the unseen will not be forgotten. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said that the parables are the keys of knowledge, and that a man who does not know them has missed a door of the Qur'an rather than a decoration in it. Al-Suyūṭī, in the chapters devoted to the parables and to the Qur'an's figurative language in ʿUlūm al-Qurʾān and al-Iklīl fī mustagraṭ al-tanzīl, collects the exegetes' rule for interpretation: a parable is understood from the point of comparison the text itself indicates, not from every property of the thing compared, and to press an image beyond the *wajh al-mathal* its author attached is to make the parable lie. Applied to our spider, the rule is decisive: the verse compares the web to a patron's protection in *frailty*, and it does not thereby teach anything about arachnids, about God's likeness to a hunter, or about the moral status of spinning.

**"None Understand Them Save Those Who Know"**

The pairing of *ʿaql* with *ʿilm* — understanding is restricted to knowing people — is the verse's epistemology, and it is not a circle but a ladder. *ʿilm* here is not information but the settled recognition of what God is and what created things are; the word *ʿālimūn* is used in the Qur'an with that weight precisely in the verse about the fear of God: *"only those who know among His servants truly fear God"* (35:28). A person who does not know God will not get the parable of the spider, because the point of the parable is that every refuge besides Him is silk. Al-Rāzī draws the consequence in his usual rationalist idiom: the *ʿālim* is the one who knows the proportion between things, and a parable is nothing but a known proportion; hence ignorance of the proportions — of how large the world is, how small the self, how real the Hereafter — is what makes a parable inert. The prophetic tradition locates the same capacity in God's giving rather than in human cleverness, which prevents the verse from becoming an boast about the gifted:

> *"Whom God wills good for, He makes deeply discerning in the religion, and teaches him the interpretation of matters."* (a wording transmitted in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī in the Book of Knowledge and in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim from Ibn ʿAbbās)

And the practice of teaching by image is itself attested in the prophetic reports: he would strike a parable to make a meaning plain, and the Book's own stock of comparisons — a donkey carrying scriptures (62:5), a dog that pants if you attack it or leaves it alone (7:176), a grain of wheat sending up seven ears (2:261) — shows the range of materials the parable is permitted to use, from the barnyard to the field, provided the point of comparison is not lost.

**Why God Uses Gnats and Spiders: al-Tustarī's Interior Reading**

The source commentary on this verse preserves Sahl al-Tustarī's (d. 283/896, as quoted by al-Sulamī) reflection on the same clause, and it is worth setting out in full, because it is a complete theory of the religious image:

> "God sets forth parables for mankind in general, in order that they may arrive through parables at understanding. Then He said: *none understand them save those who know* — as much as to say that knowledge is rare and comprehension of God still rarer. Whoever knows that his own life is not by himself knows that his life is by the Living which does not die; and whoever knows that his sustenance is not by himself knows that his sustenance is by the Provider who does not perish; and whoever knows that his retribution is not by himself knows that his retribution is with the Just who is never wronged in his judgment. And thus were the parables set forth for those who possess understanding."

The movement of the passage is exactly the sūrah's: the parable of the spider, which needs the *ʿālim*, is unpacked into three confessions — life, provision, reckoning — each a rejection of self-reliance, and al-Tustarī's point is that the aim of the image is not a better analogy but a transferred dependence. A second tradition on the same problem is preserved in the commentary on 2:26, where the Quraysh objected to being compared with a gnat: *"God does not shy away from coining the similitude of a gnat or what is above it"* (2:26). Al-Ṭabarī reports from Ibn ʿAbbās and others that the objection to low materials was answered by the fact that guidance and misguidance are both in God's hand, and that the smallness of the thing mentioned is not an argument against the greatness of what it illustrates. And the phrase "what is above it" was taken by the commentators to include what is greater than a gnat, so that the Makkan objection to the gnat is the same objection to the spider; the sūrah's answer serves both, since the greatness is in the meaning carried, not in the size of the carrier.

**ʿaql: The Organ a Parable Addresses**

*ʿyaʿqiluhā* is from *ʿ-q-l*, and the root occurs in this sūrah only three times — at 29:35, where the preserved ruin of the punished nations is “a sign for a people who understand”; here; and at 29:63, where the rain that revives the dead earth draws the answer “God” from the objectors and the comment “but most of them do not understand.” Verse 43 is the middle of the three, and that placement is worth noticing, because the sūrah's anthropology has been building around the root. The *ʿaql* in classical Arabic is not intellect-in-general but the capacity to hold: *ʿiqāl* is the cord that ties a camel's foreleg, and to be *ʿāqil* is to be able to restrain an impulse by holding a consideration. A parable, precisely because it is an image rather than an argument, cannot be processed by syllogism; it must be *held*, compared, and allowed to constrain one's conduct. This is why the sūrah says of the man who has the parable but not the disposition that he does not "understand" it — the failure is not in the head but in the grip. The same root explains the sūrah's sequence: trial (29:2–3) is the practical assay that reveals who can hold, and 29:43 confirms what the assay showed: the parable does not create the *ʿāqil*, it discloses him. Al-Māwardī's dedicated study of the genre, *al-Amthāl fī'l-Qurʾān*, and Ibn al-Qayyim's treatment in *al-Fawāʾid* both make the same point in their own terms: the value of a parable lies in the proportion it establishes, and the same proportion is therefore restated in many images, because minds differ in which one they can hold.

**The Modern Lesson in the Clause**

A last observation, which the commentators' insistence on the *wajh al-mathal* makes easy to state. Every serious discipline today proceeds by models — a diagram, a simulation, a case study, an analogy — and the Qur'an's fourteenth-century audience was being taught, in this single verse, the two rules that still govern the use of models: they are addressed to everyone, and their meaning is available only to those who know what the modelled thing is. A parable, like a model, is neither the reality nor a decoration on it; it is a handle by which a mind lifts what it cannot hold directly. And the sūrah's warning against idolatry is, in the same register, a warning against mistaking the handle for the thing: those who took the spider's house for a house were people who had taken a description of safety for safety itself. The last phrase of the verse, *illā'l-ʿālimūn*, thus closes 29:41–43 with an invitation rather than a rebuke: if you want to understand the spider, become the kind of knower for whom the image works — and the sūrah has already told you, at verse 2, that this kind of knowledge is not acquired by being told but by being tried.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:44

> **God created the heavens and the earth in truth. Surely in that is a sign for the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Turns the Sūrah from Parable to Book**

*khalaqa Allāhu's-samāwāti wa'l-arḍa bi'l-ḥaqq, inna fī dhālika la-āyatan li'l-muʾminīn.* From here to the end of the sūrah the argument runs on a new register, and the source introduction to this section states the change precisely: the discourse moves from the parable of the spider to the creation of the world "in truth" and then to the revelation of the Book in truth, because the Book, like the cosmos, is described as *ḥaqq*: "and those who hold fast to what has been revealed to you are on a right path" and "the Book which We revealed to you is the truth (ṣidq) confirming what is before it" (35:31), and "it is the descent of the Book from the Lord of the worlds" (40:2). The logic is straightforward and important: if the world is made *bi'l-ḥaqq*, then a true report about the world can be given, and a report given by the Maker of the world is the Qur'an; verse 45, the command to recite and to pray, follows immediately because a Book that comes from a world made in truth expects to be recited and enacted. The structure of the passage, as al-Biqāʿī's analysis of Qur'anic coherence would describe it, is: parable (41), the Knower's knowledge (42), the conditions of understanding (43), the ground of all understanding — the created order's truthfulness (44), and the address to the one who recites it (45).

**bi'l-ḥaqq: Three Things Denied**

The prepositional phrase is dense, and the commentators unpack it by negation. Al-Ṭabarī: "God created the heavens and the earth *bi'l-ḥaqq*, that is, for a true purpose (*ḥaqqan*) and not in idle play, so that He might reward the obedient and punish the disobedient." Ibn Kathīr adds the same: "He created them with truth, containing justice and wisdom, so that He might reward those who do evil for what they did and reward those who do good with goodness." Al-Zamakhsharī's gloss in *al-Kashshāf* stresses the operative consequence: the affair of the servants is settled by truth and justice, so that no claim of the wronged and no plea of the evildoer is left unweighed. From the three glosses together, the phrase denies three claims. First, it denies that the world is *play*: "We did not create the heavens and the earth and what is between them in play" (21:16), and, with an addition the sūrah needs, "We did not create the heavens and earth and what is between them except in truth and an appointed term" (30:8). Second, it denies that the world is *in vain*: "do you suppose that We created you only in sport, and that you will not be returned to Us?" (23:115), and the prayer of the people of understanding in Āl ʿImrān: *"Our Lord, You did not create this in vain; glory be to You, so save us from the punishment of the Fire"* (3:191) — the closest verse to 29:44 anywhere in the Book, and the source commentary notes it too. Third, it denies that the world is *illusion*: the created order is *ḥaqq*, which means it is really there, and really legible. The verse's first clause is thus the metaphysical foundation of the whole sūrah — a cosmos that is not a game, not a whim, and not a dream can function as *āya*, and the parables the sūrah has been striking are not poetry but reading.

**ḥaqq Against bāṭil: The Sūrah's Own Opposition**

The word *ḥaqq* is one of God's names, and the sūrah will name its opposite once more before it ends, so the pairing matters. *"That is because God is the Truth, and whatever they invoke apart from Him is the false, and because God is the lofty, the great"* (31:30; the same formula at 22:62). And sūrah al-Ḥajj puts the whole contest in one sarcastic test-case: *"whoever supposes that God will not help him in this world and the next, let him stretch out a cord to the roof and then strangle himself with it, and let him see whether his device will remove what enrages him"* (22:15) — a verse whose irony only works because God's help, like the world, is *ḥaqq*. In our sūrah, 29:52 will address those who "believe in the false and disbelieve in God," and 29:44 answers them in advance: the false is not a rival reality but the absence of footing in a world created in truth. The consequence for the spider's worshippers is exactly the one 29:42–43 prepared: their refuges are not another kind of truth but a misreading of the one kind the world has, and the world, being *ḥaqq*, will not sustain them. This is why the verse's testimony to the heavens and earth, rather than to the sanctuary or the camels of Quraysh, is the right answer to Makkan pride: the things the Quraysh claimed as evidence for themselves — the House, the months, the water, the trade routes — are themselves evidence for something else, because they are created and, being created, they are *signs*, not patrons.

**"A Sign for the Believers": Who the Address Is Now**

The sūrah has addressed "those who were tried," "those who do evil," "those who disbelieve," and now, for the first time since verse 9, the *muʾminūn* are named as the recipients of the proof. The restriction is not an act of favoritism but an epistemological fact, and the tradition is unembarrassed about it: a sign requires the eye that reads it, as rain requires soil. The Qur'an states the mechanism elsewhere: *"and We have made the night and the day two signs; then He effaced the sign of the night and made the sign of the day seeing, so that you might seek bounty from your Lord and know the number of years and the reckoning"* (17:12), and *"in the heavens and the earth are signs for the believers, and in your creation, and the beasts He scattered, are signs for a people sure of faith"* (45:3–4). Al-Saʿdī's comment on verses like these is that the same sky gives a sign to one person and a rock of stumbling to another, and that the difference is the heart's orientation, a reading consistent with this sūrah's account of trial in verses 2–3: the world assays, and what it assays is the heart. The past tense of the verb in our verse — "God *created*" — makes the same point in another key, as the rhetoricians observe: the creation of the heavens and earth is reported as a completed act because it is a datum, open to inspection, not a promise awaiting fulfilment. What awaits fulfilment is the reckoning, which the phrase *bi'l-ḥaqq* already implies: a true creation is a creation with an honest end.

**The Moral Seriousness of Nature, and Its Modern Echo**
