Two applications follow for the reader, one of which the commentators stress and the other of which the contemporary world has rediscovered. First, the world's truthfulness is a demand for interpretation: every element of the sūrah's catalogue is an *āya* — the cities left desolate (29:38: "you pass by among them in the mornings"), the vanished nations (29:35: "a sign for a people who reason"), the caravan routes (29:20: "travel through the land"), the human body's history in a drop (29:19), the heavens raised without pillars and the earth spread out (13:2; 50:6–7; 88:18–20). The sūrah has trained its audience to read; 29:44 supplies the licence: read the world, because it is not a fiction. Second, the world's createdness in truth means it is not our material for play, and this, at least, is what the modern environmental situation forces on us from the other direction: a cosmos treated as a toy has begun to answer with floods, heat, and exhausted soil, and the Qur'anic affirmation that the heavens and earth were made *bi'l-ḥaqq* — with a purpose not delegated to us — is the only secure ground on which the language of stewardship (*khilāfa*, 2:30; *amāna*, 33:72) can mean more than sentiment. Ibn Taymiyyah uses precisely the pairing of 29:44's two clauses against the fatalists and the idle: because God created in truth, human acts have real consequences and are really reckoned; and the classical *kalām* debate between Ashʿarī and Muʿtazilī about whether good and evil are known by revelation or by reason finds here one of its scriptural test-cases, since a world made "in truth" is a world in which "truth" is not only a revealed label.

**Why the Verse Is Quiet, and Why That Is Right**

The last thing to notice is the verse's restraint. After forty verses of argument, narrative, threat, and parable, the sūrah states a fact in seven words and stops, with the assurance attached not as an argument but as a designation: *"inna fī dhālika la-āyatan li'l-muʾminīn"*. The construction is the Book's characteristic one — the emphatic *inna*, the lam of the predicate, and the prepositional phrase marking the class for whom the proof works. The formula "inna fī dhālika la-āya(tan)" with its specification of the recipients occurs dozens of times in the Qur'an, and this sūrah has used it twice already — at 29:20 for those who travel through the land to see the end of the deniers, and at 29:35 for "a people who reason" — so that 29:44's *li'l-muʾminīn* is the third and highest term in a series the chapter has been building. No further demonstration is attempted, because the sūrah's subject is no longer the unbeliever's argument but the believer's vision: what is asked of you now is not to prove the world but to see it. The verse that follows (29:45) turns from the world's text to the recited text and to the prayer that answers it — but it turns there because 29:44 has just licensed the move: a Book from the Maker of a true world is the world's own interpretation, and the one who recites it is reading the thing the parables were likened to.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:45

> **Recite what has been revealed to you of the Book, and establish the prayer. Truly the prayer preserves from indecency and the reprehensible — and the remembrance of God is greater — and God knows what you are earning.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command at the Centre of the Sūrah**

*utlu mā ūḥiya ilayka mina'l-kitābi wa-aqimi aṣ-ṣalāh, inna aṣ-ṣalāta tanhā ʿani'l-faḥshāʾi wa'l-munkar, wa-la-dhikru Allāhi akbar, wa-Allāhu yaʿlamu mā taṣnaʿūn.* The verse is placed immediately after the parable of the spider and the statement that the world was created *bi'l-ḥaqq*, and the position is the argument: if the refuge a person spins out of himself is a web, then what is not a web must come from elsewhere. The two commands here are precisely things that come from elsewhere — a recitation that is *revealed* (not composed, as 29:48 will insist) and a ritual that is *instituted* (not devised). The sūrah of trial, which began by asking what belief consists of when it costs something, answers at its centre: belief consists of taking the Book and standing in prayer, and both are gifts before they are achievements. Ibn al-Jawzī and al-Qurṭubī both take *aṣ-ṣalāh* here as the obligatory prayers, and some of the earlier exegetes, including al-Ṭabarī, report the reading that the "prayer" in the verse means the act of reciting, since recitation itself is a form of worship; al-Rāzī argues against that, on the ground that the verse has already commanded recitation and would not then repeat it under another name. The dispute is instructive: the verse's own structure joins the two, and the joining is what matters.

**"Recite": tilāwa as Following**

*utlu* is from *t-l-w*, "to follow, to come after," and *tilāwa* in the Qur'an keeps that shape: the reciter follows the text, does not lead it. The same root is used for the followers of a prophet and for the nights and days that succeed one another, and the word chosen here rather than *iqraʾ* ("read," the first word of revelation) is deliberate: what is commanded now is not the first seizure of the text but the sustained following of it, recitation as a way of walking behind a thing. This is why the verse can be read, as Ibn al-Jawzī reads it, as an instruction about the inner content of the prayer as well as about the prayer's performance: one who establishes the prayer recites in it, and one who recites in it follows what he recites, and the following is what makes the prayer a prohibition of the reprehensible. The same ordering recurs in the sūrah's later claim about the Book — "an Arabic *dhikr*" (43:3; cf. 21:50, "a blessed Book We have revealed") — and in the report that the Prophet ﷺ would return home after the night prayer and recite sūrah al-Muʾminūn, beginning with *"successful indeed are the believers"* (23:1), according to the tradition in Sunan Abū Dāwūd.

**"Establish": Form as the Carrier of Meaning**

*aqim* is from *q-w-m*: not "do" but "set up," so that the prayer is imagined as a thing standing upright that the worshipper raises. The verb is the one used for justice ("stand firm for God, witnesses to justice" — 5:8) and for the child's right, and it carries the whole tradition's insistence that the prayer has an exterior that must be observed exactly: purity of body, garment, and place; the covering of the *ʿawra*; the facing of the *qibla*; the *takbīr* that opens it and the *salām* that closes it; the standing, the bowing, the two prostrations, and the sitting, with the recitation and the glorifications prescribed in each. The well-known formulation of the jurists on this point is preserved in a report from ʿAlī in the Sunan: "the key of the prayer is purification, its *taḥrīm* is the *takbīr*, and its *taḥlīl* is the *salām*." The reason the sūrah's promise is attached to the form is not magical: the form is what the heart does not get to choose in the moment, and so it holds the person when his feelings do not. Trial (29:2) is exactly the situation in which feeling fails; prayer is the act that continues regardless, which is why the Book commands the Prophet ﷺ to "establish the prayer for My remembrance" (20:14) and why the *muḍarriʿūn* of 29:18–19 who were told to fasten their loins for the fight also stood for prayer at night.

**"Tanhā": The Mechanism by Which Prayer Restrains**

*tanhā* is from *n-h-y*, "to forbid, to turn away," in the imperfect, so the verse describes a standing property, not a one-time event; and the two things restrained are the sūrah's own vocabulary of Midian and Sodom: *faḥshāʾ*, what is grossly indecent (29:28, "you commit an indecency that no people in the world preceded you in"), and *munkar*, from *n-k-r*, "what is disowned" — disowned by revelation and by sound nature, the opposite of the *maʿrūf* that the Qur'an elsewhere makes the measure of a community (3:104, 3:110, 7:157, 9:71, 22:41, 31:17). The commentators, including al-Ṭabarī, explain the mechanism in several ways that are complementary: the prayer's recitation reminds, its repetition catches the worshipper five times before a sin has settled, its requirement of purity makes the body conscious of being in God's presence, and its timing in the day — before work, in the middle of it, at the edges of the evening and the night — makes it a structure over the hours rather than a respite from them. The most quoted support for the restraint being real is the Prophet's ﷺ own parable for it:

> *"Tell me: if a river ran at the door of one of you and he washed in it five times a day, would any of his filth remain?" They said: no filth of his would remain. He said: "That is the likeness of the five prayers; God blots out the sins by them."* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Jābir)

**But the Form Alone Is Not the Whole of It**

The sūrah's own seriousness about the interior is what gives the verse its edge, and the classical tradition states the corollary bluntly. The saying preserved by Ibn al-Jawzī, al-Rāzī, al-Suyūṭī and al-Ṭabarī, sometimes raised to the Prophet ﷺ and sometimes left with the Companion, is:

> *"He whose prayer does not prevent him from indecency and abomination — his prayer has only increased him in distance [from God]."* (attributed to ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd, cited in the tafsīr of al-Rāzī and Ibn al-Jawzī)

And the same requirement appears in the sūrah's own logic of acceptance: "God accepts only from the reverent" (5:27). Al-Tustarī's comment on our verse, quoted by al-Sulamī, puts it as a duty of repair rather than a dismissal:

> "Every prayer that does not restrain shameful and unjust deeds and is devoid of the adornment of shunning these deeds is tainted, and it is incumbent upon one to purify it."

Al-Ghazālī's treatment in *Iḥyāʾ ʿulūm al-dīn* is the systematic version of the same insight: the prayer was not legislated merely for its words and motions but, as he says, for "polishing the heart and renewing the remembrance of God" (Iḥyāʾ 1:142), and he lists among its interior conditions presence of heart, magnification of the One addressed, hope, shame, and understanding of the meaning of what is said — without which the act is a body without a soul. The two positions, the form's efficacy and the form's insufficiency, are held together in the tradition by the story the sources cite on our verse: a young man who prayed with the Prophet ﷺ and yet inclined to indecency was not dismissed, and the Prophet said of him, *"his prayer will soon restrain him"* — a report preserved in the musnad and ḥadīth-collections whose chain the muḥaddithūn judged variously, and whose meaning the exegetes take as the verse's own promise read over time: prayer works as a leaven, not as a lock.

**"The Remembrance of God Is Greater": The Great Ambiguity**

*wa-la-dhikru Allāhi akbar* is one of the most famous double-meaning clauses in the Book, and the source commentary lists the readings with care. (i) *Dhikr* as your remembrance of God: to remember God is greater than anything else, including other acts of obedience (Ibn al-Jawzī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Suyūṭī) — hence the report *"the world and what is in it is accursed, except the remembrance of God and what brings one closer to it"* (Sunan al-Tirmidhī and Sunan Ibn Māja, from Abū Hurayra). (ii) *Dhikr* as God's remembrance of you: "God's remembrance of human beings is greater than their remembrance of Him," as al-Bayḍāwī, Ibn al-Jawzī, al-Suyūṭī, al-Tustarī and al-Ṭabarī all report — a reading grounded in the Qur'an's own reciprocal: *"remember Me and I will remember you"* (2:152) and *"so remember Me; I will remember you"* with the added promise that "the remembrance of God is greater" in the sense that His mention of you lifts you in a way your mention of yourself never could (cf. 94:4, "We raised high your renown"). Al-Suyūṭī and al-Ṭabarī allow that both meanings obtain at once, and al-Tustarī's school draws the consequence that the source spells out: fullness of remembrance is reached when the self's part in it falls away, so that the worshipper realises that his remembrance is itself a remembering done in him — which is why the greater thing is never the size of the act but the presence in it. (iii) A third reading, also attested, is that remembrance of God *inside* the prayer is the greater thing, so that the verse praises the prayer by naming its soul. (iv) And al-Rāzī notes the comparative sense others take: the believers' remembrance is better than the idolaters' remembrance of their gods, since the gods do not remember at all (cf. 46:6 — the idols are "heedless of their invocation").

**Why This Verse Answers the Spider**

Placed where it is, 29:45 does the sūrah's central work. The web-house of verse 41 is self-spun: its owner chooses its design and it is made of his own substance. Here are two things he did not design and cannot manufacture: a Book that is *revealed*, and a prayer that is *instituted* — with fixed times, fixed words, a fixed direction, and a fixed humility of the forehead on the ground, which is why the Prophet ﷺ said that "the closeness of a servant to his Lord is greatest in prostration, so multiply supplication in it" (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 479). To establish the prayer is thus the practical opposite of taking a *walī*: it is to be placed, five times a day, inside a structure one did not choose, facing a house one did not build, in a direction that everyone's does not depend on. And the verse's last clause closes the trapdoor on self-importance: *wa-Allāhu yaʿlamu mā taṣnaʿūn*, "God knows what you are making" — the same verb-form as 29:52's *"what you fabricate"* and 29:23's *"what you fabricate"*; the prayer you establish and the web you spin are both, in the sūrah's vocabulary, *ṣanāʾa*, things made, and only one of them was made for you. Ibn Kathīr takes the clause as the sanction on the promise: the one who has been taught by the prayer to shrink from the *faḥshāʾ* is watched when he does not, and the watching is not surveillance but the fact that nothing is hidden in a world whose Maker "knows what you are making" as a present and ongoing thing.

**The Psychology and the Practical**

The verse's claims can be checked, and the tradition encourages that they be. As a discipline of attention, a practice repeated five times at fixed hours is a countermeasure to the two things the sūrah identifies as the sources of moral failure — heedlessness (*ghafla*, the quality of the people of the Book in 7:179, "they are heedless," and of the idols' worshippers in 46:6) and the pressure of other people ("the trial of men" — 29:10): a person who has stood before God five times has, each time, rehearsed the judgment that relativises every human audience, which is why the prayer is described elsewhere as the thing that "refreshes" the Prophet ﷺ — "be at peace with it, Bilāl" (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 611), and "the coolness of my eye has been placed in the prayer," in the report transmitted by Aḥmad and al-Nasāʾī. As a social fact, the verse's promise runs against the plain observation that ritual does not automatically reform character — which is exactly what the saying of Ibn Masʿūd states, and what the exception in the verse itself concedes: prayer *tanhā*, it forbids, and a forbidding can be resisted. Practically, the classical manuals derived from this verse a rule of self-examination that any reader can apply after every prayer: did what I just recited change anything I was about to do? The sūrah's own measure of the trial in verses 2–3 was honesty under cost; verse 45 gives the instrument by which the honest believer pays, day after day, the cost of being re-made — the recited Book and the standing prayer — and leaves the assessment, as the sūrah always does, with the knowledge of God.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:46

> **And do not argue with the People of the Book except in the best manner, except those of them who do wrong, and say: we believe in what has been sent down to us and what has been sent down to you; our God and your God is one, and we submit to Him.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rule of Debate, Placed After the Rule of Prayer**

*wa-lā tujādilū ahlu'l-kitābi illā bi'llatī hiya aḥsan, illā alladhīna ẓalamū minhum, wa-qūlū āmannā bi'lladhī unzila ilaynā wa-unzila ilaykum wa-ilāhunā wa-ilāhukum wāḥid, wa-nahnu lahu muslimūn.* The sequence in the sūrah is not accidental. Verse 45 gave the two acts that hold the believer in truth — recitation and prayer; verse 46 gives the manner in which he conducts the argument that truth provokes. It follows too that a chapter which has spent forty verses on the end of those who denied has reached the point where it must say how to speak to those who have not yet denied but who possess scripture: Makkah's Jews and Christians, and by extension every community that came before with a book. The *khitāb*, "you argue," is addressed to the whole community, and the sūrah's own practice with the Quraysh has been the model: Abraham in 29:16–24 reasoned ("my father," "you worship," "do you then not fear?"), and the passage on the People of the Book now generalises the method as a rule.

**"Bi'llatī Hiya Aḥsan": The Grammar of the Superlative**

The phrase is one of the Qur'an's finely pitched constructions. *Ḥusn* is the word for beauty and for goodness in act and intention, and its elative *aḥsan* means "more beautiful/better"; here it is embedded in a relative clause in the feminine, *bi'llatī hiya aḥsan* — "by the manner that is the best" — and the commentators, beginning with al-Ṭabarī, explain that the feminine refers back to an understood *ṭarīqa* or *khilāla*, "a way" or "a course," so that the construction has the force of a superlative while retaining a comparative shape: you are to argue by the way that is better than any other way open to you, and, as the sūrah's parallel in 16:125 specifies, "call to the way of your Lord with wisdom and good exhortation, and argue with them by the way that is better (*bi'llatī hiya aḥsan*)". Al-Zamakhsharī remarks on 16:125 that the comparative in the second clause sharpens the command: even in an argument, and therefore in an argument where the other side is wrong, the better register remains obligatory. The source commentary on our verse records al-Ṭabarī's gloss: "argue with them by that which is fairest — calling them by God's signs and drawing evidence from God's proofs," and adds what al-Ṭabarī takes to be included in the rule: "even if they speak badly, the Muslim speaks well."

**"Except Those of Them Who Do Wrong"**

The exception is the verse's only permission to alter the register, and its extent was debated. Ibn Kathīr takes "those who do wrong among them" as those who turn away from the signs brought by God's prophets and persist in stubborn arrogance; al-Ṭabarī takes them as those who fight the Muslims and refuse a treaty. Both readings are restrictions, not expansions: the exception applies to conduct, not to creed, so that a Jew or Christian who errs in belief is still argued with "in the best manner," whereas aggression against persons or the abrogation of the agreement removes the protection that makes argument the mode. Al-Qurṭubī discusses the related case of those who abuse the Prophet ﷺ, and the verse's own principle is worked out in 6:108, "do not revile those they call upon apart from God, lest they revile God out of spite and without knowledge" — the prohibition of provocation is grounded, in that verse, on the certainty that insult only generates insult. On our verse's exception the jurists built their treatment of the *muḥārib* and of the insult to the religion (*sabb*), where the ruler's discretion replaces the debater's courtesy; the important thing for the reader is that verse 46's default is courtesy, and the exception's logic is about harm, not about the strength of the opponent's error.

**"Not Abrogated": al-Ṭabarī Against the Claim**

Some early authorities held that 29:46 had been abrogated by the sword-verse, 9:29 ("fight those who do not believe in God nor in the Last Day, nor forbid what God and His Messenger forbid, nor follow the religion of truth, from among those who were given the Book, until they pay the tribute willingly, humbled"). Al-Ṭabarī argues against this and insists that a verse is not treated as abrogated without decisive proof, and the argument is not merely technical: 9:29 concerns the conditions of war and of political submission, while 29:46 concerns the manner of speech, and a command about the rules of engagement does not repeal a command about how a proposition is defended. The consequence for the community reading the sūrah is that the etiquette of verse 46 remains in force, and the tradition's practice matches it: the Prophet's ﷺ treatment of the delegation of Najrān, who were permitted to pray in his mosque at their hours (an account transmitted in Ibn Isḥāq's *Sīra* and used by the jurists on this point), and the standing instruction on how to answer their scriptural claims:

> *"Do not affirm what the People of the Book say and do not deny them; say instead: we believe in what has been sent down to us and what has been sent down to you, and our God and your God is one, and to Him we submit."* (from Abū Hurayra, in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī; cited by al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī and Ibn Kathīr on this verse)

The hadith is a practical rule for a community that cannot verify another community's scriptures: neither to endorse what has been corrupted nor to dismiss what was once revealed, but to locate the point of agreement — one God — and to state the difference in the open as a matter of belief rather than of insult.

**The Statement of Common Ground and Its Exact Wording**

*"Our God and your God is one"* is not a general endorsement of every proposition the other side holds, and the verse's own grammar shows what it is. The *ḥikāya* ("say") governs three clauses: belief in both revelations, the oneness of the God addressed, and the community's own submission — and each is a claim, not a concession. The first puts the two revelations side by side as objects of belief, which means the Muslim affirms the *origin* of your book while disputing the *present text's* sufficiency; the second affirms a single referent, not a single religion, and it is the premise of the argument, since two people cannot argue about God if they are not talking about the same being; and the third, "to Him we submit," is a self-description that the sūrah has already made the definition of prophetic religion generally: "say: my prayer, my offering, my life and my death are for God, Lord of the worlds" (6:162), and Abraham's and Jacob's dying words in 2:133, "we will worship your God and the God of your fathers, Abraham and Ishmael and Isaac — one God — and to Him we submit." The formula of 3:64, addressed to the People of the Book in a Medinan context, is the same method in a shorter form: *"Say: people of the Book, come to a word just between us and you, that we worship none but God, that we associate nothing with Him, and that we do not take one another as lords apart from God"* — a verse this sūrah's own spider-parable (29:41) explicates, since "taking lords apart from God" is the exact fault the parable diagnosed. The source commentary rightly notes the parallel with 2:136 and 3:84, where the list of what is believed includes what was sent down to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob and the Tribes, to Moses and Jesus — an affirmation whose scope is theological history, not a ratification of every later sect.

**"Some of the People of the Book Believe" — the Reason the Verse Is Not a Polemic**

The next verse will say that those given the Book believe in it and that among them are some who believe (29:47; cf. 3:199, "among the People of the Book there are those who believe in God and in what has been sent down to you and what has been sent down to them"), and verse 46 is written with that in view: the manner of argument is "best" partly because the audience is not a single mass of opponents but contains, in the sūrah's own words elsewhere, "a community that guides by the truth and by it acts justly" (7:181). The Qur'an's sharpest language about the People of the Book is, on the reading of the commentators, directed at specific parties — those who concealed what God revealed, who traded it for a small price (2:174), and who claimed "the Fire will not touch us for numbered days" (2:80, 3:24). This is why the ethics of the verse survived the change in political circumstances: the command concerns speech, and the tradition records its application in the classical period both in inter-faith disputation in the courts of Baghdad and in the ordinary dealings of Muslim-majority towns, where the neighbour's difference was a fact of life to be handled by the etiquette of 29:46 rather than by the law of 9:29.

**Practical Limits and the Argument's Purpose**

Two clarifications keep the verse from being misused in either direction. First, "in the best manner" does not require the debater to abandon his conclusion, and the same Book that commands the manner commands the content. The Qur'an pairs the two without embarrassment: "argue with them by the way that is better" (16:125) sits beside "say: you will not be asked about what we have committed, and we will not be asked about what you do" (34:25), and beside the sūrah's own clause of disengagement: "ours are our deeds and yours are your deeds; no argument between us and you; God gathers between us, and to Him is the journey" (42:15). The etiquette is not a concession that the outcome is unimportant; it is a rule that the outcome is in God's hands and the manner is in yours. Second, "the best manner" is not a technique for winning. The manuals of disputation written later by the tradition's own scholars — al-Ghazālī's treatise on the etiquette of inquiry and argument is the best-known of them — insist that the purpose of an argument is the recovery of the truth and not the defeat of the opponent, that the debater must state the other's position in its strongest form before answering it, and that rejoicing at an opponent's confusion is a defect of the act. Al-Tustarī's interior reading of the same verse, quoted in the ḥaqāʾiq literature, goes further and is consistent with this sūrah's whole treatment of the self: the one who argues "in the best manner" is the one who has first stopped arguing with God's decree about himself. Ibn Taymiyyah, who wrote extensively on disputation with the People of the Book, states the discipline from inside the tradition: the one who argues must argue with what is better in the very act of showing the other's position to be weaker, and must not attribute to an opponent what the opponent does not say. In a world where argument is a sport, verse 46 is one of the Qur'an's most demanding commands, because it removes the debater's two pleasures — the hard line at the other's expense, and the relief of declaring the matter closed.

verse 46 is one of the Qur'an's most demanding commands, because it takes away the two temptations every debater has — the pleasure of a hard line at the other's expense, and the relief of claiming that the whole argument is already over.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:47

> **Thus We sent down to you the Book, and those to whom We gave the Book believe in it; and among them are some who believe in it. And none reject the signs of God except the disbelievers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Thus" — the Manner of the Descent Matches the Manner of the World**

*wa-kadhālika anzalnā ilayka'l-kitāb.* The opening particle of likeness reaches back over the previous three verses, and the exegetes treat it as more than a connector: just as We made the parables and set them forth (29:43), just as the heavens and the earth were created *bi'l-ḥaqq* (29:44), so too — in the same manner, out of the same truth — We sent down the Book. The Book belongs to the order of the world's making, not outside it; both are acts of the same sender, and the verse's verb is chosen accordingly. In the Qur'an's own vocabulary, *anzala* is used for the sending down of the Book as a whole and for the revelation that comes with what it contains — "We sent down iron in which is might" (57:25), "He sent down the Book to you with truth" (3:3) — while *nazzala*, the form of repeated and staged descent, is used where the gradual unfolding is in view: "a Qur'an We divided into parts so that you would recite it to people over a long period, and We sent it down in stages" (17:106; cf. 76:23). Here, at the moment when the sūrah states the fact of the Book as evidence, the verb of sending is the simple one: the Book that is set before the disputants of verse 46 is a single, publicly available given, recited in their hearing (29:51).

**Two Subjects, and Why Both Matter**

The verse contains two clauses of belief and must be read slowly. *Alladhīna ātaynāhumu'l-kitāba hum yuʾminūna bihi* — those to whom the previous scripture was given believe in this one. And *wa-minhum man yuʾminu bihi* — and among them are some who believe in it. That the second clause is not a repetition is shown by the commentators' refusal to merge them: most (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī) take the first to be the Jews and Christians who became Muslim — ʿAbdullāh ibn Salām and his companions, Kaʿb al-Aḥbār, Salmān al-Fārisī, Waraqa ibn Nawfal before him and the Najrān delegation's scholars after — and read the second either as the rest of the People of the Book who acknowledged the truth without yet embracing the practice, or, as al-Zamakhsharī suggests, as the people of Makkah, "some of whom believed in the Qur'an while many did not." Whichever reference is preferred, the verse's structure is the same: it insists that the acceptance of the Qur'an comes from inside the communities that were in a position to check it, and that acceptance does not arrive as a mass transfer of nations but as individuals. This is the sūrah's own theology of trial applied to the history of belief: no people is believed into or disbelieved into as a bloc (cf. 29:48's careful "among them are some"), and the verse that ends with "none reject Our signs except the disbelievers" (29:47) is followed by its near-twin "none reject Our signs except the transgressors" (29:49), so that the two clauses bracket the section with a single assertion: rejection is always personal, and never innocent.

**The Belief of the Competent as Evidence**

The verse's argumentative function is easy to miss because it looks like a piece of comfort. It is also a proof, and the type of proof is one that the tradition of *kalām* and the practice of argument both recognise: the concession of a qualified opponent. A community that possesses a scripture and the sciences of its transmission is in a position to test a claim to revelation, and its belief is therefore evidence of a kind that an untested acceptance is not. The Qur'an states this repeatedly: *"those to whom We gave the Book recognise him as they recognise their sons"* (2:146); *"and a witness from among the Children of Israel has testified to the like of it and believed, while you were proud? God does not guide a wrongdoing people"* (46:10) — the reproach in that verse falls on pride, not on a shortage of evidence; and *"they do not call you a liar, but the wrongdoers deny the signs of God"* (6:33). And the effect on those who hear is described in terms this sūrah's audience would have known: *"and when they hear what was sent down to the Messenger, you see their eyes overflow with tears because of the truth they have recognised; they say: our Lord, we believe, so write us among the witnesses"* (5:83). To this the same passage attaches the reason belief is credible in such people: *"and why should we not believe in God and in the truth that has come to us, while we hope that our Lord will admit us with the righteous people?"* (5:84).

**Not a Ratification: What the Believing People of the Book Are Believing**

Verse 47 must be read with 3:199, which the source commentary rightly cites as its interpretation: *"and truly among the People of the Book there are those who believe in God and in what has been sent down to you and what has been sent down to them, humble before God"* — and with the sūrah's own verse 46, whose declaration of common ground ("our God and your God is one") is not an endorsement of every doctrine the partner holds. What the verse attests is a specific act: the recognition, from inside the scriptural traditions, that the Qur'an comes from the God those traditions worship. The consequence is the double statement the sūrah keeps making: the previous revelation and this one have a single sender, so the Qur'an confirms and guards what came before (*muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi wa-muhayminun ʿalayh*, 5:48), and at the same time the belief of the knowledgeable is what makes rejection inexcusable. The source commentary notes in the same place that this need not be limited to those who became Muslim, adducing 3:199's description of people who "would not have rejected God's signs and thus would not have been those who opposed the Muslims" — and points the reader to the treatment of 3:110–15 and 5:82–83, where the Qur'an divides the People of the Book into a believing remnant, a wrong-doing party, and a middle group. That differentiation is the ground on which 29:46's ethics of argument is possible: a rule for dealing with "the People of the Book" in general only makes sense in a scripture that insists, here, that the category is not uniform.

**"None Reject the Signs Except the Disbeliever" — the Verdict Clause**

The final clause is the sūrah's recurring conclusion and has the shape of a verdict delivered after evidence: *wa-mā yajhadu bi-āyātika illā'l-kāfirūn*. *Jahada* is stronger than "disbelieve": it is to deny what one knows, to contest a matter that has become plain, and the root is used for a denial that amounts to a contest (cf. 43:78 — "and whoever is blinded to the remembrance of the All-Merciful, for him We assign a devil"). The Qur'an uses it of those who knew and denied — "they do not call you a liar, but the wrongdoers deny the signs of God" (6:33) and here of every one who rejects after the parable, the Book, and the competent witness. Al-Rāzī's observation on this family of verses is that the phrase eliminates the last refuge of the sceptic — the claim that he simply did not have enough: when the sign, the recitation, the testimony of the knowledgeable and the frailty of the alternative refuges are all on the table, the remaining position is *kufr*, which the sūrah has consistently described as an act of the will rather than of the intellect (29:3, "God knows the liars"; 29:23, "who does more wrong than one who invents a lie against God").

**What the Verse Gives the Reader**

Three things, in order. To the believer it gives reassurance of the kind the sūrah has been dispensing throughout: the Book's truth does not rest on the size of the following but on the quality of the witnesses, and the number of Makkans who believed in the first years was small (cf. the source introduction's account of the dispute about whether these verses came before or after the number increased). To the person of scripture it gives an invitation phrased as a fact: the resources you already have are enough to recognise this, and the verse's list of those who did is a precedent, not a threat. And to the disputatious it gives a limit: the argument is not with "the People of the Book" as a mass but with individuals, some of whom already believe what the argument is about; the "best manner" of verse 46 is owed to those who are being addressed, and the verdict in this verse falls on those who, knowing, contest. Finally, the reader of the whole sūrah notices where this clause of belief in the Book sits: one verse after the command to recite and pray, one verse before the statement that the Prophet neither read nor wrote. The Book is not established here by the miracle it excludes but by the witnesses it attracts — and the next verse will state why the second kind of evidence cannot be corrupted by suspicion of human authorship.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:48

> **And you did not recite, before it, any book, nor did you write it with your right hand; otherwise the makers of false claims would have doubted.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Argument Drawn from a Biography Everyone Knew**

*wa-mā kunta talwū min qablihi min kitābin wa-lā takhuṭṭuhu bi-yamīnik, idhan la-irtāba'l-mubṭilūn.* The sūrah's case for the Qur'an has, until here, been cumulative: the parable, the created world, the testimony of the knowledgeable. Verse 48 introduces a different kind of proof, and the source commentary states its point without hedging: "the Prophet had no part in authoring the Qur'an, one of the fundamental beliefs of all schools of Islamic thought." The proof is a negative fact, public and verifiable, and it is offered to an audience that was in a position to check it because it had lived with the man. This is why the verse says "before it" — *min qablihi* — and not "in your life": the claim is that there is no prior text and no prior act of composition, and the Qur'an elsewhere puts the same appeal in the form of a question addressed to the whole city: *"have you considered? If it is from God and you disbelieve in it, and a witness from among the Children of Israel has testified to the like of it and been proud? God does not guide a wrongdoing people"* — The verse is the sūrah's own wording of the appeal: *"say: if God had willed, I would not have recited it to you, nor would He have made it known to you; I remained among you a lifetime before it — do you not reason?"* (10:16), on which the commentaries agree that "a lifetime" is the forty years before the revelation, and to which the Book adds, in the same place, *"say: God is sufficient as witness between me and you, and whoever has knowledge of the Book"* (13:43). A man's previous sixty years in a small trading city, known to his rivals, is a datum no adversary can dismiss, and the sūrah counts on that.

**"Any Book," "With Your Right Hand": The Two Skills Denied**
The verse denies two abilities in a deliberately chosen order. *Mā kunta talwū min qablihi min kitābin* — you were not, before this, in the position of reciting any book: not a scripture, not a learned text, not a text of your own; the negation is of the practice of recitation, which for an Arab meant the whole transmission of knowledge, since what a person knew he *had*, that is, recited. *Wa-lā takhuṭṭuhu bi-yamīnik* — you did not write it with your right hand: the hand is specified because writing was done with the right and the phrase *bi-yamīnik* gives the idiom its emphatic force, in the way the Arabs used it in oaths. The point of denying both is that these were, in that society, the only two routes by which a man could come to possess a text he had not heard: reading, which was rare in the Ḥijāz, and writing, rarer still, and the sūrah excludes each in turn. The result is a specific kind of *ummī* — not a man who lacked education in general, for the Prophet ﷺ had the fullest possible education in the affairs of his people and in the moral knowledge of his tribe, but one who had no access to textual sources, and this is what the verse says, and what the *ummī* of 7:157 ("the Messenger, the unlettered prophet, whom they find written in the Torah and the Gospel with them") names. The Prophet's ﷺ own community's self-description, recorded in the authentic collections, preserves the same usage: *"we are an umma ummiyya; we neither write nor count"* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, on fasting and the moon).

**"Otherwise the Makers of False Claims Would Have Doubted"**

*idhan la-irtāba'l-mubṭilūn*: the particle *idhan* ("then, in that case") introduces the counterfactual, and the verb *irtāba* — to fall into suspicion, from *rayba*, the doubt that unsettles — identifies the party whose doubt would have been legitimate. Al-Ṭabarī takes "the makers of false claims" as the Quraysh, and al-Zamakhsharī as the opposing party among the People of the Book; either way the sense is the same, and it is subtle. The verse does not claim that unletteredness proves revelation; it claims that if the man had been a reader and a scribe, the charge of human composition would have acquired a foothold, and the doubters would have had something to point at. The tradition reads this as a statement of divine care for the quality of the evidence: God preserved the argument from the only objection that could never be answered, by preserving His messenger from the only accomplishments that would have made the objection stick. The Quraysh's own alternative is quoted in the next breath of the sūrah's world — *"they say: legends of the ancients, which he has had written down, and they are recited to him morning and evening"* (25:5) — and the answer to it, given in the same passage, is the one our verse supplies: *say: it was sent down by the One who knows the secret in the heavens and the earth* (25:6). What is remarkable is that the accusation itself concedes the fact: a man who needed to have things written down for him was known not to write; and the sūrah also excludes the third route a self-made text could take, poetry — "and We did not teach him poetry, nor does it become him; it is only a reminder and a clear Book" (36:69; cf. 26:224–225, "the poets are followed by the deviant").

**The Classical Argument, and the Mutazilite Philosophers' Use of It**

Mutazilite and Ashʿarite theologians both made this verse one of the pillars of the demonstration of prophethood, because it supplies a premise neither school had to derive: the impossibility of natural acquisition. Al-Jāḥiẓ's *Bayān* and the *dalāʾil* literature of the fourth/tenth century press the argument further with the second half of the premise, which the verse leaves implicit — that the text produced by this man is of a kind that could only come from learning: it legislates, it narrates earlier scriptures with accuracy, it corrects them, it founds a social order, and it carries a legal and eschatological vocabulary the desert had not developed. Al-Rāzī handles the pair in *al-Irshād* and in his tafsīr: an *ummī* of forty years producing a book of this sort is not explicable by the ordinary gradations of talent, and since the alternative explanations — mendacity, madness, borrowing — are each excluded by his biography and by the content, the remaining explanation is that it is *tanzīl*. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr present the same case with less formality and more quotation, and both note that the verse is the scriptural answer to a question the sūrah itself raises two verses later: *"why have no signs been sent down upon him from his Lord?"* (29:50) — because the sign of the Book in the life of its unlettered bearer is the sign, and it is of the kind that can be investigated (cf. 29:51, "does it not suffice them that We sent down to you the Book, recited to them?").

**The Epistemology of a Negative Fact, and Its Modern Shape**

There is a lesson in the verse's logic for anyone who reasons about evidence. Provenance is established as much by what is absent as by what is present: a claim about the origin of a text is tested against the biography of its author, and a public biography constrains the available hypotheses. The verse's argument is therefore not an appeal to mystery but an appeal to the audience's own records — and the Muslim tradition kept those records with an attention that is the reason the verse has remained checkable: the *Sīra* of Ibn Isḥāq and Ibn Hishām, the biographical dictionaries of the *ṭabaqāt* and *ism al-rijāl* literature, and the *dalāʾil al-nubuwwa* books of Bayhaqī and Abū Nuʿaym are, whatever their limitations, a corpus assembled to answer exactly this kind of question, including which Companions could read, who taught whom, and how many of the Quraysh could write at all — the report transmitted in the *sīra* literature that one of the prisoners taken at Badr was released on condition that he teach ten of the children of the Anṣār to write, literacy being rare enough for that to be considered a ransom. And the verse's counterfactual is a rule for any claimant: an argument should be conducted so that the strongest objection to it is one that can be met, and the verse's claim is that God arranged the prophecy so that the fatal objection was impossible by construction.

**What the Verse Asks of the Reader Here and Now**

Three applications close the passage (29:44–48). For the sceptic, the verse relocates the burden: the Qur'an does not ask to be accepted because its bearer was supernatural, but because the most economical explanation of an illiterate man's book, given everything Makkah knew, is the one the Book itself states; the same Book elsewhere invites the test that follows from this — "if it is not from God, they will find much contradiction in it" (4:82), and "produce a sūrah like it and call your witnesses besides God, if you are truthful" (11:13). For the believer, the verse supplies the ground of the reverence with which the tradition handled the text: what he recites is not a man's composition, so its rulings are not negotiable as opinions are, and this is why the sūrah joins the recitation to the prayer (29:45) rather than to an argument. And for the community, the verse forbids a certain dishonesty in the opposite direction: the religion cannot be defended by pretending that its founder was a scholar, by inventing a library he never had, or by presenting the Qur'an as a summary of prior literature, since the verse's whole case is that there was nothing of the sort before it. The claim is austere and it is exactly the kind the sūrah has been making since verse 2: nothing here can be taken on trust in the way a web-house is trusted, and everything rests on what is open to inspection — the man, the book, and the frailty of every refuge except the One who sent it.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:49

> **Nay, but it is clear signs in the breasts of those who have been given knowledge. And none reject Our signs except the transgressors.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Nay" — the Correction of a Demand**

*bal huwa āyātun bayyinātin fī ṣudūri'lladhīna ūtū'l-ʿilma.* The verse opens with *bal*, the particle of correction and transition, and what it corrects is the expectation produced by the previous demand for signs: the Quraysh wanted something sent down *upon him*, and the sūrah answers that something was sent down — but it is not in the sky, in a stone, or in a written scroll. It is in *ṣudūr*, breasts. The pronoun *huwa* is masculine, though its predicate *āyātun* is feminine, and the grammarians (al-Zajjāj, and al-Ṭabarī before him in effect) explain the mismatch by the referent: "it" is the Book of verse 48, and the feminine predicate describes what the Book consists of. So the verse's claim is not "there are no signs" but "the sign is of a kind you did not reckon," and the kind — verses established in the memory and understanding of knowing people — is deliberately set against the other kind of divine address the Qur'an reports as having been requested and refused: *"why was not an angel sent down upon him? If We had sent down an angel, the matter would have been judged, and then they would not be reprieved"* (6:8), followed by *"and if We had made him an angel, We would have made him a man and confused for them what they confuse"* (6:9). The signs that arrive from outside compel; the signs that reside in breasts persuade, and the sūrah's theology of trial (29:2–3) has already fixed which kind it is in the business of providing.

**"In the Breasts": The Seat of the Clear**

The *ṣadr*, the breast, is in Arabic the container of what is held, and the Qur'an uses the idiom literally and figuretively in the same breath: Moses prays "expand for me my breast" (20:25), God "strengthens the heart" of Moses's mother so that she keeps her faith (28:10), and the disbelievers' breasts are "tight and constricted" for the message (6:125; 39:22). To say that the clear signs are *in the breasts* of those given knowledge therefore asserts two things at once: that the Qur'an is carried intact in living memory, and that it is understood by minds that have been opened to it. The first of these is the fact the verse is best known for, and the tradition's practice confirms it as a fulfilment rather than a promise: the Book was preserved, recited, and expounded by people who "had been given knowledge" (the gloss of al-Jurjānī, Ibn Kathīr and al-Zamakhsharī on the phrase), and the class of *ḥuffāẓ* to which the verse is widely taken to refer is the reason the text has never depended on manuscripts — Ubayy ibn Kaʿb, Zayd ibn Thābit, ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd and Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī are named in the traditions as its bearers, and the Prophet's ﷺ saying "the best of you are those who learn the Qur'an and teach it" (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, Book of the Virtues of the Qur'an, from ʿUthmān) makes the carrying of it a work in its own right. The second is the epistemological point: the "clearness" of the signs is located where clarity is a property of a mind and not only of ink. Al-Saʿdī's gloss on the verse captures both: the Qur'an is settled in the hearts of the knowers, which is the most perfect form of guarding, since what is in the breast cannot be burned, lost, or forged.

**"Those Who Have Been Given Knowledge": Who Is Intended**

Two referents are argued in the commentaries, and the verse sustains both. Al-Jurjānī, Ibn Kathīr and al-Zamakhsharī take the phrase in its plain sense: the people of knowledge among this community who carry, recite, and expound the Book. al-Ṭabarī also reports, and al-Jurjānī and Ibn Kathīr allow, that "those given knowledge" is here the People of the Book who found in their own scriptures clear signs of the Prophet (cf. 26:197: "and it is in the scrolls of the earlier peoples"; 61:6, where the Children of Israel are told of "a messenger coming after me, whose name is Aḥmad"). The double reference is a feature, not an ambiguity: the same verse says that the sign is intelligible both to the Muslim memoriser and to the scriptural scholar, and it makes each of them a witness against the other's sceptics. The gradient the sūrah has been laying down makes this the climax of the passage: at 29:47 it said that "those to whom We gave the Book believe in it"; at 29:48 it said that the bearer of the Book had neither read nor written; now it says that the Book consists of clear signs *in the breasts of those given knowledge* — that is, its authentication is not in a prior manuscript nor in a physical miracle but in the minds it has taken possession of, and the class it names is defined by what they have been given (ūtū, passive: this is a gift, not an achievement, which is why the verse also rebukes the person who refuses it).

**"None Reject Our Signs Except the Transgressors"**

The verdict clause repeats that of verse 47 with a change that carries the weight: there it was "none reject Our signs except the disbelievers (kāfirūn)"; here it is "except the transgressors (fāsiqūn)." The distinction between *kufr* and *fisq* is exactly the point the sūrah has been arguing since verse 3. *Fisq* is from *f-s-q*, "to slip out" — the date slips out of its shell, and the lexicographers note that the mouse is called *fuwaysiqa* for the same reason, because it slips out of its hole to do damage — and the *fāsiq* is the one who has slipped out of the order he recognises; which is why the Qur'an uses the word of the person who knows the truth and does not act on it, and why the tradition's fiqh borrowed it for the believer who persists in sin. That is the class the verse here says rejects the signs: not the person overwhelmed by insufficient evidence, but the one who has departed from the position the evidence has already granted him. The sūrah has used the word in its only other appearance in the chapter for the people of Lot, in the angels' own verdict: *"We shall bring down upon the people of this town a punishment from heaven, because they were transgressing (fāsiqūn)"* (29:34), so that the rejection of verse 49 is put on the same moral plane as the behaviour that brought stones from the sky. Al-Rāzī generalises it: the love of the appetites and of rank displaces the *ʿilm* that would otherwise compel assent, and hence "the only one who denies is the one who has slipped"; and the Qur'an's own statement of the mechanism is elsewhere unambiguous: *"they deny it, while their own selves are convinced of it, out of wickedness and pride"* (27:14).

**Why the Verse Is a Rebuke of the Demand for a Sign, and Not a Refusal of Evidence**

Taken together, the two clauses of 29:49 complete the sūrah's answer to the sign-seekers of verse 50 before verse 50 even arrives: the evidence exists, and it is of a kind that only a moral fault could make a person ignore. This is not fideism; the Qur'an's epistemic claims, as this section of the sūrah states them, are deliberately modest and deliberately checkable: the Book is recited (29:51), its bearer's biography is public (29:48), its contents are clear in the minds of those who know them (29:49), and the scriptures of others attest to it (29:47). What the sūrah refuses is not investigation but the demand that investigation be replaced by a demonstration that leaves no room for choice — which is the demand the next verse will put in the Quraysh's own mouth, and to which the answer will again be that the signs "are with God alone." For the reader, the practical lesson is in the last clause: when a person finds himself unable to accept what he already understands, the diagnostic question the sūrah puts is not "what more do I need to know?" but "what am I committed to that the knowledge would overturn?" — which is the same question the sūrah posed at verse 10 about the man who fears "the trial of men" as he should fear God's punishment. The transgressor is the one whose *ʿilm* is intact and whose conduct has slipped, and the sūrah's cure for him has already been named at verse 45: recite and establish the prayer.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:50

> **And they say: why have no signs been sent down on him from his Lord? Say: the signs are with God alone, and I am only a clear warner.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question the Quraysh Keep Asking**

*wa-qālū lawlā unzila ʿalayhi āyātun min rabbihi.* The demand for signs is the most frequently reported objection in the Qur'an, and this sūrah puts it here because the preceding verses have just finished describing a sign of a kind the objectors did not want: a Book borne in the breasts of knowers, coming from a man who had neither read nor written. The list of parallel demands is long and uniform: *"we will not believe your ascension until you send down for us a book we read"* (17:93), with the preceding list: a spring gushing from the earth, gardens of palms and grapes with rivers bursting through them, the sky falling in pieces as he had claimed, God and the angels brought before them face to face (17:90–92); *"what is the matter with this messenger that he eats food and walks in the markets? If only an angel had been sent down on him, to be a warner with him — or a treasure thrown down to him, or a garden he could eat from"* (25:7); *"why has not a sign been sent down on him from his Lord?"* (20:133, which the source commentary pairs with our verse); and the same demand in sūrah Hūd, with the answer that fits this one: *"why was not a treasure thrown down on him, or an angel come with him? — you are only a warner, and God is over all things a guardian"* (11:12), and in sūrah Yūnus: *"say: the Unseen belongs to God, so wait; I am with you among those who wait"* (10:20). The particle *lawlā* is a reproach or a wish — "if only," "why not" — and the plural *āyātun* shows that one sign will not do: the demand is for an accumulating demonstration, of the sort that would end the matter, and the sūrah's answer is that this is not the sort of transaction revelation is.

**"The Signs Are with God Alone" — Why That Is an Answer**

*qula inna'mā'l-āyātu ʿinda'llāhi.* To locate the signs "with God" is not to withhold evidence but to state whose prerogative it is and what it would mean for Him to use it. Two consequences the commentators draw here are decisive. First, a sign sent at the request of a challenger would be a verdict rather than an invitation, and the Qur'an says so: *"and if We had sent down an angel, the matter would have been judged, then they would not be respite"* (6:8); a miracle given on demand removes the possibility of faith, because faith is what the sūrah defined at verse 2 — a belief that holds when it costs something and before it is vindicated. Second, the respite the objectors are enjoying is itself the reason no verdict-sign has come: *"and God would not punish them while you are among them"* (8:33), a verse this sūrah has already invoked (29:33) for the people of Lot, where the request "bring on us what you promise us" was answered by the departure of the intercessor and the arrival of the wind. Al-Zamakhsharī's note on "the signs are with God" in 6:109 is the sharpest form of the reasoning: *"they swear by God their most sincere oaths — if a sign came to them they would surely believe it. Say: the signs are with God only; and what will convey to you that when it comes they will not believe it? And We shall turn their hearts and their eyes aside, as they did not believe in it the first time, and We shall leave them in their rebellion wandering blind"* (6:109–110). The verse's implication is uncomfortable and intended: the person who says "give me a sign" has already decided what a sign is, and would not accept the one he gets, and the Qur'an's most detailed report of a sign granted on request — the she-camel of Thamūd, "this is God's she-camel, a sign for you, so leave her eating God's land" (11:64) — ends with the hamstrung animal and three days of grace (11:65–66).

**"I Am Only a Clear Warner" — the Function Stated**

*wa-innamā anā nadhīrun mubīn.* The self-description is fixed in a class of verses where the Prophet ﷺ is told exactly what his office is and is not: "I am only a clear warner" (26:214, 46:9, 67:26, 38:70, 44:18; "you are only a warner" 13:7, 79:45; "you are not a watcher over them" 88:22; "perhaps you will kill yourself with griefs because they do not believe" 18:6, 26:3). And the paired disclaimer: *"say: I do not tell you that with me are God's treasuries, nor that I know the Unseen, nor that I am an angel; I only follow what is revealed to me"* (6:50; 7:188, "I have no power for myself over benefit or harm except as God wills"). The source commentary notes the cluster, and it is worth noticing what our verse omits: no rebuke of the objectors, no threat, no concession of the request — only the re-statement of the boundaries of the prophetic function, because the demand for a sign is a category error about that function. A warner's authority is not the authority of a demonstrator who can be commissioned by his audience; a warner's clarity is the property of the message rather than the power of the messenger, hence *mubīn*: the clarity is a property of what is delivered, not a claim about the one who delivers it, and the same distinction runs through the Book — "remind; you are not a watcher over them" (88:21–22), "upon you is only the conveyance" (3:20; 5:92; 24:54), "guidance has become distinct from error" (2:256).

**What the Qur'an Offers Instead of the Sign They Demanded**

The sūrah's own answer, in the two verses on either side, is that the demanded sign has been given in the form that can bear weight: "does it not suffice them that We sent down to you the Book, recited to them? in that is a mercy and a reminder for people who believe" (29:51); and, in the passage we have just traversed, that the Book is clear signs in the breasts of the knowing (29:49), sent down upon a man whose unlettered life is public knowledge (29:48). The tradition of the Prophet's ﷺ practice confirms that this was his position and not a polemic: when his uncle demanded proof he offered the Qur'an; when asked for the splitting of the moon the report in the two Ṣaḥīḥs says it was shown, and the Qur'anic comment on that event is quoted in the same breath by the exegetes here — *"the Hour drew near and the moon split, and if they see a sign they turn away and say: passing magic"* (54:1–2). The last clause is the sūrah's diagnosis and the reason "signs are with God": the same people who asked for the moon asked also for a book they could touch (6:7), and having the thing was not going to change the disposition. Ibn Kathīr draws the practical lesson that the verse is a permission to stop chasing: let the demanders ask, since the burden on the warner is clarity, not compliance.

**The Hadith That Closes the Question**

The strongest statement of the sūrah's position in the prophetic reports is the one the source commentary quotes on 29:51 and which belongs at least as much here:

> *"There is no prophet among the prophets but that he was given what made people believe in him; what I was given is revelation that God revealed to me, and I hope to be the most followed of them on the Day of Resurrection."* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Hurayra)

The hadith makes two claims the sūrah makes: that miracles are a prophet's credential but not his argument, and that the distinctive credential of this prophecy is a text — which continues to act when every physical sign would have perished. Al-Tustarī, reading the verse in the ḥaqāʾiq literature, puts the interior form of the same point: the one who demands that God be demonstrated has asked to be relieved of the need for faith, and the signs "with God" are given to the heart that is already soft — hence the sūrah's restriction, "a reminder for a people who believe" (29:51). For a modern reader the verse is the antidote to two opposite errors that share one premise: the claim that religion is refuted by the absence of the miracle the sceptic specifies, and the claim that a religion needs the spectacular to be credible. The Qur'an refuses both by refusing the premise: the divine policy is that signs come when God wills, in the forms He chooses, at the cost to the observer of having to decide — and the Prophet's office is to make the warning clear, which is why the sūrah never lets its audience forget that the next verse's Book "is recited to them" (29:51) in their own language, where they can be held to it.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:51

> **Does it not suffice them that We sent down to you the Book, recited to them? Surely in that is a mercy and a reminder for a people who believe.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rhetorical Question That Ends the Argument About Signs**

*a-wa-lam yakfihim annā anzalnā ʿalayka'l-kitāba yutlā ʿalayhim.* The interrogative *a-* prefixed to *wa-lam* is not a request for information but a rebuke that expects one answer. Its closest relative in the whole Book is the verse that will supply the answer to the next: "is it not enough that your Lord is witness over all things?" (41:53) — the same verb *k-f-y*, the same construction, the same rhetorical appeal to what the objectors already possess. Here the rebuke lands on the sign-seekers of 29:50. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss states the point plainly: "is it not enough for them, as proof and as sign, that We sent down to you this Book which they hear recited to them, containing the account of what came before them and of what comes after them, so that they would believe in it?" The commentary of Ibn Kathīr adds the parallel the source quotes: "they say: why has he not brought us a sign from his Lord? Has there not come to them the clear proof of what is in the former scriptures?" (20:133), so that the verse is a summons to a case already documented. The force of *yakfī* (to suffice) deserves emphasis because it is the word the whole debate turns on: sufficiency is not a property of the evidence in isolation but of evidence in relation to a purpose, and the sūrah's claim is that the purpose — belief, guidance, deliverance on the Day of trial — is achieved by the Book, so that whatever else the objectors might have got from a miracle they do not need.

**"Recited to Them": the Sign That Can Be Inspected**

The participle *yutlā ʿalayhim*, "recited to them," is the verse's quiet rebuttal of the demand for a spectacular sign, and it carries two facts. First, the medium is public: the Qur'an is not a private revelation to be believed on the Prophet's say-so but a text performed in the hearing of the people it addresses — "We did not send any messenger except to be obeyed by God's permission" (4:64), and "say: have you seen what God sent down to you as provision and you made some of it forbidden and some permitted? Say: did God permit you, or are you inventing lies against God?" (10:59) — so the content is open to interrogation, as the sūrah's own invitation in verse 46 assumes. Second, the language is theirs: "a clear Arabic Qur'an" (15:1), "in the tongue of the man they hint at it is foreign, and this is a clear Arabic tongue" (16:103), "and if We had made it a Qur'an in a foreign language, they would have said: were not its signs detailed? A foreigner and an Arab?" (41:44). A sign in one's own tongue, recited in one's own market, is a sign of a different order from a stone or a storm, because it can be understood, obeyed, and remembered; and the sūrah's choice to define the miracle as speech explains the verse's position between the unlettered biography (29:48), the signs in breasts (29:49), and the appeal to God as witness (29:52).

**"A Mercy and a Reminder": the Two Faces of One Book**

*inna fī dhālika la-raḥmatan wa-dhikrā li-qawmin yuʾminūn.* The two nouns are the two functions of revelation, and the tradition's reading of them is precisely the one the source commentary records: the Qur'an is a *raḥma* because it tells of God's blessings and bounty toward His servants, and a *dhikrā* because it admonishes them about His punishment — good news and warning — *bashīr* and *nadhīr*, the pair that describes this Book from the start of the Medinan discussion of the prophets ("We sent you with the truth, a bearer of glad tidings and a warner," 2:119; "a Book whose verses are detailed, in Arabic, as glad tidings and a warning," 41:3–4) and which the sūrah put on the lips of Hūd ("I convey to you the messages of my Lord, and I am for you a trustworthy adviser," 7:62). What is striking is the word order: mercy precedes reminder. The Book is not first a threat that also consoles; it is a gift whose warning is one of its parts — which is why "mercy" here is the same mercy the sūrah has already made the stakes of the whole argument: God "punishes whom He will and has mercy on whom He will" (29:21), and those who deny the meeting with Him "despair of My mercy" (29:23). Verse 51 answers that despair: the mercy is still being sent down, in recitation, to the very people who asked for something else. The pairing recurs elsewhere as a single self-description of the Book — "a healing and mercy for the believers, and it increases the wrongdoers only in perdition" (17:82); "a guidance and a mercy for people who have sure faith" (45:20; cf. 27:2, 31:3) — and those verses supply the reason the sūrah's clause ends with a restriction: the same recitation is mercy for one hearer and perdition for another, because in the second case it removes the excuse.

**"For a People Who Believe" — and Why the Restriction Is Not Arbitrariness**

The restriction at the end of the verse is the third time in nine verses that the sūrah has made the benefit of the evidence dependent on the condition of the recipient (29:43, "none understand the parables except those who know"; 29:44, "a sign for the believers"; 29:51, "for a people who believe"), and its consistency is an argument in itself rather than a hedge. The sūrah is describing how revelation works as it has described how trial works (29:2–3): a trial does not create the qualities it reveals, and a recited Book does not create the faith that profits by it. That is why the verse's logic is not "the Book is enough — therefore believe," but "the Book is enough for the people who believe," with the second half of the sentence quietly asserting that the disposition to believe is itself a mercy given through what is recited. Al-Rāzī draws the useful corollary against the sign-seekers: if the granting of a demanded miracle were a sufficient condition of faith, God would have given it — "and had your Lord willed, all who are on earth would believe all together; will you then compel people to be believers?" (10:99); and the same sūrah that contains 29:51 states the outcome of compelled belief elsewhere in the Book: "if We had sent down an angel, the matter would have been judged, then they would not be reprieved" (6:8). The Book as mercy and reminder is a sign offered in the only form in which it can function as mercy rather than as verdict.

**The Tradition's Use of the Verse: the Sufficiency of Revelation**

The hadith that the sources attach to this verse is the classical statement of the same position, and it belongs to the verse rather than to the argument about miracles in general:

> *"No prophet came but that he was given what people believed in because of it; what I was given is revelation that God revealed to me, and I hope to have the greatest following of them on the Day of Resurrection."* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Hurayra)

The wording explains the comparative claim the sūrah makes for the Book: a physical miracle is witnessed by a generation and then perishes; a recited text is witnessed by whoever hears it and remains, so that the Prophet's hope of the largest following is not vanity but a fact about media. The tradition's literature of the Qur'an's inimitability (*iʿjāz*) — al-Bāqillānī's *Kitāb al-Iʿjāz* and ʿAbd al-Qāhir al-Jurjānī's *Dalāʾil al-iʿjāz* are its classical monuments — rests on this verse together with 29:48 and the challenge-passage cluster: "produce a sūrah like it and call upon your witnesses besides God, if you are truthful" (11:13); "if humankind and jinn gathered to produce the like of this Qur'an, they could not produce its like, even if each other's helper" (17:88); "or let them produce a speech like it" (52:34). Because the Book itself suffices, the tradition also treated it as the standard against which additions to evidence are judged — and this, at least, is a live discipline: the modern habit of pressing Qur'anic words into the service of this or that scientific finding is, whether the finding is right or wrong, a kind of asking for another sign, and the scholars' repeated warning against saying in the Qur'an what its language does not carry (a maxim transmitted in the tafsīr literature with reports from Ibn ʿAbbās to the effect that whoever interprets the Qur'an by mere opinion should take his seat in the Fire) rests on the same conviction that 29:51 expresses: what is in the Book is mercy and reminder — enough, and enough for a purpose.

**What "Sufficiency" Means for the Reader Who Is Already Inside the Practice**

The verse is not only aimed at Makkah. In the sūrah's own terms the believer is the one who is tried (29:2–3), and the trial is precisely the period in which one wants more: more confirmation, more miracle, more certainty before paying the cost. Verse 51 closes that demand at the level of the individual too, and does so with a comfort rather than a rebuke — the Book you have been given is a mercy, not a test you might fail to verify — and it directs the rest of the need to the instrument the sūrah named at its centre: recite and pray (29:45). The result is a settled economy of evidence and desire: the Qur'an is sufficient because it is what a mercy is for, namely to be acted on, and the person who keeps postponing action in search of stronger grounds has, in the sūrah's diagnosis, already refused the kind of sign that would have satisfied him — "if a sign came to them they would not believe, except as they did not believe the first time" (cf. 6:109–110; 7:146). The Community’s own foundational instruction about the Book matches the verse’s claim: "hold fast, all of you, to the rope of God, and do not divide" (3:103) — a rope is something held, not something demonstrated; and the exegetes read our verse as the reason the Companions did not go looking for a different kind of proof when the Meccan period pressed them hardest, since what was recited to them contained, as al-Ṭabarī says, "the account of what came before them and the account of what comes after them." The verse thus ends the sūrah's evidential section exactly where the chapter began: with a question to the person who thinks something is missing.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:52

> **Say: God suffices as a witness between me and you. He knows what is in the heavens and on the earth. Those who believe in the false and disbelieve in God — it is they who are the losers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict the Verse Takes Out of Everyone's Hands**

*qul kafa bi'llāhi shahīdan baynī wa-baynakum, yaʿlamu mā fi's-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ; alladhīna āmanū bi'l-bāṭili wa-kafarū bi'llāhi ulāʾika humu'l-khāsirūn.* The passage that began with a demand for signs (29:50) and met it with the sufficiency of the Book (29:51) now ends with a declaration that transfers the case: the debater does not have to win. The verb *kafa* is used absolutely, without an object of comparison, and the preposition *bi-* before the name of God is what the grammarians call the *bāʾ* of association in estimate — "God, together with Him as witness, is enough" — so the sentence means that nothing else needs to be added to God as a witness for the matter to be settled. This is the same idiom the Prophet's ﷺ companions were taught to say at the hour when the evidence of success was entirely absent:

> *"God is sufficient for us, and an excellent disposer of affairs."* — and the commentator adds: "Ibrāhīm said it when he was thrown into the fire, and Muḥammad said it when the people said to them, 'mankind has gathered against you, so fear them,' and it increased them in faith" (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, Book of Tafsīr, from Ibn ʿAbbās, on 3:173).

To read that report next to 29:52 is to see the verse's function in the sūrah's own argument about trial: *ḥasbunā* is what a persecuted community says when no sign has come, because the Witness is enough.

**"A Witness Between Me and You" — the Cluster of Verses**

The formula is used of the Prophet's ﷺ relation to his deniers in a group of verses whose contexts all involve a contested claim to authority. *"Has God sent down a human being as a messenger?"* is the objection in Israel, and the command that follows it is: *"say: God suffices as a witness between me and you; He knows and sees what you say"* (17:94–96). In the same sūrah the demand for angels is refused (17:92) and the answer is the same transfer to a witness. The formula belongs to a small family of verses that share its verb and its name: *"say: God suffices as a witness between me and you, and whoever has been given knowledge of the Book"* (13:43); *"has God sent down a human being as a messenger?... say: my Lord knows the word in the heavens and the earth, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing. God suffices as a witness between me and you"* (17:94–96); *"those who convey the messages of God and fear Him and do not fear anyone except God — and God suffices as a reckoner"* (33:39); *"Muḥammad is the messenger of God... God suffices as a witness"* (48:28); and the most exact twin of all, *"is it not enough that your Lord is witness over all things?"* (41:53). The source commentary lists these (4:79, 4:166, 13:43, 17:96, 46:8, 48:28), and their shared logic is the point: the appeal to a witness who already knows is not a confession of defeat but a claim about where the case actually stands. It is also a moral instruction in the middle of a sūrah that has commanded gentleness in argument (29:46): the believer is not required to produce an outcome, only to be veracious, and the pairing "God is sufficient as witness" with "He knows what is in the heavens and on the earth" makes the witness's knowledge the ground of his judgment.

**al-Shahīd: The Name That Includes Presence and Knowledge**

*Shahīd* is from *sh-h-d*, whose basic sense is presence and vision — the *shāhid* is the one present, the *mashhad* the scene — and when it is applied to God it therefore connotes more than testimony in the forensic sense: He is present at the event and aware of what the parties will not say about it. The name occurs in the Qur'an both as an attribute ("is not your Lord witness over all things?" — 41:53, quoted above as the twin of our verse) and as the ground of the record ("that Day We shall seal over their mouths, and their hands will speak to Us, and their feet will testify to what they earned" — 36:65; "nothing on earth or in heaven is hidden from God" — 3:5). Read with our verse, the significance is that the sūrah's earlier appeal to God's knowledge — "God knows what you were doing" (29:5, 29:22), "He knows the treachery of the eyes and what the breasts conceal" (40:19), "God knows the truthful and knows the liars" (29:3) — is now gathered into the name that does both things: witnessing the act and knowing the intention. The two clauses of 29:52 are thus a single assertion in two forms, and the second, "He knows what is in the heavens and on the earth," prevents the first from being heard as an invocation of an indifferent judge: the witness is not a passive third party but the one whose knowledge constitutes the facts of the case.

**"Those Who Believe in the False" — the Sūrah's Final Definition of Error**

The last clause is the most compact statement of unbelief in this chapter, and it is built on the opposition the sūrah has been constructing since the parable of the web: *ḥaqq* versus *bāṭil*. Verse 44 said that God created the heavens and the earth *bi'l-ḥaqq*; verse 52 says that the disbelievers believe in *al-bāṭil*. *Bāṭil* is from *b-ṭ-l*, the wind that blows and finds nothing, the thing that is void, unsubstantial, perishing — and in the sūrah's own image the paradigm case of *bāṭil* is precisely a house made of silk: a structure with the appearance of shelter and no substance of shelter. The verse's ordering is therefore not a casual antithesis: first the positive error (believing in the false) and then the corresponding loss (disbelieving in God), which is the order of real cases — a person does not usually begin by rejecting God but by trusting something that cannot hold him. Al-Rāzī's comment on the formula in similar passages is that the *īmān* here means "to consider firm," so that the clause says: they make the void their confidence, and that is why they disbelieve in God. The Book's own gloss on the consequence follows: *"and We shall turn their hearts and their sight aside, as they did not believe in it the first time"* (6:110), and the summary of 29:52's verdict — *humu'l-khāsirūn*, "they are the ones who lose" — picks up a root the sūrah has already used of the man who trades guidance for punishment (29:22 "whoever is ungrateful, God is free of the worlds") and which will be repeated of the people who "sold" themselves at the end of the chapter's account of the lost.
