Al-Ṭabarī: "they have lost the mercy of God and the reward of the next world, and in exchange they have what does not profit."

**Losing, in the Sūrah's Own Ledger**

The word *khusr* is commercial, and in a chapter that has spoken repeatedly of trade (29:11 "what is earned"; 29:12 "your deeds"; 29:22 "God is free of the worlds"; and 29:62's provisions weighed out) the choice of the merchant's vocabulary for the fate of the false-believer is deliberate: it is the verdict on a transaction in which something was given for nothing. The pairing is at its sharpest in the verses this one echoes: *"say: shall we tell you who are the greatest losers in respect of deeds? Those whose effort is lost in the worldly life, while they think that they are doing well"* (18:103–104), and the surah al-Muṭaffifīn's image of the "transaction" of the Day. On 29:52 the losers are not named as "the unbelievers of Quraysh" but as a class defined by a cognitive error — *taking as firm what is void* — and this is what gives the verse its range: the man who builds his security on a system, an idol, a patron, a school of thought, or a self-image that cannot survive contact with death is, on this definition, a loser, whatever his balance sheet says.

**The Verse as the End of the Evidential Section, and Its Practical Use**

Structurally, 29:52 closes 29:41–51: the parable (41), the knowing God (42), the conditions of understanding (43), the world made in truth (44), the recited Book and the prayer (45), the manner of argument (46), the witnesses inside and outside the community (47), the unimpeachable biography of its bearer (48), the signs in the breasts of the knowing (49), the refusal of the demanded miracle (50), the sufficiency of what was sent (51), and here, finally, the sufficiency of the Witness. Nothing further can be asked for, and the sūrah will spend the remainder (53–69) on what follows from having refused: the punishment hastened for, the death every soul tastes, the emigration for God's earth, the chambers of the next world, the lessons of provision, the ship, the sanctuary, and the last promise — "those who strive for Us, We shall guide them in Our ways" (29:69). For the reader the verse gives a rule for every argument of religion, including the ones that go badly: state the case, argue in the best manner, and then say *kafa bi'llāhi shahīdan*, without pretending that the outcome depends on your success in the room. That is not indifference; the same sūrah that hands the verdict to the Witness commands striving (29:69) and forbids the believer from trusting his own house (29:41). Between those two the verse 29:52 marks the only sane position: full effort, no illusion about who judges, and the clear knowledge that the currency of the final transaction is truth and not victory.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:53

> **And they ask you to hasten the punishment. And were it not for an appointed term, the punishment would have come to them; and it will come upon them suddenly, while they do not perceive.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand That the Sūrah Repeats Rather Than Answers**

*wa-yastaʿjilūnaka bi'l-ʿadhāb, wa-lawlā ajalun musammal la-jāʾahumu'l-ʿadhāb, wa-la-yaʾtiyyannahum baghtatan wa-hum lā yashʿurūn.* The verb *yastaʿjilūnaka* — "they ask you to hasten" — is not a request but a mockery, and the sūrah uses it twice in successive verses (53, 54) precisely so that the same sentence should frame two different answers: first, why the punishment is delayed; then, that it will not be escaped. The Qur'an reports this demand in the words of every late community: *"when will this promise come, if you are truthful?"* (10:48; 27:71; 34:29; 36:48; 67:25); *"if this is the truth from You, rain down stones on us from the sky or bring upon us a painful punishment"* (8:32) — a prayer of contempt that the sūrah of al-Anfāl immediately answers with the reason the Prophet ﷺ was still among them (8:33, quoted above at 29:33). Al-Ṭabarī's gloss on 29:53 names the tone: they asked for it "on the assumption that it was a fiction and to shame the one who promised it," and the answer is not to argue about the promise but to explain the timing. The sūrah has already heard the same demand in its own chapter, put in the mouth of Midian: "bring us God's punishment, if you are truthful" (29:29) — a verse which shows that the demand for hastening is not a request for information but a wager, and that the sūrah's audience has been listening to this objection since the middle of the chapter. The response is the one given elsewhere to the same demand: *"say: the hastening you ask for is not in my hand; the judgment is God's alone; He relates the truth and He is the best of judges"* (6:57), and *"they ask you to hasten the punishment, and God will not fail His promise — but a day with your Lord is like a thousand years of what you count"* (22:47).

**"An Appointed Term": the Word Taken from Contracts**

*ajal musamman* — a term named, specified, recorded. The source commentary on this verse lists the uses, and the range is instructive: it denotes the span of a human life (6:2, 60; 11:3; 14:10; 39:42; 40:67; 71:4), the term of the cosmos (30:8, 46:3), the fixed courses of the sun and moon (13:2; 31:29; 35:13; 39:5), the Day of Judgment itself (35:45; 42:14), and — the item in the list that changes how the phrase sounds — the due date written into a loan contract (2:282, *ajalin musamman*). The Qur'an's vocabulary of divine forbearance is borrowed from the most exacting genre of human record-keeping: a term that is "named" is a term entered in the book and enforceable, not a vague deferral. This fits a sūrah whose opening already set the whole argument under a term: "whoever hopes for the meeting with God — God's term is coming, and He is the Hearing, the Knowing" (29:5), and whose later verse will state that everything is "with a measure" (29:62, 65:3, 13:8 "every woman's pregnancy and what the wombs lose and gain — everything with Him has a measure"). Al-Rāzī's comment on the *ajal* family of verses is the point: the delay of punishment is not an omission but a scheduled item, and the schedule is what makes history a moral process rather than a series of accidents.

**"Were It Not for…": the Grammar of Mercy**

The counterfactual *lawlā* — "were it not for" — is the Qur'an's most quietly theological construction, and in the Book it almost always credits the restraint of God rather than the strength of the guilty: *"were it not for a word that preceded from your Lord, it would have been decided between them"* (42:14; cf. 20:129, 11:110, 10:19). And the strongest of the parallels is the verse that says what the restraint is for: *"and your Lord is the Forgiving, full of mercy; were He to take them to task for what they have earned, He would hasten for them the punishment — but they have an appointed time from which they will find no escape"* (18:58). Read with 29:53, the last clause of 18:58 ("they will find no refuge from it") shows that the two halves of our verse belong together: the deferral is mercy to the guilty while it is space for the persecuted, and it is not an amnesty. The classical *asmāʾ al-ḥusnā* literature makes the same point from the name *al-Ḥalīm* — forbearance is power that does not strike, and is therefore a mercy rather than a weakness — and and the tradition's preaching on verses of this family has drawn from them the same rule in plainer language: every breath a sinner draws is taken inside a deferral that was not owed to him. It is also the reason the sūrah's own advice to its threatened audience has been, all along, patience rather than vindication — the reward it holds out at the end of the chapter is for "those who are patient and put their trust in their Lord" (29:59) — and the Prophet's ﷺ own example at Ṭāʾif, when he refused the offer of the mountain-crushing angel and said instead: "I hope that God will bring from their loins people who worship God alone" (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim).

**"Suddenly, While They Do Not Perceive"**

*baghtatan* is the Qur'an's word for the character of the end: it comes at the moment when the case is not being considered. The root gives the noun *baghṭa*, the sudden event, and the Book uses it both of the Hour — "the Hour will not come to you except suddenly" (7:187) — and of the ends of nations: "nay, it will come upon them suddenly and confound them, and they will not be able to avert it, nor will they be reprieved" (21:40); "how many a town whose punishment We sent came upon them by night, or while they slept at noon — then their cry, when Our punishment came to them, was only that they said: we were wrongdoers" (7:4–5); and, in the narratives this sūrah has already told, the dawn that catches Midian (29:37, with its morning verb *ṣabāḥat-hum*) and the portion of the night in which Lot was told to carry his family (11:81). The clause *wa-hum lā yashʿurūn* — "while they do not perceive" — is what makes the sentence a description of a psychological state rather than only a chronology: the people of *lā yashʿurūn* are those for whom the present arrangements have become unimaginably permanent, and the punishment's suddenness is measured by the strength of that assumption rather than by the clock. The Qur'an's diagnosis of the condition is stated most exactly at the beginning of sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ, which the commentators pair with our verse: *"the reckoning of the people drew near while they are in heedlessness, turning away"* (21:1). Al-Ṭabarī's gloss: "they are not mindful of what is coming, because they have made themselves at home in what they see."

**Why the Verse Is Addressed to the Prophet ﷺ and Read by the Persecuted**

The objectors are not the only audience the sūrah has in view, and the sentence's function in the chapter's argument is best seen by asking who else wanted the punishment to be hastened. The opening verses described believers who were "afflicted" and who had been told to be patient for a term (29:2–11); a community under pressure wants vindication as urgently as its persecutors scoff at it, and the sūrah has already said of the man who fears "the trial of men" that God's punishment is what should occupy his attention (29:10). Verse 53 addresses both at once: to the scoffers, that the delay is not immunity; to the afflicted, that the delay is not neglect. The tradition's ethics of that second hearing are unusually well developed, because the same verse was recited to people who could not see justice being done. Two of the Prophet's ﷺ sayings belong to that use, both transmitted in the two Ṣaḥīḥs: "fear the prayer of the wronged, for there is no veil between it and God" (from Anas), and, when he met a woman weeping at a grave and she did not know him, the definition of the virtue that makes an unrepaired loss survivable — "patience is at the first stroke" (from Anas ibn Mālik). Together with our verse, they give the believer's share of the teaching: the appointed term is not the horizon within which you must see the case settled, and the settlement that comes "suddenly" is precisely what makes patience — not prediction — the discipline of this religion.

**The Modern Case for Slowness**

A last reflection the verse invites. The demand "hasten the punishment" is not extinct; it has only changed registers, and it is the reflex of an age that wants verdicts the same day it wants news. Verse 53 states that the divine schedule is not an injustice but a structure with two functions the human appetite for immediacy defeats: it preserves space for repentance, since a punishment that could not be deferred could not be turned from: "there was no town that believed, so that its faith should profit it, except the people of Jonah — when they believed, We removed from them the punishment of disgrace in the life of this world and let them enjoy ourselves for a while" (10:98), and the principle follows in the next verse: "if your Lord had willed, all who are on earth would believe; will you then compel people to be believers?" (10:99). And it preserves the reality of moral choice, since a punishment that arrives on demand would end the test the sūrah spent its opening verses describing. The "appointed term" is thus not a postponement of justice but the condition under which justice and mercy can both be true in the same act — which is exactly the reading the sūrah will complete at verses 54–55, where the encompassment that was held back finally closes.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:54

> **And they ask you to hasten the punishment — and Hell will surely encompass the disbelievers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Sentence, a Different Answer**

*wa-yastaʿjilūnaka bi'l-ʿadhābi wa-inna jahannama lamuḥīṭatun bi'l-kāfirīn.* Verse 53 opened with this demand and answered the question behind it (why not now?) with the appointed term; verse 54 repeats the opening and answers the other question behind it (and then what?) with the destination. The repetition is not redundancy, and the Qur'an has its own theory of it: when the objectors asked why the Book was not sent down all at once, the reply named the effect of being given it in instalments — "thus, that We may make it firm in your heart, and We recited it with a recitation" (25:32). Within a single sūrah the device works on the listener's attention: the same clause, heard twice in thirty seconds, becomes the place where the mind expects an echo and instead receives something new. Al-Ṭabarī marks the second clause as the answer to the demand itself: "they ask you to hasten the punishment, thinking that it will be escaped; and Hell encompasses the disbelievers, so that hastening it changes nothing about its arrival, and delay nothing about its certainty." The verse therefore does not answer "when" at all; it says that "when" is the wrong variable, since an enclosure has no opening that timing can exploit.

**"Muḥīṭ": The Root of Total Enclosure**

*muḥīṭa* is from *ḥ-w-ṭ*, to go round a thing so that nothing of it is left outside. The verb *aḥāṭa* is used of an army encircling a town and of a wall enclosing a garden; it is used of ruin, "its fruit was encompassed, so it withered, and they clapped their hands over what they had spent on it, and it was ruined" (18:42) — a vineyard whose loss arrives from every side at once; and it is used of complete information, "I have encompassed what you have not encompassed, and I have brought you from Sheba sure news" (27:22), and "everything He has encompassed in number" (72:28). The double use is not accidental: in the Qur'an what encloses and what knows are described by the same verb, and the sentence before ours has just said of the Encloser "He knows what is in the heavens and on the earth" (29:52). The choice of a participle of geometry, rather than of burning or of seizing, is what gives 29:54 its chill. Al-Ṭabarī glosses it "an enclosure round them from every side, so that they find no way out," and the near-synonym the Book uses for the same fact is *ḥāqa*, "to settle on a people and close over them": "the evils of what they earned will appear to them, and what they used to mock will encompass them" (39:48). The best commentary on the word is Pharaoh's end, told with the verb of covering that this very sūrah will use for the punishment at 29:55: "so he pursued him, and there covered him of the sea that which covered him" (20:78) — water rising from every side at once. That is *iḥāṭa*: the element you chose becomes the enclosure. Hell's "encompassing" is the same fact, eschatologically stated.

**The Inversion of the Spider's House**

Set 29:54 against 29:41 and the sūrah's central image completes. There, the protectors built a house out of themselves, and the verdict was that it was the frailtest of houses — permeable, wind-torn, unable to keep anything out. Here the disbelievers are given the enclosure they will actually inhabit, and its property is the opposite of the web's: it lets nothing out. The sūrah has throughout described the two abodes in terms of shelter and exposure (29:12 "the earth of God is vast" for the emigrant; 29:56 "My earth is vast" again; 29:58 "lofty chambers in gardens beneath which rivers run"), and 29:54 is the negative of that vocabulary — the only other structure in the chapter that is total is the one from which the doors are gone. The Makkan audience would have felt the image physically: the desert's worst fear is not exposure alone but being surrounded, and the Book's idiom for it is the language of a night raid: "there came round it a night-circler (ṭāʾif) from your Lord while they were asleep, and it became as though it had never been harvested" (68:19) — the verb *ṭāfa*, to go round by night, and the same image of enclosure applied to a garden that thought itself secure. When the sūrah says Hell *muḥīṭa*, it says: what you feared from your enemies' numbers is what your own earned deed will be to you, and no negotiation with it is possible, because it is not an opponent outside the walls but the condition of the place.

**"The Disbelievers" — the Class Named, and Why It Is Not a List of Individuals**

The verse's object is *al-kāfirūn*, plural and definite, and its function in this passage is not to identify particular persons but to name a disposition: the one who "covers over" what he knows (the root *k-f-r* means to cover, as the night covers, as a farmer's seed covers itself with soil, and as the ingrate covers a blessing). The sūrah has been narrowing that definition: the disbeliever of 29:52 is "the one who believes in the false and disbelieves in God," the rejector of 29:47 and 29:49 is the one who contests the signs he has seen, and the encompassed party of 29:54 is the same person described from the point of the outcome. The result is an unbroken line from the first of these verses to the last: a *choice* of trust (29:41) has become a *place* (29:54), and the place is only the choice made permanent. It is also why the verse is a comfort in the sūrah's own situation rather than a gloating one: the people being threatened are the persecutors of the small community at 29:2–13, and the sūrah's promise to them is not that they will be punished on the believers' schedule, but that what they have made — the covering-over of known truth — will be, in the end, the only thing around them. Ibn Kathīr's gloss is exactly this: "the punishment they asked to have hastened will surround them from all sides, and they will have no way out of it."

**The Encompassing Fire and the Encompassing Mercy**

No verse in this sūrah should be read without the Book's other encompassing statements, and the tradition's preaching on 29:54 has always kept the balance. The verse that speaks of Hell's enclosure sits in a Book in which God says of the same root: "My mercy encompasses all things" (7:156), and in which the most comprehensive statement of the sūrah's theme is not the fire but the record: "He knows what is in the heavens and on the earth" (29:52). The Prophet's ﷺ own words, transmitted in the two Ṣaḥīḥs, give the proportion in a single sentence: "this fire of yours, which the son of Adam kindles, is one part of seventy parts of the Fire of Hell," and, on the other side, "when God completed creation, He wrote with Himself above the Throne: My mercy precedes My wrath." Read together with 29:53's appointed term, the two statements hold the sūrah's whole doctrine of delay: the enclosure is real and the forbearance is prior, so that a person's lifetime is the space between a mercy that precedes and a punishment that is only earned. The verse's ethics for the believer follow from this, and the tradition's are unsentimental: one is forbidden to rejoice at an enemy's stumble — a prohibition transmitted in the sunan collections — because the encompassment of 29:54 is not a victory to be enjoyed but a fate to be feared for oneself; and the sūrah's own instruction for such situations was verse 46's, "argue with them in the best manner," which remains in force up to the verse before this one.

**The Verse's Address to the Present**

Finally, 29:54 asks a reader who has never been tortured or persecuted to consider what it means for a consequence to become an environment. In ordinary experience this is a familiar enough thing: a habit that was chosen freely in the beginning comes to define the range of the possible, a concealed dishonesty eventually encloses a whole professional life, an addiction is exactly a case of "the thing chosen surrounding the chooser." The sūrah's claim is that the eschatological case is not a metaphor for this but its completion — the act has become a place. The word *jahannam* itself, which the Arabic Qur'an uses and which the lexicographers trace (through the Syriac *gēhannām*, "the valley of Hinnom," the ravine outside Jerusalem associated in Jewish memory with the fires of child-sacrifice and later with the place of judgment), contributes to that lesson: the name is a location, and the Qur'an's hearers knew the geography of a valley outside their own city where fires were lit. The punishment is therefore described, in this verse, as a where rather than a how — and the sūrah has made its point about "where" by telling its audience that the only place they will actually be enclosed in is the place their own covering-over has built around them.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:55

> **On the day when the punishment covers them from above them and from beneath their feet, and He says: taste what you used to earn.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Fills In the Geometry of Verse 54**

*yawma yaghshāhumu'l-ʿadhābu min fawqi'him wa-min taḥti ajlu'lihim, wa-yaqūlu dhūqū mā kuntum taksibūn.* Verse 54 said the punishment would *encompass* (muḥīṭa) them; verse 55 explains what encompassment consists of — coverage from above and pressure from below — and it is a commentary in the strict sense, since the source commentary on this verse notes that the phrase "from above and from beneath their feet" is an idiom for being punished on every side, and lists the parallel verses where the idiom is used of the same total exposure (5:66, "they would have eaten from above them and from beneath their feet" — the very idiom, there for provision; 6:65, "He has power to send upon you a punishment from above you or from beneath your feet, or to clothe you with faction and make some of you taste the violence of others"; 33:10, the troops that came at the Medina of "from above you and from beneath you"). The sūrah has already, in its own narrative section, given the two halves separately: the cities of Lot were turned inside out — "We made their highest their lowest" (11:82; 15:74) — and the earthquake shook Midian so that they "lay prostrate in their homes" (29:37). Verse 55 is what happens when both directions operate at once, and that is why it is placed after verse 40's catalogue of four elements: here the punishment has no longer one instrument but every direction.

**"Yaghshāhum": The Verb of Waves and Veils**

*ghashā* is to cover as a wave covers or as darkness covers: the ark "sailed with them through waves like mountains" (11:42), and "on their hearts are veils (ghishāwāt) so that they cannot understand it, and in their ears is heaviness" (18:57). The word's field is the loss of sight and of options that comes from being covered. The consequence of the verb is that the punishment removes the possibility of seeing an exit, as a wave removes the sight of the shore; and this is why the verse does not say simply "they will be punished" but that the punishment "covers" them — a word for an event one is inside rather than one receiving. Two things follow. First, the covering is total: a wave does not surround and then wait — it passes over. Second, the image is impersonal: the punished in this verse are not being attacked, they are being covered, which fits the sūrah's repeated account of punishment as the consequence made environment (29:40, "God did not wrong them, but they were wronging themselves"). The verse's first word, *yawma*, "the day when," is governed, on the reading of the commentators, by the statement about encompassing in 29:54 — the punishment will be an encompassment, on the day when it covers them — so that the two verses are a single sentence in everything but the recitation's division, and the day named is the Day that the sūrah has held out since 29:5's "whoever hopes for the meeting with God — God's term is coming."

**"From Above Them and From Beneath Their Feet": the Reversal of Paradise's Idiom**

The most striking feature of the Arabic is that the phrase is borrowed from the description of the Garden. Paradise in the Qur'an is "beneath which the rivers run" — *tajrī min taḥtihe'l-anhār* — and this sūrah uses exactly that idiom for the believers' abode three verses later: "We shall settle them in lofty rooms of the Garden, beneath which rivers run, therein to abide" (29:58; the same phrase is the Book's standard for paradise, as at 4:57 and 9:72). The phrase "min taḥti ajlu'lihim" belongs to the world of shade, water, and ownership — a house in which the good flows under your feet — and here the same spatial figure is filled with fire. The sūrah's rhetoric is thus built on a substitution: the geometry of blessing is retained and the contents are inverted, which is the most precise way the Book has of saying that the two abodes are one place understood two ways. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the covering "from above" recalls the forms of punishment that come down — stones, torrential rain, the thunderbolt — and that "beneath the feet" recalls the ground giving way, the loss of the earth's cooperation. The Makkan hearer, whose whole safety consisted in a roof, a walled house, and the arable patches of the Ḥijāz, is being told that both the roof and the ground will be the agents.

**"Taste What You Used to Earn": The Address, Not Only the Pain**

*wa-yaqūlu dhūqū mā kuntum taksibūn.* The verb *dhāqa* is the verb of direct experience — the taste that cannot be delegated to a representative or a record — and in this Book it is the standard verb for what the scene of the Day does to a person rather than what it contains. The formula "taste for what you used to do" runs through the Qur'an's accounts of the reckoning — 3:106, 6:30, 7:39, 8:35, 32:14, 46:34 — always as a sentence addressed to people who are being given, not sentenced, and its positive twin is the command to the saved to eat and drink: "eat and drink with delight for what you used to do" (52:19; cf. 56:19). The punishment is here named a tasting because it is not inflicted from a distance: it is undergone, and the one who undergoes it is the same person who did the deed. The command form is the point: they are ordered to taste, so that the punishment arrives as a fulfilment rather than an accident — the object of the verb is not "the fire" but "what you used to earn", the same *k-s-b* vocabulary that has run through the whole sūrah — "We shall absolve them of their bad deeds and repay them with the best of what they used to do" (29:7), "every soul has tasted death" (29:57), and the verdict on the punished nations of the chapter — "God did not wrong them, but they were wronging themselves" (29:40) — with its implication that nothing has been added to them and nothing subtracted, only paid out. Al-Rāzī observes on such clauses that the address is a second punishment: to be told to taste is to be made to attend, and attention is what the *ghashāwa* (the covering) removes in the world but never in the next.

**The Shift of Voice: From "He" to the First-Person Plural**

The verse's second half in the Arabic of 29:55 places divine speech alongside divine action without a change of grammatical person, and the sūrah has been using the iltifāt (the turn of address) for its effect throughout — as at 29:23, where "they despair of My mercy" puts the first person inside a third-person report, and at 29:56, "O My servants… My earth is vast." Here the "and He says / We say" of the different readings serves one purpose: the sentence is short, and the speaker in it is not a narrated figure but the one who is being addressed in the sūrah's own audience. Al-Suyūṭī's treatment of such turns in *al-Iklīl* and in the *Itqān* describes the effect in the terms the rhetoricians use: the shift from report to direct speech brings the distant scene into the hearing of the listener, and the Makkan recitation of this verse performs exactly that. It is also why the verse's consolation for the persecuted is contained in the tone rather than in the content: the God who says "taste" to the persecutors is the God whose name the sūrah put in the mouth of the emigrants — "and we submit to Him" (29:46), "so worship Me" (29:56).

**Why the Verse Belongs to a Sūrah About Trial**

It would be a misreading to take 29:55 as the chapter's threat and the rest as its teaching. The sūrah began by establishing that belief is tested and that the testing is not information-gathering for an ignorant God (29:2–3), and it has named, repeatedly, the two things the persecuted need: that the term is appointed (29:53), and that the punishment they were mocked for will not be escaped (29:54–55). Verse 55 adds the precise shape of the answer: the mockers will be covered from the same two directions the believers were made to fear — the sky and the ground, the two things 29:34 and 29:37 have already said God uses when He chooses. In the sūrah's own moral economy this is not revenge but coherence: the "from above and beneath the feet" of the verse corresponds to the fact that the persecution of the believers had been conducted by people who relied on the sky's favour (augury and the omens read in the flight of birds, against which the Book sets its own call to look upward: "do they not see the birds above them, spreading out their wings and folding them in? None holds them up but the All-Merciful" — 67:19, a verse whose point is that the sky is held up by mercy, not read for signs) and on the ground's resources (their trade, their harvest, their cities). The punishment's two directions name, precisely, the two places their security had been.

**The Practical Teaching: Coverings Already Present**

The commentators allow the verse a second, gentler function: it is read as a description of what remorse consists of, since "covering from above and below" is the condition of a person who finds no direction of hope open — which is why the classical preachers used this verse and 6:65 together to warn against the specific sin of despair, since the punishment that covers is *what they used to earn*, and what is earnable can be unearned while the term is still running. The prophetic practice that the tradition records at the sight of weather belongs to the same instruction: the Prophet ﷺ, when a cloud or a wind appeared, would change colour and go in and out, and when asked said: "what assures me that there is not a punishment in it? A people were punished with wind; a people saw the punishment and said: this is a cloud bringing us rain" (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from ʿĀʾisha) — a fear of the *covering* that the believer is not asked to share, but whose logic he is asked to respect: the directions of danger are not the directions a person chooses. And for anyone who has ever lived inside a concealed wrongdoing, the verse's picture is not exotic: a lie that must be managed from above (by those in authority) and below (by those beneath) is a person covered from both sides, and the sūrah's diagnosis is that the only exit from such a condition is not more management but *tawba*, repentance — the one act that changes what is being "earned," while the day of tasting is still ahead.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:56

> **O My servants who believe — My earth is vast; so worship Me.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Address in the First Person, in the Middle of Threat**

*yā ʿibādiya'lladhīna āmanū, inna arḍī wāsiʿatun fa-ʿbudūn.* Two things about the Arabic are worth noting before anything else. The verse is an address from God in the first person, the plural of majesty and the singular of intimacy at once: "My servants," "My earth" — the possessive pronoun attached first to the people and then to the created order, so that the same *yāʾ* that makes them His makes the ground they stand on His as well. The sūrah has been turning between third-person report and first-person speech through its last section (29:51 "We sent down," 29:52 "between me and you," 29:55 "and He says"), so that when the voice arrives here it sounds like a change of temperature. And the address uses *ʿibād*, "servants," a word the Qur'an prefers in contexts of mercy: "O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of God's mercy" (39:53), and "My servants — you have no authority over them, except those who follow you of the deviants" (15:42; cf. 17:53, 25:63, 78:26). The masters of this sūrah's threatened community are told, at the point of maximum pressure, that they belong to God — which is the same claim that made verse 25's "then turn away from them" possible.

**"My Earth Is Vast": the Amplitude Set Against the Enclosure**

The sentence stands in the most deliberate juxtaposition in the chapter. Verse 54 had said that Hell *muḥīṭatun bi'l-kāfirīn*, "encompassing the disbelievers," and verse 55 described the covering that comes from above and from below. Verse 56 now says *inna arḍī wāsiʿa*, "My earth is ample," using the root *w-s-ʿ*, from which the Book's own name *al-Wāsiʿ* and the description of God's attributes: "your Lord is ample (wāsiʿ) in mercy and in knowledge" (cf. 2:115 "to God belong east and west — wherever you turn is the face of God; God is ample, knowing"; 40:7 "our mercy encompasses all things"). The opposition is between enclosure and amplitude, and the point is not that the earth is large but that the space available to a worshipper is not fixed by his enemies: the disbeliever's future is an enclosure because he chose a refuge that had no room in it; the believer's present is an amplitude because his refuge is the One who made the room. This is why the verse is not only about geography. Al-Qurṭubī, and before him al-Ṭabarī, read "My earth is vast" as the ground of the duty to leave a place where the prayer cannot be prayed openly and the religion practised freely, and both add that the amplitude is God's possession — *arḍī* — so that no ruler on it can close what God has opened.

**The Occasion: the Migration to Abyssinia**

The reports collected in the source commentary on this verse give the historical situation: it was revealed as advice to certain Muslims to emigrate from Makkah to Abyssinia, where they would be able to worship freely (Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī cite the Abyssinian migration; al-Zamakhsharī generalises it to the duty of anyone who cannot be true to his religion where he lives). The two Abyssinian migrations are among the best-attested episodes of the Makkan period: the first, when a small group under Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib crossed the sea at a time when "the earth of God" had, for the Quraysh's purposes, shrunk to the Ḥaram and its alliances; the second, larger, a few years later. The Quraysh's response and the outcome are part of the same tradition: the embassy sent to the Negus with gifts, the refusal of the Christian king to hand over the emigrants, Jaʿfar's recitation to him of the sūrah of Mary, and the Negus's reply that the difference between his own faith and theirs was no wider than the line he drew with a stick (all in the *sīra* and *maghāzī* literature as transmitted by Ibn Isḥāq and Ibn Saʿd, and cited by the exegetes on this verse). It is worth adding the detail that the tradition preserves for the verse's purpose: the Negus refused the embassy and told the emigrants to live in his land secure, "and whoever wrongs you shall answer for it," and the Muslims who remained in Makkah stayed exposed — which is the exact comparison that makes "My earth is vast" a command rather than a platitude. When the Prophet ﷺ heard of the Negus's death years later he announced it to the Companions and prayed for him, in the report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī: a worshipper of God, in a foreign land, was worth a funeral prayer in Madina.

**The Verse's Sister: 4:97, the Most Uncomfortable Migration-Verse in the Book**

The source commentary cites the parallel that gives 29:56 its edge, and it must be quoted in full because it is the text on which the jurists built the whole discussion of *ḥijra*:

> *"Those whom the angels take while they are wronging themselves — they will say: what were you doing? They will say: we were oppressed in the land. They will say: was not God's earth vast, so that you could have emigrated in it? Those — their refuge is Hell, and an evil destination it is. Except the oppressed among men, women and children who cannot find a way and are not guided to a path — those God may well pardon, and God is pardoning, forgiving."* (4:97–99)

Three things about this passage shape the reading of our verse. First, the excuse "we were oppressed" is refused, which means that a person's situation is not, in the sight of the Day, identical to the pressure on him; there were directions he did not take. Second, the exception (4:98) is absolute: the ones who genuinely had no way, no means and no route, are handed to God's pardon, so that the command in 29:56 cannot be turned into a condemnation of the captive, the sick, the indigent or the surveilled. The jurists derived from the pairing the rules for *dār al-islām* and *dār al-ḥarb*, and the practical test they settled on — whether a person can perform the pillars and keep his faith — is our verse's own criterion: *fa-ʿbudūn*, "so worship Me," names the purpose, not the convenience. Third, the verse's verb for the earth is "vast" but its verb for the person is "emigrate"; the amplitude is potential, and it has to be entered. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the Qur'an does not say "My earth is vast so that you live comfortably in it."

**"So Worship Me": the Purpose of the Room**

The command's brevity is the sūrah's whole theology in three words, and the logic of the section makes it plain. God did not promise the believers a safe country; He promised them a vast earth whose point is *ʿibāda*. This is why the sūrah of trial has, all along, described worship as something that costs and is therefore real (29:2, "do people suppose they will be left to say 'we believe' and not be tried?"), why the prayer at its centre is what prevents the indecency and the reprehensible (29:45), and why the emigrant of 29:26 (Lot: "I am emigrating to my Lord") is the model: the destination of a *hijra* in the Qur'an is never a place but a Lord. The Makkan hearer would have registered the contrast with his own practice: his city's sacredness was the ground of his identity — the Ḥaram, the months, the water, the trade — and the verse tells him that a land's value is derivative, that God's earth is not the Quraysh's earth, and that the only thing for which a believer would leave or stay is worship. The Prophet's ﷺ own remark on his expulsion from Makkah belongs to this verse's subject: the report transmitted by al-Tirmidhī and Ibn Māja from Ibn ʿAbbās preserves his words on leaving the city: "how sweet you are, and how dear you are to me! Were it not that your people drove me out, I would not have settled anywhere but you." The love of a place and the priority of worship are held together there, as the sūrah holds them here.

**The Verse as the Remedy for 29:10, and Its Modern Range**

Structurally, 29:56 answers the condition diagnosed at 29:10: the man who "takes the trial of men as God's punishment" is the man for whom the world has become an enclosure, and the cure the sūrah prescribes is not courage in the abstract but the physical fact of amplitude — you can go. In the classical period the verse governed cases of conscience that recurred across the Muslim world, and its application was argued with care: the chapters on *hijra* and on residence in the lands of unbelief that open the books of jihād in the ḥadīth collections, and the jurists' long discussion of when staying becomes sinful and when it is excused; and the practice of the community itself, from the two Abyssinian migrations to the emigration to Madina, treated the verse as a law of motion for a group under pressure. For readers today the verse's range is wider than the legal question it generated, because "vast" can name more than a distance: a believer trapped in a family, a firm, a town or a state that makes open worship difficult is told that the resources available to him are not exhausted by the arrangement that confines him — that a job, a marriage, a congregation, a school, a country, or a way of spending an evening are options which the enclosure did not authorise. What the sūrah does not permit is the other move: to accept the enclosure as final, and to call one's inability to leave a piety. The verse's exception clause in 4:98 makes room for those who truly cannot leave, and the phrase *fa-ʿbudūn* holds them and the emigrants alike in the same obligation — worship, where you are, as far as the room allows — and gives them both the same promise, which the sūrah states in the next two verses: death, and then return to the One who owns the earth.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:57

> **Every soul shall taste death; then to Us you will be returned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Most Quoted Line in the Sūrah, and Why It Comes Here**

*kullu nafsin dhāʾiqatu'l-mawt, thumma ilaynā turjaʿūn.* The sentence is one of the Book's most frequently recited clauses, and its placement is the thing most readers miss: it does not arrive as a general piece of wisdom but as the *reason* for the command in verse 56. The source commentary states the connection in a line: "in the immediate context, this frequently quoted verse explains why the previous verse enjoins worship of God — since death is inevitable, everyone must prepare for it by worshipping God." In other words, "worship Me" (29:56) is followed by "you are going to die" (29:57) because the verse's real subject is not the difficulty of worshipping where you are but the shortness of the time in which it can be done. The emigrant who is told the earth is vast is told in the next breath that the size of the earth is irrelevant to the question at hand: every soul's term is one, and the only use of amplitude is to spend it in the direction it points. This is the same logic the sūrah installed at verse 5 — "whoever hopes for the meeting with God, God's term is coming" — now stated as a biological fact about everybody rather than as a promise to the hopeful.

**"Every Soul": the Scope of the Sentence**

The subject is *kullu nafsin*, not *kullu insānin*, and the choice matters in two directions. Inward: *nafs* in Arabic is the self, the whole living thing, and the verse therefore claims nothing about status, age, belief or power — the Pharaoh and Hāmān of 29:39 and the "lofty rooms" of 29:58 are on the same side of this sentence. Outward: the same word is used in the Book for every animate being — "there is no creature that moves on earth nor a bird that flies with its two wings but that they are communities like you" (6:38) — and the classical exegetes note that *kullu nafsin* includes the animals, whose death is the same event without the reckoning. The verse's universality is thus exact, and the Qur'an says it three times in the same words (3:185, 21:35, 29:57) with slight variations of what follows: at 3:185 the clause is followed by "and the recompense of the deeds will be paid in full on the Day of Resurrection," and at 21:35 by "every soul tastes death; We test you with evil and with good, and to Us you are returned" — a version that is our verse's twin in more than wording, because it puts *fitna* (trial) and death in the same sentence, which is the sūrah's own opening pair (29:2–3). Reading 21:35 beside 29:57 yields the chapter's whole doctrine: trial and death are the same fact seen from inside and from outside, and both end in return.

**"Taste": the Word the Sūrah Has Already Used for Punishment**

The participle *dhāʾiqatu* uses the root of verse 55's command "taste what you used to earn," and the repetition is the sūrah's quiet argument about continuity: the death that everyone tastes is the first instalment of the tasting that the punished are ordered to, which is why the tradition's preaching on this verse is so rarely about the end and so often about the middle. The grammar is also worth noting: the sentence does not say "every soul will die" but "every soul is a taster of death" — the noun-pattern of permanent disposition — and Arabic rhetoricians treat such a construction as stronger than the verb it replaces, because it makes the predicate part of the subject's definition. Death is not an event that befalls a soul; it is what a soul is, in the sense that being mortal is included in what it is to be a self. The Prophet's ﷺ use of the same idea as a discipline of attention is preserved in a short report transmitted by al-Tirmidhī and others: "increase the remembrance of the destroyer of delights" — death, named not as an evil but as the thing that ends the arrangement, "delights" being in the Arabic *ladhdhāt*, the pleasant states we build a life around.

**"Then to Us You Are Returned": the Passive Verb and the First Person**

*thumma ilaynā turjaʿūn* — two features of this half-verse carry its theology. The verb *turjaʿūn* is passive: you are returned, not you return; the sūrah's entire argument about human capacity has been pointing here (29:19 "then see how He brought the second creation into being," 29:21 "He punishes whom He will and has mercy on whom He will," 29:44 "God created the heavens and the earth in truth"), and the passive of the return is the counterweight to the active voice of earning: "taste what you used to *earn*" (29:55, *taksibūn*) — yours the deed, His the raising. The preposition *ilaynā*, "to Us," with the royal first person, names the destination without specifying whether it is mercy or justice, and that indeterminacy is the point: the return is to a presence, and the quality of the meeting is determined by what was sent ahead of it. Al-Rāzī, in the passage of his *Mafātīḥ* devoted to this clause, notes that the sūrah could have said "to the Garden or to the Fire" but chose the bare "to Us," because the return to God is the common fact and the division into abodes is its consequence — which is also why the sūrah will immediately, in verses 58–59, say who gets the lofty rooms.

**Death in the Sūrah's Own Situation**

The verse must be read with what the sūrah has been describing since verse 2: a small community under pressure, told that their persecutors will not outstrip God (29:4), that their own term is fixed (29:5), and that the trial of men is not to be mistaken for God's punishment (29:10). For people in that position 29:57 is at once a consolation and a reproach. A consolation, because the death of the persecutor is a fact about him — "the one who asked for the punishment to be hastened is a mortal" — and because it makes the emigration of verse 56 less frightening: a person who will die anyway loses less by leaving his house than by leaving his prayer. A reproach, because the same sentence removes the last alibi: if death is certain and return is certain, the arrangements one stays inside out of prudence are not, in the end, prudent. The reports of the Companions' conduct at the Prophet's ﷺ death show how the verse functioned as an instrument of public steadiness rather than of melancholy: when the announcement came that God's Messenger had died, Abū Bakr recited *"Muḥammad is only a messenger; messengers have passed before him"* (3:144) and *"every soul shall taste death"* (the wording of 3:185/29:57), and ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb said afterwards, by his own account, that his legs gave way when he heard it recited, as though he had never seen the verse before (the report is transmitted in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī in the accounts of the succession and in the *sīra* literature). That is what the sūrah expects from its audience: not that they know the sentence, but that they act on it.

**What the Verse Contributes to the Chapter's Argument**

Taken in its place, 29:57 performs three jobs at once. It closes the section on the punishment that the objectors demanded be hastened: they were told about the term (53), about the encompassment (54–55), and now about their own deaths — the sūrah's answer to "bring it on" is that the punishment they asked for will arrive by the same route as theirs, and no one's term belongs to anyone else's schedule. It grounds the ethics of migration and endurance: if return is to God, then neither the city one leaves nor the city one stays in is the seat of judgment, and the loyalty owed to a place cannot exceed the obedience owed to the One to whom all are returned. And it prepares the last movement of the sūrah (58–69), in which the abodes, provision, the ship, the sanctuary, and the final promise are described as the consequences of that return. The whole of the chapter's practical teaching is, in this sense, contained in the two verbs: taste and return. The tradition's devotional literature made a discipline out of exactly that pairing — al-Ghazālī's "remembrance of death" in the *Iḥyāʾ* is a set of exercises in holding the two clauses together so that a person's choices are ordered by the second rather than by the first — and the caution the same authors add is worth preserving: the remembrance of death is prescribed to make the will decisive, not to make life uninhabitable, and the sūrah itself pairs the verse with a command to worship and a promise of rooms, not with a command to withdraw.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:58

> **And those who believe and do righteous deeds — We shall surely settle them in lofty chambers of the Garden, beneath which rivers run, therein to abide. Excellent is the reward of the workers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer to "Was Not God's Earth Vast?"**

*wa-lladhīna āmanū wa-ʿamilū'ṣ-ṣāliḥāti la-nubawwiʾannahum mina'l-jannati ʿujalan tajrī min taḥtihi'l-anhāru khālidīna fīhā, niʿma ajru'l-ʿāmilīn.* Verse 56 told the beleaguered believers that God's earth is vast and that its purpose is worship; verse 58 now tells them what the amplitude of the next world looks like, and the sūrah's choice of verb is the hinge of the whole exchange. *La-nubawwiʾannahum* — "We shall surely settle them in an abode" — is from *b-w-ʾ*, the root of *mabāʾ*, "home," and the verb means to assign a place of return. It is the same verb, in the same emphatic construction with the lam of oath, in the verse the source commentary cites as this one's twin: *"those who emigrated for God's sake after they were wronged — We shall settle them (la-nubawwiʾannahum) in this world in a good place, and the reward of the Hereafter is greater, if they but knew"* (16:41). The pair is the sūrah's complete teaching on displacement: the same God settles people in this world and in that one, the first settling is a promise with a condition attached ("if they but knew"), and the second is the one the verse promises outright. A Makkan who gives up his house to keep his prayer is therefore not being consoled for a loss but told where his house is.

**ʿUjul: The Word for Rooms So High They Are Almost the Same as Light**

The noun *ʿujal* occurs in the Qur'an only here and in a verse this sūrah's listeners would have heard in the same circles: *"but those who feared their Lord have ʿujul above them, built with ʿujul"* (39:20) — chambers above chambers, the plural of *ʿūjūla*, an elevated place. The lexical range of the root is height with visibility: a *ʿajalān* in the poets is an eagle, the high flyer, and the *miʿjal* is the tall man; the word does not mean "room" but "room raised so high that it is seen." Ibn Kathīr, on our verse, brings the report about precisely this property:

> *"In the Garden there are chambers whose outside is visible from their inside, and whose inside is visible from their outside. God prepared them for the one who feeds the hungry, speaks gently, persists in fasting, and prays at night while the people sleep."* (a report from ʿAlī transmitted by Aḥmad and others; the muḥaddithūn differ on its chain, but its sense is the verse's own)

The transparency of the chambers is not decoration; the tradition's picture of paradise is a world in which nothing is hidden — the opposite of the dissimulation of the *munāfiqūn* the sūrah named at 29:10–11 and of the concealed malice of the mockers at 29:55 — and it corresponds, too, to the verse about those "who lie prostrate in their homes" (29:37), houses that could not hide anything from what came. Degrees within degrees are also taught elsewhere: the two Ṣaḥīḥs preserve the Prophet's ﷺ words that "the people of the Garden will see the people of the upper chambers (al-ghuraf al-ʿulā) above them as you see a brilliant star setting in the east or the west, because of the difference in merit between them," with the Companions asking what about the messengers, and the reply that they are too many for anyone to outrank — an image that keeps hierarchy and beauty together, and stops 29:58 from being read as an undifferentiated reward for everyone who merely survived.

**"Beneath Which Rivers Run": the Idiom of Habitable Goodness**

The phrase *tajrī min taḥtihi'l-anhār* is the Qur'an's standard for what makes a place worth living in, and the sūrah's geography makes its force clear: in an environment of stone, thirst and imported water, the description of the good as *water moving beneath your feet* is the exact reversal of the Makkan condition — the same idiom that runs through the parable of the two gardens in al-Kahf — "two gardens of grape-vines, and We enclosed them with palms and set between them a field, and both gardens gave their yield and withheld nothing from it, and We made a river flow between them" (18:32–33) — where the water under the feet is exactly what the owner's sons lose, and the same idiom used for the "fine dwellings (masākin ṭayyibatan)" of 61:12. This sūrah's audience had heard the phrase applied to the homes of the dead nations: to Thamūd, whom God "settled (bawwaʾakum) in the land, so that you built fortresses in its flat ground and hewed the mountains into houses" (7:74) — the verb of settlement given to a people who then supposed their masonry would last. The promise of 29:58 is what those house-builders lost, with the vulnerability taken out of it. Note also that the rivers run *beneath*: the good is underfoot, dependable, not the thing you must climb for — while the height is in the chambers, which God builds. The two ideas together, elevation and secure provision, are the sūrah's answer to the fear that drove the demand for haste: what the objectors wanted was to be handed their outcome now, and what the believer is promised is a place in which nothing needs to be seized.

**"Therein to Abide": the Reversal of Verse 57**

*khālidīna fīhā.* The sentence about abiding comes immediately after the sentence "every soul tastes death," and the sūrah's structure makes the sequence a syllogism rather than an ornament: death is certain; the abode after the return is permanent; therefore what a person builds toward cannot be measured in the terms of the life being ended. The same reversal appears in the Book's own description of the Garden's people: "they will not taste death therein, except the first death" (44:56). This is also the point at which the sūrah's long argument about *houses* concludes. The chapter began with people who had houses, clans, and cities, and whose houses were described as the very thing that failed to protect them — the dwellings of the punished whose ruins passers-by inspected (29:38: "their dwellings are evident to you"), the fortress towns (59:14, "they did not think We could come to them, and their buildings which they built were of no use"), the tent-homes of Midian struck down in the morning. Verse 58 names the one abode that is not a web, because it is not self-spun but assigned, by the One whose giving of a home is named with the same root in the Book's own vocabulary of salvation history: "and We settled the children of Israel in a settling-place of truth" (10:93); and for the first community of Madina, "those who settled in the Abode and in the faith before them love whoever emigrates to them, and do not find in their breasts a need for what they were given, and prefer others over themselves even when they themselves are in strait" (59:9) — a verse whose *tabawwuʾ* (taking up a home) is joined to the generosity that makes a home shareable, and whose next sentence is the prayer of the comers after: "our Lord, forgive us and our brothers who preceded us in faith" (59:10). The two verses say, in effect, that the settling of 29:58 is the fulfilment of a social act begun in this world.

**"Excellent Is the Reward of the Workers": Religion as Labour, and Wages**

*niʿma ajru'l-ʿāmilīn.* The clause is the sūrah's commercial idiom reaching its term: *ajr* is a wage, *ʿāmilūn* are workers, and *niʿma* is the verb of approval ("excellent is…"), the counterpart of *biʾsa*, "evil was…" — the two evaluative verbs the Book uses for what people deserve. Two consequences in the tradition's reading of such phrases deserve stating. First, the sūrah's ascetic reputation is unfounded: the reward is for *ʿamal*, deed, and the deed named in verse 58 is "righteous deeds" in the plural — the same collocation ("those who believe and do righteous deeds") that the sūrah has used four times already (29:7, 9, 58, and 29:69's striving), each time attaching the promise to the person who worked and not to the person who escaped. Second, because the reward is a wage, the tradition derived from it an ethic of the working day and of the labourer's claim — the Prophet's ﷺ words, in the report transmitted by Ibn Māja and others, quoting God: "I will be the opponent of three on the Day of Resurrection… a man who hired a worker, took his full work from him, and did not pay him his wage." The verse's praise of *ajr* is thus not only other-worldly arithmetic: it sacralises the act of working and the duty of paying, which is why the same language of settlement and reward is used for the emigrants and for the property that came with their displacement (59:1–10, and "the first preceders among the emigrants and the helpers, and those who followed them in excellence — God is pleased with them and they with Him, and He has prepared for them gardens beneath which rivers run, abiding therein forever; that is the great triumph" — 9:100).

**The Verse's Place in the Sūrah's Final Movement**

Taken as the opening of the last section (58–69), 29:58 does what a conclusion should: it takes each of the chapter's burdens and gives it a corresponding share. Fear of persecution becomes "lofty chambers"; the demand to hasten becomes "therein to abide"; the encompassed enclosure becomes rooms with rivers under them and light through them; the "frailtest of houses" becomes the one house called, in the sūrah's own vocabulary, *ṭayyib* — good, pure, lasting. What is striking is that it is a verse about refugees, in a chapter that began with the trial of believers under pressure and that has, for fifty verses, been arguing with a people who were settled, housed, and prosperous. The tradition read the pairing of this verse with the next (29:59, "those who are patient and put their trust in their Lord") as the sūrah's final word on how the promise is to be held: not by predicting its arrival, and not by manufacturing it, but by the two dispositions the verse names — patience toward what is and trust for what is coming. That is the reason the sūrah can, five verses later, still be sending its audience out to look at the birds and the sun, and at the end promise that those who strive will be guided: the reward is for workers, and the work described in 29:58–59 is the ordinary labour of staying believer, keeping the prayer, and not hastening anything.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:59

> **those who are patient and put their trust in their Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Relative Clause That Redefines the Reward**

*alladhīna ṣabarū wa-ʿalā rabbihim yatawakkalūn.* Grammatically this is not a new verse but a qualification of the last words of 29:58 — "excellent is the reward of the workers" — so that the workers are now specified: they are "those who are patient and who put their trust in their Lord." The sūrah's rhetoric is exact. Having promised lofty chambers, the chapter does not leave the reader with a list of achievements but with two dispositions, and both are about what a person does with time and with outcomes. This is the same pair the source commentary finds in the sūrah's twin passage on emigration: *"those who emigrated for God's sake after being wronged — We shall settle them in this world in a good place, and the reward of the Hereafter is greater, if they but knew — those who were patient and in their Lord put their trust"* (16:41–42). The two sūras close their teaching about displacement with the same two words, and the closeness is not accidental: patience and trust are what a person needs precisely when the outcome of his obedience is not in his hands.

**Ṣabr: Holding, Not Enduring**

*ṣabr* is from *ṣ-b-r*, whose core sense is to hold something in place, to restrain; the *ṣabūr* is the horse that does not shy, and the *ṣabra* in pre-Islamic usage is a thing left fixed while time passes around it. The word therefore names an activity, not a mood: the patient one is the one whose grip holds when the reasons for letting go are real. The tradition's moralists classified it accordingly — the Sufi writers and the jurists both used the three-fold scheme of patience in carrying out God's commands, patience in withholding oneself from prohibitions, and patience in bearing what was not chosen at all — and the Qur'an's own uses cover the same field: patience as the refusal to weaken under harm — "many a prophet fought with him a great company of devoted followers; they did not weaken for what befell them in God's cause, nor did they lose their resolve or submit, and God loves the patient" (3:146) — patience as persistence under provocation ("be patient over what they say, and part from them with a gracious parting" 73:10; "be patient, and your patience is only by God, and do not grieve for them and do not be in distress at what they devise" 16:127), and patience as a command to a community ("O you who believe, be patient and surpass one another in patience," 3:200). In the verses nearest ours the promise attached to it is the one the sūrah needs at its climax: *"God is with the patient"* (2:153; 8:46) — a companionship, not a compensation — and *"nothing will be given to it except those who are patient, and nothing will be given to it except the one with a great share"* (41:35), said of the quality that turns an enemy's hostility into friendship, which is the exact problem 29:46 raised.

**Tawakkul: The Language of Delegated Business**

*yatawakkalūn* is from *wikāla*, the contract by which a person authorises another to act in his affair: the *wakīl* is the agent, and *tawakkul* is handing over the business while continuing to do one's own part. The word's commercial origin matters because the sūrah has been speaking of wages and loss since 29:40, and because it blocks the reading that the tradition had to argue against repeatedly: trust is not the refusal to act, it is the transfer of the part of the affair that was never in the person's hands. The Prophet's ﷺ answer to the man who asked whether he should hobble his camel and trust, or turn it loose and trust, was "tie her up and put your trust" — a report transmitted by al-Tirmidhī and others — and the most complete statement of the disposition in the authentic collections is a sentence that pairs it with effort rather than with resignation: *"be keen for what benefits you, seek God's help, and do not be incapable"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Hurayra, in the context of seeking refuge from anxiety and from helplessness). The pairing's necessity was argued in both directions by the commentators: the worker who has no trust collapses when the work fails, and the "truster" who does not work is putting God to the test rather than relying on Him. The story the tradition tells about the man who sat in the mosque declaring that God would provide his sustenance, and whom ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb drove out with the words that "the sky does not rain gold or silver," is preserved in the *zuhd* and *ḥilya* literature for exactly this point, and the verse's own grammar supports it: the trust is placed *on* a Lord, and the patience is a verb the subject performs. And the verse's object is not an abstraction: *ʿalā rabbihim* — "in their Lord," the *Rabb* being the owner, the sustainer, and the one who brings a thing gradually to completion (from *r-b-b*, nurture and increase), so trust is placed in a person-like relation with a master who is also, in the sūrah's own phrase at 29:22, the only *walī* and *naṣīr*.

**The Two Words as the Answer to 29:53**

Placed where they are, patience and trust complete a debate the sūrah has been conducting since verse 53. The objectors demanded acceleration — "why is not the punishment brought on us?" — and the believers' virtue is defined as the refusal to accelerate anything: patience with what is, and trust about what is coming. The chapter's own theory of time, the *ajal musamman* (29:5, 53), is the ground of both: a scheduled universe does not need to be pushed. This is also the reason the same two words answer the sūrah's opening problem. The man of 29:10, who "takes the trial of men as God's punishment," fails in exactly these two: he cannot bear the present harm patiently, and he has delegated his affair to other people rather than to his Lord. *Tawakkul* is thus the sūrah's cure for the fear of human beings, because what a person entrusts to is what he will fear — and the verse of 29:52, "God suffices as a witness," uses the very same root of sufficiency that 65:3 attaches to trust: "and whoever puts his trust in God — He is sufficient for him." The sūrah's vocabulary of provision, beginning in the next verse, is the third member of the family: the One who is sufficient is the One who feeds.

**"In Their Lord" — and the Reward Without Reckoning**

Two further touches belong to the verse. The first is its restraint about the scale of what it secures: the sūrah does not say that patience and trust are worth so many chambers; it says they are the qualities of the workers whose reward is "excellent." But elsewhere the Book says of the patient what no wage-vocabulary can hold: *"only those who are patient are given their reward in full, without measure"* (39:10) — *bi-ghayri ḥisāb*, without being counted out. Read against 29:58's "niʿma ajru'l-ʿāmilīn," the combination is a small piece of theology: the relationship to God is a wage-relationship in its justice and not a wage-relationship in its generosity; the worker is paid, and the patient are paid without an audit. The second touch is that the verse keeps "Lord" in the plural of the community — *rabbihim*, "their Lord," not "my Lord": the patience and trust the sūrah describes are held in common, which is how the tradition understood the Anṣār's act of "settling in the Abode" (59:9) and the emigrants' dependence on strangers' generosity. The end of a *ḥijra* in this sūrah is not an individual in a private room but a community in chambers.

**The Limits of the Command: Patience Is Not Acquiescence**

Because this verse is so often used to counsel passivity toward injustice, its limits should be stated from the sūrah's own context. 29:59 addresses people who have been told to emigrate if they cannot worship (29:56) and who were promised that God settles the displaced (29:58); patience here is the temper in which a person acts rather than the reason not to act, and the same Book that commands *ṣabr* commands resistance where the weak are oppressed: *"and what is the matter with you, that you do not fight in God's cause and among the men, women and children who are counted weak, who say: our Lord, take us out of this town of oppressors, and appoint for us from Yourself a protector and appoint for us from Yourself a helper?"* (4:75). The permission to fight the wronged was given "because they were wronged," and "God is indeed able to help them" (22:39–40) — verses revealed later, in a different situation, which the jurists read together with Makkan patience verses rather than against them. What 29:59 excludes is not the work of justice but the two temptations that arise while justice is delayed: the hastening that invents its own outcome, and the despair that pretends the outcome is already decided. Al-Ghazālī's treatment of trust in the *Iḥyāʾ* ends on precisely that note, and it is a fair summary of the verse's practical teaching: the heart that has handed its affair over stops trying to control what it cannot and starts doing what it can — which is, in the sūrah's terms, patience, work, and prayer (29:45), all at once.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:60

> **And how many a creature does not carry its own provision — God provides for it, and for you. And He is the Hearing, the Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Answers the Question Nobody Asks Out Loud**

*wa-ka-ayyin min dābbatin lā taḥmilu rizqahā, Allāhu yarzuquhā wa-iyyākum, wa-huwa's-Samīʿu'l-ʿAlīm.* Verse 56 said: My earth is vast, so worship Me; verse 59 said: be patient and put your trust. Between the command to move and the command to trust lies the actual obstacle, which the sūrah names here instead of letting it be said: the fear that leaving means not eating. The source commentary on this verse notes exactly this function — the way wild animals depend on God for provision is brought as an example for believers — and it is worth observing how the sequence works. The Makkan who contemplated the Abyssinian crossing were not primarily afraid of drowning; they were afraid of a life without a trade, since a man's standing in Makkah was his position in the caravan economy that the sūrah's neighbours (106:1–4) name as the reason the city exists at all. Verse 60 answers the arithmetic of that fear with a fact about birds.

**"How Many a Creature": ka-ayyin, and the Grammar of Humility**

*ka-ayyin* is an expression of uncountable multiplicity — "and how many" — and it is used in the Qur'an to make the audience count what it has not been counting: "how many a town We destroyed" (7:4), "how many a prophet fought with him" (3:146), "how many a creature in the earth there is that does not carry its provision" here. The word *dābba* is from *d-b-b*, to move along the ground, and it denotes any living thing that walks or crawls — the same word used for "the beast of the earth" that will be brought forth as a sign at the end of the world (27:82), and it is the word of the Book's comprehensive statement about animal life: "God created every living creature (dābba) from water" (24:45) — a phrase that includes, in the commentators' reading, everything that moves, from the insect to the whale. The provision is *rizq*, what is apportioned and given, and the clause *lā taḥmilu rizqahā* is glossed by al-Ṭabarī as "she does not carry it, that is, she does not store it up or lay it by for the morrow, and yet God gives it to her." The negative is important: the creature is not described as weak, or ignorant, or unlucky, but as one who has nothing put away. A Makkan hearing this would think immediately of the *ḥaram* economy he lived in — a city that imported its grain, hoarded it in the houses of its merchants, and counted the winter and summer journeys as God's favour (106:1–4). The verse tells him that the God who feeds what has no granary is not bound by granaries.

**"God Provides for It, and for You": the Order of the Pronouns**

The Arabic puts the creature first in the verb's object and then adds *wa-iyyākum*, "and you," with the independent accusative that gives the "you" emphasis — a construction whose force is: and you too, though you were counting yourself apart. The rhetoricians of the Book drew fine distinctions in the same family of verses, and one of them belongs here: the two verses that forbid killing children arrange their pronouns in opposite orders, and the rhetoricians — al-Zamakhsharī among them — take the difference as deliberate. Where the motive named is actual poverty, "do not kill your children for poverty; We provide for you and for them" (6:151), the parents come first, because it is their deprivation that is being answered; where the motive named is anticipatory fear, "do not kill your children for fear of providing for them; We provide for them and for you" (17:31), the children come first, because the anxiety being treated is precisely the imagination of the child as a mouth that consumes. In our verse the creature comes first and the hearer second, and the same courtesy is at work: you are not the first term in the economy you are worried about. The commentators on our verse apply the identical logic: God mentions the bird's provision before the hearer's in order to break the assumption that the provider is the one who plans. This is not an argument against planning. Joseph's own advice in the sūrah that bears his name is an instruction to store: "you shall sow for seven years as usual; then what you harvest, leave the ear as it is, except a little of what you eat" (12:48), and the verse that follows the command to work in the market is: "and when the prayer is concluded, disperse through the lands and seek God's bounty, and remember God much, so that you may prosper" (62:10). The Qur'an's economics of trust are therefore double-entry: seek, and remember that seeking is not what produces.

**"They Go Out Hungry and Come Back Full": the Ḥadīth the Verse Explains**

The prophetic saying the sources quote on this verse is the tradition's own commentary, and its wording contains the same balance:

> *"If you were to put your trust in God with the true trust due to Him, you would be provided for as the bird is provided for: it goes out in the morning with an empty belly and returns in the evening with a full one."* (from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, in Sunan al-Tirmidhī, graded ḥasan by al-Tirmidhī)

The bird in the ḥadīth is not stationary. It *ghadā* and *rāḥa* — it goes out early and comes back — so the image that illustrates *tawakkul* is movement and labour performed in the certainty that the result is not in the labourer's hand. The Sufi manuals of conduct on livelihood drew the conclusion in a form that became proverbial in the piety literature: provision is of two kinds, what you seek and what seeks you, and negligence about the lawful means is negligence about what has been guaranteed — for the guarantee was given to a creature that "goes out" and "comes back." The sūrah of 29:60 itself makes the same distinction, since it placed "be patient and trust" (29:59) after the commands to emigrate and to worship, not after a permission to sit.

**"And He Is the Hearing, the Knowing": the Names That Make the Verse Practical**

The source commentary, following al-Rāzī, notes the sense of these two names in this position: God is aware of and provides for the need of every thing, and He hears the supplication of the one who asks Him and knows the need of the one who is silent. That reading gives 29:60 a structure the sūrah uses elsewhere: a command about provision, then the two names that make the command addressable. And it puts the verse in the sūrah's largest frame, because the identical pair of names closed 29:5 — "whoever hopes for the meeting with God, God's term is coming; and He is the Hearing, the Knowing." The chapter has thus enclosed the whole of its middle argument between two verses that end the same way: what is coming on a term (5) and what is given day by day (60) are both held by a God who hears and knows. Al-Qurṭubī's comment on the verse's ending is the simple, pastoral one: if He hears the cry of the creature that has no voice of reason and knows the need of the one who cannot articulate it, then the petition of a person who asks is not addressed to a deaf sky. The practical consequence for a community that had been told to move and to worship is that provision is not one more thing to be secured alone: it is asked for, in the language of the *duʿāʾ*, in a sūrah that has just commanded prayer (29:45) and just promised that the striving believer is guided (29:69).

**The A Fortiori Argument, and What It Does Not Prove**

Set out as the sūrah sets it, the verse's reasoning is of the *a fortiori* type the logicians call the argument from the lesser to the greater: if what neither earns, plans, saves, nor deserves is fed without fail, then the being addressed with command, promise, and threat is not going to be left unfed — and the *ḥamdu* clause of 29:63 ("praise be to God, but most of them do not reason") confirms that the sūrah treats this as an argument whose conclusion is available to anyone who thinks, not to one who must be told. Two limits, however, are built into the verse and should be named, since both are ignored by readers who want the verse to say either too little or too much. It does not teach that the world is a machine for your benefit: the birds of 29:60 are not promises to the birds, and the sūrah's own catalogue of punished nations (29:35–40) is there to prevent the reading of providence as prosperity. Nor does it license passivity, since its own imperative is "worship Me" and its own exemplar is a creature that goes out and returns. What it does teach, and what a person under real economic pressure can actually use, is a reordering of causality in the mind: the provision is not in the granary, the firm, the exchange rate, or the passport; it is in the hand of the One who hears, and the place where a person can be found when it arrives is wherever worship can be kept. That is why the verse stands exactly where it does — after the command to emigrate, before the reckoning of the sun and moon (29:61) and before the statement that God "strips and widens provision for whom He wills of His servants" (29:62): the amplitude or the constriction of a livelihood is itself in the same hand, and neither of the two states is evidence about God's pleasure.
