## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:61

> **If you ask them who created the heavens and the earth, and subjected the sun and the moon, they will surely say: God. How then are they turned away?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Method of the Sūrah's Final Movement**

*wa-la-in saʾaltahum man khalaqa's-samāwāti wa'l-arḍa wa-sakhkhara'sh-shamsa wa'l-qamara la-yaqūlunna'llāh, fa-annā yuʾfakūn.* The last nine verses of the sūrah argue by asking, and the asking is the point. Twice in three verses the chapter says "if you ask them … they will surely say," with the answer fixed in advance (29:61 and 29:63, where the question is about the rain and the reply is again "God"). The verb is *saʾala*, not *qāla lahum* — the sūrah does not announce conclusions to the objectors but puts a question whose answer they already own, and then shows them the distance between that answer and their conduct. This is the same method the sūrah taught for argument with the People of the Book (29:46: "argue with them in the manner that is best"), and it is the method of a whole family of Qur'anic passages: "who provides for you from the sky and the earth?" (10:31), "who owns the dominion of all things?" (23:84–89), "were they created out of nothing, or are they the creators?" (52:35), and, in the sūrah of the Prostration, "say: He who gave you hearing and sight and hearts — little do you give thanks" (32:23). The technique is not a rhetorical flourish but a dialectic: it begins from premises the opponent will not dispute, so that the conclusion cannot be refused without refusing what the opponent already said.

**"Subjected the Sun and the Moon": Creation as Service**

The verb *sakhkhara* — to subdue, to make compliant, to press into service — is chosen because the heavens' obedience is the sūrah's real subject. The sun and moon are not deities, not signs of fortune, not eternal; they are *musakhkharāt*, conscripted, each "running to an appointed term" (13:2; 31:29; 35:13; 39:5), and the Book's comprehensive version of the same claim: "and He has subjected to you whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth — all from Him" (45:13). The phrase "an appointed term" is the sūrah's own vocabulary as well, at 29:5 and 29:53: the heavens keep a schedule, and so do you. The Qur'an states the same fact as a command about worship: "and among His signs are the night and the day and the sun and the moon; do not prostrate to the sun or to the moon, but prostrate to God who created them, if it is He you worship" (41:37) — a verse at which, in the practice of the schools that count it among the prostration-verses, the reciter goes down to the ground, and the reason is visible: the verse's argument ends in the body. The Makkan setting makes the point sharper, since the Arabs knew the sun and moon as objects of devotion elsewhere in the region and the sūrah's own Abraham-section had already argued from them: the Abraham of 6:76–79 refuses the star, then the moon, then the sun as they set — "I do not love those that set" (6:76) — and arrives at "I turn my face to the One who originated the heavens and the earth, upright, and I am not of the associators" (6:79). Verse 61 is the sūrah's own restatement of that Abrahamic argument in the third person, and it belongs with 29:16–27 as the second half of the chapter's answer to the same error: Abraham argued from the bodies' setting; this verse argues from their being conscripted.

**"How Then Are They Turned Away?" — the Grammar of Astonishment**

*fa-annā yuʾfakūn* is the sūrah's single word of moral geometry, and the source commentary on this verse spells out the lexicography: *annā* is "how, from where," and *yuʾfakūn* is the passive of *a-f-k*, "to turn, to shift" — hence *ifk*, the slander, which is a turning of the truth into its opposite, and *muʾtalif/muʾtafir*, "divergent." The verb's passive voice is what the commentators press: not "how do they turn away" but "how are they turned away," so that the objectors are described as already displaced, moved from a position their own lips confirm. Al-Zamakhsharī hears in the clause astonishment rather than information — "how can this be?" — and the source notes that the phrase always carries the negative connotation of a turning from truth to falsehood, from fair acts to ugly ones. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss is plainer: "how are they deluded, and from what direction do they come by the turning of their hearts, when they acknowledge that God is the creator and the subjecter?" The sentence thus performs the sūrah's whole diagnosis of the Makkan position: the problem is not a missing belief but a wrong direction of consequence — a God confessed in one breath and bypassed in the next. This is the same structure as 29:41, the people who "know" the web is frail and continue to live inside it (29:41's "if they but knew"), and it is why the chapter can close its evidence section without new evidence: the deficit is a turning, not a datum.

**The Concession, and Why It Does Not Save Anyone**

The verse's "they will surely say: God" is a remarkable piece of theological reporting, and the sūrah's audience is being credited with real monotheism before it is reproached. The Quraysh's position, as the Qur'an states it elsewhere, was not atheism: "and if you ask them who created them, they will surely say: God — how then are they turned away?" (43:87, the verse the source commentary names as this one's twin), and "most of them believe in God while associating partners" (12:106). The sūrah of Yūsuf's clause is the reason the classical *ʿaqīda* discussions treat this passage as decisive: association (*shirk*) is not a denial of God's existence or even of His creatorhood; it is the redistribution of what belongs to Him — worship, trust, the disposition of fear, the claim on the future. Ibn Taymiyyah's analysis of the Makkans' *tawḥīd* is that of a "Lordship" conceded ("they knew that God creates and provides, and that their idols could do neither") and a "divinity" withheld, and he reads verses like 29:61 as the proof that knowing God is not believing in the sense the Qur'an requires — which is why the sūrah's own definition of belief has been, from verse 2, that it is tested, lived, and costed, not recited. The verse's rebuke, then, is aimed at a believer's theology with an unbeliever's practice; and it is aimed at the reader, for whom the confession "God created" is cheap and the subjection of sun and moon is an argument he has not yet finished with his own calendar.

**From the Heavens to the Harvest: the Verse's Place in the Sequence**

Structurally, 29:61 sits between two provision-verses and does the connecting work. Verse 60 said that God feeds the bird that has no store; verse 61 says that the same God runs the sun and the moon; verse 62 will say that "God strips and widens provision for whom He wills of His servants"; and verse 63 will ask who sends down rain and revive the dead earth, then command the reply "praise be to God, but most of them do not reason." The chain is an argument in four links: livelihood depends on rain and heat, rain and heat are not in anyone's hands, the distribution of livelihood is therefore not a human achievement, and the conclusion is worship (60's "so worship Me," 56's, returning in 64's "the life of this world is nothing but play and diversion"). This is why the sūrah chooses the sun and moon here rather than, say, the camels or the sanctuary: the two bodies are what Makkah's economy could not negotiate with. Al-Rāzī, who loved the astronomy of the argument, makes the point in his own manner: whoever controls what the sun and moon do controls the field and the purse, and since nobody but God controls it, the man who fears hunger and trusts a merchant has misassigned his fear and his trust — the exact error of the people of 29:41 with their protectors. The verse thus continues to speak in the same terms it has used since verse 2: the trial of this world is a matter of where you send your fear, and the sun and moon are the proof that you are not the one who arranges what you fear losing.

**What the Verse Teaches About Argument**

For a reader whose task is persuasion rather than private conviction, 29:61 supplies a method with three moving parts. Ask a question whose answer the other person already holds (not "do you agree that God exists?" but "who made the thing you are anxious about?"). Establish the answer as a fact about their own practice, not about your tradition, since "they will surely say" is what makes the move cooperative. Then locate the inconsistency, without pretending the inconsistency is ignorance: the objectors are "turned away," not uninformed. The Prophet's ﷺ practice at Ṭāʾif is an illustration from the other side of the same rule — he was offered the destruction of his mockers and asked instead for their children's future (the report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, cited above at 29:29), because an argument that ends in the opponent's annihilation has abandoned the point of asking. And the sūrah itself supplies the tone: verse 61 does not say "how despicable they are"; it says "how are they turned," in the voice of astonishment, which is the register of a person who cannot understand why anyone would leave a house that stands for one that does not. The reader of the chapter will hear, in that small clause, the whole of its mercy: the argument is not being won, it is being grieved.

## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:62

> **God outspreads provision and narrows it for whomever He will of His servants. Truly God is Knowing of all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Creator to Provider: the Transition the Verse Is Placed To Make**

*Allāhu yabsuṭu'r-rizqa liman yashāʾu min 'ibādihi wa-yaqdir, inna Allāha bi-kulli shayʾin 'alīm.* The source commentary on verses 62–63 describes the function exactly: this verse is the transition from God as Creator to God as Sustainer. Verse 61 had the objectors conceding that God made the heavens, the earth, the sun and the moon; verse 63 will get the same concession about rain and the revival of dead land; between them stands this verse, which names the thing no one disputes in theory and everyone misreads in practice — who gives and withholds the means of living. The Makkan economy made the point acute: a city of merchants, whose prestige was measured in the stretching of a purse and whose theology of fortune had room for omens, soothsaying and the blessing of a patron's house. Against all of that the sūrah puts one clause with two verbs, and the two verbs are the whole of the verse's argument: provision comes and provision is measured, and neither state is a human achievement.

**"Yabsuṭu": The Hand, and "Yaqdiru": the Measure**

*baṣṭ* is the act of spreading out, of opening the hand and stretching the arm; the noun *basīṭ* is used for a plain, *absaṭa* for extending a favour, and the Book uses the idiom of the hand and of the grip for God's power without qualification: "they did not measure God with His true measure when they said: God has not revealed anything to a human being" (6:91), immediately followed by "and the earth will be a handful [of His grip] all of it on the Day of Resurrection, and the heavens rolled up in His right hand; glory be to Him, and exalted be He above what they associate" (39:67). So the verse's two verbs describe, in the order of Arabic's own etymologies, a generosity that is unbounded in itself and a generosity that is bounded in its delivery — the outspread hand and the measured cup. This is why the pairing is quoted by the tradition against two opposite errors: the person who thinks abundance is simply the result of his own cleverness, and the person who thinks constriction means he has been abandoned.

**"For Whomever He Will of His Servants" — and the Ending That Rules Out Caprice**

The phrase *li-man yashāʾ* is the crux of the verse's theology, and its meaning is fixed by the clause that closes it: "God is Knowing of all things." The verse is not saying that provision is distributed at random but that it is not distributed according to the criterion the hearers were applying, which was worth — Qārūn's criterion, precisely: "he said: I was given it only because of knowledge I have — did he not know that God had destroyed before him generations greater than him in might and more abundant in wealth?" (28:78) Against that, 29:62 sets the pair "will" plus "knowing," which is the sūrah's own settled formula: at 29:21 it said "He punishes whom He will and has mercy on whom He will, and to Him you are returned," and at 29:5 it set hope for the meeting with God under "His term is coming; and He is the Hearing, the Knowing." The classical debate between the Ashʿarīs, who insisted that nothing is obligatory on God and that He "is not questioned about what He does, while they will be questioned" (21:23), and the Muʿtazilīs, who held that the wise agent must do what is best (*aṣlaḥ*) for His servants, has in this verse one of its shared texts, because both verbs of the verse are governed by the same will and the whole is sealed by knowledge: the distribution is free, and it is not blind. Al-Zamakhsharī, whose Muʿtazilī commitments are visible in his *Kashshāf* on such passages, reads "li-man yashāʾ" as a rebuke of presumption rather than a statement of arbitrariness, and adds "wa-yaqdiru — and He narrows with wisdom," which the Ashʿarī commentators of the sūrah, Ibn Kathīr among them, accept as the point of the ending even while denying that wisdom obliges anything. The verse's own practical target is neither school but the reflex of both: the reading of one's bank balance as a verdict on one's soul.

**The Verse's Narrative Twin: Qārūn's People Recite It in the Morning**

The strongest commentary on this verse is a verse elsewhere in which the identical clause is put into the mouths of characters in a story. Qārūn, told by his people to seek the abode to come and not to forget his share of this world, answers: "I was given it only because of knowledge I have" (28:78) — the claim that wealth is a competence and a verdict at once. The next morning, after the earth has swallowed him and his house (28:81), the men who had the day before been wishing they were in his position say something else entirely: "alas — God outspreads provision and narrows it for whom He will of His servants. If God had not been gracious to us, He would have caused the earth to swallow us. Do we not see that the disbelievers do not prosper?" (28:82). Our verse's clause is, word for word, their conversion; and the sūrah's point in telling the story is that the reversal took one day, so that the lesson is not that wealth is bad but that its direction of travel is not in the owner's hand. The Book elsewhere draws the general law the same way: "and if God were to extend provision to His servants, they would transgress in the earth — but He sends down, with a measure, what He wills" (42:12), and "your Lord outspreads provision and narrows it for whom He will; He is Aware, Seeing, of His servants" (17:30), where the closing names "Aware, Seeing" do for that verse exactly what "Knowing of all things" does for ours. The answer to the question "why does the wicked man have more?" is therefore not that he does not, and not that a simple swap is coming later, but that the measure is part of the trial and the giving is part of it too: "and We try you with evil and with good, and to Us you are returned" (21:35). Al-Saʿdī's gloss on verses like these is the practical one: do not read the gift as approval or the withholding as anger, for both are tests, and the one thing the two states have in common is that each of them will be asked about.

**"Knowing of All Things": What the Verse Asks of the Rich and of the Poor**

Because the distribution comes from knowledge, it can be examined without being despaired of, and the tradition's use of this verse was therefore overwhelmingly ethical rather than fatalistic. To those with amplitude the Book turns the same clause into a duty of the hand: "your brothers whom God has placed under your right hand — whoever has his brother under his right hand, let him feed him of what he eats and clothe him of what he wears" (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim) is how the Prophet ﷺ applied the logic that what you have was given and not earned, and the Book's own form of the instruction is the clause that pairs hope with work: "whoever hopes to meet his Lord, let him do a righteous deed and let him not associate anyone with his Lord's worship" (18:110) — the same hope this sūrah named at 29:5, where the hope of the meeting with God is answered not by a claim but by "God's term is coming; and He is the Hearing, the Knowing." To those in constriction it gives two instruments, and both are in the sunna: "look at those who are below you and do not look at those who are above you, for it is likelier that you will not despise God's blessing upon you" (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Hurayra) — a rule of comparison that changes what "narrowing" means by changing the standard against which it is measured — and the Prophet's ﷺ own practice of seeking refuge from anxiety and from helplessness, which is the recognition that poverty's real danger is not discomfort but the fracture of trust. Neither instrument is a consent to injustice; the verse says that God narrows and stretches, not that the powerful should, and the sūrah's own account of the defrauded (29:36, "do not diminish people's things") stands between this verse and any comfortable reading of it.

**The Verse and the Sūrah's Economics of Trust**

Placed between the bird of 29:60 and the rain of 29:63, verse 62 completes the sūrah's small treatise on provision, and the treatise's argument is that the anxiety and the arrogance which money produces are both versions of the same mistake: treating provision as something in the possession of the creature. Verse 60 said the creature without a storehouse is fed; verse 62 says that the fed creature is still given or withheld by a will guided by knowledge; verse 63 will say that the mechanism itself — rain on dead land — is a sign whose author everybody names. The three verses hold together the two things the Qur'an always holds together, providence and effort: the believer still sows, still trades, still stores what Joseph's people were told to store (12:48), but he does not locate his security in the store, and so he can afford both generosity in the years of outspread and patience in the years of measure. The sūrah's final word on this is its last verse, "God is with the *muḥsinīn*" (29:69) — the doers of beauty, whose conduct in prosperity and in privation is what the trial consists of, and who, having no claim on the outspread hand, have nothing to lose by using it.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:63

> **And if you ask them: who sends down water for you from the sky and revives the earth with it after its death? they will surely say: God. Say: praise be to God — but most of them do not understand.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Question, and the Answer That Concedes Everything**

*wa-la-in saʾaltahum man yunazzilu mina's-samāʾi māʾan fa-aḥya bihi'l-arḍa baʿda mawtihā la-yaqūlunna'llāh, quli'l-ḥamdu li'llāh, bal aktharuhum lā yaʿqilūn.* This is the third of the sūrah's three "ask them" verses (29:61 on creation, 29:62 on the measure of provision, 29:63 on rain), and its answer — "God" — is not the objectors' conversion but their own theology. The source commentary cites the cluster in which the Qur'an reports this same concession: 10:31, 23:84–85, 29:61, 31:25, 39:38, 43:9 and 43:87. The point of the repeated concession is that the Makkan dispute was never about who runs the weather. The Quraysh called God the creator and the provider — "do you call upon Baʿl and leave God, the Best of Creators, your Lord and the Lord of your first forefathers?" (37:95–96) — and they set aside a portion of their produce and their cattle for God and a portion for their partners, so that what was for their partners reached God and what was for God did not reach their partners (6:136), and they still asked those partners for rain when the sky closed. The sūrah's argument runs on the gap between the conceded God and the practised trust, and verse 63 places its finger on the mechanism by which the gap is maintained: a failure of *ʿaql* — "but most of them do not understand."

**"Who Sends Down Water and Revives the Earth After Its Death"**

The two halves of the claim are one act in the Qur'an's telling, and each is separately an argument. The descent of water is a sign of mercy in a land where nothing else is: "and it is He who sends the winds as good news before His mercy, and We send down from the sky purifying water, that We may revive thereby a dead land and give drink to many of the creatures We have made" (25:48–49), and "God sends down water from the sky and revives thereby the earth after its death; in that is a sign for a people who listen" (16:65). The second half, that the earth *was dead* — the Arabic *mawtihā*, its death, is the noun used for the soil in the two halves of the year — is the Book's standing proof of the resurrection: the source commentary lists the family, and its two clearest members are "so look at the traces of God's mercy: how He revives the earth after its death — surely that One is the reviver of the dead, and He is over all things powerful" (30:50), and "a sign for them is the dead earth: We give it life and bring forth from it grain, and from it they eat" (36:33) — the verse's own proof, in the very words the sūrah of Yā Sīn uses for the resurrection. Revival is thus an argument from repetition: what happens every spring is the resurrection practised. It also makes the sūrah's theology of provision concrete, since the Makkan's water came from a sky they could not order and their crops from an earth they could not warm.

**"Say: Praise Be to God" — Why the Verse Praises Instead of Concluding**

The strangest clause in the verse is the reply God commands when the opponents have just been defeated by their own answer. The commentators give several senses, and they are compatible. (i) Praise is due to God because the proof has been established on the enemy's own tongue: the truth of God does not depend on the objectors' conversion, and the believer's joy is that God's case has won, not that his own argument has (cf. 35:34 — the people of the Garden saying *"al-ḥamdu li'llāhi'lladhī hadānā li-hādhā wa-mā kunnā li-nahtadiya lawlā an hadāna'llāh"* — "praise be to God who guided us to this, and we would not have been guided had God not guided us" and 6:1 *"wa-qūlu'l-ḥamdu li'llāhi'lladhī lam yattakhidh waladan wa-lam yakun lahu sharīkun fi'l-mulki wa-lam yakun lahu waliyyun mina'dh-dhulli wa-kabbirhu takbīrā"* — the verse that ends the association of partners and the need of a protector, the two things this sūrah has been arguing about, with praise and greatness). (ii) Praise is due because the revival of the dead earth is the promise of resurrection, and the believer praises God for a mercy he is certain of even while most people fail to understand it — the same sense in which the Qur'an teaches the faithful to praise before the gift is finished: "that God may forgive you your past and future sins, complete His favour upon you, and guide you a straight path — and that God may aid you with a mighty aid" (48:2–3). (iii) Praise is a rebuke: it says that the case is closed and that the appropriate response to a conceded truth is gratitude rather than more evidence (cf. 39:69, "and the earth will shine with the light of its Lord, and the book will be placed, and the prophets and the witnesses will be brought," and the sūrah's own instruction to recite and pray, in which the remembrance is the point (29:45). The single phrase thus does the work of three: it thanks, it wins, and it dismisses.

**"But Most of Them Do Not Understand" — the Sūrah's Realism About Crowds**

*bal aktharuhum lā yaʿqilūn*, and the wording is careful twice over. *Akthar* — "the majority of them" — and not "all of them": the Qur'an's sociology never totals the other side (cf. 3:110, "they are the best community brought forth for the people" said of the believers, and 7:181, "and among the people We created is a community that guides by the truth and by it acts justly"; the source commentary on 29:47 cites 3:199 for exactly such a person: "and among the People of the Book there is whoever believes in God and in what has been sent down to you and what has been sent down to them, devout, not selling the signs of God for a small price". And *lā yaʿqilūn* — they do not *hold* what they know, which is the third and closing use of the root in this sūrah (29:35's "a sign for a people who understand," 29:43's "none understand them except those who know," and here the majority of the concede-then-continue). The chapter's epistemology is thus stated three times at its three hinge-points, and always in the same shape: the datum exists, the inference is available, and what fails is the grip — not the evidence but the *ʿaql* that would restrain a person from living inconsistently. This is why the verse is not a complaint about the intellect but about the will's use of it: the objectors already know who sends the rain, and knowledge of that kind obliges at once (cf. 61:2–3: "great hatred with God that you say what you do not do"). Al-Rāzī, who never lets an *akthar* pass, notes that the Qur'an's use of "most" is a mercy to the exception and a warning to the majority: the few do understand, and the many will be asked about what they knew.

**The Rain in the Prophet's ﷺ Practice: How Belief in the Giver Is Kept Alive**

That the verse's argument is meant to become a habit rather than a conclusion is shown by the practice it generated. The Prophet ﷺ, on seeing the first cloud of the season, would change colour, and when ʿĀʾisha asked him about it he said: "what assures me that there is not a punishment in it? A people were punished with wind" — and when he saw that it would rain he said: *"O God, I ask You for its good and the good of what it is sent with, and I take refuge in You from its evil and the evil of what it is sent with"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim); and when the rain fell he said: *"O God, make it a rain that brings benefit (ṣayyiban nāfiʿan)"* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, in the Book of the Prayer for Rain), and when the rain fell he lifted his garment so that the drops would fall on it, saying: "because it has recently come from its Lord" (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim and Sunan Abū Dāwūd, from Anas). Those few words are 29:63 lived: the same water that the sūrah offers as an argument for God is, in the Prophet's ﷺ hands, an event to be asked about, thanked for, and sheltered under — so that the man who sees rain as merely weather and the man who sees it as proof are, in the sūrah's diagnosis, the same failure of understanding at two different levels of refinement. And the *ṣalāt al-istikāʾ*, the prayer for rain with which the community answered drought — the Prophet ﷺ going out to the prayer-place with his companions, raising his hands in supplication so that the whiteness of his armpits was seen, and turning his cloak inside out at the end, in the reports transmitted in the two Ṣaḥīḥs from Anas — is the verse's inverse: when the sky withholds, the remedy is not more cleverness but the address.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Sequence**

Verse 63 also supplies the missing third premise in the chapter's argument about worship. Verse 60 established that the creature without a storehouse is fed; verse 61 that the sun and moon are conscripted; verse 62 that provision is stretched and narrowed by a knowing will. But provision could still, in a hearer's imagination, be a machine — a system of causes that needs no address. Verse 63 closes that by putting the revival of the dead earth, the most obvious case of the impossible becoming ordinary, in the same mouth as the resurrection: the water is sent down (the verb is *yunazzilu*, the form of descent in instalments, the very form the Book uses for its own coming down: "a Qur'an We recited to people at intervals, and We sent it down in stages" (17:106; cf. 76:23). Sky, rain, soil, resurrection and revelation are, in this sūrah, one family of gifts — and the verse ends where the sūrah's whole final movement is about to go: to the question of what a life is, when the life of this world is, in the next verse, called play (29:64). "Praise be to God" is therefore not a pious reflex but the correct response to an argument that has run out of its opponents: the case is not being lost, the people are simply not holding what they have.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:64

> **The life of this world is nothing but play and diversion; and the abode of the Hereafter — that is the life, if they but knew.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Definition That Comes After the Proof**

*wa-mā ha'dhiḥi ḥayātu'd-dunyā illā laʿbun wa-lahw, wa-inna d-dāra'l-ākhira hiya'l-ḥayawān, law kānū yaʿlamūn.* The sūrah has just taken from the objectors three concessions — that God created, that He sends the sun and the moon into their courses, that He sends rain and revives the dead earth (29:61–63) — and verse 64 draws the consequence, which is not a proof but a re-ordering of value. If the world's water, weather and harvest come from outside anyone's power, then the world's occupations are not the business that God is being consulted about; they are play. The verse's position is therefore the hinge between the sūrah's two halves: the argument about evidence ends here, and the last five verses speak about what to do with a world so described. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss is characteristically spare: "the life of this world is play and diversion for its people, in that it passes away and does not last; and the abode of the Hereafter, that is the life — because it is the abiding, and its people do not die and its dwellers do not depart from it."

**"Laʿb" and "Lahw": Two Words for the Same Thing Lost**

The two nouns are near-synonyms in ordinary speech and are separated by the lexicographers with care. *Laʿb* is an act done for the sake of the act, without a purpose beyond it — play in the strict sense; *lahw* is what diverts the mind from what it should be attending to, and the root gives *lahā*, to be diverted, to lag behind, and its cousin *laghw*, idle and worthless speech, from which the successful believers of the sūrah of the Faithful "turn away" (23:3). So the pair names two defects of the same activity: it aims at nothing, and it turns you away from something. The Qur'an uses them together most fully in the verse this one echoes: *"the life of this world is nothing but play and diversion; and if you believe and are God-fearing, He will give you your rewards, and He does not ask you of your wealth"* (47:36) — where the pairing is followed at once by the statement that God is not asking for anything out of what you have, which is the answer to the anxiety that makes play seem necessary. The lexicographers note, on the parallel statement in the sūrah of the Cattle (6:32), that the two words are joined because *laʿb* vitiates the act and *lahw* vitiates the attention: play is purposeless, diversion is displaced attention. The verse is thus not a sermon on frivolity; it is a claim about direction: an activity that has no end beyond itself and that occupies the mind which was made for something else.

**"That Is the Life": The Word the Sūrah Chooses and Why**

The predicate *hiya'l-ḥayawān* is not the ordinary word for life. The Qur'an says *al-ḥayātu'd-dunyā* for this world's life a few lines earlier in the same sentence, and for the other abode it uses *ḥayawān*, a form that Arabic uses to name an abiding state or a thing in the fullness of its kind, and the effect of the substitution is that this life is left without the word: the other is "the life," and this one is its play. Raghib al-Iṣfahānī, whose *al-Mufradāt* is the standard dictionary of Qur'anic vocabulary, puts the point as a rule of the Book's usage: life in the Qur'an sometimes means the present life and sometimes the life to come, and where the latter is meant the word is used with a definite article that excludes the other, since real life is what does not end. The same reasoning produces the verse's own comparative elsewhere: "and the last abode is better for you than the first" (93:4), "and the abode of the Hereafter is better for those who are God-fearing — do you not reason?" (12:109), and "do you prefer the life of this world, while the Hereafter is better and more lasting?" (87:16–17). Note also the paradox the word acquired in later Arabic, where *ḥayawān* came to mean "animal": what the sūrah calls the only true life would, in a later idiom, name the being whose entire existence is this life — a shift that is itself a warning, since the animal in that sense is the creature for which nothing beyond the present exists.

**"If They But Knew": The Sūrah's Own Refrain**

*law kānū yaʿlamūn* occurs first in the chapter at the parable of the spider (29:41: the frailty of the web-house is a fact "if they but knew"), and it returns here at the verse that says what the world is. The repetition ties the two together: the knowledge that the refuge is frail and the knowledge that the world is play are, for this sūrah, one knowledge, and the reason the verse can claim that a mere change of knowing would change everything is that it has spent sixty-two verses showing that nothing is missing from what the objectors know. The refrain also closes the argument against the demand for hastened punishment: the punishment is delayed and "for soon they will know" (29:66), a phrase that turns the same verb from a wish into a threat. Al-Biqāʿī's analysis of such correspondences notes that the sūrah brackets its parable and its world-analysis with the same conditional wish, so that the reader who arrives at verse 64 has already been told once, in the chapter's centre, that he is not short of information.

**The World's Five Names, and the Verse That Completes This One**

The fullest description of the world in the Qur'an is in sūrah al-Ḥadīd, which the source commentary cites here, and it is worth setting out entire because it itemises exactly what 29:64 compresses: *"know that the life of this world is play, diversion, adornment, boasting among you, and vying in increase of wealth and children — like rain whose vegetation delights the sowers, then it withers and you see it turn yellow, then it becomes stubble; and in the Hereafter is a severe punishment, and forgiveness from your Lord and pleasure. And the life of this world is nothing but a provision of delusion"* (57:20). Five things are named — the pointless act, the diversion, the surface beauty, the status competition, the accumulation — and the verse ends by conceding that the world is a *matāʿ*, a provision of use, not a delusion in itself: the delusion is taking it as a dwelling. This is the balance the whole sūrah keeps: it calls the world play in the same chapter that commands emigration across it (29:56), travel through it (29:20), work for provision in it (29:60), and praise for the rain that makes it grow (29:63). The verse that follows 29:64 in the sūrah of the Lowest Rank makes the ratio explicit: "but you prefer the life of this world, while the Hereafter is better and more lasting" (87:16–17), and the Prophet's ﷺ own parable for the same choice is preserved in the tradition: *"what have I to do with the world? My likeness and the world's is a man who rides under a tree, takes shade, and then leaves it and goes on."* (from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿUmar, transmitted in Musnad Aḥmad and Sunan Ibn Māja)

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

Because this verse is the standard text for ascetic world-rejection, its limits need to be stated from the sūrah itself. First, it does not say the world is evil or illusory: verse 44 said it was created *bi'l-ḥaqq*, in truth, and verse 63 said its rain is a sign; a thing that is true, good, and significant is not an evil, and *laʿb* describes our use of it, not its make. Second, it does not forbid enjoyment: the Book's own summary of the balanced life is "seek, in what God has given you, the abode of the Hereafter, and do not forget your share of this world" (28:77), and the practice of the Prophet ﷺ — racing with ʿĀʾisha, letting the Abyssinians play in the mosque on a festival day, telling Ḥanzala, when he complained that he was one man in the Prophet's ﷺ presence and another at home with his wife and children, "Ḥanzala, an hour and an hour — were you to continue as you are when you are with me, the angels would shake hands with you on your beds and in the roads" (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim) — belongs to a religion that does not read play as a sin. Third, it does not devalue work or wealth as such: it has just praised the workers (29:58) and described provision as God's giving and withholding (29:62), and the verse's *takāthur* — "vying in increase" (57:20) — is a sin of comparison, not of production. What the verse denies, and denies absolutely, is ultimacy. The world is the place of the trial, and a trial is not a punishment nor a reward; it is an examination whose results are kept for another place.

**The Psychology of "Play", and Why the Verse Is Timely**

The distinction between *laʿb* and *lahw* maps with unusual precision onto what the modern study of attention has to say about a society built on entertainment, notification and status display: activities that supply immediate reward without cumulative purpose, and a competition for relative standing that resets as soon as it is won. Two facts known to every reader make the sūrah's diagnosis bite: the hours pass in ways the person would not choose if asked, and the satisfactions decay — the "hedonic treadmill" of the psychological literature is the empirical name of *lahw*, the diversion that must be renewed because it never lands. The verse's cure is not the abolition of pleasure but the transfer of the centre of gravity: a person who believes that the abiding life is real behaves differently about money, reputation, and the twenty minutes before sleep, and the sūrah's promised proof for that claim is the whole of its previous argument, from the assay of 29:2 to the rain of 29:63. Ibn al-Qayyim's treatment of the world in *al-Fawāʾid* states the practical rule that the verse leaves behind: the world is a place for crossing, not for settling; the error is not to want good things but to build on them, and a building on a good thing is what makes it into an idol. This is how 29:64 belongs to the chapter's beginning as well: the man of 29:10 who fears "the trial of men" is the man who has made other people's judgement ultimate, and verse 64, read as the sūrah reads it, is the instruction to stop treating as life what is, in the end, play.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:65

> **And when they ride in the ship, they call upon God, devoting the religion to Him entirely; but when He delivers them to the land, behold, they associate partners.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Last of the Sūrah's Nature-Arguments Is a Storm at Sea**

*wa-idhā rakibū fi'l-fulki daʿawu'llāha mukhliṣīna lahu d-dīn, fa-lammā najjāhum ilā'l-barri idhā hum yushrikūn.* The chapter's final series of proofs has moved from the heavens and the earth (29:61) to provision (29:62) to the rain and the dead earth (29:63); verse 65 completes it with the sea, because the sea is where the human being is most visibly not in charge. The sūrah has already used the ship once, in the Noah narrative: "then We saved him and whoever was with him in the ship, and We made it a sign for the worlds" (29:15) — and the repetition is meaningful: in the first case the ship is the vehicle of salvation for a believing minority, in the second it is the place where a polytheist majority briefly becomes monotheist and then goes back. The Qur'an tells the story of that reversal at greater length in sūrah Yūnus, and the two verses are best read together: *"It is He who carries you on the land and the sea, until, when you are in the ships and they sail with them with a fair wind, and they rejoice in them, a storm-wind strikes them and the waves come upon them from every side, and they think that they are encompassed (ẓannū annahum uḥīṭa bihim), they call upon God, devoting the religion to Him, saying: if You deliver us from this, we shall surely be among the grateful. But when He delivers them, behold, they act tyrannically in the land without right"* (10:22–23). The verb of the wave's closing in — *uḥīṭa bihim* — is the same one this sūrah used for Hell at 29:54, and the sūrah's own logic is in the grammar: the sea is a rehearsal of the encompassment, and the shipboard prayer is what a person says when the rehearsal has begun.

**"Devoting the Religion to Him" — Sincerity Extracted by Fear**

*mukhliṣīna lahu d-dīn* is the phrase the Qur'an reserves for worship purged of shared intention, and the source commentary on this verse (following al-Ṭabarī) explains why it appears on a sinking ship and nowhere else in these people's lives: "the sincerity of their devotion in the face of the severity of the affliction that has overtaken them." On deck there is no room for the patron, the ancestor, the idol, the peer-group, or the appearance — the things that divide a person's intention in ordinary conditions (cf. 39:3, the answer given by those who take protectors: "we worship them only that they may bring us near to God"; 39:14, "say: God I worship, devoting to Him my religion"; 40:14 and 65, the two verses the source lists here). The verse is therefore making an assertion about human nature that the tradition has always found credible: under pressure of death the hierarchy of loyalties declares itself, and every created intermediary collapses, because at that moment nothing created can help. The Qur'an states the corresponding principle as a general rule: *"and when harm touches the human being, he calls upon us, lying on his side or sitting or standing; but when We lift it from him, he passes on as though he had not called upon us about a harm that touched him"* (10:12), and *"when affliction touches the human being he calls upon us; then when We change it for a blessing from Us, he says: only because of knowledge it was given to me"* (39:49). And the sūrah of the Ant describes the same prayer from the inside: *"is He who answers the desperate one when he calls upon Him and removes the evil, and makes you successors in the earth — is there a god with God? Little do you remember?"* (27:62), the verse that pairs the answer to the drowning cry with the command to remember, which is precisely what the delivered passengers of 29:65 fail to do.

**"Behold, They Associate" — The Astonishment of *Idhā***

The small word *idhā* before "they associate" (not *fa-yushrikūna* but *idhā hum yushrikūn*) is an exclamation of astonishment, the way a narrator says "and look — they do it again," and the Qur'an uses it to make the reader see the switch as a spectacle rather than a sequence; it is the same abrupt *idhā* that introduces the scenes of the Balance, "then behold, he is given his book in his left hand" (69:25) and the scene of the Trumpet: "it is but a single shout, and behold, they are looking" (37:19). Al-Rāzī, whose reading the source commentary records, draws the verse's sharpest application: the storm is a parable for the afflictions of life in general, and the ship's moment of sincerity is a picture of a religion that exists only in emergency — the man who prays when the diagnosis comes and forgets when the remission comes, who is close to God in the queue outside the operating theatre and absent from it afterwards. Rāzī adds that it is this attachment to what follows the deliverance — the resumed business of the world — that prevents many from coming to belief at all, because a person who has tasted the clarity of verse 65's shipboard hour and then returned to *laʿb wa-lahw* (29:64) has, in effect, made the emergency into his only temple. The verse thus performs its argument differently from the previous three: not a concession extracted by questioning ("they will surely say: God") but a confession extracted by drowning, and the sūrah's point is that a God acknowledged only under duress is an acknowledgment that convicts, since the duress was itself His speech to them.

**The Social Sea: What the Ship Adds to the Sūrah's Ethic**

The ship in the Qur'an is also a society, and the tradition used it that way, which is why this verse has a practical dimension beyond psychology. The well-known parable the Prophet ﷺ gave for a community in which some take and others lose is told in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī: the people of a ship who drew lots for the deck, some of whom began to bore holes in the part of the hull allotted to them, with the answer that if those on deck let them go on they would all perish, and if they restrained them by the hand all would be saved — a hadith the jurists cited for the duty of commanding the right and forbidding the wrong, which Midian's crime at 29:36 was and which the ship's crew cannot afford. The image fits 29:65 exactly: those on a ship are the paradigm case of people whose individual and collective fates are the same, and the verse's charge of *shirk* is not only a error about God but a false account of the situation — the idols are the passengers who promise to stop the leak. The verse's Makkan audience, for whom the Red Sea crossing to Abyssinia was a real route of flight and trade (29:56, the migration the sūrah commands), would have heard the danger as concrete, and the Community's own practice at the moment of embarkation keeps the verse's realism and dependence together. The words the Prophet ﷺ is reported to have said when he settled on his mount are the verse's own: *"glory be to Him who subjected this to us, and we were not going to master it, and to our Lord we shall indeed be returned"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim; the words are Qur'an 43:12–13, where the riding of ships and cattle is itself numbered among the signs), and Noah's own sentence at the launching of the first ark — "ride in it; in the name of God is its running and its anchoring; surely my Lord is Forgiving, Merciful" (11:41) — became the formula recited on boarding, transmitted in the sunan collections with chains the muḥaddithūn graded variously. The difference between the ship of 29:65 and the ship of 29:15 is, in the end, that one set of passengers named God before the storm and the others only during it, and the practice of the second group's Prophet ﷺ was to make the naming a habit of the dry land.

**The Verse's Consolation and Its Mirror**

Two lessons, one for the delivered and one for the watching. The verse's promise is buried in a conditional: "when He delivers them" — the deliverance is attributed to God's acting, not to seamanship, and the sūrah has already established the same grammar for the sea at 29:15 and 11:41 (Noah's ark as rescue) and 20:39 (the infant's basket on the Nile, "and I cast upon you love from Me" (20:39). Whoever is reading the verse in safety is therefore reminded that safety is an answered prayer they do not remember making, and whoever is reading it in danger is reminded that the prayer of the ship is available before the storm: the sincerity the sea extracts is not a gift of the sea but the human being's real religion, which verse 45 told him how to keep in ordinary weather. The verse is thus the sūrah's warning against the emergency-only God, and its comfort to those whose emergency is not a storm but a persecution, since they too are calling upon Him "devoting the religion" under pressure (29:11 "God knows those who believe"), and the sūrah has promised them what the delivered passengers refuse: not merely land, but "lofty chambers" (29:58). The last thing to notice is the tense. The verse does not say the polytheists will go back to their partners; it says *idhā hum yushrikūn*, in the dramatic present, and the sūrah's audience is expected to recognise the tense as their own: each of them has, at some hour, been the drowning man who meant every word and the dry man who forgot it, and the chapter's final verse (29:69) is addressed precisely to the ones who strive to keep the two hours identical.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:66

> **Then let them be ungrateful for what We have given them, and let them enjoy themselves — but soon they will know!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command That Is Not a Permission**

*fakfurū mā ātaynāhum wa-tamattaʿū fa-sawfa taʿlamūn.* The sūrah's most startling sentence is a pair of imperatives addressed to the ungrateful: "be ungrateful," "enjoy yourselves." No one in the tradition read them as a licence, and the grammar explains why: this is the imperative of abandonment and threat (*amr al-tahkīm wa'l-ta'yīs*), the tone of a speaker who has stopped warning because the hearer has stopped listening — "go, you are free" — and the Qur'an's own gloss of the same idiom is in the scene with Iblīs, whose demand is granted in the very terms that sentence him: "he said: my Lord, because You have led me astray, I will sit in ambush for them on Your straight path, and I will come upon them from the right and from the left and from before them and from behind them — and You will not find most of them thankful. He said: go forth from it, despised, driven off; and whoever of them follows you — I shall fill Hell with you all" (38:82–85). A permission that is at the same time a verdict is exactly the force of "let them be ungrateful" here. Here the two imperatives do the same work in a single breath: the pleasure is granted and the pleasure is indicting. The source commentary on this verse puts it exactly: "though they think the diversion and play of this world is good… in fact this respite that God has granted them lets them dig a deeper hole for themselves."

**"Let Them Enjoy Themselves": the Verse's Link to 29:64**

The verb *tamattaʿa* (to enjoy a provision, *matāʿ*) takes the reader back two verses, where the world was defined as *laʿb wa-lahw* and the other abode as *ḥayawān*. The logic of the sequence is severe: if this life is play, then enjoyment of it is neither a crime nor a possession; it is the short use of something already on loan, and the sūrah's phrase for the loan is elsewhere explicit — "say: the provision of God is more lasting, and the reward of the Hereafter is better and more lasting" (87:16–17 with 62:11: "when they see a trade or an amusement they scatter to it and leave you standing. Say: what is with God is better than amusement and than trade"). Two further Qur'anic sentences supply the psychology: "leave them to eat and enjoy themselves, and let *am-al* (hope, long expectation) divert them" (31:24), where the "enjoyment" and the "being diverted" are bound together as cause and effect, and the word *am-al* names the specific temptation of the prosperous — the indefinite extension of a lease they were told is short; and the warning that goes with the permission: "and do not incline toward the wrongdoers, lest the fire touch you, and you have no protectors apart from God, and you will not be helped" (11:113). The verse's tone is thus not indifference but a physician's: the enjoyment is the diagnostic symptom, and "soon they will know" is the prognosis.

**"Soon They Will Know": the Sūrah's Last Word to the Defiant**

*fa-sawfa taʿlamūn* is the closing formula of a class of Qur'anic threats, and its work is to transfer certainty from the present to a moment the hearer cannot schedule. Its twins are the sūrah of the Moon — *"tomorrow they will know who the liar, the insolent one, is"* (54:26) — and the sūrah of the Sovereignty — *"but when they see what they are promised, the faces of the disbelievers will be distorted, and it will be said: this is what you used to call for"* (67:27). In our chapter the phrase completes a chain of knowledge-claims: the demand for evidence ("why have signs not been sent down upon him from his Lord?", 29:50), the demand that the threat be executed on the spot ("and they bid thee hasten the punishment. Yet were it not for a term appointed, the punishment would have come upon them. And it will surely come upon them suddenly, while they are unaware", 29:53), and the verdict of the covering Day ("taste that which you used to do!", 29:55); the sūrah's refrain was "if they but knew" at 29:41 and 29:64, and now the conditional becomes a future indicative — they *will* know. Al-Ṭabarī's note is the blunt one: "God has informed them that they will know the reality of what they are being promised, in a knowledge that cannot be denied." The shift from "if they knew" (a wish, addressed to those who could still learn) to "soon they will know" (a fact, addressed to those who will not) is the sūrah's way of marking the end of the argument it has been conducting for sixty verses: at some point the invitation stops and only the schedule remains.

**"For What We Have Given Them" — Ingratitude as the Actual Sin**

The object of the imperatives is worth pausing on: *mā ātaynāhum*, "what We gave them." The ungratefulness the verse commands them to continue in is therefore not directed at the goods but at the Giver, and this is the point at which 29:66 gathers up the whole of the sūrah's final section: provision was attributed to God (29:60), the sun and moon were declared subjected by Him (29:61), the stretching and narrowing of livelihood to His will and knowledge (29:62), and the rain and the revival of the dead earth to Him (29:63). What the polytheists "ascribe" to partners, on this account, is not the creation but the thanks, and thanks is a distribution — which is why the sūrah's theology of prosperity is an economics. The same diagnosis produces the Book's most detailed story of ingratitude, that of the two towns and the three messengers in Yā Sīn (36:13–16), and the most detailed story of gratitude, Sulaymān's answer to the news of the hoopoe and the ants' community: "this is from the favour of my Lord, to test me whether I am grateful or ungrateful; and whoever is grateful is grateful only for his own self, and whoever is ungrateful — my Lord is Rich, Generous" (27:40). Between these two, the sūrah of 29:66 also has a word for its own audience, since it has spent its middle on people who had been given and were being tried: "whoever is grateful is grateful for his own self, and whoever is ungrateful — my Lord is Rich, Generous" is exactly the logic of 29:6, "whoever strives, strives only for himself; and God is Rich of the worlds," repeated now in the vocabulary of thanks instead of effort. The two verses are the sūrah's bracket: the same God who needs nothing either way, and the same self which is the only thing that gains or loses by the transaction.

**The Doctrine of *Istidrāj*, and the Pastoral Point**

The tradition read this verse through the concept of *istidrāj* — being "dragged on gradually" by being given what one wants while one is disobeying. The commentary literature cites for it the verse of the sixth sūrah: "so when they forgot what they had been reminded of, We opened to them the doors of everything, until, in what they were made to rejoice, We seized them suddenly, and behold, they were in despair" (6:44), and the prophetic saying that the exegetes attach to that passage: *"when you see God giving a servant, while he disobeys Him, what he loves — that is only an istidrāj"* (a report transmitted in Musnad Aḥmad and judged by the muḥaddithūn variously). The doctrine is pastoral before it is polemical, because its first use in a congregation is to break a habit of mind: prosperity is not a certificate, and a run of successes is not evidence of being right. It also explains the sūrah's earlier refusal to let the objectors set the timetable — they ask for the punishment to be hastened (29:53) precisely because their enjoyment has begun to feel like vindication; and it is why "enjoy yourselves" here is not a reward for their indifference but its exposure: what is being given to them, on this reading, is time, the same time the patient believers are told to use (29:59) and the same term that is already appointed for both (29:53). Ibn Kathīr's summary of the family of verses is: the enjoyment is short, the reckoning is not skipped, and the person who reads a delay as an approval has misread the very verse that grants the delay.

**What the Verse Leaves the Reader to Do**

A final observation about the verse's two audiences. To the persecutors it hands a threat that needs no enforcement by the community, and the sūrah's repeated "soon they will know" is why the Makkan believers could be told, in the same chapter, to turn away from their tormentors and leave the judgment (29:24, "then he turned away from them and from what they worship besides God"; 29:25, "and he said: I take God as my witness — do you not see what I am leaving?"), because the punishment in this verse belongs to God's calendar rather than to theirs. To the believer who envies, the verse hands an antidote that the tradition used constantly: look at the enjoyment as a loan with a term, not as a verdict, and remember the "doors of everything" of 6:44 as the description of a condition, not of a blessing. And to the one who is himself being given the enjoyment, the verse hands the hardest question in the sūrah, which is also the question verse 2 was asking all along: when the ease is total and the warning is only words, does the gratitude hold? The sūrah's answer to that question is in its last verse, where the striving ones are promised guidance and God is said to be "with the doers of beauty" (29:69) — with the implication that beauty of act, and not comfort of circumstance, is the sign the verse leaves us to look for.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:67

> **Have they not seen that We made a safe sanctuary, while people are being carried off around them? Then is it the false they believe in, and the blessing of God they deny?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Sign Is Their Own Street**

*a-wa-lam yaraw annā jaʿalnā ḥaraman āminan wa-yutakhaffafu'n-nāsu min ḥawlihim.* The series of arguments has run from the cosmos (29:61) to sustenance (29:62) to rain (29:63) to a storm at sea (29:65) to a threat (29:66); verse 67 stops the procession and points downward, at the pavement under the audience's feet. The verb of seeing is the sūrah's own verb of evidence — the same *a-wa-lam yaraw* that opened the section at 29:63 about the rain and that the Book uses wherever a blessing is being thrown back at the beneficiary: "do they not look at the sky above them — how We built it and adorned it, and it has no flaw?" (50:6); "have they not seen that God, who created the heavens and the earth and was not tired by creating them, is able to give the dead life?" (46:33) — and here its object is not the sky but the city. Makkah's peace was an anomaly in its geography, and the anomaly was the miracle: an armed, tribal, trade-dependent town in a peninsula of raiding, whose men slept outside walls with their wealth unguarded, while the great powers on either side were perpetually at war. The verse's *ḥaram* is thus offered as a datum in the same argument as rain and navigation: an explanation is required, and the people who refuse to give it are the same people who credit the explanation to idols.

**Abraham's Prayer Has Two Halves, and They Kept One**

Two verses frame the sanctuary's theology, and both come from the mouth of the man the sūrah has already presented as its own (29:16–27). *"And when Abraham said: my Lord, make this city secure, and keep me and my sons from worshipping idols"* (14:35) — the prayer asks, in one breath, for physical safety and for deliverance from *bāṭil*; and *"and when Abraham said: my Lord, make this a secure city and provide its people with fruits — whoever of them believes in God and the Last Day"* (2:126), to which the answer came: "He said: and whoever disbelieves, I shall grant him a little enjoyment, then compel him to a punishment of the Fire — and what an evil destination" (2:126). The Makkan audience inherited the first half of Abraham's request and refused the condition attached to the second: which is exactly the structure of 29:67's two questions — the *niʿma* they deny is not only safety but the tawḥīd that Abraham asked to be preserved alongside it, and the Qur'an elsewhere makes the pairing explicit: *"and they say: if we were to follow the guidance with you, we would be swept from our land. Did We not establish them in a sacred sanctuary, secure, while people are snatched away around them?"* (28:57), the objection that answers itself by the very security 29:67 cites — for in that verse the Qur'an quotes the polytheists admitting, in their own words, that the sanctuary is God's doing and not theirs; and *"is God not sufficient for His servant? And they threaten you with those besides Him"* (39:36), the verse that turns an inherited safety into a question about whom one actually fears.

**"People Are Carried Off Around Them": the Verb of the Prey**

*yutakhṭafu'n-nāsu min ḥawlihim* — the passive of *khaṭfa*, the snatching of a bird that drops on a chick, a word the Qur'an uses for what is taken by force from the air: "as though the birds snatch him (takhṭafuhu al-ṭayru) from a place far away" (22:31). The passive is important: the neighbours are not described as dying or being defeated in battle, but as *prey*, seized by a raid or an army or an empire, and the image's terror is its arbitrariness — the frontier tribes of Arabia lived in a condition of permanent, unplanned vulnerability, and the ḥaram was the one zone where the rule of prey was suspended. The source's commentators (Ṭabarī, Zamakhsharī) record the concrete referent: the fighting that was taken up everywhere except this valley, and the historical memory behind it — God's handling of the army of the Elephant, which the sūrah of Quraysh sets beside the two blessings of this verse in a single couplet: *"for the covenants of Quraysh, their covenants for the winter and summer journeys — so let them worship the Lord of this House, who fed them from hunger and secured them from fear"* (106:1–4), a passage whose two nouns, *juʿ* and *khawf*, are precisely provision (29:60–62) and safety (29:67), and whose verb "let them worship" is the response 29:67 finds missing.

**"The False" — What Unreality Means in This Sūrah**

*a-fa-bi'l-bāṭili yuʾminūn*: the question marks not the idols as objects of worship but as the *kind* of thing believed — *bāṭil*, that which is void, without weight, without purchase on what is. The Qur'an's own definition is in a single line: *"and say: the truth has come, and the false has vanished; surely the false was ever vanishing"* (17:81). Truth, by contrast, in this sūrah is something that *comes*: "or he denies the truth when it came to him" (29:68), and the sūrah's theology of *bāṭil* is thus not that it is a rival reality but that it is what does not survive contact with what is — the fly in the simile of 22:73, "the fly robs him of what it takes, and he cannot take it back from it." To believe in *bāṭil* is therefore to stake one's security on the one entity in the picture that cannot even retrieve its own theft. And the verse's second half names the mechanism: *wa-bi-niʿmati'llāhi yakfurūn*, "and the blessing of God they cover over" — *kufr* here in its root sense of wrapping and hiding, the same verb as at 16:83, *"and they recognize (yaʿrifūna) the blessing of God and then deny it, though most of them are ungrateful"*, a verse worth quoting in full because its first verb concedes what the last denies: they are not ignorant of the blessing, they are hiding it, and the concealment is what makes it ingratitude rather than error.

**The Believer Is More Sacred Than the House**

The verse's second movement belongs to the Community as much as to the idolaters, and the Prophet's ﷺ use of the sanctuary makes the transfer explicit. In the sermon he gave on the day of the conquest, reported in the two Ṣaḥīḥs, he said: "surely God made Makkah sacred on the day He created the heavens and the earth; it is sacred by God's sanctity until the Day of Resurrection. It was not lawful for anyone before me and it is not lawful for anyone after me; it was lawful for me only for an hour of a day. Its game is not to be driven and its trees are not to be cut"; and in the Farewell Sermon at Minā, in the same collections: "God has made your blood, your properties and your honours sacred to you, like the sanctity of this day of yours, in this month of yours, in this city of yours". The logic of 29:67 is preserved entire: the *ḥaram* was an unmerited trust, and the trust was discharged by extending its protection to persons rather than places, "the Muslim is sacred to the Muslim: his blood, his property and his honour" (Muslim 2564). This is also why the sūrah's ethics of persecution matter here: verses 29:10–11 already established that the real sanctuary is a conscience, and the man who has the pavement's safety without the conscience has the blessing without the Giver, which is the condition verse 67 describes. This is also the ground on which the jurists argued that a sanctuary's inviolability is a covenant with consequences rather than a property of soil: it binds whoever enters it, and its protection is forfeited by those who break it, which is why the law of the ḥaram was said to be lodged in the Community's keeping, and why a person may carry a ḥaram in his breast while the ḥaram itself is being raided.

**Why Verse 67 Is the Pivot into the Last Two Verses**

Structurally, the verse is the hinge between the section about the world's distraction (29:64–66) and the two closing sentences about falsehood and striving (29:68–69), and its two halves do the hinge's work. The first half — "have they not seen" — completes the evidentiary sequence: everything from provision to rain to safety has been attributed to a single Agent, so no excuse remains; the second half — "then is it the false they believe" — states the alternative to that sight, and the alternative has a name that the next verse takes up and indicts as the greatest of wrongs, "fabricating a lie against God" (29:68). A lie against God is, on the verse's own account, exactly what a *bāṭil* is when it is made an object of belief: not a neutral error but an attribution, and the attribution is of the blessing (29:63's rain to "God," 29:61's sun and moon to "God," 29:67's safety to nobody). The verse therefore sets up the sūrah's final pair as a choice between two ways of being unsafe: to be snatched around one's own sanctuary while trusting an unreality, or to strive and be guided into a way that ends with God's being "with the doers of beauty" (29:69). And the last thing the verse says about its hearers is a question, twice, in a chapter full of questions; a chapter that began by asking whether people "think they will be left to say: we believed, and they will not be tested" (29:2) ends by asking whether they see the sanctuary they live in. The first question was a challenge to a claim of belief; the last is a challenge to a life of unexamined safety, and both are answered, in the end, by the same thing: *fa-sawfa taʿlamūn* and *la-nahdiyyannahumul-ṭuruq*.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:68

> **Who does greater wrong than one who fabricates a lie against God, or denies the truth when it comes unto him? Is there not within Hell an abode for the disbelievers?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Formula the Qur'an Uses Nine Times**

*wa-man aẓlamu mimman iftarā ʿala'llāhi kadhiban aw kadhdhaba bi'l-ḥaqqi idhā jāʾahu.* The construction — "and who is more unjust than the one who…" — is one of the Book's fixed rhetorical instruments, and it recurs almost verbatim in the sūrah of the Cattle three times (6:21, 6:93, 6:144), in al-Aʿrāf (7:37), in Yūnus (10:17), in Hūd (11:18), in the Cave (18:15), in the Family of ʿImrān ("whoever then invents a lie about God after that — those are the wrongdoers", 3:94), and in the Ranks ("and who does greater wrong than one who fabricates a lie about God, while he is being called to Islam?", 61:7). The version nearest ours is the one the source cites as its parallel: *"so whoever does greater wrong than one who fabricates a lie about God, in order to mislead people without knowledge — surely God does not guide the wrongdoing people"* (6:21), and the twin in the sūrah of the Troops: *"then who does greater wrong than one who tells a lie against God, and denies the truth when it comes to him — is there not in Hell an abode for the disbelievers?"* (39:32). That the formula is repeated so often is itself a piece of the sūrah's argument: the Qur'an does not treat this sin as one offence among many but as the genus of which the others are species, because a lie told about God is the one misstatement that corrupts the standard by which every other statement is measured.

**The Root of Lying, and Why Fabrication Is Its Worst Case**

The word is *iftirāʾ*, from a root whose basic sense the lexicographers give as a cutting or cleaving apart — a statement severed from what is the case — and the intensive stem marks the deliberate construction of an unreality rather than the accidental mistake (the classical lexica distinguish *kidhb*, a report that fails to match the fact, from *iftirāʾ*, a kidhb that is fabricated and then asserted with a claim to authority). The sūrah's own vocabulary shows why the distinction matters. Its objectors had already made precisely this accusation in the other direction: "and those who disbelieve say: this is nothing but a lie he has fabricated, and a people other than him helped him with it. They have come with an evil thing and a lie. And they say: tales of the ancients, which he had written out" (25:4–5); and the sūrah of Prostration puts the matter at its simplest: "or do they say: he fabricated it? Rather it is the truth from your Lord" (32:3). Verse 68 answers the accusation not by defending the Prophet's ﷺ honesty point by point but by taking the word out of their hands and returning it with a superlative attached: whoever is inventing about God is the one doing what they feared, and the invention is the association they were already practising. This is why the second half of the verse is joined to the first with *aw* rather than explained separately: to fabricate about God and to deny the truth when it arrives are the same act viewed from two sides, since a denial of a delivered message is itself an assertion about God — that He did not send it, or did not mean it.

**"The Truth When It Comes to Him" — Three Referents, One Fact**

The source commentary records the classical range: "the truth" may be the Prophet, the Qur'an, or the message of divine unity and everything it entails, and the exegetes did not have to choose, because the verse's grammar makes the truth an event — *idhā jāʾahu*, "when it comes to him," not "when it exists." The Makkan situation gave the phrase a literal texture: the truth came to them as a man in their own district, forty years old, known to them, unlettered (29:48 already made his unread books the proof), claiming to be sent, and asking them to give up the religion of their fathers in exchange for nothing they could inspect in advance. Against this background, three kinds of denial are in view: denial of the messenger, denial of the book, and denial of what both assert; and the chapter has already addressed all three in order — the demand for a more visible sign (29:50), the accusation of forgery (answered at 25:5 and by 29:48 here), and the refusal of the theology of "worship Me alone" (29:56 and 29:61–63's concession-questions). A rhetorical question then closes: "is there not in Hell an abode for the disbelievers?" — *a-laysa*, the form that demands a yes the hearers cannot give without convicting themselves; the source records Ṭabarī's and Zamakhsharī's judgement that this is "a rhetorical question meaning that they are undoubtedly in Hell." The word for the abode is *mathwā*, a place where one stays and where one's bed is spread, and it is the same noun the sūrah of the Troops uses twenty-eight verses later for the arrogant (39:60), so that the punishment is described not as an arbitrary infliction but as the fitting residence for people who made untruth their dwelling.

**Lying as the Sūrah's Own Subject**

Read inside the sūrah, verse 68 is the last of the chapter's five *fitna*-related passages, and the fitna-word appears in the verse that best explains it. When the inventors of unreality are confronted, "their trial (*fitna*) was only that they said: by God our Lord, we were not associating anything. See how they lie against themselves, and what they used to invent has strayed from them" (6:22–24). The three motions in those lines are the motions of this verse: a lie about God, a denial of the truth when it arrives, and then the loss of the invented thing at the one moment it was needed — which is the spider-house of 29:41 arriving in its final state. And the sūrah has already promised the questioning: "then they will be questioned about what they used to invent" (29:13), the passage it belongs to is about the social mechanics of fabricated authority: "those who disbelieve say to those who believe: follow our path, and we shall bear your sins — but they will not bear anything of their sins; they are surely liars. They will surely bear their own burdens and other burdens along with their own, and on the Day of Resurrection they will be questioned about what they used to invent" (29:12–13). The claim being tried is the one that makes a lie about God a communal enterprise: that obedience to one's elders or one's leaders excuses what a man says in God's name. Verse 68 is the verdict on that economy, and it is the last word the sūrah addresses to the defiant before it turns back to the believers: no one inherits another's lie, and no lie about God can be defended on the ground that it was inherited.

**The Practical Sin: Speaking for God Without Knowledge**

The verse's severity is easier to grasp when the offence is described as the tradition described it — not a false statement about God's attributes in a lecture hall, but the use of God's name to end a human argument. The Qur'an's own prohibition of the practice is explicit: "and do not say, concerning that which your tongues describe in falsehood: this is lawful and this is unlawful, so as to fabricate a lie about God — surely those who fabricate a lie about God will not prosper" (16:116); and the sūrah of the Heights names the principle: "say: my Lord has forbidden indecencies, open and secret, and sin, and tyranny without right, and that you associate with God what He did not send down for it an authority, and that you say about God what you do not know" (7:33). Four prohibitions, and the last is the one on which the first three depend, because whoever can license an act in God's name has thereby rewritten the law. This is the level at which verse 68 addresses the believing community too — anyone who settles a matter of conduct with "God wants this" or "God forbids that" without an authority is inside the verse's scope, and the Prophet's ﷺ warning that "whoever says about me a lie deliberately, let him take his seat in the Fire" (Bukhari 106; Muslim 3) makes the danger concrete at the second order: the lie about the Messenger is the road to the lie about God.

**The Inverse Verse, and the Verse's Consolation**

The Qur'an supplies the positive counterpart in the same formula, and it is the sūrah's own standard for what a believer is: "and the one who brings the truth and the one who confirms it — those are the ones who are careful of God. They will be forgiven, and they will have a generous provision" (39:33). The structure is exactly inverted: where 29:68 has a fabrication against God plus a denial of the arriving truth, 39:33 has a truthful saying about God plus an assent to the arriving truth, and the two rewards attached — forgiveness and a generous provision — answer the verse's two questions, an abode in Hell replaced by a provision, and the loss of the invented thing replaced by being forgiven for the things invented about one. The same inversion appears in the vocabulary of character: the Prophet ﷺ said "hold to truthfulness, for truthfulness leads to righteousness (*birr*), and righteousness leads to the Paradise, and a man keeps being truthful until he is written with God as a truthful one; and beware of lying, for lying leads to wickedness, and wickedness leads to the Fire, and a man keeps lying until he is written with God as a liar" (Bukhari 6094, in the Book of Trade; Muslim 2614, in the Book of Righteousness). The record of habit is what 29:68's last question is about: a man does not arrive at fabricating about God in a single leap but by the accumulation of small untruths that make the last leap thinkable, which is why the sūrah's verdict is addressed to a type, not to a moment.

**"Is There Not in Hell an Abode for the Arrogant?"**

The final clause of the source's note on this verse gives the variant of the same question two places later in the Qur'an: "is there not in Hell an abode for the arrogant?" (39:60), and the shift of the noun from "the disbelievers" to "the arrogant" is the commentary's last clarification: the lie about God is not an intellectual accident but a posture, and the posture is refusing to be told what is the case by anyone who has no standing in one's own eyes. Verse 39:60 supplies the imagery: "and on the Day of Resurrection you will see those who lied against God — their faces blackened," the blackening of faces being the visible form of a self-invention that failed at inspection. The commentators' summary of the verse keeps the same practical tone: the one who fabricates a lie about God, or denies the truth when it comes to him, has wronged his own soul — the offence lands on the sinner, not on God. The sūrah has already said the same in the register of effort ("and whoever strives, strives only for himself; surely God is Rich of the worlds", 29:6), and the Qur'an says it in the register of thanks ("whoever is grateful is grateful only for his own self, and whoever is ungrateful — my Lord is Rich, Generous", 27:15) and in the register of deeds ("whoever does righteous deeds, they are for himself; and whoever is evil, it is upon himself", 41:46). Verse 68 is thus the last of the sūrah's indictments, and its function is to remove the final excuse: the objectors' claim that their religion is ancestral, courteous and harmless. On the verse's account it is none of those things; it is the one act whose wrongness is maximal, because it fixes the meaning of every other wrong. What remains is what the last verse of the chapter says about the people who do not need this verdict — those who strive.

---
## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:69

> **But as for those who strive for Us, We shall surely guide them in Our ways. Truly God is with the virtuous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Last Sentence Is a Reply to Its First**

*wa'lladhīna jāhadū la-nahdiyyannahum subulanā, wa-inna'llāha la-maʿa'l-muḥsinīn.* Sixty-eight verses ago the chapter asked: "do the people suppose that they will be left to say: we believe, and they will not be tested?" (29:2). The question supposed two things — that belief would be tested, and that the tested would be left alone to face it.
