The last verse answers both halves: the testing is real and is called *jihād*, and the tested are not left, because they are guided and because God is with them. The word-for-word reversal is deliberate. *Yutraku* ("be left") at the beginning becomes *la-nahdiyyannahum* ("We shall surely guide them") at the end; the passive of abandonment becomes an active divine verb with an oath attached; and the sentence's final noun, *al-muḥsinūn*, is the plural of the one who makes his act beautiful, which is the state the sūrah's whole ethic has been building toward: patient (29:58–59), grateful (against the ingratitude of 29:66), trustworthy (29:10's opposite of hypocrisy), and at last beautiful in what he does. A chapter that opens with metallurgy — "does mankind suppose that they will be left to say: we believe, and that they will not be tried, though We have indeed tried those who were before them? Surely God knows those who are truthful, and surely He knows the liars" (29:2–3) — closes with the assayed people named by their quality rather than by their ordeal.

**"For Us": The Object of the Striving**

The verse does not say "whoever strives" but "whoever strives *for Us* (lanā)," and the source commentary's summary of the classical reading turns entirely on that pronoun: "those who strive for Us refers to those who strive with their very souls against Satan, against their own lower souls, and against the enemies of religion." The three fronts are ranked, and the ranking is the point — the enemy one cannot see, the enemy one is born with, and the enemy one meets in the open, in that order of difficulty. That the effort must be "for Us" is what prevents the first two from collapsing into self-improvement: the same hours of vigilance over one's temper or one's appetite can be an act of worship or an act of vanity, and the pronoun is the difference. The Prophet ﷺ said "the strong man is not the wrestler; the strong man is the one who owns himself at the moment of anger" (in the two Ṣaḥīḥs), and in a report transmitted in Musnad Aḥmad: "the mujāhid is the one who strives against his own self in God's obedience, and the muhājir is the one who flees sins and wrongs." The verse's promise, then, is not addressed to exertion as such but to exertion with an addressee — and if the addressee is God, the striver is not performing for an audience, which is the sin the sūrah already located at the centre of the spider's house (29:41).

**"We Shall Surely Guide Them": An Oath, Not a Suggestion**

*la-nahdiyyannahum* carries both the oath-*lām* and the two *nūn*s of emphasis, the strongest affirmative construction available in Arabic, and the sūrah has used the same doubled emphasis for its other great promise: "We shall surely lodge them in the lofty chambers, beneath which rivers flow" (29:58). Housing and guidance are thus bound together with the same grammatical solemnity, and the pairing is worth pausing on, because in ordinary speech a promise of direction would be subordinate to a promise of reward. Here the order is reversed, and reversed twice: the verse promises guidance *first*, and it promises guidance in *ways* — *subul*, plural, in the phrase "Our ways." The plural is the sūrah's answer to the demand that a single, inspectable route be laid out (29:50 "why have signs not been sent down upon him from his Lord?"), and the Qur'an itself glosses it: *"God guides by it whoever follows His good pleasure to the ways of peace (subula's-salām), and brings them out of the darkness into the light by His permission, and guides them to a straight path"* (5:16) — many ways of peace, arriving at one path. The guidance promised is therefore not a map handed over at the start of the journey but a continuing provision supplied along it, which is why the same verse-family speaks of increase: *"and those who were guided — He increases them in guidance and gives them their consciousness of God"* (47:17), and of the Companions of the Cave: *"We were with them when they rose up and said: our Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth"* — "and We increased them in guidance" (18:13–14).

**The Law the Verse States: One Good Earns the Next**

The source commentary records the classical readings of the promised guidance as a set of cases, and the principle underneath them is stated plainly: "This verse is subject to many interpretations, each reflecting the belief that striving to obtain one good will lead to another good." Its list is worth quoting in full, because each item is a pastoral instruction as much as an exegesis: "some say it means that those who strive to act upon what they know will be guided to greater knowledge that they did not have before; those who strive to obtain knowledge will be guided to the ways of acting in accord with it; those who strive to do what is pleasing to God will be guided to the attainment of contentment; those who strive in repentance will be guided upon the paths of sincerity; or those who strive to serve God will be guided upon the path of intimate converse with God." The maxim the exegetes attached to the first of these — "whoever does what he knows is given what he does not know," the saying of al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī and of much of the piety literature — has the shape of a law rather than a hope, and the law is what makes the verse so frequently quoted by teachers: the second increment of understanding is not purchased by more information but by obedience to the information already held. It is also, in plain terms, the mechanism of every skill: capacity is built by use, and no one acquires the ear of a musician or the eye of a diagnostician by reading about it. The Qur'an states the same causal chain in a warning form: "that is because they believed, then they disbelieved, so their hearts are sealed over, and they do not understand" (63:3) — and the negative of that sentence is 29:69's positive: as refusal consolidates into a disposition, so striving consolidates into guidance.

**"God Is with the Virtuous": The Only Verse of Its Kind**

The source note on the verse marks its uniqueness: "although several verses speak of God's love for the virtuous (2:195; 3:134, 148; 5:13, 93), this is the only verse to say that God is with the virtuous." Love names a verdict; *maʿiyya* names a position — and the position is the thing the Makkan believers needed, since they had been told to leave fathers, mothers, and city (29:8, 29:56) and were being told that the emigrant's destination was a fire-proofed nowhere. "Withness" is used in the Qur'an in two registers, and the commentators' rule is the plain one: when the withness is coupled with a cause — patience, piety, iḥsān — it means help, support, and defence, and when it is stated without a cause it names God's knowledge and power over all things ("He is with you wherever you are," 57:4). Thus "God is with the patient" (2:153), "God is with those who guard against evil and with those who do beautiful things" (16:128), and here "God is with the muḥsinīn," in words the sūrah of the Bee has already paired with piety: "God is with those who guard against evil and with those who do beautiful things" (16:128). The hadith qudsi states the economy of it with a directness that the verse's abstractions do not: "God says: I am as My servant thinks of Me, and I am with him when he remembers Me; if he mentions Me in himself, I mention him in Myself, and if he mentions Me in an assembly, I mention him in an assembly better than it; and if he draws near to Me a hand's-span, I draw near to him a cubit; and if he comes to Me walking, I come to him running" (Bukhari 7405). *Maʿa*, *dhakara*, *taqarraba*, *ātā*: the sūrah's last word is the hadith's whole vocabulary, and the "striving" of 29:69 is, in the hadith's terms, a walking that is met with running.

**Iḥsān: Doing What Is Beautiful, in Every Category**

*iḥsān* is from the root ḥ-s-n, and, as the source note observes, *ḥusn* means both "good" and "beautiful," so that *muḥsin* is one who "does what is beautiful or makes things beautiful." The hadith of Jibrīl, in ʿUmar's report, defines it as a mode of attention rather than a category of act: "iḥsān is to worship God as though you see Him, and if you are not seeing Him, He is seeing you" (Muslim 8), and the wording of the second half preserves the point that the witness is not transferred to the worshipper's imagination but remains God's. The other ḥadīth the source note quotes is the more startling because of its scope: "God has ordained iḥsān in all things; so when you overcome, kill well, and when you slaughter, slaughter well, and let one of you sharpen his blade and let him spare suffering to the animal he slaughters" (Muslim 1955). A chapter on persecution, migration and the spider's frail house ends by generalising the standard to *everything*: the ethics of war, of butchery, of the sharpening of a tool, and by implication of a letter written to a father, a contract, a complaint lodged with a judge, a story told about an opponent. Fudayl b. ʿIyāḍ, commenting on "which of you is best in deeds" (67:2), said that the best of deed is the most sincere and the most correct, and when asked what that meant he said: "when a deed is done sincerely but not correctly it is not accepted; and when it is done correctly but not sincerely it is not accepted — until it is both sincerely for God and correctly on the pattern of the Messenger." Those two conditions are the two halves of this sūrah's ethic: "for Us," and in the ways — the intention and the route — and the verse's closing noun is what remains when both are present.

**What the Verse Does Not Promise, and What It Does**

It says nothing about comfort. The strivers are not promised that the storm will not come (29:65), that the *laʿb wa-lahw* of the neighbours will not look enviable (29:64, 29:66), or that their own house will not look frail (29:41). What is promised is that they will be shown the way and that they will not be shown it alone — and the promise's second half is what makes the first endurable, since guidance without company is information, and information has never been what the verse's audience lacked (29:48–52's whole argument was that they knew). This is the practical reason the sūrah's last sentence is a statement of accompaniment: the believer who is losing a lawsuit, failing to reform a habit, or being estranged from his family for his conversion is told that the deficit in his outcome is not the absence of a route but the presence of a Companion on it. And the sūrah's own psychology has prepared the reader for the claim: 29:69 is what 29:11's knowledge ("God surely knows those who are deceived") looks like from the other side — the same God who sees the pretence sees the effort, and verse 69 tells the second person what He does about it.

**Coda: The Spider's House and the Company of God**

The chapter is named after the frailest of houses, and its final verse leaves the reader inside a different kind of shelter — not a structure but a withness. Everything in between has been an argument for that shift: the fathers and mothers a convert may honour but not obey (29:8); the emigration that trades a city for a conscience (29:26, 29:56–58); the prophets whose salvages were the few who believed with them (29:14–27); the objection that the whole enterprise of prophethood is a story of human invention, answered by a proof that could not have been rehearsed (29:48); and the concession, extracted again and again from the polytheists' own mouths, that the rain, the sun, the moon, the livelihood and the safety of their city were not theirs (29:61–63, 29:67). What the sūrah has dismantled is the assumption that a house — of stone, of lineage, of idols, of a good reputation, of a settled life — is what keeps a person alive; and what it has left standing is a God who is said to be *with* the people who do beautifully, which is a category that no raid, no exile, and no death can enter. Two threads close. The first is Abraham's, whose departure is reported in the words of the one man who believed him: "and Lot believed him and said: truly I am fleeing unto my Lord; truly He is the Mighty, the Wise" (29:26) — to leave a false thing by fleeing to the true one is, in the end, the same act as striving for Him, and the sūrah has already given that flight a term and a lodging: "We shall surely settle them in lofty abodes of the Garden with rivers running below, therein to abide. Excellent indeed is the reward of the workers" (29:58). The second is the sūrah's own last word about those who were tested: they were not left. The verse's promise — *la-nahdiyyannahum subulanā* — is the answer to the question of 29:2, and the sūrah of the Spider ends where it began, with the trial re-named as the way, and the way re-named as company. God knows best, and to God belongs the praise for what He has enabled in His Book, whose ways are many and whose mercy is what gathers them into one.

---
**[End of the commentary on Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt]**
